# EL CONCEPTO DE LA HISTORIA UNIVERSAL Y LA FILOSOFÍA DE LOS VALORES — THE CONCEPT OF UNIVERSAL HISTORY AND THE PHILOSOPHY OF VALUES

### PRELIMINAR — PRELIMINARY NOTE

[p. 5] This effort does not imply a new philosophy of history, but a thought, perhaps plausible, on the very nature of historical knowledge, and its intimate differences and relations with Science, Art and Philosophy. As he went on drafting the work, the author told himself at moments that it was almost superfluous; for it seemed to him that common sense suffices to affirm that History is not science, art or philosophy, but history; that is, an irreducible knowledge, sui generis, with as much right to exist autonomously as any others that may be, positively, individual and irreducible. In the pages that follow, only this one concept is discussed, and nothing more than an opinion is put forward.

### CAPÍTULO I. LA INTERPRETACIÓN DE LA HISTORIA — CHAPTER I. THE INTERPRETATION OF HISTORY

#### 1. Un problema último y complejo — 1. An ultimate and complex problem

[p. 7] There has been an ultimate and complex problem for all modern thinkers: the interpretation of history. It became the rule to close the most elaborate philosophical expositions with it. The data of the question were supplied jointly by the various philosophical sciences, on the one hand, and by the history of humanity itself, on the other: “Among all the philosophical disciplines, the philosophy of history developed last. It is the only one to which antiquity contributed nothing essential, and it is, at the same time, the only one about whose object and task no agreement has yet been reached today.”<sup>1</sup> Hegel, historian of philosophy and philosopher of history, whose system is the apotheosis of the idea of progress, and Auguste Comte, under the name of sociology (in Comtean sociology there is something more and better than a pure philosophy of history; but that is there, essentially, besides the apotheosis itself): the greatest idealist philosopher and the greatest realist philosopher of the last century set forth, at great length, their systematic ideas on the meaning of the unfolding of human actions in time, and with these developments brought their own systematic philosophies to a close.

<sup>1</sup> Külpe, Introducción a la filosofía, p. 129.

#### 2. La causa de importancia superior — 2. The cause of superior importance

At the bottom of every interpretation of history carried out by modern philosophers, there stirs the common belief that Adolph Franck formulates thus: “Above the transitory, accidental causes, above the free individual wills that determine each event and suffice to explain each link of the chain, there is a cause, a law of superior importance that governs the whole. The search for such a cause and law constitutes, essentially, the philosophy of history.”

[p. 8] This is the universal characteristic of the various philosophies of history. Note in Franck’s statement the concern to find an entity, end, cause or law of importance superior to the whole; that is, the realist hypothesis, in the scholastic sense of the word, of granting to Humanity, or to some other universal entity, a certain peculiar action superior to concrete and diverse human acts; the metaphysical concern for a mysterious but most real reason that acts upon the transitory and accidental causes: the individual human actors of history.

#### 3. La filosofía de la historia es un problema moderno — 3. The philosophy of history is a modern problem

The problem of the philosophy of history is modern, essentially modern, as will be shown presently. The name of the discipline, philosophie de l’histoire, is due to Voltaire. The ancients knew neither the name nor the thing. They, who inherited the religious belief in a remote golden age, situated in the past, never sought an ideal plan or design of historical reality, a preconceived project for its gradual fulfilment in human actions; a teleology of their small and glorious world; and this, not because they had ceased to seek a meaning in life, or a supreme value of existence; nor because they were incapable of forging, like the moderns, utopias and cities of the sun —witness Plato’s Republic, generous paradigm of impossible redemptions,

“Aurea prima sata est aetas, quae, vindice nullo, sponte sua, sine lege fidem rectumque colebat” [The first age to be sown was golden, which, with no avenger, of its own accord, without law, cherished faith and right]

So sings Ovid in his Metamorphoses, explaining the four ages of the world, to plunge Saturn thereafter into Tartarus, and bestow the sceptre upon thundering Jupiter. Our iron age followed that of bronze, already fierce, but not criminal, as this one followed the silver age of Jupiter.

Under the reign of Saturn men lived like gods, free from cares, toil and suffering. Old age did not afflict them; and their feet and hands preserved the integrity of their own vigor. They died, the Greek poet Hesiod (from whom Ovid drew amply) tells us, “as if lulled by a gentle sleep”; and the fertile earth produced, of itself, abundant treasures. This first generation of men became, upon disappearing, the protector and tutelary guardian of humanity.

The second human race that Hesiod imagines was far inferior [p. 7] to the first. In this silver age, children, always inept, “grew for a hundred years in their native home”; and, on reaching puberty and adolescence, lived only a few years, weighed down by sorrows, the sad fruit of their folly; and since they offered no sacrifices to the gods, Jupiter annihilated them in his wrath.

The father of the gods formed a third human generation endowed with speech; and the age of bronze in nothing resembled that of silver. Robust, violent and terrible, men delighted in the “bloody labors of Mars,” and their implacable heart had the strength of steel. They bore weapons of bronze, of bronze were their houses, and they knew nothing of iron, “which did not yet exist.” Slain by their own hands, they descended to the gloomy abode of Pluto, leaving no name of their exploits; and, despite their fearsome strength, “death made them abandon the bright light of the Sun.”

Finally, Jupiter formed “the fourth race of heroes and demigods.” The deadly combats in which they found themselves engaged annihilated them all, before the seven gates of Thebes or in front of Troy. These fortunate heroes dwell in the Blessed Isles of the Ocean, and, three times during each year, “the earth lavishes upon them the delight of its fruits.”

“Would that the gods had not let me live among the men of the fifth generation!” exclaims Hesiod. For the reigning iron age is beginning, and men will not cease to toil and suffer by day, nor to be corrupted by night. “Jupiter will destroy this race of men endowed with speech when, almost from their birth, their hair turns white. The father will not be united with his son, nor the son with his father, nor the guest with his guest, nor the friend with the friend, nor will the brother, as before, be loved by his brother; and even children will despise the old age of their parents.” The evils of men will be forever irremediable.

The whole doctrine of antiquity on the movement of societies is summed up, as Ferrón says,<sup>2</sup> in this passage from the book “On the Nature of the Universe,” by Ocellus Lucanus: “As for this world, it is shifting and changeable. Societies are born, grow and die like men, to be replaced by other societies, as we ourselves shall be by other men”… But the most terrible of all the thoughts of antiquity on human development is the idea of the Eternal Return, which the Stoics held, and which Marcus Aurelius has preserved for us in his Soliloquies, declaring: “The things of the world are always the same in their [p. 8] orbital revolutions from age to age.” This is the thought that Nietzsche developed, upholding the eternal recurrence of things.

<sup>2</sup> Théorie du Progrès, p. 12.

#### 4. Antigüedad del concepto de evolución — 4. The antiquity of the concept of evolution

The concept of evolution is as old as philosophical thought itself; it may be said to have been born with it. Independent philosophy was scarcely dawning in Greece, and already Heraclitus made becoming the thesis of his philosophy, essentially opposed to the immobilism of the Eleatics. Antiquity philosophized about universal change, about the impermanence of beings and their attributes, but did not treat of the betterment of things. The concept of progress (pro, forward, and gressus, step, path), the idea of attaining something better in the course of time, was always unknown to the noble philosophy of the ancients.

It might, perhaps, be thought that the slight extent of the historical experience of antiquity prevented its thinkers from posing the problem adequately; but it is clear that, taking into account the great political, juridical and social experience, lasting and not meteoric, like the magnificent dream of Alexander; the greatest and, at any rate, the best in history, which was the Roman Empire, teleological ideologies might have been elaborated in the manner of those of Saint Augustine and Bossuet, for example, which would explain universal history as the providential march of the world toward the formation and integration of Caesar’s work. The wise impassivity of the fragment of Ocellus Lucanus is, nevertheless, a faithful synthesis of the ancient philosophy of history.

#### 5. El origen del concepto de progreso universal está en el profetismo hebreo — 5. The origin of the concept of universal progress lies in Hebrew prophetism

Isaiah, the highest representative of prophetism, formulated the most definite of the affirmations of the principle of progress. The wonder-worker teaches that, however dark the times may be, others will come, luminous and good for all peoples and not only for the Jews. Together in that age, as in that of Saturn, lambs and wolves will live; tender infants still at the breast and asps that will not harm them with their venom. Then, “The world shall be filled with the knowledge of God as the waters cover the sea.”

Nothing is more different from the Greek spirit than the Oriental. It seems impossible that two such dissimilar cultures could have been combined in the [p. 9] history of European civilization. Progress turns out to be the illusion of a small people, who lived by hoping and have not yet learned to renounce their messianism. Our Lord Jesus Christ, who taught us to hope in another world different from this one, offered us beatitude if we knew how to hope for other goods different from earthly goods; he is not, therefore, the Messiah of whom the Israelite dreamed. Isaiah preaches the triumph of Judaism under the aegis of his national myth. Jesus affirms the conservation of the highest human values, on another plane of reality, in another region of the universe. The kingdom of heaven is not the vision of deep patriotic bonds that inspires Isaiah. They taught their race the way to a distant good, yet realizable on earth; and they still foster those nostalgic for a mystical national power, unassailable. They disturbed the opinion of peoples with the zeal of their Jewish faith, theoretically contradictory, because it strives to achieve on earth what cannot be achieved here.

It is in Israel that the true origin of the idea of progress, as the modern philosophers developed it, must be seen. This faith is earthly, and its ultimate consequences, remote but undoubted, are the systems of Hegel and Comte, and the deification of Humanity. Historical materialism, which, according to Spengler’s observation, “does not think of renouncing, but of dividing up,” is the very faith of Israel, set up as a lure for humanity.

#### 6. La edad de oro fuera del mundo — 6. The golden age outside the world

During the Middle Ages, the golden age, which the Oriental religions, Greece and Rome placed in the past, and which modern times will place in the future, was constructed outside earthly existence. To this mystical City of God, which the genius of Saint Augustine defined for all time, the enlightened, orthodox and heterodox, resorted, to ask consolation for the sickness of living, so intensely felt in the most ill-fated moments of that era of human history.

The true creator of the philosophy of history was the great theologian who lived in the last years of the Roman Empire and eloquently celebrated its grandiose funeral; Saint Augustine is the first modern man, pagan in thought and Christian in heart. The philosophy of history has not succeeded in surpassing the mystical standpoint of the Bishop of Hippo. Those who profess the Catholic faith possess, in the City of God, the best systematic interpretation of history.

[p. 10] Only at the close of the renaissance of the thirteenth century, with Roger Bacon —a sporadic phenomenon of culture— is the idea once more voiced that the passage of time will improve men and give them greater good, by assuring them more knowledge. The future will know what we ignore, says Bacon, and will be astonished that we were ignorant of what is known to it. The later men arrive in the world, the better they enjoy the goods accumulated by the labor of the generations.

At the beginning of the sixteenth century, another herald, like the Franciscan of the thirteenth, insists on prophesying his faith in the advancement of humanity. This is Paracelsus, a chaotic man of the Renaissance, alchemist, theosophist and physician, who exclaims: “I address this book to those who think that new things are worth more than old ones, solely because they are newer”… Sublime reason!

The second Bacon is the true precursor of the great futurist superstition. His New Organon seeks to overthrow the Aristotelian canon of thought, systematized and formulated scholastically by the subtle medieval thinkers. His Great Instauration inaugurates the modern arrogance of striving to tear down human traditions (which are humanity itself in what it has most true and victorious), in order to give culture, as its support, the direct contact of man with the world. As if one could understand anything of the world without thinking it through the ideas that other generations bequeathed to us!

But if Bacon is the precursor of the great superstition of necessary and fatal progress, Descartes and his school are the true authors of the philosophical thought of progress, conceived as it is usually conceived in our day. Pascal founds the theory of equating the development of one man with the development of the whole of humanity, inspired, perhaps, by a thought of Saint Augustine. He personifies the isolated efforts, often disconnected, probably disharmonious, of peoples, in a single entity (Humanity) that constantly learns and constantly improves itself. Malebranche, another brilliant and frenzied Cartesian, disdains the historical sciences and abominates erudition and philology. Antiquity hinders him with its glory; he would like to destroy it, so as to oppose to a nonexistent past a radiant future. His mockery of humanistic culture seems the closest antecedent of Nietzsche’s Untimely Meditations. Malebranche proclaims that erudition is a retroactive ballast and a narcotic of action.

[p. 11] At last Leibniz arises, the great doctor of necessary progress, pontiff of optimism: “everything is arranged for the good in the best of possible worlds,” which improves itself each day for the greater glory of God. There is continual progress in the universe. Evil is only the preparation of good. The pain that revolts us will serve perhaps for a remote and sublime redemption: Nous profitons en quelque sorte du dommage lui-même [we profit in some way from the very harm]. Candide, the hero of Voltaire’s novel, is the pedagogical realization of the theological and moral ideas of the fervent Germanic thinker, who placed all his immense genius at the service of the modern preoccupation with progress. And after him, in France, in Germany, in Italy, in England, everywhere, the phalanx of believers springs up: Fontenelle,<sup>3</sup> Vico,<sup>4</sup> Lessing,<sup>5</sup> Herder,<sup>6</sup> Schiller,<sup>7</sup> Turgot,<sup>8</sup> Condorcet,<sup>9</sup> Hegel,<sup>10</sup> Comte,<sup>11</sup> so many others, followers of the doctrine [p. 12] contradictory. Only now and then, in the concert of alliance, a discordant note. The cry of Rousseau in the eighteenth century, which is an anguished question and a terrible admonition, and, in the last century, the peremptory negation of Schopenhauer and the disciples of his systematic pessimism. Exclusive, intrepid answer and reaction; but with as much right to be formulated, at least, as the very thesis of European culture, which gave rise to the disquieting rectifications of the philosopher of Geneva.

<sup>10</sup> Hegel is, after Leibniz and before Comte, another of the great leaders of the faith in progress. Spontaneously, universally, the idea will develop in an infinite dialectic up to its fullest plenitude, from pure being or abstract concept. In this celebrated system, law, nationalities, traditions, customs, sciences, letters, arts, religions and philosophies, human and nonhuman fruits of universal evolution, everything marches toward the realization of God in the world. Progress is the universal law. Renan, in a diversion of an ideologue (his own words to characterize his production), has formulated the best moral and political synthesis of Hegelianism. See in the book of the French polygraph entitled Dialogues et fragments philosophiques, the third dialogue.

<sup>11</sup> Heir of the utopian socialism of Saint-Simon; author of the Course of Positive Philosophy, of the System of Positive Polity and of the Positivist Catechism; books whose ambition would be to suppress Roman Catholic deism, leaving, nonetheless, the Catholic organization almost intact, and exploiting it on behalf of a new fetish: humanity. Progress is, for Comte, the law of history. At the end of his life, the celebrated ideologue declared himself supreme pontiff of the superstition he was inaugurating. The phantom of progress engendered in his suffering soul one of the most curious and absurd systems of universal domination.

<sup>3</sup> See, nonetheless, on Fontenelle, the dialogue between Socrates and Montaigne. Dialogues des morts.

<sup>4</sup> Creator of the philosophy of history in his great fundamental book, printed at Naples in 1725, for the first time. The work is entitled: Principi di scienza nuova d’intorno alla comune natura delle nazioni.

<sup>5</sup> Apostle of the religious or spiritual progress of the human race, in his short work on Education. See: Dictionnaire des sciences philosophiques. Art. Progrès.

<sup>6</sup> Read the chapters of Book XV of his work Ideen zur Philosophie der Geschichte der Menschheit. There he maintains that “according to laws inherent in their own nature, reason and justice are bound to increase their power among men and to found humanity on durable bases.”

<sup>7</sup> Nietzsche uttered the cruelest sarcasm against so generous a poet of human perfectionment, calling him: The trumpeter of Säckingen of morality.

<sup>8</sup> Champion of objective social progress. In his Discourses at the Sorbonne, he already enunciates clearly the Law of the three States (theological, metaphysical and positive), which Comte would later formulate as the fundamental principle of his philosophy.

<sup>9</sup> Theorist of scientific and practical progress, as Lessing was of moral progress. His is the celebrated book in which he sketches a historical picture of the progress of the human spirit.

### CAPÍTULO II. LA DEFINICIÓN DEL PROGRESO Y LA FILOSOFÍA DE LA HISTORIA — CHAPTER II. THE DEFINITION OF PROGRESS AND THE PHILOSOPHY OF HISTORY

#### 9. Definición del progreso — 9. Definition of progress

[p. 13] Progress may be defined as effort toward perfection. The clearest notion of perfection is due to Saint Thomas Aquinas, who asserts, following Aristotle (God is pure act), that this virtue consists in attaining the end, in realizing what is in potentia, in fulfilling the virtual. Progress, the march forward (progressus), will thus be the effort to attain the end, to actualize the potential. But, since all effort takes place in time, adding the notion of time, of succession, of history (only things that endure have history, as Bergson says), historical progress will turn out to be the effort accomplished, carried out in time by humanity for the attainment of all its ends; the effort, in sum, to realize humanity completely.

#### 10. Los órdenes del progreso — 10. The orders of progress

Human activity is physical, intellectual, aesthetic and moral. In these four orders progress will be realized a fortiori, historically. Let us examine whether progress has been made in each of the several orders, so as to try to ground a total conclusion on the partial conclusions of the analysis.

#### 11. El progreso físico — 11. Physical progress

To begin with, it might be affirmed that physical progress does not exist. As mere animals we are inferior to savages, at least to certain savages. Our senses, our muscular vigor, our physical and biological ego is inferior to the sturdy individuality of primitives. Civilization, instead of increasing it, has considerably diminished the physical vigor of human units. The initial human beast turns out [p. 14] to be a true physical superman. In this sense, contemporary humanity seems headed toward the engendering of the inferman…

#### 12. El progreso intelectual — 12. Intellectual progress

Progress, in the intellectual order, may be philosophical, scientific and practical. Scientific and industrial progress are beyond doubt. It cannot certainly be denied that the sciences and the mechanical arts (which were called servile) have achieved, since the Renaissance, immense advances, by engendering the distinctive character of modern civilization —saving, for example, slavery in order to foster the proletariat—; by modifying profoundly always, but not exclusively, as the Marxists would have it, the whole of what they themselves have called superstructures or social epiphenomena. In reality, the materialist interpretation of history, economic determinism or causalism, is as genuinely intellectualist as any other intellectualism; which goes to show, once more, that materialism and intellectualism are kindred systems. Materialism is, in effect, the most elementary form of intellectualism.

#### 13. La esfera del progreso intelectual — 13. The sphere of intellectual progress

Mathematics, astronomy, physics, chemistry, natural sciences, experimental psychology, scientific methods of history, sociology, political economy, ethnology; pure and applied sciences (each of which fosters one or several arts); industries ever more numerous, complex and varied, truly fruitful, irrepressible, all this advances and grows complicated within the exquisite division of social labor, advances and grows complicated with unheard-of vigor, as is proclaimed, in a dithyrambic but not unjust tone, by the spirits who live with the enthusiasm of their historical moment, among whom are counted, certainly, some journalists and authors of books for primary schools; but also exceptional men, of real ability and talent, and others of genius, who, in laboratories, universities and museums, struggle successfully to fulfil the intellectual, scientific and practical end of humanity (defined by Bacon in his generous aphorisms); and are venerated by national and international opinion, and lavishly supported, at times, thanks to the beneficent snobbery of systematic donation, practiced by multimillionaires of the type of Nobel, Carnegie and Rockefeller.

#### 14. El progreso filosófico — 14. Philosophical progress

[p. 15] More, much more difficult to resolve is the point of finding out whether philosophical progress really exists; and this is because Philosophy, unlike the sciences, has as its object not the general, the abstract, the generic, uniformity; but the concrete universal, which can be investigated only by intuition. Which metaphysical intuition is true? The atoms of Leucippus, Democritus and the modern chemists? The monads of Anaxagoras, Bruno and Leibnitz? The single being, the infinite substance of Spinoza, which seemed to Hegel to represent the very negation of the world, fundamental principle of a systematic acosmism? The Spencerian unknowable, as impenetrable to human reason as Kant’s noumenon? The élan vital of M. Henri Bergson or, perchance, the simple and dogmatic positivist negation or abstention?

#### 15. La medida del progreso filosófico — 15. The measure of philosophical progress

A metaphysical principle is not demonstrable like a scientific law, and can be judged only by the consequences it engenders; that is, by the difficulties of synthesis it spares, by its extension and simplicity. By contrast, scientific effort is always, in a certain way, cumulative, quantitative; it admits, being purely abstract and rational, resolution by parts, gradual, really progressive.

It is true that some philosophical errors do not reappear after the advent of certain criteria and systems; but do we not witness at this very moment the renewal of the individualism and humanism of Protagoras (“Man is the measure of all things, of those that are as well as of those that are not”), brilliantly defended by pragmatists such as the celebrated Oxford professor, Mr. F. C. S. Schiller?

Contemporary anti-intellectualism is a mystical reaction led by thinkers as illustrious as William James and Bergson himself, which in his Évolution créatrice recalls Plotinus and the distant days of Alexandrian philosophy.

And against the anti-intellectualism of Eucken, of James, of Bergson, of so many others, intellectualism takes heart and develops. Fouillée said in one of his last books, referring to the illustrious author of Matière et mémoire: Amicus anti-Plato, sed magis amica veritas [a friend of anti-Plato, but a greater friend of truth], and he always struggled to find a synthesis of consciousness and will: the idea […] these [p. 18] forms, the properly moral part, varies, does not improve. Today man is as bad as he ever was. More ingenious, perhaps, in acting; more cunning, perhaps, in taking advantage of his fellow; more cowardly and eager to exploit him; more skillful, perhaps, and malevolent than before, he does not incur juridical sanction; but if he can mock it to his profit, he mocks it; if he can violate it, he violates it. Moral progress consists not in conceiving but in willing the good; and today there are as few devotees of the good for the good’s own sake as in any other epoch of history. Much is lacking for the realization of the Kantian desideratum: to act considering one’s fellow as an end in himself; as a final end and not as a means.

Culture has fostered, perhaps, intelligence, making it free, sovereign, independent; but, it occurs to us to ask, did it thereby perfect the will? Does the progress of the Enlightenment, which the men of the eighteenth century loved above all things, also signify the flourishing of the moral conscience?

#### 19. Rousseau, Fontenelle y Montaigne — 19. Rousseau, Fontenelle and Montaigne

The competition theme of the Academy of Dijon, which Rousseau resolved, against the spirit of his century and of all the centuries of the modern age, in the negative, can be resolved today as the citizen of Geneva did, affirming, at least, that the advances of science have not engendered moral progress. The world needs fewer scholars and more apostles.

Fontenelle, in his Dialogues of the Dead, has written an admirable fragment on the constant immorality of man. Those who speak are Montaigne, type of the perspicacious and discreet man of the Renaissance, most human of skeptics, symbol of his century, and Socrates, eternal symbol of the moralist who, as the Castilian verse says:

“Matches thought with life.”

Montaigne notes the intellectual differences characteristic of the various centuries of history, and says to Socrates: “Why should there not be centuries more virtuous and others more perverse?” Socrates replies: “That will not easily be admitted as a consequence. Habits change; but not so the form of bodies. Courtesy or rusticity, science or ignorance, greater or lesser ingenuousness, a serious or playful temper may vary; they are the outward part of man; but his heart does not change; and the whole man is there. In [p. 19] one century men are ignorant; the fashion of knowing may arrive; they are self-interested; but the fashion of disinterestedness will never come. In the prodigious number of unreasonable men born in a hundred years, nature has formed two or three dozen reasonable beings, whom it must distribute everywhere. Consider that they will never be found in sufficient quantity to form a fashion of virtue and honesty.”

It seems, on verifying this fact, painful above all others, that the center of the destinies of the species is not, as modern rationalism has believed against the Christian faith, this earthly existence. Man, prosperous in his action, his science and his industry, is not equally so in other, higher values of life. To one who might despair of destiny on account of so profound a truth, one would have to say, with another famous verse of a Castilian poet:

“Blind man, is the earth the center of souls?”<sup>12</sup> The last words that Fontenelle puts in the mouth of Socrates, in the dialogue cited above, are the expression of the truth about the problem of the moral progress of humanity through history: “Tout au plus il y aurait quelque inégalité imperceptible. L’ordre général de la nature a l’air bien constant.” [At most there would be some imperceptible inequality. The general order of nature seems quite constant.] The Christian religion places the good in the world of grace, far from this vale of tears. Believers in progress judge it to be realizing itself, gradually, in this perishable world; but both will have to conclude with the subtle moralist, upon verifying that evil, like matter and cosmic energy, according to the physicists, changes form but in the end remains unaltered: “L’ordre [p. 20] général de la nature a l’air bien constant.” Such is the melancholy true conclusion on the problem of moral evolution accomplished in history.

<sup>12</sup> It can be maintained that Hartmann, despite his systematic pessimism, has set forth with real philosophical impartiality the plain and simple truth regarding the moral evolution of humanity. Here is his thought: “Immorality, from the foundation of the first human society to the present, if appraised according to the measure of conscience, has not become less in the world: only the form that moral evil assumes has changed. Let us set aside the fluctuations of the moral character of each people; as a totality, the same constant relation between egoism and charity is noticeable. If the cruelty and brutality of past times offend, we must not forget that rectitude, sincerity, the lively sense of justice, the pious respect for the sanctity of customs characterized the ancient peoples: whereas today there reign lying, falsehood, perfidy, the spirit of deceit, contempt for property, disdain for instinctive probity and legitimate customs, whose value often would not assure the infallible execution of the repressive laws, the brutal savagery of the first ages would be seen again… The degree of moral corruption is identical; it no longer wears clogs, it wears a frock coat; but the cause and the effects remain the same, only the form is more elegant.” Philosophy of the Unconscious. Third Stage of Illusion.

#### 20. Resumen — 20. Summary

In sum: only intellectual, scientific and practical progress has been a fact. All-sided progress has not existed and does not exist. Hence the belief in the betterment of humanity is a genuinely modern superstition. “Humanity does not represent a development toward the better, toward something stronger, more elevated, as is now thought. Progress is merely a modern idea; that is, a false idea. In worth the European of today falls far behind the European of the Renaissance. To develop does not at all mean necessarily to rise, to surpass oneself, to grow stronger.”<sup>13</sup>

<sup>13</sup> Nietzsche, The Antichrist (an essay toward a critique of Christianity).

Never did ancient science, not even in the days of the Museum of Alexandria, attain the development it has acquired in modern times, nor exercise the influence it has today on the manifestations of our activity. Alexandria has spread over the universe. The Ptolemaic empire has widened until it has become the entire world. The scientific and practical spirit has covered the face of the planet with laboratories and industries; it is the unchallenged ruler of our utilitarian, industrial and scientific civilization.

The intellectualist character of the great illusion is thus made manifest; and the problem of the philosophy of history, its only problem, reduced to the determination of a prejudice engendered by the distinctive characteristics of a historical epoch.

“Philosophy, according to Seneca, teaches us to be independent of all material substance, of all mechanical invention. To attribute to a certain man a part in the discovery or perfecting of a plow, a mill or a ship is to insult him. It is the task of vile slaves to invent such things. Philosophy dwells on higher ground. Non est instrumentorum ad usus necessarios opifex [the philosopher is not a maker of instruments for necessary uses]; the philosopher is no manufacturer of instruments for necessary uses.” Macaulay comments, rightly: “Strike out the non at the head of the sentence and you will have the definition of philosophy, according to Bacon.”<sup>14</sup>

<sup>14</sup> Opus cit., Histoire de l’idée de progrès, Bacon.

Seneca’s paragraph and Macaulay’s commentary can, indeed, make precise the difference between the anti-intellectualist, [p. 21] aristocratic, classical prejudice, which removed philosophy from contact with the world, and the intellectualist, bourgeois, modern prejudice, which has created, with the apotheosis of science and industry, the illusion of progress.

#### 21. El error antropomórfico del progreso — 21. The anthropomorphic error of progress

Modern faith in progress is also an anthropomorphic and realist error, founded on imagining humanity as a real being, as a universal distinct from human individuals and implicit in them (universalia sunt realia), capable of inversive development in historical succession, in the same way as each man considered individually.

Haeckel has studied and defined the biological principle that ontogenesis is a recapitulation of phylogenesis, that is, that the individual being, the embryo, runs through in its development the phases of the evolution of the species. Several thinkers, in the course of modern times, have upheld a historical law or pseudo-law, homogeneous and reciprocal to Haeckel’s, which declares that the spiritual phylogenesis of Humanity is a reproduction of the spiritual ontogenesis of the individual. Pascal says:(15) “Not only does each man advance continually in the sciences; all men considered together likewise make constant progress, as the Universe grows old, because the same thing happens in the succession of men as in the different ages of one man considered individually; so that all of them, in the course of so many centuries, may be considered as one and the same man who always subsists and continually learns.”

Goethe, transposing to the sphere of the individual the once-famous law that, according to Cousin, governed the historical unfolding of systems, says:<sup>6</sup> “Philosophy repeats all the ages we ourselves have lived through. As children we are sensualists; idealists when we love and place in the beloved object qualities that truly are not in it. Love wavers, we doubt fidelity and, without knowing it, we become skeptics. The rest of life passes in indifference; we let things go as God pleases, and we end by the

<sup>6</sup> Conversations with Eckermann (Tuesday, 17 February 1829).

Jean Paul Richter, in his Aesthetic Theories (Classicism), affirms: “The youth of a people is not a metaphor but a reality: a [p. 22] people reproduces, though in relations of time and circumstance of greater importance, the history of the individual.”

Auguste Comte, in setting forth the celebrated law of the three states, writes:<sup>7</sup> “This general revolution of the human mind can be easily verified in a very clear, though indirect, manner by considering the development of the individual intelligence. Since the point of departure of the education of the individual and of the species is necessarily the same, the various principal phases of the former must represent the fundamental epochs of the latter. Now, looking back upon his own history, who does not recall having been a theologian in his childhood, a metaphysician in his youth, and a natural philosopher in his manhood?”

<sup>7</sup> Cours de philosophie positive.

This concept of the parallelism between individual and specific development also impressed Max Stirner,<sup>8</sup> who interprets it in his own way, formulating in turn a law homogeneous with those we have seen subscribed to by Pascal, Goethe, Richter and Auguste Comte: In childhood man is a realist. Every child is a being guided by the decisive urgings of his desires, subjecting his actions in no way to the commands of a moral law. Childhood remains submissive to the promptings of the reality of its being, of its imperious and triumphant ego.

<sup>8</sup> Der Einzige und sein Eigentum.

As he grows, the total self divides in two thanks to continually fostered introspection, and then the intelligence distinguishes a low, emotional and instinctive being, and a high and noble being, the intellectual being. This second self forms the world of ideas. The youth is an idealist; to the notions shown him by his spirit, differentiated from his body, he subjects his interpretation of the world and of life.

The years pass, the youth dies in his turn, and becomes a man. Abstract ideas no longer exert on him their all-embracing power of seduction. The heart has been filled with disappointments. A sincere egoism is now the supreme ruler of conduct. The man is a realist like the child; like him, spiritual, inclined to follow the inspirations of his total being, disillusioned as he lives with the phantoms that once marked the courses of his life.

Humanity, says Stirner, in the course of its mental history, reproduces the life of a man. It too is egoistic in childhood, intellectualist in youth, and egoistic again in its manhood.

The analogy, noted and repeated several times, is ingenious but not profound. It implies belief in a nonexistent being imagined as a man; and both suppositions are false. There is no childhood, [p. 23] nor youth, nor manhood, nor old age of humanity, nor even a humanity, which is only a name (flatus vocis); the name of a select biological group, incongruous as a metaphysical principle, even though Comte made of it a new god, and it is or has been the Great Fetish of the philosophers of history. The error is not only anthropomorphic, but also anthropolatric, irreligious. The present perfection of God, an all-sided, absolute perfection, is turned into belief in the progress of man, in the deification of Humanity in the course of time. It is the intellectualist belief of Hegel, who sees in the succession of historical processes the realization of the absolute Idea; the faith of Comte, who builds up the being of his fetish through the continuity of progressive human beings; the dream, too, of the intellectualist and Hegelian aristocratism of Renan; the thought that Diderot already expressed when he declared: Si Dieu n’est pas, il sera peut-être [If God does not exist, he will perhaps exist]. Purest anthropolatry, undoubted self-deification!

Before such excesses of modern rationalism, one hears with deep religious emotion the epic cry of Nietzsche, pure as the very conscience of the hero: “Your pettiness even in sin, that is what cries to heaven! Where, then, is the lightning that will lick you with its tongue? Where the frenzy that would need to be instilled in you? Behold: I proclaim to you the superman. He is that lightning! He is that frenzy!”<sup>9</sup>

<sup>9</sup> Also sprach Zarathustra [Thus Spoke Zarathustra].

#### 22. El Materialismo histórico y el progreso — 22. Historical Materialism and Progress

Let us grant to historical materialism the final determination of the prejudice of progress. It is well known that this doctrine explains the history of humanity by the modifications of technique in the production of social wealth and by the class struggle. Well then, belief in progress turns out to be concomitant with the development of the European bourgeoisie, from the communal movement of the late Middle Ages down to its definitive triumph in the French Revolution. Progress is the illusion of the bourgeoisie, a militant and triumphant class in the Modern Age. Those who, inflamed by the Declaration of the Rights of Man, paraded through the streets of Paris the Goddess Reason over the ruins of the most Christian medieval monarchy, after beheading the king, grandson of Saint Louis, were bourgeois carrying out the apotheosis of their class. They prepared the great historical drama and conquered in it. The intellectualism, the scientism, the encyclopedism [p. 24] of the eighteenth century (precursors of contemporary industrialism) define the culminating moment of the idea. Our century is no longer bourgeois, even though it still is so to a great extent, despite its inveterate socialism and its continued anti-bourgeois protests; for that reason it is losing, along with faith in progress, its love for the Fetish, for the Idol of the European tribe... for that reason, too, it has ceased to believe in the philosophy of history. Socialists, such as Georges Sorel,<sup>10</sup> speak of the illusions of progress. The intellectualist, realist, anthropomorphic, anthropolatric, Judaic and bourgeois error should no longer matter in contemporary philosophical consciousness.

<sup>10</sup> See Les illusions du progrès [The Illusions of Progress].

#### 23. El concepto contradictorio de filosofía de la historia — 23. The Contradictory Concept of the Philosophy of History

But there is more still. Supposing that progress existed objectively,<sup>11</sup> which is inadmissible, the very concept of the philosophy of history proves contradictory in itself, independently of the progressive evolution of humanity. In effect, philosophy aims at the synthetic conception of the world as a totality, founded on the intuition of the necessary principle of existence. History, on the other hand, sets out to describe or intuit contingent things, beings and situations, irreducible, unique in their individuality. Philosophy is intuition, but not of the individual, but of the universal. All the great philosophical syntheses, from Platonism to Bergsonism, are intuitions of the universal principle of existence. The Universe, which for the sciences is a set of formulas, of laws, of uniformities, of analogies, is for the philosopher a plural or a single being, substance or monads, but always a being, a totality, an intuition. History is an evocative imitation; not a creative invention like art, nor an abstract synthesis like the sciences, nor an intuition of the concrete universal like philosophy. The historian relives the past, reanimates it, resurrects it. His labor is, like the artist’s, essentially and fundamentally an intuition of individualities and peculiarities. Therefore Croce is right in declaring that a philosophy of history conceived, at bottom, as an intellectualization of history, as a history of the second degree, ideal, eternal, as Hegel wanted it, a negation of real and truly temporal history, is impossible. One might add: a systematic sleight of hand with history.

<sup>11</sup> See the study of the criteria of objective progress in the article by René Worms entitled “L’idée de progrès,” published in the Revue de Sociologie, issue for March 1911.

[p. 25] Let us admit more; let us concede the absurdity. What use would the philosophy of history have beside logic, aesthetics, ethics and the philosophy of religion? The value of existence and of science is the only philosophical question these disciplines discuss. Consequently, they would consider a fortiori the history of progress (if there were any) in the sciences, art, morality, religion, and so on. There can then be no content proper to the philosophy of history. The philosophy of history would be a double emploi with the normative philosophical disciplines, those of the value of existence. It would be a repetition of studies already undertaken; a confusion of philosophical efforts already accomplished; that is, something, besides being contradictory, useless.

Far from invalidating these conclusions, the morphology of Universal History by Oswald Spengler comes, perhaps, to corroborate them definitively. This philosopher of history, “superior to all his critics,” as Keyserling says (an assertion we fully accept, including, as must be, Keyserling himself in the long list of Spengler’s critics), agrees on the fundamental affirmation that Universal History lacks meaning. In his opinion, only the singular evolution of each historical culture has meaning, but the relation among the various cultures does not possess it. Spengler thinks that there is no constant progress, but that each great culture among those elaborated by humanity is like a plant that thrives and then dies: “A culture is born when a great soul awakens from the primary state, and detaches itself from the eternal human infantilism, when a form arises from the formless, when something limited and ephemeral emerges from the limitless and enduring; it then flowers on the soil of a region, to which it remains attached like a plant. A culture dies when this soul has developed the sum of its possibilities in languages, dogmas, arts, States, sciences, and sinks back into primitive spirituality. When the goal has been reached, when the idea, the multitude of inner possibilities, has developed and been fulfilled outwardly, then, suddenly, the culture stiffens and dies; its blood congeals, its forces are exhausted; that is, it is transformed into civilization. This is what we feel and understand in the words Egypticism, Byzantinism, Mandarinism. And the gigantic corpse, a dried-up trunk without sap, can remain in the forest for centuries and centuries, raising its dead branches to heaven. Such is the case of China, of India, of Islam. This is the meaning of all the decadences of history, inner and outer fulfillment, a completion that inevitably overtakes every living culture. The one with the cleanest outlines lies before our eyes: it is the decadence of antiquity.” That is, for Spengler, there is not a single [p. 26] human progress, but cultural cycles that begin and end, singularly. There exists, rather than a philosophy of history, a synthetic investigation of the biology of each human culture.

Spengler’s essential thought is made precise and corroborated when he refers concretely to the extinct civilizations of ancient Mexico. Before the terrible episode of the Spanish Conquest, the philosopher, overcome with stupor, exclaims: “Meanwhile, in Mexico a new culture had been born, so remote, so far removed from all the others, that there could be no knowledge of it in them, nor of them in it. All the more admirable, then, is the similarity of its evolution to the evolution of ancient culture. Philologists will be filled with dread when, before these teocallis, they think of their Doric temples. And yet it was precisely an ancient trait, the lack of will to power in technique, that determined here the nature of the armament and, consequently, made the catastrophe possible.

Because this culture is the only example of a violent death. It did not perish by decay, it was neither hindered nor repressed in its development. It was murdered, in the fullness of its evolution, destroyed like a flower that a passer-by decapitates with his stick.

“The most terrible thing about this spectacle is that such destruction was not even a necessity for the culture of the West. This is the best proof that human history lacks meaning.”<sup>12</sup>

<sup>12</sup> The Decline of the West, pp. 67 ff. of the Spanish version by Manuel

### CAPÍTULO III. LA HISTORIA COMO CIENCIA — CHAPTER III. HISTORY AS A SCIENCE

#### 24. ¿Es ciencia la historia? — 24. Is History a Science?

[p. 27] “In three groups, according to Altamira, thinkers (philosophers, historians, sociologists) can be classified in relation to this problem: some flatly deny to history every scientific condition; others recognize it in part, and others, finally, affirm it and even claim to constitute a new kind of science with it.”<sup>13</sup>

<sup>13</sup> Cuestiones modernas de historia, p. 106.

No error can arise, or at least it would be inexplicable that one should arise, unless it were somehow founded on real data capable of being interpreted as the one who falls into error interprets them. Spencer said—and his expression met with good fortune afterward—that there is a ground of truth in false things. If with respect to the nature of history and its scientific or artistic character there exists grave discrepancy of opinions, this will probably depend on the fact that the very characters of history are susceptible of diverse interpretations, because perhaps the thing one wishes to define is incoherent in itself, and, as Stuart Mill teaches, “there is no agreement as to the definition of a thing except when there is agreement as to the object of the definition”; but it may also happen that, history being coherent in itself, it is so complex that in one of its aspects it appears as science and in another as art; or as art and science at once, or as science and art sui generis. It might, finally, happen that, not entirely coherent in itself, history joined to its incongruence, at least relative, its heterogeneity; then the difficulty of its definition would explain the discrepancy of opinions to which Altamira refers.

Between a history book like Herodotus’s and one like Polybius’s there are great and notable differences. Between history as Saint Augustine conceives it and as Fustel de Coulanges understands it, the differences are also palpable. But they are not so deep and decisive as to prevent us from considering the poetic books of Herodotus and the theological ones of Saint Augustine as differing in essence from those of Polybius and Fustel de [p. 28] Coulanges. Philosophical history is, fundamentally, one and the same knowledge. Just as the science of Albertus Magnus and Roger Bacon and that of Descartes and Galileo is one, even though it always refers to the set of ideas of the century that engendered it and is tinged with the spirit of the epochs that go on succeeding and rectifying one another.

Let us consider, then, not the whole content of history books, but what is historical in them; let us eliminate, even if only conventionally, what is auxiliary, contingent and extrinsic: political and moral speculations, philosophical and religious reflections; mystical or pedagogical tendencies, and so on, there will always remain a sui generis core which will be, precisely, the object of the definition of history; whether it be considered as art or science, or art and science at once, or as a sui generis art or science.

History, at first sight, does not reproduce the general type of the sciences. This is, probably, the opinion of the impartial reader. While physics, chemistry, biology and sociology reproduce the physiognomic features of one and the same ideological family, history departs from the common type. Let us suppose that this is a superficial opinion. Nevertheless, some profound cause must sustain it, since it insinuates itself so commonly.

History, in effect, proceeds ad narrandum, reconstructing, reliving the past. The sciences, instead of turning their gaze to the past, set it on the future. History goes to investigate, in the perennial unfolding of life, the life that was, the world that perished, the societies, traditions and customs that have disappeared. Its object of knowledge does not exist at present; time incorporated it in its passage and turned it into the present moment, or undid it forever.

What science is this, different from the others, a science that does not know in order to foresee but in order to relive? How can history preserve its scientific character and refer to the past instead of devoting itself to the future? What general facts will it discover? What symmetries and oppositions of nature will it make precise, valid only for the past and limited, therefore, in their universality; contingent in relation to the future?

#### 25. La historia y las ciencias — 25. History and the Sciences

The logical procedure of the sciences is deductive or inductive, but always implies a general element to which one is to arrive or from which one must set out in order to carry out the reasoning. By virtue of induction, the scientific investigator derives from the study of a few facts a general result that admits no restrictions of time or space other [p. 29] than the restrictions of its statement; and if it admits them, it is because it is not a true induction but an apparent one. It is then a matter of stating synthetically what has previously been discovered; but there is no passage from something known to something unknown, scientific knowledge is not increased. An astronomical, physical, biological law embraces an indefinite multitude of possible cases, which will confirm it uniformly as soon as they occur. By contrast, what has been called historical generalization is nothing but the synthetic statement of previously defined attributes of a certain people, individual or civilization; in short, of things that will not vary in time. The possibility of variation was exhausted by the very essence of the historical fact, which always refers to the past.

Deduction, likewise, in deriving less general or particular conclusions from a general proposition, does so with the same character of uniformity, not only for the real but for the possible, without restrictions of place or time. By contrast, if the historian generalizes, deduces or induces, he will always do so restricted by the very definition of his study. He will always refer to what has been once and will never again be identical with itself. The historian will choose ad libitum the importance of the object of his knowledge; he may propose the history of a special individual, the biography of Cromwell or of Frederick the Great, the history of a city like Florence or Paris, that of Italian civilization in the Renaissance; but Cromwell, Frederick the Great, Florence, Paris and Italian Renaissance civilization are equally individual, equally unique in space and time. The role of inductive generalization, or of the reflections obtained by deduction from this or that historical generalization, can never be equated with the function these same logical procedures fulfill in the sciences. In history they are rational elements of the final constructive elaboration; in the sciences they are, as has been said, far less contingent results; not mere generalizations but laws; not summaries of observations but uniformities of relations with no limits of time and space other than those of their statement; not auxiliaries of final intuitions but efforts that fulfill their end when formulated; that carry their object in themselves.

#### 26. Los historiadores de sucesos particulares — 26. Historians of Particular Events

All historians are historians of particular events. A historian of genius, Thucydides for example, will describe the Peloponnesian War; will reveal the essential and profound rivalry of Athens and Sparta; will evoke [p. 30] the terrible and admirable period; will exhume from oblivion peculiar attributes of men and things contemporary to him (the present always forms part of the past for the consciousness that perceives it). It is true that his philosophical spirit will shade the work with perspicacious moral reflections and grave political considerations; true that he will put magnificent speeches in the mouths of heroes, such as Pericles; but those disquisitions will not make up the ultimate end of his labor, even though they abound in it. And what will perennially make of his writings historical books and not treatises of moral philosophy will have to be the final synthetic vision, reconstructive intuition, the accomplished purpose of animating situations utterly singular in time and space, with data organized by the creative imagination and previously refined by reason.

The innumerable mythological, theological, historical and metaphysical allusions that fill the Divine Comedy do not take away its unmistakable poetic seal, just as they do not undermine the final philosophical sense of Bergson’s Évolution créatrice, the noble aesthetic qualities of its style, the magic of the expression of what would seem, at first aspect, ineffable. The ultimate end, which in the great poet is wholly free creation and in the French philosopher universal intuition of the élan vital of existence, is in the Greek historian the ideal resurrection of heroes and personages who in reality lived and acted, engendering of themselves a historical epoch of memorable remembrance for humanity. If the historian, like Thucydides, succeeds in giving us the illusion of making those revived Hellenic nations move and develop before our eyes; if their action manifests itself to our consciousness like that of our contemporaries, he will have achieved his design. He revealed the past in its uniqueness and singularity. Such is the ambition of history.

#### 27. Síntesis. — 27. Synthesis.

In sum: whereas the sciences refer to genera, uniformities and laws, history, even when it practices rational procedures, does not formulate laws in generalizing, but does so simply in order to serve thereby its ultimate end, which is the intuition of the individual.

Whereas the sciences study what is repeated universally what is once, and more than once and always, history refers to the unique, to what never again is as it was.

Whereas the sciences are masters of time, and develop in order to foresee the future, history sets its gaze on the past and investigates only the past and confines itself to it.

Given the conditions of individuality, uniqueness and pastness of history; detaching from what constitutes the essence of this [p. 31] knowledge everything that is not properly historical, even though it be intimately<sup>14</sup> intertwined and confused in the books of the great historians (which is, let us say in passing, where the peculiar mission of history is to be sought and not in the speculations of critics, rhetoricians and philosophers), one may ask, as at the beginning: would not common sense be right in having distinguished from the sciences the sui generis knowledge that possesses so many differential attributes?

<sup>14</sup> “The human mind—says Bacon—once seduced by certain ideas, whether by reason of their charm or by the sway of tradition and the faith it lends them, finds itself obliged to yield, agreeing with them; and although the proofs that belie them be very numerous and conclusive, the mind forgets them, despises them, or, by a subtle distinction, sets them aside and rejects them, not without grave harm; but it must preserve intact all the authority of its cherished sophisms.” Novum organum, aphorism 46.

Let the point be resolved as one wishes; but let this first unquestionable proposition not be denied: if the concept of science is not modified, there will always be a species of the genus that will not reproduce the generic attributes; which is absurd.

#### 28. El concepto de ciencia y la Historia — 28. The Concept of Science and History

Various thinkers have preferred to modify the concept of science so as to make the concept of history fit within it. Others, by contrast, more respectful of the truth and fewer in number, consequently, have preferred to declare that History is not a science.

Sir Alexander Grant, referring to Hegel, said that to take philosophy from Hegel’s Philosophy of History is like taking poetry from Shakespeare; an almost universal debt. With how much greater reason could it be affirmed, removing every limitation, that it is a universal debt to take philosophy from Aristotle. Let us resort for the discussion of the point to the Stagirite and ask him for his concept of science, in the first place, and then his concept of history.

Aristotle was the very opposite of a recalcitrant Cartesian, a sworn enemy of erudition and philology. We do not resort, then, to an apriorist and violent philosopher, to whom tradition would matter little; but to the one who, among all the Greeks, was the first historian of philosophy. That is: we resort to a great philosopher who was also an illustrious historian: “Aristotle, says Boutroux, devoted himself to profound historical studies in all domains of science.

Philosophy is, for Aristotle, knowledge of the universal; and [p. 32] each science a partial philosophy, restricted to a certain object of knowledge. But without an element of generality there is no science. Aristotle declares, in effect, that there is no science of the particular as particular.<sup>15</sup>

<sup>15</sup> Grande Encyclopédie, article ARISTOTE.

Concerning history, the Stagirite teaches: “The true difference (between the historian and the poet) lies in that one relates what has been and the other what could have been. This is what makes poetry something more philosophical and serious than history, since poetry deals more with the universal, and history more with the particular. The universal, in general, is the set of words or actions that concern such and such a person, according to probability or necessity; and the aim of poetry is this, in giving proper names to those generalities. The particular is, for example, what Alcibiades has done or what he has suffered.”<sup>16</sup>

<sup>16</sup> Metaphysics, II, 4. Nicomachean Ethics, VI, 3.

Joining both ideas in the rigor of a syllogism, we would have (major premise): there is no science of the particular (minor premise): history knows the particular (conclusion) therefore history is not a science.

It is true, as Altamira wishes, that the notion of science held by Aristotle is not precisely the contemporary notion (the concept of science is one of the most discussed points in the philosophy of our time); but consider the concept in Greek intellectualism, in the philosophy of contingency of M. Émile Boutroux, or in the contemporary pragmatism of James and Le Roy (who affirms the contingency, no longer of laws, but of scientific facts themselves), and it will always be true to declare that, without an element of generality, there is no science; that is, that history, if it were knowledge of the particular, should not be counted in the group of the sciences, as a species of the common genus.

Who will fail to admit that without types, genera, ideas, forms, uniformities or values, science is impossible? Who can speak of a science of the particular without perceiving at once the contradiction in the terms of the statement? No, the problem which Aristotle resolved so clearly and philosophically is not one to be resolved by the mere vicissitudes of the concept of science, as the Spanish historian cited above would wish; because at any time and in whatever philosophical system is in question or may be chosen at random, without a substratum of universality (even if not necessary, as Aristotle exclusively thought it),<sup>17</sup> [p. 33] science is impossible. The antithesis is indubitable, profound; and the conclusion of the proposed syllogism, perfect: history is not a science.

<sup>17</sup> See in the respective chapter, which the reader will find further on, the hypothesis of Windelband and Rickert, who believe they have found in values the universal element of history.

Of the four great forms of intellectual activity: philosophy, science, art and history, philosophy and science always refer to things that are not individual, universal or general, art to absolute, possible individuality, and history to the particular and real, never to abstraction or generality.

The greatest of philosophers also teaches, in the cited passage, his preference for poetry over history; because “poetry deals more with the universal and history more with the particular.” On this point the Stagirite does not belie the aptness of his genius. A philosopher is one who knows how to find in the universal the explanation of the individual, in the necessary that he intuits, the contingent that is given to him in experience. The poetry of a Shakespeare, in creating eternal types of human passion, such as Hamlet, Othello, Lear, is closer to the philosophical intuition of the universal than the supreme historian who, like Tacitus, only succeeds, in referring to this or that distinguished living personage, Caligula, Nero or Domitian, in pinpointing a contingent, historical wickedness, less real, less perverse in sum, within its essential particularity, than the most real wickedness aesthetically intuited, through innumerable experiences, by the creative genius of the artist.

#### 29. El pensamiento de Schopenhauer sobre la historia como ciencia — 29. Schopenhauer’s Thought on History as Science

This same philosophical preference of Aristotle was shared by other great thinkers who, following his example, denied to history the character of science. Schopenhauer, as will be seen presently, subscribes to it; and let it be noted in passing that the German philosopher, like the Greek, were men amply endowed with that form of universal sympathy which, according to Höffding, is the historical sense. Schopenhauer’s philosophical system has rather a psychological than a logical unity.

Poetry contributes more than history, according to Schopenhauer, to the knowledge of human nature: “In this sense, we must expect from the former more true lessons than from the latter. Aristotle recognized it when he said: Est res magis philosophica et melior poesis quam historia [Poetry is a thing more philosophical and better than history].

“History cannot aspire to take its place in line with the other sciences, for it cannot claim for itself the qualities that distinguish them. It lacks the fundamental character of all science, namely: the subordination of known facts; in place of which it can only [p. 34] offer us their coordination. There is, then, no system in history as there is in any of the sciences. It is a knowing, not a science, for nowhere does it know the particular through the general, but is obliged to take the individual fact directly, and to crawl, so to speak, along the ground of experience, while the sciences fly above, because they have acquired vast general notions, by means of which they dominate the particular, and can, at least within certain limits, embrace at a glance the possibility of the things belonging to their domain, so that they can contemplate with tranquility even the eventual and the future. The sciences, being systems of general notions, deal only with genera; history always deals with individual things, according to which, were scientific character granted it, it would be a science of individuals, which implies contradiction. It also follows from the foregoing that all the sciences, without exception, deal with what always exists, whereas history relates what has existed only once and will never exist again.”<sup>18</sup>

<sup>18</sup> The World as Will and Representation, chap. XXXVIII.

The concept implies, in truth, a certain undoubted inferiority for history; but also a constant superiority. The sciences fly over the ground of experience and history crawls. Let us accept the metaphor and continue the allegory. But in flying, the sciences take for themselves only abstract aspects, that is, ideal and therefore unreal ones; whereas history, crawling, comes upon the most real individual, describes it, and delivers it to us as concrete and unique intuition. Flying will imply advantages; but it must be confessed that crawling has them too, if we try to grade the importance of both kinds of knowledge. In the world there are no astronomical or physical or biological facts; there are beings, natural systems, as Bergson would say, historical facts which, compared with one another, offer common attributes, the object of scientific laws. History interpenetrates, welcomes, in crawling, the same reality; whereas the sciences do nothing but fly and see above it. The eagle, on its summit, does not distinguish everything; the serpent, on the other hand, by limiting its horizon, feels the earth with its creeping body. Philosophy is an eagle; history, a serpent. Both are sacred beings.

In sum, in history there is serial coordination, but no system, no hierarchization of notions as in science and philosophy. History is a knowing, not a science. It is, perhaps, a form of irreducible knowledge, even though it partakes of the nature of science [p. 35] and of art.

#### 30. Los caracteres de los hechos históricos, según Meyer y Andler — 30. The Characters of Historical Facts, according to Meyer and Andler

But it is not enough to affirm the particularity or individuality, the singularity or uniqueness of the facts that constitute the object of history, nor to affirm their past character; it is necessary to define those characters, completing, with the corroborations furnished by contemporary professional historians, the philosophical teachings of Aristotle and Schopenhauer, which deny the scientific value of historical knowledge.

“The essential affirmation of descriptive historical study, says Andler (summarizing the conclusions of the illustrious historian Eduard Meyer), is that the object of this science is formed by individual facts. This does not mean that it deals only with facts occurring in human individuals. Groups, peoples, civilizations are collective individuals that have their particularity. There are not even two centuries, nor two actions, that resemble each other. History has as its object to trace the differences existing between those particular structures of men or of the human groups that it describes as they are, changing and active, but irreducible.”

From this fundamental concept of the object of the historical disciplines Meyer deduces highly important consequences:

I. In the first place, general causes do not fall within the province of historical inquiry. Such causes exist, but they are not to be defined in history. The action of general facts, psychological or economic, is merely limiting and not explanatory of the particular phenomena it studies. Such facts do not suffice to achieve the foresight of the particular events that unfold within the closed enclosure which the general facts themselves bound.

II. Furthermore, “permanent states of things are not history. Nothing like the existence of the Alps has predetermined the historical existence of the peoples of central Europe; the history of Switzerland, of Italy, of Germany and of France is not intelligible except through the existence of the Alps; but the existence of the Alps does not belong to history. Historical facts are those that change and act by their change; uncivilized peoples whose social state does not change are not historical peoples. They are like isolated and immovable granite blocks, around which the tide of the peoples moves. It may happen abruptly that inorganic masses of the uncivilized hurl themselves upon evolving civilizations, like the Huns and the Mongols<sup>15</sup> [p. 36] who laid waste to Europe and who then acted as mechanically projected missiles.”

<sup>15</sup> La filosofía alemana en el siglo XIX.

III. On the other hand, “collective facts are not historical facts. The fate of the multitudes routed in one of Caesar’s battles is of no importance whatever. The strategic and tactical plan that gave the victory, that is the fact that deserves attention.” The masses are the substratum of history, the matter upon which events take place and institutions are found. Matter is of interest only for the form it takes, and this form is the work of individuals.

IV. However far history may extend, it will never go beyond particular facts. Particular is the life of a civilization such as that of the oriental and occidental peoples of Antiquity. History is never the science of the general. It is not only difficult to discover the laws of history; it is contradictory to seek them.<sup>16</sup>

<sup>16</sup> Op. cit., p. 216.

Once history is conceived as the description of the individual—unique, irreducible and past—whether it concerns men, peoples, civilizations or races, it is clear how its object is properly distinguished from that of the sciences. The latter study universal repetitions, oppositions and adaptations reducible to generic uniformities; resemblances, contrasts and symmetries, everything that falls under the dominion of the rational act, everything that is formulated in ideas or notions that generalize attributes, in judgments that compare ideas, in reasonings that compare judgments or that result from elementary reasonings. History (which makes use of the abstract scientific notions that limit or circumscribe its proper field), the knowledge that crawls along the better to feel its object, refers to what in logic are called infimae species, that is, to real beings, to facts that analysis does not select, to entities that do not spring up at the summons of theories, to what is not an object abstracted from life, but life from which all objects have been abstracted; always individual, singular, irreducible, different.

### CAPÍTULO IV. LA HISTORIA COMO CIENCIA SUI GENERIS — CHAPTER IV. HISTORY AS A SCIENCE SUI GENERIS

[p. 37] “Senonché, fenomeni soltanto successivi o soltanto di ripetizione non esistono e non sono concepibili...” [“But phenomena that are merely successive, or merely of repetition, neither exist nor are conceivable...”] Croce

#### 31. Repetición y sucesión universales — 31. Universal repetition and succession

Xénopol’s celebrated book, first entitled Principios fundamentales de la historia in the edition of 1899; enriched with new data later on; and entirely recast in the most recent edition, published under the title Teoría de la historia, constitutes, without doubt, the most important effort made to sustain the scientific character of historical facts themselves.

The concept of science is modified in accordance with Xénopol’s new cosmological principles, and in this way history seems to keep its essence as knowledge of the individual and, at the same time, not to have to abdicate the scientific character that some of its theorists grant it. The old classical dictum, There is no science of the particular, if the categories of succession and repetition proposed by Xénopol were admitted, could not be counted as a pertinent argument against the scientific character of history.

Xénopol begins by affirming, in the first chapter of his book—Repetition and Universal Succession—the relation of all phenomena to space and time. To sustain this relation the author professes a realism completely opposed to the Kantian thesis of the ideality of time and space, considered as a priori forms of sensibility: “Space, he says, extends outside us, and time passes independently of us.” Were it not so, one would have to admit what Hartmann declares in referring to the Schopenhauerian conception of time (at bottom, as is known, the Kantian): namely, “that history is a purely illusory phantasmagoria of subjective thought.”<sup>17</sup>

<sup>17</sup> Filosofía del Inconsciente, vol. I.

[p. 38] This anti-Kantian realism of Xénopol immediately provokes the grave difficulties attached to every naive realism: “Space is necessary for the production of every kind of fact. Even intellectual facts which, properly speaking, lack extension, cannot be conceived by the mind except as moving in an ideal space (?); and every idea, even the most abstract, moves in the mind.”

Undoubtedly space is unnecessary as the ideal medium of intellectual facts, which, say what Xénopol will, like all other states of consciousness, need no ideal medium other than time. In all strictness of doctrine, there are not even states of consciousness. This is an intellectualist fiction that carves up the continuous stream of consciousness of which James speaks; a projection of the atomism of the chemists into the orbit of the properly psychological; something convenient, but unreal.

The essence of the phenomena of the spirit is time, la durée réelle (of which Bergson speaks so eloquently), so different from the time-frame or time-space (a contradiction in two words) of which Xénopol treats. To conceive the spirit as a kind of ideal space is to project time too into space, to denature the reality of time and of the spirit. Mental facts and elapsing time are identical.

“Facts that have been produced or are produced in both frames, that of space and that of time, without being influenced by modifying forces, constitute facts of repetition. On the contrary, those that are or can be influenced and transformed by the forces that act in time constitute facts of succession.”

The two categories that appear for the first time in the paragraph just quoted form the object of two different disciplines: the sciences (knowledge of facts of repetition); history (knowledge of facts of succession). Facts of repetition are the whole, a part of which detaches itself to give rise to those of succession. “Repetition is the foundation of everything that exists; succession is only its flowering.”

To every impartial reader this idea of a time or of forces that are inactive with respect to certain facts and active in relation to others, which are nevertheless declared in the end to be so intimately bound to the former as a flowering of them, will surely seem strange.

Such is the fundamental error of the thesis: to believe in the inactivity of the time-frame. To think that succession or historicity is not an attribute of facts of repetition. To conceive two general orders of [p. 39] existence, intimately bound to each other and yet partly irreducible; and, what is more and harder to accept, forces and time active in relation to certain facts and inactive with respect to others. If the universal action of force is admitted, how are we to explain this strange selection or abstention?

#### 32. Los fenómenos astronómicos — 32. Astronomical phenomena

The type of facts of repetition is offered by astronomical phenomena, in which, nevertheless, careful analysis uncovers a certain unquestionable succession, a certain historicity, if one may say so.
