Leaving aside the problem of whether time has no influence on the natural laws themselves (laws whose uniformity seemed necessary to Montesquieu, and seems merely convenient or effective to contemporary philosophers such as Mach, a simple corollary of the principle of the economy of intellectual effort); leaving aside, too, the question of investigating whether, outside our solar system, outside the point of the infinite that we inhabit, that is, today, at another point of space, our scientific uniformities hold (a problem that once preoccupied Stuart Mill, and which the illustrious English logician in the end did not resolve in the affirmative); and other considerations of this kind, relating to the contingency of natural laws, that is, to the history of universal repetition, as Gabriel Tarde would say (by whose ideas the Rumanian writer seems to have been inspired); but granting that uniformity, repetition, is a corollary of the conservation of energy or of the persistence of force (which imposes the persistence of the relations among forces; that is, the diverse universal repetitions, the laws of Nature); and, referring simply to astronomical repetition, the type of the best-defined repetitions scientifically; considering, for example, Kepler’s law of the elliptical orbit of the planets, it is beyond doubt that: in the first place, the planet never describes its hypothetical elliptical orbit properly speaking; in the second place, it never describes the same quasi-ellipse; and, thirdly, it never describes it at the same point of space. The best-verified astronomical repetitions therefore partake of the character of successions; they are, at one and the same time, the plant and the inflorescence of which Xénopol speaks; they have a history, a minimal or limit history, because they are the maximal repetition; but history or historicity, in short. The vastest and least numerous laws to which human knowledge can be reduced are verified only as tendencies: what they gain in extension they lose in exactness.

[p. 40] Nor could it be otherwise. Time passes for all things. We must not imagine it as an abstract frame of existence. Nor, therefore, is the arbitrary selection that Xénopol maintains confirmed. Time runs or passes for all facts, because it is all facts; or, in other terms: all facts become; all are of succession.

Xénopol says: “How would it be possible to admit that it is the spirit that introduces into facts the static or historical considerations that give rise to the two ways of perceiving phenomena? By what miracle could man evoke history if development did not exist? By what miracle could he invent laws, if these were not found in the reality of things? Therefore, we do not imagine the static or dynamic side of things; matter itself is what presents both aspects, and the spirit is their faithful mirror, it can do nothing but reproduce in mind these two ways of being of reality itself: that of repetition and that of succession.”

To be able to answer that it is the spirit that introduces into reality the categories of universal succession and repetition, let us see how the concept of facts of pure succession proves incongruent in itself, just as we have seen that the concept of facts of pure repetition proves incongruent in Astronomy.

#### 33. La historia de la humanidad — 33. The history of humanity

In the history of humanity, which would be the type of the study of relations of simple succession and not of repetition, it is unquestionable that such knowledge, as Croce asserts, would be impossible without attending at all to conceptual elements and repetitions: perché, come si farebbe la storia dei fatti politici se non si tenesse conto della constante natura politica di quei fatti; o della poesia senza tenere conto della constante natura poetica di tutte le manifestazioni storiche di essa? [“for how could the history of political facts be written without taking account of the constant political nature of those facts; or the history of poetry without taking account of the constant poetic nature of all its historical manifestations?”] To Xénopol’s series of questions we shall answer: Yes, it is the spirit; it is the intelligence that selects what is succession-repetition, indissolubly. If the value of the two great categories of phenomena, the basis of Xénopol’s somewhat arbitrary cosmology, is subjective; and if history were to be founded on such a subjective differentiation, it would have a phantasmagorical basis, as one would have to say of the foundation that Xénopol assigns it, perhaps with more reason than Hartmann had to declare “purely illusory phantasmagoria of subjective [p. 41] thought” history understood according to the epistemology of Schopenhauer.<sup>18</sup>

<sup>18</sup> Lógica, Parte cuarta. Sapere Storico, III, p. 40.

See, still more clearly, the impossibility of a real differentiation of the two subjective categories: Xénopol declares that, for a succession to deserve this name, it is indispensable that important and continuous changes intervene, and he thinks that this implies an objection against the uniformities of succession which Stuart Mill maintains, as one of the types of uniformities of nature, together with those of existence, coexistence, causation and resemblance.<sup>19</sup>

<sup>19</sup> Véase Lógica inductiva y deductiva.

Using Mill’s own example, which the Rumanian author cites, that is, the law by which “a body moved around a center of force describes circles proportional to the time,” it can be affirmed that the repetition consists in the fact that bodies so moved always describe circles proportional to the time employed in traversing them; and the succession, in the fact that the proportionality of the time to the trajectories is the result of the movement around the center of force, that is, is its effect; which implies a relation of unconditional antecedence, or, what is the same, of succession. It is shown by the foregoing consideration that there is no contradiction between repetition and succession, and that, in consequence, it is impossible to assent to the synthesis Xénopol makes of his argument when he says: “succession can never be uniform and uniformity can never be a succession.” Croce is right when he concludes: phenomena of succession, as distinct from phenomena of repetition, neither exist nor are conceivable.

#### 34. Una distinción sutil; sin objetividad — 34. A subtle distinction; without objectivity

Moreover, Xénopol himself, convinced of the relativity of his differentiation, declares: “Facts of repetition are those that repeat themselves without important differences; those whose variations oscillate and can be forgotten, in order to concern oneself only with the essence, with the general part of the fact. Facts of succession, on the contrary, are those in which the repetition is carried out in such a way that the dissimilarity exceeds the common element; and in which the variations are continuous.”

It is well that, for the purposes of study, the astronomer should neglect the historicity of a planet’s movements, and that the historian should see succession by preference; but, properly speaking, there are neither facts of repetition nor facts of succession. Xénopol’s distinction is ingenious and subtle; not profound; subjective, in truth, not objective.

[p. 42] The exposition of Xénopol’s doctrine ends with a new attempt at a classification of the sciences, based on the differentiation just discussed. The sciences are divided into theoretical, or of repetition, and historical, or of succession. Xénopol affirms, with all contemporary philosophers, that subjective classifications, that is, those founded on the intellectual faculties employed in the elaboration of the various sciences, must not be admitted, even though, from Plato to Bacon and D’Alembert, all the classifications proposed have been of this order; and that it is necessary, therefore, to found the classification of the sciences on objective criteria. In referring to the classifications of Ampère, Comte and Spencer, he does not treat the first specially, even though he concludes for all of them, and he rejects the Comtean criterion of increasing complexity and decreasing generality, which the French philosopher called the logical criterion of his serial ordering.

Given the observations that have been adduced regarding the distinction between Xénopol’s two categories and their merely subjective value, it is evident that the Rumanian author falls into the very error he tries to avoid. The theoretical and the historical sciences would be differentiated as subjectively as the unreal categories of repetition and succession are differentiated, and only in this way.

“It is supremely inexact to make of history a particular science on a par with physics, chemistry, biology or psychology, as is seen in all the classifications of the sciences. History must be assigned another role in the distribution of human knowledge.”

We shall not be the ones to deny the thesis; but we shall not affirm it for the reasons Xénopol has developed, but because history, in our conception, is distinguished from the sciences in that the latter deal with general laws or relations of facts among themselves (scientific facts), while history, in accordance with the profound thought of Aristotle,<sup>20</sup> is concerned with the study of facts irreducible to general laws, individual facts (historical facts).

<sup>20</sup> Poética, Cap. IX, III.

Irreducible individuality, which can be known only intuitively, is the object of history.

#### 35. La crítica de Croce — 35. Croce’s criticism

Croce records two great merits of Xénopol’s historical theory: “having understood that history embraces all the manifestations of reality”; and “having restored, against naturalism, the consciousness of individuality.”

[p. 43] Indeed, Xénopol writes: “History, in the broad sense of the word, is not a special science, as it has been wished to consider it up to the present; a science that should be placed alongside biology, psychology and sociology; rather it constitutes one of the two universal modes of conceiving the world, the mode of succession as against that of repetition. This idea of history shows the importance of the discipline, whose principle, applied to material nature, has regenerated its study with the so fruitful idea of evolution. Far from having to defend itself against the charges that certain thinkers level at it, of not even being a science, history reveals itself, in our eyes, with rights equal to the scepter of human reason, which is its twin sister: the science of facts of repetition.”

Bacon, in his brilliant subjective classification, had already assigned historical knowledge to memory (succession), while conceiving it as embracing, as Croce says and Xénopol teaches, all the manifestations of the real (history of the heavenly bodies, of the regions of the air, meteors, comets, etcetera, of the lands, seas, mountains, rivers, etcetera, of the elements or greater congregations, of the species or lesser congregations, which for him made up natural history; and civil history, that is, ecclesiastical, civil properly so called, and literary, or of letters and the arts).<sup>21</sup> So that the autonomy of historical knowledge and its universal extension are two ideas of Bacon, which Xénopol has reproduced, trying to found them on the differentiation of categories so often cited. Rather than attributing to the author of the Teoría de la historia the merit that belongs to Bacon, let his be that of having reacted, in the Baconian sense, against the theses of the moderns who deny history both genuine attributes of universality.

<sup>21</sup> De dignitate et augmentis scientiarum, Lib. II, Caps. I, III y IV.

As to Xénopol’s having restored, against naturalism, the consciousness of individuality, it seems to me inexact to admit it. It would be better said that the intellectualist error that assimilates history to the sciences has reached its culminating point in the book of the distinguished historian. Interesting, in truth, is the history of this error. It presents three successive and characteristic phases. First it is affirmed that history is sociology or the science of social facts, that is, it is granted a special scientific character. Then it is affirmed that it is not sociology, but that it has a scientific character nonetheless, and that it coexists, as an analogous inquiry, with the science of human societies. Finally, it is affirmed, as Xénopol does, that it is a science sui generis, neither sociology [p. 44] nor twin science of the others, but of a private nature, by reason of its object of knowledge, universal succession, opposed to universal repetition. The intellectualist error has become more ductile, more capable of insinuating itself into consciousness, more dangerous; and, as happens in the general history of human errors, it has assumed, in its latest transformation, thanks to the subtlety and learning of an intelligent historian, the form of its greatest assent to the truth.

Xénopol vindicates not individuality but succession as the object of history; which is not the same thing; his hypothesis realizes the progress of the idea; and this, to cite an ingenious phrase of Nietzsche’s, becomes finer, more insidious and uncoercible; it becomes better; but not for that reason is it to be confessed as the truth.

The history-science, through its vicissitudes, is the same traditional error of intellectualism which, in affirming generality as the object of history, denies the autonomy of intuition as an irreducible form of the knowledge of unique beings and things; that is, things that are also irreducible in themselves to uniformities, laws and genera.

### CAPÍTULO V. LAS TEORÍAS AXIOLÓGICAS. EL SUBJETIVISMO, EL ONTOLOGISMO Y EL OBJETIVISMO SOCIAL — CHAPTER V. AXIOLOGICAL THEORIES. SUBJECTIVISM, ONTOLOGISM AND SOCIAL OBJECTIVISM

#### 36. Las ciencias filosóficas fundamentales — 36. The fundamental philosophical sciences

[p. 45] Philosophy must always answer two questions: What is it? What is what is worth? Or, more simply still: What is it? What is it worth? Ontology is the theory of being. Axiology is the theory of value. Values can give rise to a subjectivist theory, a social objectivist theory, or an ontological objectivist theory. In subjectivism the subject determines the value, by its qualities. We shall call the axiological theory subjectivist when it decides, in effect, on value by the subject. The objectivism of values can be: either a social objectivism or an ontological objectivism. Strictly speaking, the ontological objectivist theory disregards the division of philosophy into ontology and axiology, inasmuch as it makes of values entities, at least beings that are valid, or forms of reality that are valid, or realities that are valid and are not. Human language itself is not made to express this peregrine conception, inasmuch as we have to say: real beings whose being is to be valid and not to be.

#### 37. Lo que produce siempre la abstracción — 37. What abstraction always produces

Abstraction is a mental activity that selects the real and thinks it apart. In thinking it apart, it removes it from time and space and thinks it, then, in a second thought, without change or modification. This is what produces the illusion that there are things that do not endure. No; thoughts, when they are thought again without the marks of space and time, do indeed not endure, but this is because, a priori, the conditions of duration and of situation have been excluded. I can think of red, independently of the thing that is red, and then think my thought of the red. The red object endures and extends, red as a color remains immobilized in the mental world, and upon it consciousness, which has excluded the condition of time, [p. 46] can admit it without temporality. But I am the plaything of myself if, objectivizing the thought, I erect it into an entity because I think it separately. If I then invert the history of my reflection, or simply ignore it, I shall be able to give myself the satisfaction of exalting my own prejudice to the category of an entity. Abstraction always produces the negation of real complexity, through the schematism of concepts.

#### 38. La vieja sentencia escolástica — 38. The old scholastic maxim

Occam, combating realist thought (in the medieval sense of the word), taught that “entities must not be multiplied without necessity.” The philosopher’s celebrated aphorism is well worth recalling now that we are witnessing the renewal of a philosophical scholasticism. But when it is a question of values, the difficulty mounts and is refined, because now we shall have to admit, in accordance with these new ontologists, realities that are not. The mere statement of this stupendous contradiction makes one smile imperceptibly. And not because we cannot understand it, as Aloys Müller, who sponsors it, half understands or perceives it, but because no distinction can make realities that are not and yet are valid spring from nothing. This way of thinking is completely primitive. One of the characteristics of the primitive mentality is to turn qualities and attributes into things. Natural peoples believe that if a chief is brave, his courage is something that is in or rests upon the chief, just as the partisans of ontologism think that beauty rests or nests or is reflected in a beautiful painting. The painting, they say, exhibits aesthetic value; but one thing is the beautiful painting, and another the beauty of the painting or in the painting. Value has been reified as something that is not, but that is added, as a valid form of the real, or as a real valid thing. Neither more nor less than the primitive thinks that courage (in the sense of warlike virtue) is deposited or rests or is reflected in the chief, but can be detached from him by magical practices. The old scholastic maxim comes in the nick of time for these subtle metaphysicians who disdain a priori anyone who does not confess their thought, even though they themselves assure us that they can offer no proof of their conception, just as no proof of the existence of light can be offered to one blind from birth. He who does not intuit values thus, as Müller would say, “was not born to be a philosopher,” in the words of Hegel. We would add: “was not born, certainly, to be a philosopher...” an ontologist; but the grave maxim returns to our mind: “non sunt entia multiplicanda praeter necessitatem” [“entities are not to be multiplied beyond necessity”].

#### 39. Ontología o axiología — 39. Ontology or axiology

[p. 47] We said at the beginning of this chapter that philosophy has to answer two distinct questions: what is reality? what is reality worth? According to ontologism, there are no longer two distinct questions. There is no longer aesthetics, ethics, philosophy of religion, philosophy of history, philosophy of law. There is only ontology. The just is a valid reality that reveals itself in just acts. If we grasp the just, we shall be just; if we do not grasp it, but grasp the unjust, we shall be unjust. Here everything is a matter of capture, because there are forms of reality that are not, and which, by being valid, determine just acts, as well as good ones, as well as holy ones. Everything has become thing, entity, solid reality. In this world, instead of thinking, we have to see; instead of relating, we have to intuit. Does not this reduction of the axiological to the ontological, and of thought to the intuition of imaginary entities, repel the common sense?

#### 40. El subjetivismo — 40. Subjectivism

In referring to values, subjectivism maintains: things are in themselves and are worth in consciousness, or for consciousness. For example: a rose is in itself something that gives off perfume; and the perfume is worth for the consciousness that savors or perceives it. A rose is not in itself valuable; it is worth for the one who enjoys its perfume. If the subject is suppressed (whence the name subjectivism is derived), value is annihilated. The valuable is given in a consciousness and is suppressed with it. The rose is beautiful, yes; but only for me who see it. In itself it is neither beautiful nor ugly: it is; simply, it is. How could a rose be worth in itself? How, independently of the one who savors its perfume, would the perfume be valuable? How, independently of a contemplator of the form and coloring of the rose, could the rose be beautiful? Beauty implies relation and subjectivity. That is: values are relations between objects and subjects. The world in itself is neither beautiful, nor holy, nor good, nor true; it is real, it is. For subjectivism, ontologically there is neither truth, nor beauty, nor holiness, nor goodness, but reality. Only in the relativism of knowing, of intuiting, of understanding, of acting, are there beauty, goodness, truth and holiness. In sum: things are in themselves and are worth in me.

#### 41. Diferencia con el ontologismo — 41. Difference from ontologism

Anyone will grant that subjectivism has a notable advantage over ontologism, namely, that it distinguishes where consciousness [p. 48] distinguishes, that it does not confuse the ontological order with the axiological, in declaring: one thing is reality and another is value; whereas ontologism says: one form of reality is reality without value, and another form of reality is value without reality. Whence arises this curious contradiction in terms: value is a form of reality without reality, with only the reality of being valid.

#### 42. Objeción contra el subjetivismo — 42. Objection against subjectivism

But subjectivism leaves us in the presence of two fearful ignorances: what reality is cannot be known. Of it we shall say that it is, but we shall not be able to say how it is in itself, as reality, independently of the thinking subject. Now value is given in me on the occasion of a thing in itself, of which I know only that it is. How are we to understand the subjective explanation of value? In no possible way. There must be something in the thing that is worth for me, that makes it worth for me. I cannot be the sole author of values. The object claims its share. Ontologically, in the values that the subject affirms. Therefore it seems that neither subjectivism nor ontologism is true; because the one puts value in the object, as a valid reality, and turns the subject into a purely unintelligible receptivity; it suppresses the autonomy of the human psyche, imposes on it the acknowledgment of absolute, unintelligible entities. Whereas subjectivism removes all significance from the object in the problem of value, and renders reality absolutely unknowable. It is very easy to opt for the extreme terms in a difficult problem. Each of the two options is represented by one of the rival systems. But the human subject is not a purely passive receptivity of values, nor is reality something unknowable, upon which man builds a world absolutely his own. The thesis and the antithesis are false, despite the faith which the ontologist Aloys Müller spiritedly displays, with the comic arrogance of the convicted.

#### 43. Una descripción del ontologismo — 43. A description of ontologism

Plato would have recoiled, astonished, before the ontologism of values. These are reflected in the covering. In it they are given. Values cannot be demonstrated, they can only be shown. One who does not admit them cannot be convinced of error, because an intuition one does not have cannot be acquired by any other means. Let us suppose ourselves in the presence of a work of art, and that it exhibits grace, for example. Is there any way of proving that this work of art is graceful? None, absolutely. When the artist finished his labor, [p. 49] grace was deposited, magically, from another region of reality into the region of the reality of tangible objects; but the artist has not created the value, the lure of the work of art. It (the lure) came from its own region, to diffuse itself, enchantingly, over the piece of music or the graceful statuette. The artist thus turns out to be a pure evoker of valid creatures, who smile at him from their ethereal and luminous limbo. Michelangelo captured the terrible in his Prophets, and did not create it. It came, from its abode, to the vault of the Sistine Chapel. In this way one lives in a pure magical and celestial enchantment. One is left rapt, edified, perplexed, before so many things that there were in the world, and one did not know it. If someone is a hero, it is that he captured the value heroism. This came from its region of reality, and was infused into the heroic act. The holiness of Teresa or of Francis is likewise the same: something that nests or rests in ascetic and mystical acts. The whole world is enchanted. Besides the things that are, there are those that are worth without needing to be. The human psyche is a kind of great arena upon which nonexistent and valid creatures vibrate. But there are also those who capture the profane, the bad, the foolish, the ugly. Then, in these negative psyches, negative values are the only ones responsible, the real valid ones. It is the occasion to recall the ingenious rectification that has been made of Shakespeare’s verse:

“There are more things in heaven and earth, Horatio, than are dreamt of in your Philosophy”..., exclaiming: “there are more things in Philosophy than exist in heaven and earth.”

#### 44. La Naturaleza y los valores — 44. Nature and values

In Nature values are not given. They are given, on the other hand, in Culture. Let us take the case of a tribunal that judges the life of a defendant. From the standpoint of natural science, there are only men gathered together who discuss and manifest this or that natural behavior. But it would be impossible to speak of justice. Naturally, one can consider being and efficient causality; culturally, the ought-to-be and final causality. This world of ends is the sector of Culture. In it values are reflected.

#### 45. La Historia y los valores — 45. History and Values

History is a cultural science, not a natural one, say the partisans of the theory of values. It is a science because the element of universal [p. 50] significance that integrates it as such is, precisely, value. Each page of history reveals to us a moral conduct that grasps this element of universal significance: the value good; or this other, the value just; or this other, the value beautiful, or this other, the value holy. It is clear that not all the pages of history express the same values in the same forms; but if art varies, if the sciences and religions vary, and so on, as the historical expression of each human society, the eternal values neither vary nor are transformed. Egyptian art, Greek, Romanesque, Byzantine, Arab, Gothic, and so on, are diverse expressions of something grasped in man's intuition: Beauty. Moses, Jesus, Muhammad, Luther are the historical intuiters of a single value: Holiness.

#### 46. La filosofía y los valores — 46. Philosophy and Values

What is more, philosophy, which results above all from the investigation of the meaning of the world, must not place itself in the naturalistic position, blind to values. Positivism, which, attentive to efficient causality, formulated a conception of the Universe, will irremediably mutilate philosophical work, through its blindness to the interpretation of the meaning of cultural reality. Only a philosophy based on history can aspire to obtain a fully philosophical concept.

#### 47. Lo útil — 47. The Useful

Between subjectivism and ontologism there is an intermediate theory, which seems to us to constitute the true synthesis of the ontologist thesis and the subjectivist antithesis: social objectivism.

Let us consider the reasons for the social objectivism of values, as it may be inspired by the work of the French thinkers of the sociological school, especially Durkheim and Bouglé. Society is not reducible to pure individual psychology. Institutions are not explained by what is purely subjective. If something, for example, were useful to a man alone, it would not be universally useful, it would not be the useful; but since all of us men have the same needs, this universality determines the objectivity of the value utility.

A piece of iron is useful to me, but it is also useful to you. Here we must distinguish two things: utility, that is, the value which is the object of our investigation; the relation that obtains between us and the piece of iron, and the relation that exists between the two of us (I and you) with respect to the piece of iron. The latter is identical for both, and has the very characteristics that distinguish it from other things that are not iron. These [p. 51] properties satisfy me and satisfy you too, because you and I have the same needs (by our homogeneous organization) and, consequently, the same desires. A desire is a need reflected in consciousness. Then, socially, we esteem iron. Iron is useful to us, or, iron is useful. For a thing to be useful is, then, to be socially useful. Or, what is the same: when something satisfies or tends to satisfy a collective desire, it is valuable; and value has as its own essence to satisfy a collective desire. It is, therefore, a pure social relation. Note how the position of social objectivism is intermediate between pure subjectivism, which is false, and ontological objectivism, which is also false.

#### 48. Verdad, Belleza y Bondad — 48. Truth, Beauty and Goodness

Let us apply a similar reflection to truth, goodness and beauty. If something is true for me, it is also true for you, or it is not true. As the piece of iron is useful for you and for me. The relation that obtains between us is one of identity in the affirmation of the same truth; the conformity of the object with the subject exists for you and for me; in other terms: truth is our universal conformity with the object. The true relation is this relation without objections. You could not object to the relation that obtains between the object of truth and myself, who affirm it as truth; nor can I object, but only reproduce your relation. Truth is, in sum, like utility, a social conformity without objections, an objective and social identification before something that is given as an object of knowledge. The relation that obtains between you and me, with regard to what we declare identically true, is the value we grant to a given assertion. The true is, therefore, the value of the affirmation that all admit. Reality is not truth. Reality is not social; the social is inessential to it, unless it is a question of social reality; on the other hand, the social is essential to the true. A metaphysical school has erected common sense into a criterion of certitude. “Common sense,” says Balmes, “is assent to certain truths that are not evident to us by evidence or by consciousness. This proposition: I can trust the testimony of my consciousness and of evidence, does not belong to the truths of consciousness or of evidence; and yet, who doubts it? If we deny common sense, we cannot affirm truth.”

A rose is beautiful: this means that between the rose and me, who affirm its beauty, there obtains a satisfaction, for me, which is also a homogeneous satisfaction for you. The rose, simply, is. Besides, it is beautiful, [p. 52] for me. And you say: “the rose is, and is beautiful for me”; exactly as I say. Aesthetic value consists in this uniformity of intuition before the same object beautiful for us. If an aesthetic opinion is disputed, it is because the value it proclaims is doubted, exactly as an opinion that is not judged true is doubted. The objectivity of logical value, like that of aesthetic value, is social. Aesthetic value is, then, that relation of identity that obtains between us when the

The same holds with respect to the good. If two men are improved by something, this is a good for them; and if something is better still, for the two, it is a greater good; so that the good is what is best for me and what is best for you. If something is good for me and is not good for you, it is not good, it is not absolute good. Absolute good is what cannot be bad for anyone. Absolute good is what is good for all. Let us suppress society, which supplies the note of universality, and the good as value disappears, correlatively: it would have no objectivity, it would depend on an individual idiosyncrasy. Just as what is beautiful exclusively for oneself is not beautiful, has no value.

#### 49. Conclusión — 49. Conclusion

Values are not entities; all recognize this; even the ontologists themselves, who are obliged to declare that value is not a being, but a form of reality that is worth, valent. At this point one sees clearly how they multiply entities without necessity; but, we put it badly, it is not that they multiply entities without necessity, for they do not dare so much; and so, in a shamefaced way, they introduce a reality that is not and that is worth. They do not multiply entities, they multiply semi-entities or, better said, pseudo-entities, phantoms of realities and of notions. It is because they do not know what region of reality is the one in which to situate values. This region of mystery is clear to us. It is not a magical limbo, but a social reality. Values are not entities, but social valences, necessary collective ideals, organic responses of all to one and the same interior or exterior cause. Society is the great sector of reality in which values are constructed and organized. And, since society has an unfailing and constant fruit that is called culture, values are the social integration of culture; for this reason they show themselves as entities existing apart from the subject. But their reality is only social, not ontological. They are not Platonic ideas, they are social ideas; but not only ideas, but convictions and acts of the human cultural collectivity. To seek the essence of a value [p. 53] outside society is to seek the essence of culture outside society, outside history; it is, in sum, to contradict oneself.

The useful is what is socially useful; the good is what is socially good; the beautiful is what is socially beautiful; the true is what is socially true; the holy is what is socially holy; the valuable is what is socially valuable. If society is suppressed, if culture is annihilated, where will there be anything valuable? What one believes and another does not is not true; what one believes beautiful and another does not is not beautiful; what for me (subjectivism) is holy, and for you is not holy, is not holy. Society, unity, uniformity, the need experienced without discrepancy by all, is the Absolute.

The ontologists will answer: but the true, because it is true, is universal; it is not true because it is universal. We think that this is an indemonstrable hypothesis. Universality is an essential note of truth. If it is admitted that universality is the character of values, their truth is demonstrated.

#### 50. Las dos doctrinas — 50. The Two Doctrines

Both theories remain face to face. One is mythical, ontological, unintelligible; the other is verifiable, social, fully intelligible. Neither of the two is subjective. Subjectivism is false. Ontologism is also, probably, false. Between the psychological world and the ontological, there is the sociological.

The hypostasis of values in ontologism is explained, psychologically, as a mythical hypostasis. This is the very process of all mythology devoid of critical spirit, which believes it takes reality as it is given, and only within the limits in which it is given. Thus reality is duplicated with another, unintelligible real world which, once postulated, one cannot manage to understand how it accords with the changing world of the phenomenon. Now, with the entities that are not and yet are worth, two worlds will have to be explained, saying how the world of values is real and how it is given in the cultural effort of history, through the sciences, the techniques, letters, the arts, heroism and sacrifice.

Aloys Müller, an energetic and irascible thinker, devoid of originality, with all the fervor of the catechumen, exclaims: “Whoever rejects the theory of values that I maintain [ontologism] has not a philosophical head.” That is, he consecrates us dissenters as fools; but it might also be reflected that the visionary who takes his systematic delirium for the expression of reality is mad. A confirmation of this assertion would be the prior irritation Müller suffers against whoever [p. 54] might deny his philosophical position; for the demented are wont to rage against anyone who doubts the evidence of their delirium; and this is, precisely, a note that distinguishes dementia among other psychological morbidities. Of course there will always remain open to Aloys Müller the prospect of citing Hegel, saying: “He who is condemned by God to be a philosopher”... And we shall copy, as an epilogue to ontologism, these simple words that Bossuet put in the margin of a celebrated book of Malebranche: “Pulchra, nova, falsa.”

### CAPÍTULO VI. LA HISTORIA COMO CIENCIA CULTURAL — CHAPTER VI. HISTORY AS A CULTURAL SCIENCE

#### 51. La concepción de Windelband — 51. Windelband's Conception

[p. 55] “Windelband,” says Messer,<sup>1</sup> “has carried out an important development of neo-Kantianism, giving the first impulse to extend critical reflection, of Kantian style, to the historical sciences, and bringing to the fore, before consciousness, the peculiarity of these disciplines.” He expressed his renovating conceptions in his celebrated rectoral address at Strasbourg in 1894, on History and Natural Science. Both are sciences of experience, but they differ fundamentally in the manner of using empirical means for knowledge. The natural scientist sees in the individual objects of observation particular cases of a law, specimens, in themselves indifferent, of a class; because the fixing of the individual and particular serves him only as a means for the attainment of his own cognitive end, which is the apprehension of general relations, the knowledge of laws. The historian, on the other hand, holds to the particular, to the unique, to the intuitive; his problem consists “in giving new life and ideal actuality to determinate forms of the past, with the totality of their individual physiognomy.”

<sup>1</sup> La filosofía actual, p. 107. Translation by Joaquín Xirau.

“Thus the method of natural science is not the only scientific method; beside it stands the method of historical science, which is essentially distinct and equally valid.” With this result Windelband places himself, epistemologically, in sharp opposition to naturalism. He rejects, then, also the traditional division of the empirical sciences into sciences of nature and sciences of the spirit... “Now, since the directive purpose and the consequent method determine what constitutes the object of each science in a given empirical material, the division of the sciences must not be governed by the prescientific distinction between body and spirit, as two objects distinct in themselves, but by the cognitive end and by the logical structure of each science. Now, since the end of the sciences is the knowledge of invariable laws [p. 56] or of changing events, Windelband proposes the division of the sciences into sciences of laws and sciences of events [nomothetic and idiographic].”

#### 52. La filosofía de los valores y la historia — 52. The Philosophy of Values and History

The philosophy of history, oriented in the direction of Windelband and Rickert, makes of values the universal element which, in turn, gives history its significance as a science; that is, to meet the indispensable objection that there is no science of the particular as particular, Windelband and Rickert have recourse to the element value, of universal significance. Now, without needing to be a science of laws (nomothetic), history may aspire to be a science, because culture constitutes the constant historical reflection of the beautiful, the good, the true and the holy. In other terms: all historical reality is conjugated around values. Now values are not individual but universal; therefore history possesses an objective element of universality that gives it scientific character, through the unique, bygone and irreversible facts it studies.

#### 53. El elemento universal de la historia — 53. The Universal Element of History

In the preceding chapter we analyzed the conception of values as valent beings, and we objected to the ontologist theory; in this one we shall refer only to the thesis of Windelband and Rickert, which makes of values the universal element of history.

#### 54. Los valores, la naturaleza y la historia — 54. Values, Nature and History

It is beyond doubt that if the conception of history we are analyzing is accepted, history is definitively confined to the human and cultural, and leaves nature outside its field; because values cannot be given in nature, but exclusively in culture. This was the criterion that served Windelband to divide the sciences into nomothetic or sciences of laws, and ideographic or sciences of culture.

#### 55. La historia del Universo — 55. The History of the Universe

If this were so, a universal history is no longer possible, but only a history of humanity or of culture, since man, in his action, is the only being that grasps values. But this opinion stands [p. 57] in manifest contradiction with the reality of the development of scientific investigations: for, alongside the history of culture, there is being elaborated the history of the Universe, of the worlds, of the planet we inhabit, of the mineral, vegetable and animal species, as well as that of man himself as a product of the evolution of universal life.

#### 56. El dilema de la historia universal — 56. The Dilemma of Universal History

A dilemma may therefore be set against the theory of Windelband and Rickert: either history is universal, and then it is not a science of values, because values are exclusive to culture and not to nature; or it is not universal, and then it is not explained how it can be a science, because it lacks the universal seat of the values that organize it in the cultural sphere. That is, if history is universal it cannot be a science; and if it is a science, it cannot be universal. A curious example of a pretended science that finds as an obstacle to its universality the very character of universality!

#### 57. La historia del Universo y las ciencias — 57. The History of the Universe and the Sciences

Robert Stawell Ball says, in his book Historia de los cielos: “Such is the title of our book. It is, to tell the truth, a marvelous history; and if we could narrate it fittingly, its incomparable interest and its exquisite beauty would at once be recognized, leading to the contemplation of the most powerful efforts of nature.” See how an astronomer speaks of values: interesting, beautiful, and so on, in the field of astronomical natural science.

We do not think that the historical can be reduced to the human; this, in our view, is to mutilate the work of universal history, in order to give it a scientific character which in itself it does not need. Astronomers concern themselves with the history of the heavens, as biologists do with natural history. Here is this page from Buffon: “Observers admire the intelligence and the talents of bees, which have, so they say, a peculiar genius, an art that belongs only to them: the art of good government. One must observe to be convinced of it: a hive is a republic in which each individual strives only for the collectivity, and in which everything is ordered, distributed, apportioned with admirable foresight, equity and prudence. Athens was neither better conducted nor better cultivated. The more one observes the fragile people of drones, the more marvels one discovers in it: an identical and unalterable government, a [p. 58] profound respect for each one's station, a strict vigilance and the most careful attention toward the superior; inconceivable zeal for work, assiduity in the incomparable task, the greatest disinterestedness joined to the greatest economy, and the subtlest geometry employed in the most elegant architecture, and so on. I would never finish if I were to run through the annals of this republic, and define the traits of these insects that excite the admiration of their historians.”

#### 58. La fantasía histórica — 58. Historical Fantasy

Ranke assures us that he would have wished to blot out his own self in order to show the facts in their exclusive reality. And Simmel observes, rightly: “Ranke expresses the wish to be able to blot out his self in order to see the facts as they have been in themselves. But the realization of his wish would result precisely against the object he proposes. His self blotted out, nothing would remain to him with which to comprehend the not-self.” And Max Nordau comments: “The self of the historian dominates every historical narration; that of Ranke, like that of all the rest, emerges from it, tries to impose itself on the reader. Once more we are in the case of invoking the sure judgment of the ancients: no doubt subsisted for them on this point: that history is not a science, but an art. They did not seek truth in it, but beauty, and they granted it no other value than an aesthetic value...” Theodor Mommsen concedes “that fantasy is the mother of all history as of all poetry, and he recognizes, therefore, the intimate kinship of these two kinds of intellectual activity.”

We think that history is neither a pure science nor an art, because it seems to us impossible to deny the fundamental aesthetic element that impregnates historical work. For this reason, as the reader will see further on, we maintain that history is an irreducible form of knowledge, which embraces in its knowing the entire universe from its peculiar point of view, nature as well as history, and which has the same extension as the ensemble of all the sciences, and as philosophy itself.

What would be the purpose of fantasy in all history, if not the revelation of the concrete and unique individual, whether it be the history of the heavens or that of a beehive? It would be easy for us to adduce other illustrations of the thesis, equally important and definitive. Here is what Colomb and Houlbert reflect in their “Cours de Sciences Naturelles” (Geology, p. VII): “Just as the historian uses the documents he discovers to comprehend the determining reason of human events, their profound causes and their concatenation, we have [p. 59] tried to use the documents that we owe to the patience of geologists to show the harmonious continuity of the events of which the Planet has been the theater. We have tried to show how, in the course of the centuries, the progressive development of the soil and of the beings that inhabit it has proceeded. In a word, we have wished to attempt a living reconstruction of the history of the Globe.”

Paleontology and stratigraphic geology are, in good part, nothing but history, history of the planet and of the animal species. How can one deny to this part of scientific culture its essential historical value? And, on the other hand, how can one presuppose in nature the values, essentially human and cultural, acting upon the formation of rocks and the succession of geological periods, if by definition they exclude them, as objects of the nomothetic sciences? The dilemma stands: either universal history is rejected or it is denied that the essence of history is constituted by values.

#### 59. La contradicción de Rickert — 59. Rickert's Contradiction

But there is more. Xénopol, referring to the difficulty with which we are dealing, discharges upon the German philosopher this argument, which is to be treasured like gold in cloth: “At the bottom of Rickert's thought, the conception of history is much vaster; one sees it from the start in the definition he gives of history as the discipline that treats of the individual, without any restriction, a definition that exceeds by far the field of the whole of evolution and not only that of history. The same idea results from the principle affirmed by the author, that there is only one empirical reality, and that this exclusively furnishes the matter for the disciplines of the natural sciences as for the historical. It seems evident to us that this empirical reality cannot be limited, in history, to the human race alone; because in that case it would be so also for the natural sciences, whose principal field lies, nevertheless, outside humanity. It is, therefore, natural to admit that Rickert extends the domain of history to the whole field of empirical reality, inorganic, organic and thinking. He says so expressly elsewhere: ‘History is something unique and which occurs only once, whether it be a question of reality in general, of the solar system, of the earth, of the forms of life, of humanity, of cultured humanity, or of a small part of this reality.’ But how are we to reconcile this conception of history (lato sensu), the only one that can serve as the basis of a logical theory, with the notion of cultural or moral value, considered as an indispensable element for the scientific construction [p. 60] of history, a notion which, in the sense Rickert gives it, is taken only from the domain of human history? How are we to reconcile history considered by Rickert himself as the unfolding of reality in general: solar system, Earth, forms of life, physical and civilized humanity, with the passages that determine the notion of value as exclusively human?”

### CAPÍTULO VII. LA SOCIOLOGÍA Y LA HISTORIA — CHAPTER VII. SOCIOLOGY AND HISTORY

#### 60. Sociología y filosofía de la historia — 60. Sociology and Philosophy of History

[p. 61] The unconscious project of the philosophy of history was a science of human societies as it has come to be elaborated, from Auguste Comte onward. This attitude is clearly noticed on observing, from the very titles of some books of the end of the last century, such as that of P. Barth, Die Philosophie der Geschichte als Soziologie (1897), for example, that the philosophers of history gradually agreed to cede, up to a certain point at least, the supreme place they had traditionally reserved for themselves, to the sociologists. But where the initial intimacy of both kinds of knowledge is most evident is in the systems of the founders of sociological thought. The sociology of Comte is still, in part, a true philosophy of history. It is so by the nature of the facts that serve it as support; by the exclusivism of its fundamental principle and the systematic character of the doctrine. The celebrated law of the three states (theological or fictitious, metaphysical or abstract, and scientific or positive), rather than a synthesis of social physics, as its author wished, is an intellectualist formula of the history of the classical Orient, Greece, Rome and European civilization. Nevertheless, the books of Comte (even though this has been disputed with some foundation) inaugurate the synthetic investigation of social facts as scientific facts. The creation of the word sociology implies the imperious necessity felt by its author to invent a new word to designate a new thing: the science projected by Saint-Simon and which Comte began to realize.

#### 61. El materialismo histórico — 61. Historical Materialism

So too the historical materialism of Marx and his school. It is not, properly, a sociological theory, but a new philosophy of history based on the preponderance of economic facts over the other social phenomena. For Marx, the class struggle is the [p. 62] historical fact par excellence; the modifications of the implements, within the supreme economic category of the production of wealth, the deus ex machina of history. The other social categories are epiphenomena, superstructures, derivations from the fundamental consideration.

To convert Marx's materialism into an economic causalism; that is, to transform into law or scientific determinism the metaphysics, better still the mystical theory of history of militant collectivism, it was necessary to enlarge its principle and its data; that is, to transform the philosophy of history into sociological theory.

#### 62. Intelectualistas y materialistas — 62. Intellectualists and Materialists

At bottom, the intellectualists and materialists of history, Comtists and Marxists, are intimately related. Without scientific intellectualism, the modifications of the implements are impossible; without industry and production, science is likewise impossible. Nothing is more distant from the economic social function than the biological phenomena of the elaboration of food. To produce social wealth is, principally and immediately, a psychic effort. The science closest to sociology is psychology, not general biology. Every social fact is a phenomenon of consciousness. Every act of collective life implies final causation. Which does not mean, of course, denying the immense action of biological factors in human society; but adaptation, heredity, race and population act as social forces, through the psychic phenomena of society.

After Comte and Marx, authors, as has been said, of the two most recent and most effective philosophies of history, the purpose of science was no longer to forge a new systematic ideology or social teleology of progress, but to fuse, rectifying them, in a scientific explanation, historical intellectualism and materialism with other, more properly sociological theses.

#### 63. El evolucionismo sociológico y la filosofía de la historia — 63. Sociological Evolutionism and the Philosophy of History

It was understood by Spencer, Schäffle, Lilienfeld, Gumplowicz, Letourneau, and so on, that sociology could derive great profit from the study of human facts, and erect itself into an autonomous science if, far from shutting itself up in the field of history, it undertook the study of the societies that did not merge their stream with the European historical current. The method advocated by Lamarck and Darwin for the [p. 63] sciences of life could reserve very rich and unexploited resources for the investigators of society. Thus the field of social science would be broadened, placing it decidedly above the circumscribed classical history; taking, nevertheless, from the historical peoples: Egypt, Chaldea, Assyria, Persia, India, China, Rome, Greece, ancient Peru, ancient Mexico, and so on, all the useful elements which, united and linked to the investigations on the societies that have remained outside history, would form the rich and varied stock of contemporary social science.

The philosopher of history set himself to elaborate ambitious absolute theories on the past and future of humanity, availing himself for this of a limited experience referring to extremely complex nations; for historical facts, from the first Egyptian pharaohs to our own days, and from the initial vestiges of Chaldea or Hindustan to the closing years of the nineteenth century, signify, in sum, a short lapse of time and a scanty and impenetrable material for appraising the total causes of human evolution. The most elaborated languages cannot be understood scientifically without recourse to the primitive languages. Chinese, Coptic, Sanskrit are elaborations of yesterday, barely. They presuppose an immense past, which classical philology a fortiori ignored. The deistic religions of those peoples and races are, likewise, for sociology, complicated as well as recent historical monuments, truly unintelligible without the fetishism of the African tribes, the totemism of the Canadian Indians and the practices of primitive witchcraft. The economic, genetic, political, juridical phenomena, and so on, of the historical peoples, considered evolutively, leave behind an enormous field unexplored by the philosophers of history. In sum: just as biology has had to arrive at the anatomical and physiological knowledge of unicellular animals and plants in order to rise, methodically, to the study of more complex organisms, so the sociologist will go in pursuit of the social element or of elementary society, down to the primitive tribes and hordes, in order afterward to rise, with profit, to the knowledge of those great historical syntheses, such as Egypt, Chaldea or China, which professional historians place at the beginning of their speculation and at the very dawn of history.

#### 64. El organicismo y su desastre — 64. Organicism and Its Disaster

Yet another concept, sketched since antiquity, developed afterward by Hobbes and the philosophers of the seventeenth century, but elaborated chiefly by the first sociologists, came to remove social [p. 64] science from the philological field and to refer it to the sciences of nature: organicism. The idea consisted in making of human societies an organism still more complex than that of man; but an organism, in the end, like the plant and the animal. Comte maintained the thesis; but had necessarily to correct it with his historical intellectualism. Spencer admitted it partially, in making of human society a superorganism. Other sociologists, bolder still, declared, plainly and flatly, the identification of human society with biological organisms. The error (for the doctrine was erroneous) was soon dissipated. It was admitted, as could not but happen, that the nearest antecedent of human facts is not biological adaptation, but the psychic fact patent in the religious, artistic, linguistic, scientific, juridical, political and economic phenomena of society. The ingenious organicist metaphors, which constituted veritable allegories based on the constant relation of biological anatomy and physiology with what was called social anatomy and physiology, were destroyed on contact with irrefragable objections. Then it was concluded, by the most prudent organicists themselves, that organicism, far from constituting social science, had hidden the substantive individuality of social facts, the sociological difference, to offer in its place, with an illogical extension of the laws of organisms to the phenomena of history, a new obstacle to the direct and scientific knowledge of the phenomenon analyzed. Nevertheless, organicism really influenced the purpose of making sociology a natural science like botany or zoology; that is, a knowledge diverse, by its nature and its proper method, from history; ever more diverse from it. What had formerly been the patrimony of philologists was now to be that of naturalists.

#### 65. La teoría psicológica — 65. The Psychological Theory

The disaster of organicism rhythmically directed sociologists toward the search for and attainment of a psychological theory, more comprehensive and essential of social facts. But to this direction —which perhaps counts the most illustrious names of science, the Fouillées, the Tardes, the Lester F. Wards, the Giddings— there was opposed, in part at least, by its method and design, the group of prudent investigators who, directed by the philosopher Émile Durkheim, created the excellent review the Année Sociologique; a collection of monographic studies on the diverse aspects of social life, especially on the sociology of religions. The ambition of these thinkers consisted, above all, in relating sociology with history, and in undertaking, in such a form, a [p. 65] synthesis not purely biological or psychological of superorganic phenomenality, but properly sociological.

#### 66. Psicología y Sociología — 66. Psychology and Sociology

“To raise up historians [declare the authors of the Année Sociologique, in the Preface to the first number of the publication, 1896-1897] who know how to see historical facts as sociologists or, what is the same, sociologists who possess the technique of history; this is the end that urgently needs to be realized on both sides. Thus the explanatory formulas of science will be able to extend progressively to the whole complexity of social facts, instead of reproducing only their most general outlines. Historical erudition will acquire, likewise, a new meaning, because it will be employed in solving the gravest problems of humanity. Fustel de Coulanges liked to declare that true sociology is history; which seems incontestable if history is conceived sociologically.”

There reappeared, with individualist psychologists such as Tarde, and the sociologists of the Année Sociologique, the right direction of social studies. Biology would no longer be the paradigm of science. The ambition of both rival tendencies was identical on one point: to discredit sociological naturalism in what it implied of falsity and to retain it in what served as antecedent and support to sociological study.

Durkheim sought to turn the sociologist into a technical historian, and Tarde, for his part, declared at the international congresses of sociology that social science, in his view, owed more to books such as Adam Smith's The Wealth of Nations and to the linguistic work of the German philologists than to the sterile and deceptive systematic comparison of organisms and societies.

#### 67. La psicología de los pueblos y la teoría de las formas sociales — 67. The psychology of peoples and the theory of social forms

While in France and the Saxon countries sociological thought was developing within the forms indicated, in Germany Lazarus, Steinthal and Wundt were working out a genuine social psychology, whose culmination and synthesis is the celebrated Voelkerpsychologie of the philosopher of Leipzig. The hypothesis of the collective soul, the social integration of language (intelligence), myth and art (feeling) and the customs (will) of peoples, is not, however, the fundamental sociological theory of the Germanic school, but rather the classical hypothesis for the Germans (Fichte, Hegel, Herbart, Wundt) of the opposition between Community and Society.

Society means, in this theory, the mutual and reciprocal action, cooperating or antagonistic, of individuals; material and [p. 66] biological human commerce. Community is the family and the State, chiefly; that is, the ideal and unity. Community and society struggle against each other. The position of an individual with respect to his fellows is in the one precisely the opposite of what it is in the other. The struggle between communism and individualism expresses the constant conflict between community and society.<sup>20</sup>

<sup>20</sup> See G. Richard, La sociologie générale et les lois sociologiques.

The theory just outlined recalls, in a certain way, the classical Aristotelian conception. For the Greek philosopher, the end of human association is the City. Only in this sense does the Stagirite call man a political animal. "Nature the artist," says Boutroux, commenting on Aristotle, "tends to realize an ideal which is the City; and the forms that human society actually assumes are the result of that very tendency, more or less satisfied or thwarted."

In sum: today sociology has already abdicated, definitively, its organicist, materialist, antihistorical attitude. It is, by the confession of its most illustrious representatives, a human, psychological science, though not exclusively psychological. It maintains intimate contact with history (Durkheim and his school), with psychology (Tarde, Giddings, Lester F. Ward), with philosophy (Wundt, Tönnies, Simmel); but it does not abandon, nor will it abandon, its determination to become a science comparable in extent and dignity to biology. Just as that discipline tends to bring about the philosophical unification of the natural sciences, sociology, for Worms, is a philosophy of the social sciences. It will not abandon its determination, because it aspires to elaborate, from the repetitions and symmetries of societies, the theory of human uniformity; not to be confounded with history, as Durkheim and his school wished. The forms of social coexistence, through the factors of superorganic evolution: biological adaptation, geographical environment, heredity, race, population, imitation, education, division of labor, war, language, art, religion and customs: this is the object of the science.

Now history, instead of occupying itself with social symmetries and repetitions, seeks the asymmetrical and singular; difference matters to it, the proper and accidental, not the generic and common. Hence, instead of foreseeing and generalizing, it turns its predilect contemplation toward the past. Nor is it confined only to the human world; rather, as will be set forth presently, it takes in the entire universe as the object of its knowledge. So that it might be said in conclusion: neither is the sociological historical, nor the historical sociological.<sup>21</sup>

<sup>21</sup> De l'idée de loi naturelle. Les Lois sociologiques. Consult the book Sociología genética y sistemática, by A. Caso, Mexico, 1933.

### CAPÍTULO VIII. LOS VALORES HUMANOS Y LA REALIZACIÓN DEL HOMBRE INDIVIDUAL — CHAPTER VIII. HUMAN VALUES AND THE REALIZATION OF THE INDIVIDUAL MAN

#### 68. El valor económico — 68. Economic value

[p. 67] Economics is the investigation of useful value. Every need, says Schopenhauer, is a pain to which its satisfaction puts an end. So that needs urge us to action for two motives: because if we do not satisfy them we suffer; and if we satisfy them we enjoy. Pain urges us to action, just as pleasure does. To avoid the pain of not having what is necessary, we work; and in fulfilling our task, we are pleased. Here, then, is how both forces concur in determining useful activity, or economic value.

If nature offered us everything we ask for the satisfaction of our needs, and it cost us no effort whatever to acquire it, goods would be worth little and would be little esteemed; but since we have need of effort to make use of the elements the world provides, this effort is transformed, together with the raw material, into a useful value, economic value.

The supreme law of economics consists in obtaining the maximum benefit with the minimum effort. Therefore, to produce wealth is an eminently intellectual function; and the worker with intelligence, like the worker with his hands, are but two aspects of one and the same concurrent activity: economic activity.

The philosopher Mencius already said that the mission of some men consists in forming the ideas of society, and that of other individuals in supplying the elements to nourish society itself; and Mencius added that both activities are equal in dignity and decorum.

#### 69. Los valores en la historia — 69. Values in history

But man does not live by bread alone, as the Scripture teaches, although, we add, he lives chiefly by bread. That is, there is need of useful value and of other human values. The saint is as essential to the harmony of human life as the thinker or the artist; as [p. 68] essential as the producer of useful values; and axiology studies the way of harmonizing all the goods of life, in order to realize Man himself in all his august nature.

In the Middle Ages the concern for holiness predominated and values not related to war or mysticism were despised; for this reason the Middle Ages is an epoch in which man did not realize himself in his whole being. During the Renaissance the aesthetic value leapt into the foreground. All the great men of that epoch, popes, kings and emperors, were possessed of an intense artistic feeling. It was a noble emulation among nations and individuals to attain the height of Beauty. The nineteenth century is the age of the apotheosis of the material goods of life, the era of technique or technocracy. Never had economic value been so esteemed; never before would one have been inclined to explain all social life by the production of wealth; never did money so powerfully fascinate the desires and aspirations of people; but our century wishes to make justice reign over utility, and its profile differs profoundly from that of the preceding century. Imperialism reaches its maximum of power and witnesses the beginning of its own decline, in the last epoch of capitalism and technocracy.

#### 70. Los valores en la Historia Moderna de Europa. Alemania — 70. Values in the Modern History of Europe. Germany

Only three or four European nationalities have managed to influence the others, stamping their seal upon the events of the Modern Age. Spain and Italy in the Renaissance, then France; England at last. For the influence of Germany, immense in the ideological and moral sphere —religious Reformation, music, philosophy and philology— was slight in the social and political. Its imperial action was just about to begin when the dynasty of the Prussian emperors fell vanquished before Europe and, with it, the German people. The old Germania came late to the partition of the world. Frederick Barbarossa will no longer see his mythological beard grow, for any longer, within the cave of Thuringia. His beautiful legend clings like wallflower to the ruins upon the comely castles of the Rhine. Nevertheless, Germany today exercises the hegemony of thought in Europe.

#### 71. España e Italia — 71. Spain and Italy

Modern History begins with Spanish influence. Then human enterprises were religious or artistic. Men fought for a [p. 69] mystical ideal. This purpose completed geography, placing the steel in the hands of Cortés and Pizarro, as in those of the Duke of Alba and Don John of Austria. Spain fulfilled the phrase of Menéndez Pelayo: "nation of armed theologians." In that century everyone placed the things of the soul above those of the world. Erasmus, Dürer and Luther, like El Greco, Saint Ignatius and Saint Teresa; Thomas More and Servetus, were the spiritual militia of a faith. The Italians, still more pliant, substituted the humanistic ideal for the religious one. At times, churchmen ceased to read the Vulgate, so as not to spoil their Latin. For art too was another collective ideal, that is, another universal value. If the artist was a believer, like the Christian Michelangelo, the muses gathered around the Virgin, who holds on her knees the lifeless body of Jesus —as in the marvelous Pietà—; but art was also wont to convert, out of pure adoration of form, the Christ of the Last Judgment into Hercules or victorious Apollo. If the creator was not a believer, as perhaps da Vinci was not, the smile of Mona Lisa spoke one more mystery, a new aesthetic value, which ancient beauty did not know. Finally, if the artist was Spanish, like Theotocopoulos, Greece herself humbled herself before the Cross of the Savior. The profile of that century was traced, indelibly, by the mysticism of the exceptional individualities, through Religion and Art: the two collective ideals or highest values of humanity.

#### 72. La cultura francesa — 72. French culture

France fills the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries with her action, as Spain and Italy did the fifteenth and sixteenth. An epoch of synthesis and organization. Louis XIV represents the absolute monarchical regime: the etiquette of the French court is the most systematic and rigorous of formalisms; Boileau, the most tyrannical of literary criteria; Descartes and his school, the clearest of philosophical principles and the greatest synthesis of thought since Greece; Corneille and Racine, the dramaturgy most bound to the rhetorical canons; Bourdaloue and Massillon, the most outwardly perfect oratory... Only Pascal seems a belated spirit of another age, close to the great Italians in genius and passion.

The French were the disciplinarians and organizers of European civilization. They built, with materials their own and others', the edifice of reason that equals life, as life is modeled upon reason. Active and not merely speculative, they made into living historical reality what their optimates conceived for the good of all. Hence their great men are the pedagogues of the human race, and seem, [p. 70] at times, to perform the task of contemporary publicists. Fontenelle, Montesquieu, Voltaire, D'Alembert, Beaumarchais, Rousseau, are the propagandists of the spirit that seeks reason and truth, and aims to impose them upon society, obliging it to realize them. Thus they proceeded, spreading the thought they judged true in the essay, the comedy, the drama, criticism, the historical or scientific treatise, the epigram and the academic dissertation. Every literary form was, nothing more, a vehicle for imposing rationalism upon the constitution of society. The Revolution definitively closed the Middle Ages. The world, as it was then said, "was walking on its head." The last, as the Gospel says, began to be the first. France, in the political sphere, accomplished a creation of values as great as that which Italy and Spain had worked out in the artistic and religious.

#### 73. La tabla de valores de hoy — 73. The table of values today

While the dictatorship of Bonaparte was sowing the revolutionary work throughout France and spreading it through the world; while Germany, torn apart by the imperialist armies, was obtaining her magnificent place, the Saxons of the British Isles had already laboriously girdled the planet with the power of their fleets. With the mercantile height of English domination, that of great industry concurred. The nineteenth century, of a most clearly Saxon profile, is the era of machines, money and technocracy. These values, in rising to the foreground, obscure moral greatness. All strive to share out the economic good, peoples as well as individuals; nobody wishes to renounce. Asceticism and mysticism are represented by Schopenhauer and Nietzsche; but it is an asceticism of force and a pessimistic renunciation. We are all subjects, those here and those there, of the British merchants or of the Yankees, their direct descendants as regards the force of technical invention and the power of money. Historical materialism is the true mystical theory of this century, which has set having above being and value.

Nevertheless, upon the Russian steppe there is a lake of blood; perhaps with the vapor of that spilled blood the atmosphere of the world will be cleansed and the contemporary table of values inverted. Perhaps capitalism and socialism, which machines engendered, have already fought their most terrible battle. At all events, they define the profile of the century, its double profile.

#### 74. La realización del hombre — 74. The realization of man

[p. 71] The only attainable meaning of axiology is the one Aristotle fixed forever: the realization of man. The individual man is a potentiality. A perfectible sketch in a developing world. Effort is compatible with happiness, inasmuch as it culminates in pleasure, as the Stagirite taught. There is the pleasure of being bestial, the pleasure of being an artist, of being a sage, a dilettante, a hero, a saint. At the end of each act pleasure exists. What is needed is to fix the human nature to be realized, individually. Can human nature be realized in its integrity? Must it be selected? Integration of what is properly human through selective disintegration in each man: this seems to be the individual moral persuasive. Economic value does not satisfy human desiderata, not even economic desiderata, because that is impossible. Aesthetic value cannot fill life fully. No man can live aesthetically. Nor is man a pure subject of knowledge. Only he who realizes himself as personal providence is autonomous and not heteronomous. Above Sakyamuni, Socrates and Saint Francis, Jesus realized himself as providence. Insofar as each one is Jesus, he will be autonomous, he will receive the impulse of no natural law in his originally human conduct. He will be an absolute creator of values. The only imperative of Christianity is, therefore, as Saint Augustine said: Ama et fac quod vis [Love, and do what you will].

In sum:

The realization of man is a collective work of individual attainment. Since humanity has existed, only a few men have realized themselves, sporadically, and today they are as few as ever. Progress is not collective but individual. It is not the triumph of all, but of some, in the East as in the West, and so in Faustian civilization as in the Arabian or the Magian. Cultures, nations and cities; economic, aesthetic, ethical values, etcetera, are relations of men among themselves. Through these relations, God creates, in individual creation. History has neither meaning nor value. The only thing valuable and full of meaning is personal life.

### CAPÍTULO IX. LA CULTURA Y LA FILOSOFÍA — CHAPTER IX. CULTURE AND PHILOSOPHY

#### 75. Filosofía natural y filosofía cultural — 75. Natural philosophy and cultural philosophy

[p. 73] The work of human culture is not a natural phenomenon; it is an addition, a construction raised upon the base of nature, to be sure; but which by its essence implies the human world. Naturalist philosophy claimed to sum up everything existing in the study of nature. No. Nature (which has no meaning for man except through man) is only one part of knowledge. Just as culture constitutes the other part. It would be impossible to cut off, to mutilate, the object of knowledge, preferring culture to nature or vice versa. Both are the object of knowledge, with equal rights; both constitute the synthetic object of knowing. Philosophy can be neither naturalist nor culturalist, precisely because it is philosophy, that is, synthesis. Let us also guard against falling into the error of preferring the cultural to the natural; for then culture itself would lack foundation, as it would lack finality if we preferred the natural to the cultural. Efficient causes and final causes are causes. Philosophy can be nothing but this: a synthetic theory of reality.

#### 76. Cultura y ontología — 76. Culture and ontology

Culture is a being; that is, something ontological, according to Scheler. One is cultured. This adornment of the soul, of which Gracián already speaks, adorns it in such a way that it makes it other; and makes it other because it causes it to participate in the being of other beings, by intuiting them in art or knowing them through philosophy and the sciences; by acting upon them through economy and industry. An artist who forges iron changes his being; the Venetians who blow glass exquisitely change their being; as did Galileo on watching the lamp of Pisa swing, or Leonardo on formulating the ineffable smile of Gioconda.

For this reason culture must submit to Ethics and Religion; for otherwise it leaves outside being the Being of beings. Temple, Workshop, [p. 74] Family, these are the institutions of culture; the State is only a coordinating institution; a harmony of the Workshop, the Temple and the Family. The Catholic ideal is true; the Temple can embrace everything; for this very reason the Temple cannot be Roman-Catholic.

#### 77. El sabio y el mundo — 77. The sage and the world

To know is to be able. Only the sage is able. The ignorant man is nature, not culture. He is Spengler's eternal man; the rustic, not the citizen. The countryside does not know, therefore it cannot. But the city is cultured only through the sage. The sage is cultured only through knowledge, and knowledge creates values. Through knowledge the macrocosm is integrated into the microcosm, as the surrounding reality is integrated in a diamond. When Aristotle completed his encyclopedia, it was the first integration of the world.
