Nothing is possible without the sage who knows, nor the world itself, which can never know itself, except in the consciousness of the sage. God is the consciousness in which everything is known, He is the Sage. And what we call world is what we manage to reflect in our consciousness of the Consciousness of God. To intuit the universal in personal consciousness is to know, to know philosophically.

#### 78. La individualidad histórica — 78. The historical individuality

For Hegel the "historical individuality" is a creator of cultural values, within the people that engenders it. Carlyle and Emerson hold a mystical conception of the hero. He is the creator, the "symbolic or representative man"; and the other men merely imitate. They are the law and we the phenomenon of culture. Gabriel Tarde, from a position closer to Hegel, sets in relief the mystical theory of Carlyle and Emerson, and divides the work of the creation of values between the people and the genius. Every man is inventor and imitator. The greatest copy more than they invent and the humblest are capable of inventing something. Human society is like the integral of the human differentials of culture.

Positivism, on the other hand, makes humanity, the group, groups and peoples, the creative element. Race, milieu and historical moment are the agents of culture; Shakespeare is explained by his environment. For Spengler Culture is the substantive; and the exceptional individualities, the product.

#### 79. Carlyle y Spengler — 79. Carlyle and Spengler

Spengler's error consists in maintaining the inflexibility of a destiny as the cultural axis which organizes, by its germination, culture [p. 75] itself. In this way, before this fatum, as before Taine's Race, Milieu and Moment, everything bends. Only destiny is spontaneous; apart from it, nothing is, not even the creative genius of a Beethoven or a Newton. The spontaneity of individual genius is subordinated to the germination of destiny. What there is to be set forth for each great mathematician or musician will be set forth in due course, in the work of genius, in accordance with cultural destiny.

This theory forms the antithesis of Carlyle's. It is also mystical; but now the morphological principle is the cultural group, the great Faustian, Arabian or Magian soul, whereas in Emerson the symbolic personage is the creator. And for Carlyle, history lacks meaning. Only great men are creators. From Tubalcain and Triptolemus to Pasteur and Einstein. At bottom, Spengler is a mystical individualist like Emerson or Carlyle; but the individual here is Culture and not the individual genius: the classical, the Arabian, the Faustian...

#### 80. El sentido de la historia — 80. The meaning of history

For each great culture is like an individual of genius. And individual genius loses the originality of its creative virtue, at least in part, since destiny is manifest in one of the moments of the universal development of the culture to which it corresponds.

For history, which is always biography, it matters little, at bottom, in the case of mystical individualism, whether the historical individual be a man, a people or one of the great civilizations that humanity has elaborated. All are individuals. For this reason Spengler, like Carlyle, declares that universal history lacks meaning, and only cultures individually considered have it. Cultures are here the heroes. Not Tubalcain, nor Triptolemus, nor Odin, nor Dante, nor Shakespeare, nor Napoleon.

Mystical individualism bows, according to Spengler, before macroscopic individuals who polarize in their development the very plastic force of genius.

#### 81. El materialismo histórico — 81. Historical materialism

Historical materialism has another, very different way of conceiving the work of human culture. It holds that history obeys the modifications of the tools in the production of social wealth. Technique would be the nerve of historical evolution. Everything else: religion, family, law, State, Science, Arts, customs, would be built [p. 76] as so many different superstructures upon the base of the tools of production. When the economic sector is modified, all the social superstructures would, by repercussion, be modified, unfailingly.

This goes against true social ontology; for it ignores that the other forms and other social processes, distinct from the economic, are as evident as economy itself and as fundamental as it. The very problem, called by antonomasia "social," is as much an economic problem, of "income," as a juridical one; that is, of justice and morality. The proletariat could claim nothing if it had no strength; but nothing either if it had no right. To confound the orders of social activity as appendages of the economic; to insist on formulating an "economic theory of history," is to expose oneself to the exhibition against it of a "political theory" and yet another "juridical" one, with the same flimsy unilateral foundation. In the social there is social structure, not economic structure, nor "superstructures." Everything is structured; and the religious order is as fundamental as the familial, as the juridical or the political, in the typical and real synthesis.

What happens is that, the social fact being as complex as it is, great minds, like Marx, see clearly only one of its aspects, which impassions them, and to it they seek to reduce, ignoring social ontology, the other orders of phenomena. As if in an organism one organ and its function were selected, and the rest declared superorganisms and superfunctions! This lacks meaning. Materialism is a false subjectivism; though, to be sure, only partially false. Who will ever deny, above all in the contemporary world, the influence of technique upon cultural work?

Let us respect, then, in its integrity, the social essence, or, better said, the social essences. No intellectual effort can reduce the juridical to the economic order, nor the religious to the juridical, nor, moreover, the political to the religious. Harmony does not mean reduction, but necessary and irreducible concatenation. They coexist together, in concomitant and reciprocally efficient syntheses. Social science studies, precisely, these concomitant forms, these concurrent orders, these processes that develop without ever unifying. Identification is impossible; but concomitance is evident. Social structure is formed from the essential congruence of the different orders of phenomena.

#### 82. El intelectualismo histórico — 82. Historical intellectualism

Nor does pure knowledge explain culture, as Comte thought. Historical intellectualism is false like historical materialism. [p. 77] It is not true that religion is a science. Religion is not Theology, abstract theology. This exchange of values is impossible. God is not a theorem or an axiom. God is an experience, an intuition, something that cannot be converted into abstract metaphysics. The religious has its own cultural sphere. The phenomenological reduction of the act of faith to the intellectual sphere of pure thought can never be effected. And Comte himself ended his life in adoration of a new fetish, contingent and small, in sum, as Humanity is; a fetish that is annihilated before the supreme mystery of God, before the Eternal Myth that consoles and rules us...!

#### 83. La Ciudad de Dios — 83. The City of God

There remains the old and ever new philosophy of history of Saint Augustine: "Two loves formed two Cities." The one based on egoism will perish. The other, built upon Charity, is lasting. But that sublime City which faith postulates, is it real? What proofs can be adduced in its favor? Only the form of intuition which theologians call Grace, and for those of us to whom the Eternal City is not revealed, how shall we be convinced of its truth? How then affirm it universally? Perhaps the insecure and supreme philosophy of Culture is a mystical act; but this cannot satisfy everyone. Therefore, perhaps also, there will always be skeptics.

#### 84. Los individuos de excepción — 84. The exceptional individuals

Societies, cultures, humanity itself, are exclusively developments; values are collective, interpsychic, ideal relations, which produce the illusion of absolute objectivity, because they are felt to come from outside to the self, and yet have been born in the mutual and reciprocal relation of subjects. Only the individual being is real, but he is not isolated; without another like himself he could not have been modeled as he has been modeling himself in the course of the centuries. And this whole march of history, this whole succession of societies, States, nations, religions, philosophies, techniques, arts, letters and sciences, elaborated by all, takes on meaning only in the integration of superior human individuals. Hence it is seen that humanity is not carrying out any transcendent plan or purpose. Its end is immanent: to realize complete men: such as Socrates, Aristides, Plato or Alexander, in Athens or in Greek culture in general, which turns out to be less real, less personal than Athens itself, just as the celebrated city is incomparably [p. 78] less real and personal than Plato. Superior men will always be few in number, even among the best-endowed peoples and the most select races. In the innumerable multitude of human beings, wholesale product of nature, according to Schopenhauer, exceptions appear; but these are the sole justification of the rule. The day that the genius and the saint, the inventor and the artist disappear, nothing will have meaning in a society indifferent to the creation of values. Humanity would then turn into the animal herd.

Nietzsche intuited the superman. The true superman is only the superior man, a being who, as Scheler says, can say No to life. He is the creator of values. In him the cosmic laws are contingent, because he is, in himself, another law of existence.

#### 85. La tercera potencia entre el Cielo y la Tierra — 85. The third power between Heaven and Earth

In the Tchong-young, the most profound of the four books that make up the sacred treasure of China, there stands this immortal fragment, which by itself would honor a great culture. In it one does not know what to admire more: the supreme maturity of judgment, the firmness of the dialectic, or the purity of intention:

"Only men who are sovereignly perfect can know thoroughly, in the world, their own nature, the law of their being and the duties that derive from it. As they can know their own nature and the duties that derive from it, they know thoroughly the nature of other men, the law of their being, and likewise teach them all the duties they must observe in order to fulfill the commandments of Heaven."

"As they are able to know thoroughly the nature of other men, the law of their being and the duties they must fulfill by commandment of heaven, they can, in consequence, know profoundly the nature of other living and vegetable beings, and make them realize the law of their vitality, according to their nature; as they know profoundly the nature of the beings that live and vegetate, and make them fulfill the law of their vitality, according to their nature, they can, by virtue of their superior qualities of intelligence, help Heaven and Earth in their transformations for the maintenance of all beings, to their full development; and, as they help Heaven and Earth in their transformations for the sustenance of all beings, they can, therefore, constitute a third power between Heaven and Earth."

Societies and cultures have the reality of human communities. To deny them this is to fall into an absurd nominalism; but to affirm another, distinct reality for them is to incur an inadmissible Platonic realism. Only the personal human individual is [p. 79] real. The superior man is the microcosm, "the industrious compendium of the world, the little world in the great world." But the spirit of the superior man is greater than the world. He is the plenary actuation of "the feeble reed that thinks," as Pascal said.

All the perfections of the inferior beings are found in the superior man: the physical being of minerals, the vegetative life of plants, the sensibility of animals, the will and understanding of other men. But he is the link between the temporal and the eternal, the relative and the absolute. He completes the cosmic work by being a creator of values. In himself he exhausts the meaning of reality. Nature and Culture are defined in his thought and organized in his action.

### CAPÍTULO X. EL CONCEPTO DE LA HISTORIA UNIVERSAL — CHAPTER X. THE CONCEPT OF UNIVERSAL HISTORY

#### 86. La historia y la creación poética — 86. History and poetic creation

[p. 81] To study the nature of history one must have recourse, rather than to the disquisitions of philosophers, to the very works of historians. They, preferably, are the ones who can indicate to us the essence of the activity they practice.

Let us turn to the masters of the historical genre and we shall see how their labor is not merely an essay in criticism and documentation; but rather a poetic creation, or one which, at least, has much of the artistic.

It is true that today history is not written with the aim of moralizing or delighting; even though it is also true that it never moralized anyone, and that, written by Xenophon and Plutarch, it delighted all peoples; it is true, likewise, that at every step it is strengthened by the rise of the most minute and brilliant erudition the centuries have seen. Otfried Müller was able to call philology: "the integral and full perception of ancient intellectual life," and his definition is worthy of his heroic life devoted to the perfection of historical knowledge. Nevertheless, far from being reducible to mere erudition, history, taken in its fullness, is an organic, aesthetic effort of reconstruction of the past; and only he who reconstructs the life that was and the world that came to be before and later disintegrated in the everlasting evolution of things deserves the name of historian. What once happened in time and space and will never again be as it was, whether it be humanity, the animal and vegetable species or, finally, the planet itself (great historical being that made all history possible); such is its object: unique beings among their kind; unique men among men; peoples, races and civilizations that are personal, always individual, always different.

#### 87. La biografía y la historia — 87. Biography and history

The word biography has been reserved for the history of a single human person. Biography is always history. That is, a faithful [p. 82] portrait of a unity, whether it be a being or a nation. The great historians are those who, besides possessing the indispensable endowments of erudition and criticism, know how to restore, to relive the subject of their inquiries.

The historical work begins with a critical and scientific proem. The sources are discussed; the documents are assayed; the witnesses are purified before the tribunal of "pure reason." But this effort is not enough. It is necessary to go on in the historical work until the final end is reached.

When the facts have been conscientiously discussed and the documents speak clearly, the most admirable work has still not begun. It is necessary to complete it with an intuition of the whole.

Suppose, to use a metaphor which perhaps may illuminate the doctrine, that the historian is like one who had to construct a solid body from the data which, diminished and scattered, lie in museums and ruins, in libraries and archives. Suppose that the solid to be constructed were a pyramid. But, at the same time, admit that, in spite of having computed and arranged the geometrical construction, one still lacked the swift and luminous intuition of the total pyramid; that one did not see in the mind the conjunction of the faces of the pyramid at a point. In spite of all the effort of criticism, the geometrical solid would remain to be constructed, unconstructed.

#### 88. El historiador y el geómetra — 88. The historian and the geometer

It is the same with the historian as with the geometer. One must intuit, project one's own consciousness toward an ideal point in which everything converges like the faces of the pyramid in the explanatory metaphor. If the point of convergence is divined; if one has the artistic genius to sympathize mysteriously with the character of a people or of a man of genius, historical creation is achieved ipso facto. If one remains indefinitely in bare and incomplete criticism, one is no historian.

Now, this last effort is essentially artistic. It is attained only by intuition. It is accomplished only by poetic genius.

Why does the story of Joan of Arc as told by Michelet, or the biography of Frederick the Great written by Carlyle, captivate us? Why do we always prefer, to a repertory of news about the Roman Republic, a few pages of Titus Livy, and, to all the data on the crimes of the Caesars, the severe and gallant style of Tacitus? Because criticism is not intuition, nor do voluminous repertories bring the [p. 83] past back to life. Because history is always art, the profound art of evoking, upon the dust of the centuries, the soul of the centuries.

The saying goes: "history repeats itself." It is not true; history never repeats itself. The majesty of Rome and the genius of Greece will never be reborn from their venerable ruins. Themistocles will never again lead his generous, triumphant hosts. The heroes that were will never be again. There is a ground of eternal renewal in the universe. Jesus Christ told the mystery of the perennial youth of creation: "My Father worketh hitherto."

That is why history is a noble romantic intuition; an aesthetic melancholy, a feeling of love toward what will never return. For this reason, too, beside the great artists, alongside the great philosophers and the wise men who glimpse the future, stand the historians, eagerly seeking the traces of life in the abyss of the ages.

#### 89. La concepción de Bacon — 89. Bacon's conception

The proper nature and universal scope of history could hardly be characterized better than Bacon did: "The most exact division of human learning is drawn from the consideration of the three faculties of the soul, the proper seat of learning. History refers to memory, poetry to imagination, and philosophy to reason."

"The proper object of history is individuals, insofar as they are circumscribed in time and space; for although natural history appears to deal with species, yet if it does so it is only because of the likeness or resemblance that natural things comprised in a single species have among themselves; so that whoever knows one knows them all; and such resemblance leads to confounding them. If individuals unique in their species are sometimes found, like the sun and the moon, or ones that in certain respects depart from their species, there is no less ground for describing them in a natural history than there is for describing human individuals in a civil history. Now, all these matters belong to memory."

"History is natural or civil. As for the division of natural history, we draw it from the state and condition of nature, which may be found in three different states and in a certain way undergo three kinds of regimes.

"For nature is either free and develops in her ordinary course, as in the heavens, the animals, the plants and everything that presents itself [p. 84] to our sight; or she is, by virtue of the ill disposition and stubbornness of rebellious matter, thrust out of her state, as in monsters; or, finally, by reason of human art and industry she is constrained, molded and in a certain way rejuvenated, as in artificial works."

"In civil history the deeds of man are related. Doubtless, divine things do not shine in natural history as in civil; so that they also constitute a special kind of history which is commonly called sacred or ecclesiastical history. In our opinion the importance of letters and the arts seems to us so great that we judge we ought to assign them a history of their own and particular to them, which it is our design to comprehend together with civil and ecclesiastical history."

#### 90. La historia universal — 90. Universal history

From the foregoing concepts, precise and prophetic, of the De Dignitate et Augmentis Scientiarum, it will be seen how Bacon succeeded in forming a complete concept of universal history; that is, that its knowledge cannot be confined within what is properly human, but must embrace the study of the entire universe, in its regular forms as well as in its exceptional aspects (the history of monsters). That is to say, for the great philosopher history refers to the totality of existence just as the sciences and philosophy do, only that its point of view is the consideration of the individual and not of the generic and common.

Ordinarily, when one thinks of something historical, one reflects on the history of the human lineage; especially on its political history. Nevertheless, not all history is political, nor is it simply human. The stars have their history like the animal and vegetable species. The concept of universal history must embrace human and non-human history; that is, the total history of the universe.

"All energy tends to degrade into heat, and heat to distribute itself uniformly among bodies." This great cosmological law is the foundation of history. If the physical laws were reversible like the pure mechanical laws, the principle of eternal return or orbicular recurrence, which the Stoics conceived and Nietzsche formulated in his Zarathustra, would be the enunciation of the transformations of the universe; for time is infinite, and within its infinity the possible transformations of matter and force would in the end be inevitably exhausted, reproducing one of the combinations that have occurred and, therefore, by virtue of the law of causation, all the others, in their order, and rigorously identical to themselves. As Marcus Aurelius puts it: "The things [p. 85] of the world are always the same in their orbicular turnings from above to below, from age to age."<sup>22</sup>

<sup>22</sup> Soliloquios del emperador Marco Aurelio, p. 188 of the Castilian version by don Fausto Díaz de Miranda, Biblioteca clásica, vol. CXVIII.

#### 91. El principio de Carnot y Clausius — 91. The principle of Carnot and Clausius

The great law of Carnot and Clausius, the principle of the degradation of energy, introduces historicity into existence and constantly feeds and sustains it. If only the first law of energy operated, the principle that nothing is created and everything is transformed, history would not exist; the essential reversibility, purely mechanical, would make of cosmic facts phenomena without history; pure mechanical or geometrical relations. But Carnot's law makes of time a real factor, a kind of active dimension of universal existence.

"A pendulum that moves in the atmosphere from A to B, says Boutroux,<sup>23</sup> must overcome a resistance; to overcome it it will produce a certain amount of work, and in working it will lose a part of its energy. If, then, the direction of the movement is changed, the moving body will not return to the point of departure, because it has already lost energy of its own in dislodging itself in order to go, and will lose energy also in dislodging itself in order to return. It may be established as a universal rule that whenever there is work, the primitive condition is irreparably lost with the production of heat."

<sup>23</sup> De l’idée de loi naturelle, p. 109 of the Spanish version by A. Caso.

That is to say, physics, in relation to mechanics and pure geometry, is a new science which takes into account and considers in its study not only the quantity but also the quality of forces. "Heat is of lower quality than work." "The physical laws cannot be reduced to the mechanical ones," Boutroux adds.

Existence has probably been developing in a constant change of potential; which implies a constant qualitative transformation. Energy has not been lost; it is always the same; but its quality has been degraded. Without a fall of the cosmic potential, work would be impossible. That is, the degradation of energy means an irreversible and real succession of phenomena or, in other terms, a historical order. In sum: Nothing is lost, everything is transformed, by means of the irrevocable order that is history.

#### 92. Historia astronómica — 92. Astronomical history

The solar system is a testimony of the gigantic drama that tore sun and planets from the primitive nebula, hurling them along their trajectories [p. 86] through space. The ring of Saturn, the moons of Jupiter, the melancholy little world that lights the nights of our globe, are remains, testimonies of the history that they betray in their everlasting turning.

Before, everything lay sunk in the incoherent primitive homogeneity. The acts of the cosmic drama are patiently deciphered by the astronomers, and the manifold dynamic episodes of Creation are gradually revealed to us. There was a planet, between Jupiter and Mars, that perhaps broke into a thousand irregular fragments, asteroids obedient, like the stars themselves, to universal gravitation.

#### 93. Historia química y geológica — 93. Chemical and geological history

Probably, too, the various chemical elements are data of a chemical history. The new scientific spirit seems to realize the fantasy of the old alchemy. Sir William Ramsay finds, by subjecting some chemical compounds to the radiations of the marvelous simple body, vestiges that assure the possibility of the transmutation of some elements into others. Ozone, which is nothing but electrified oxygen, allotropic oxygen, takes on properties different from those of the celebrated element discovered by Lavoisier.

The geologists reveal, in the superposition of the layers of the earth, the dramatic vicissitudes of its history; and to each layer there corresponds a special fauna and flora which the paleontologists and the naturalists analyze. Just as a historian knows how to reconstruct a certain civilization —unearthed from the venerable Hellenic or Latin soil— with only a few remains of its immortal monuments, Cuvier was able to reconstruct, from a bone or a vertebra, the complete skeletal form of the great antediluvian animals.

#### 94. Historia natural — 94. Natural history

The exemplary works of Lamarck and Darwin opened magnificent historical perspectives to science. It was then conceived that the most diverse, the most distant species could proceed from one another; and man himself, olympically isolated from his brothers, began to recognize the immense heap of qualities and attributes that link his existence with universal life. Organic species, like the simple bodies of chemistry, are not stable but historical. At one time there were no chemical or biological laws; afterwards there began to develop the magnificent complexity of attributes which chemists and naturalists, physicists and biologists now analyze together.

[p. 87] Evolution is universal and not merely human. Time has worked upon all things and has exerted its action upon all. History is not peculiar or exclusive to men and human societies. Everything that is in time, far from remaining unaltered, is bound up with the rest in an incessant transformation. The very orbits of the planets are not identical in each revolution. An eternal register lies open upon life and the world, and in it are inscribed the mournful and fruitful events, human and non-human. The only thing that never changes is the law of eternal change. To exist is to transform oneself or, in other terms, to have history.

#### 95. Ciencia, Historia, Arte y Filosofía — 95. Science, History, Art and Philosophy

In this way the proper field of the historian appears as vast as that of the scientist or the philosopher. Science is foresight, generalization for the future, "anticipation of experience." Its sphere is the future, intimately linked by the present with the remotest past. Philosophy investigates the innermost nature of things, the final and ontological causes, the being that hides in everlasting change, the essence that remains veiled through interminable evolutions. History turns its gaze to the past. It leaves metaphysics in its eternal present, science in its constant future, and applies itself to spelling out in the register of the ages the world that has already been made, the reality that was. It is an incorrigible romantic. Humbly it applies itself to learning how life unfolded upon the earth, how the globe detached itself from its origin, how each concrete being emerged from the imperceptible in the course of time.

The scientist is the heroic one. The philosopher is the saint. The historian is the poet. One symbolizes the ambition that anticipates reality. Another, the mystical quietude in unalterable being. The last gathers up with pious hands the works of the centuries, and, with the dust of the ages, reconstructs vanished civilizations, species and worlds. Time, invincible and indifferent, vindicates all and disillusions all.

### CAPÍTULO XI. LA HISTORIA COMO FORMA IRREDUCTIBLE DEL CONOCIMIENTO — CHAPTER XI. HISTORY AS AN IRREDUCIBLE FORM OF KNOWLEDGE

[p. 89]

#### 96. La filosofía y su objeto — 96. Philosophy and its object

Philosophy investigates not laws, as the sciences do, but principles; that is, synthetic intuitions of the world. To order first principles, as Spencer would say, is to philosophize. The end of metaphysics is then clearly seen. The sciences provide abstract analyses of being; but existence is concrete. If we had to be content with the compendia of efficacious and coordinated abstractions called geometry, algebra, astronomy, chemistry, and so on, our knowledge would remain perpetually truncated; it would be knowledge for action, not for knowledge itself. Scientific formulas are the ideal and practical grid of life; not life itself.

Philosophy starts from a first intuition —Platonic idea, Aristotelian potency and act, atoms, monads, Schopenhauerian will, Bergsonian élan vital— and, with the data elaborated by science, the descriptions of history and the creations of art, develops its synthesis and delivers to consciousness those several supreme approximations of the spirit to reality: Platonism, Aristotelianism, monadology, eclecticism, yesterday, and today pragmatism or transcendental phenomenology.

History, like philosophy, embraces the universal extent. While things are in the process of being, they are no concern of hers. When they cease to be, they will be hers. She has no principle to verify. Like Newton, she frames no hypotheses. She does not know whether or not the world exists as a totality. This may be a plausible theory; but, in sum, only a theory. God and the soul are for her, as for Kant, ideas of pure reason. On the other hand, plurality is her object, difference her concern. Philosophy argues that existence is creation and evolution. History knows of concrete creations and accomplished changes. Philosophy says: "everything is imagination or memory, heredity or abrupt variation, repetition or originality." History perceives only unities that never repeat themselves, even when they tend always to repeat themselves.

#### 97. La misión de la historia — 97. The mission of history

[p. 90] "The living organism is something that endures. Its past is prolonged entirely in its present and there remains actual and active. Were it not so, could it be understood that it should pass through definite phases and change with age; that it should have, in sum, history? Wherever something lives, there is open, somewhere, a register in which time is inscribed."

The mission of history is to read the register of which Bergson speaks; but it is not easy to decipher the characters, at times enigmatic, of the living text. The past must be reconstructed by drawing it out of the present, never abstracting in order to generalize, approaching each singular life with that form of spiritual approach so different from pure reason: the intuition of the concrete individual.

#### 98. Diferencia entre la Filosofía y la Historia — 98. Difference between Philosophy and History

Let us not, then, identify history and philosophy, with Croce. They have in common that both are investigations of concrete entities: metaphysical principles or singular things; they also agree in considering time as real duration, not as an empty frame of existence; but in other respects they differ. To philosophize is to tend to explain universally; to describe indefinable unities is to make history. An obvious and constant difference.

To define an object of knowledge is to reduce it to genera; to think it in relation to a genus, as a species or a less vast genus within a greater (dictum de omni et nullo). Now, the indefinable is the historical. That is why the thesis of descriptive history, of historiography, as Croce himself says, is true. Description is an intellectual operation which, founded on the intuition of the individual, serves as a complement to the generalizing and defining intelligence. Scientific analysis reduces each real being to sets of general formulas that define attributes; but a being is not a set of attributes, but an individuality (singulare quid) in which reason selects attributes; that is, aspects common to the object and other objects. Timeless, geometrical abstractions have no history. Only durable concretes have it: a sun, a planet, an animal, a man, a people; or rather: the Sun, the Earth, Bucephalus, Alexander's horse, Plato of [p. 91] Athens, Dante Alighieri; England, Holland, Rumania, New Spain, Medieval Civilization, the Indo-European Race, everything that will never succumb exhausted of attributes, despite the tireless diminution wrought by analysis, because it really exists, within its own uniqueness.<sup>24</sup>

<sup>24</sup> Bergson, L’évolution créatrice, p. 16. See Lógica, p. 11, chapter IV and relevant note.

Philosophy, which is intuition, and history, which is also, express what is indefinite and indefinable by the sciences. Neither the supreme genera nor the lowest species of the logicians are susceptible of definition.

On arriving at the ultimate notions, reason confesses its essential limitation. It is powerless to explain them and yet presupposes them as principles of all explanation.

The same holds when it touches the other limit of its sphere of action: the historical individuals and episodes, which would imply the interference of infinite genera, of infinite laws, of infinite uniformities and symmetries, are likewise indefinable by reason. Neither the simple nor the complex; neither the universal essence of things nor their unmistakable individual character; neither universality nor individuality is an object of knowledge for pure reason, tireless producer of generalizations and abstractions.

Such are the limits of pure rational knowledge: philosophy, which is intuition of the universal, and history, which is reconstruction of the individual, of the unique, of the unmistakable realities that existence deposited in its continual overflowing. Between the two all the sciences and all efforts find their place. The genius of Plato and that of Thucydides define the eternal summits of intelligence, from which one glimpses the infinite, ineffable arcanum that religions evoke or humanize, at times, with the splendor of their myths and their pious and disconsolate impotence.

#### 99. Diferencias entre la Historia y el Arte — 99. Differences between History and Art

History and art, based on essentially identical intuitions, differ from several points of view. History is intuition of the concrete-real individual; it studies what has been only once in time and space. Art deals with the concrete-possible individual. It matters nothing to the artist whether a given fact has occurred or not; whether the set of circumstances would have worked in this or in another direction; provided the fact be possible, within the antecedents defined by the artist himself, he will be able to create beautiful works in giving us his intuition of reality. Reason and its determinations are for art nothing but a limit of its action. For the historian they are a constant element of his effort; a concomitant function of his activity; for this reason [p. 92] Croce has been able to refer, rightly, to the logical elements of historical judgment. The historian, a fortiori, confines himself to the narration of what has occurred. He makes us know his particular intuition of something that was once and will never again present itself under the same conditions and with the same aspects.

But within the knowledge of the possible individual there is room also for that of the individual that has been real; at least reason conceives the second as a species of the first intuition. Thus the artist can treat of what has been and what might have been in the same work of art; that is, he can mix in one and the same representation of reality, historical intuitions and artistic intuitions. This hybrid genre, formed jointly by history and art, is constituted by the historical epic, ballad, novel and drama; only that then whatever the work of art contains of historical reconstruction, even if it be better and more exact than what might be found in some genuinely historical books, is subordinated to the final end of the work, which is aesthetic and not historical.

The opinion of Aristotle and Schopenhauer, who see in art something more profound than history, seems indubitable; which does not mean that we deny to historical knowledge its indisputable necessity. But whereas the historian presents us with only one aspect of reality —the aspect it effectively had in a determinate space and time—, the artist shows us the greatest number of individual aspects, condensed in a certain psychological moment; that is, he gives us the fundamental characteristic of reality. His mission is to destroy those generalities of common life, of the social environment in which the historical occurs, in order to extract in all its diaphanous purity, in all its pristine significance, the necessary symbol, the absolute expression of the real and contingent individualities of history.

The activity of the artist differs, then, from two essential points of view from the task of the historian. The end of history is not the same as the end of art; and if both are disinterested activities of the spirit, they are not for that reason to be confounded, since history presents itself with well-defined characteristics different from those of art, so as to be able to claim its autonomy as an irreducible form of knowledge.

#### 100. El sentido histórico — 100. The historical sense

The historical sense, according to Höffding, is a form of universal sympathy. Perhaps it is the supreme form of human sympathy. To know how to interpret, in luminous syntheses, the successive vicissitudes of the life of the [p. 93] species is not only to understand but to love; to love intellectually, as Spinoza loved, as Socrates loved, as those have known how to love who, in the indefinite development of thought, came to unify in one act of consciousness knowledge and emotion, representation and will, the precise, geometrical logic of pure reason and the vital logic of instinct and feeling. Historical truth, human par excellence, like metaphysics, is engendered only in the harmony of ideas and intuition, within the intimate coherence of the spirit.

History must be written Platonically; philosophizing with the whole spirit. Only thus is new life infused into the inert; vanished institutions and beliefs rise again, and the motley assemblage of men and things evoked upon the ruins anointed with the veneration of peoples takes on new vigor, in the vast heap of secular relics that humanity deposits upon the planet in fulfilling its constant destiny; its perpetual death and its perpetual resurrection.

History is a creative mission; not an invention like art, nor an abstract synthesis like the sciences, nor an intuition of universal principles like philosophy.

## LA FILOSOFÍA DE LA CULTURA Y EL MATERIALISMO HISTÓRICO — THE PHILOSOPHY OF CULTURE AND HISTORICAL MATERIALISM

### I. LA DISCUSIÓN DEL MATERIALISMO HISTÓRICO — I. THE DISCUSSION OF HISTORICAL MATERIALISM

#### I. ECONOMÍA POLÍTICA Y CULTURA — I. POLITICAL ECONOMY AND CULTURE

[p. 101] We are witnessing in our time the undeniable decadence of certain forms of thought that have grown old beyond remedy. On the other hand new activities in the sphere of philosophical speculation enhance their content in the spontaneous productions of our days. Naturalism, materialism, empiricism are the decrepit forms. Spiritualism, axiological idealism, intuitionism are the progressive forms of independent thought.

Political Economy concentrates all its effort around the notion of economic value. In this idea the slopes of the science converge. Now, economic naturalism is powerless to clarify the notion of value, because nature never exhibits "values," but "phenomena" subject to laws. Hence the attempt has been made to assimilate Political Economy completely to the natural sciences, removing it from its own essence, distorting what makes up its object of knowledge. Hence economic naturalism represents, by itself, a great fallacy of "ignoring the question."

Economic value must be classified together with the other values of culture; but it is not, like the good, the beautiful and the holy, intrinsically valuable. It is only an instrumental value.

If it is true that all economic notions revolve around the idea of value; that the economist deals not directly with "things" but with "goods," with wealth, rent, interest, wages, money, credit, then Political Economy as a whole stands apart, by its object of knowledge, from the sciences that speculate on nature, in order to refer itself, unquestionably, to the sciences that deal with values; and since the sciences that deal with values are cultural and not natural sciences, Political Economy is, then, by full right, a cultural and not a natural science.

An inadequate division of the sciences, which long prevailed, is the cause of the economists' having seen in the knowledge they cultivate the characters (in truth nonexistent) of the natural sciences. It is because knowledge was divided into sciences of "nature" and sciences of the "spirit." But today psychology itself is a natural science, which takes its place beside general biology. Political Economy, on the other hand, in referring to values, falls irremediably [p. 102] within the field of the sciences of culture. Because, instead of dividing knowledge today into natural sciences and sciences of the spirit, it is divided into "natural sciences," whose object of investigation is Nature, and "cultural sciences," whose proper object is Culture.

Being and value are different. There are things that are and are worth nothing; there are things that are not and are worth much. Value may refer to an ideal not attained. In this case, it is beyond doubt that, though the ideal pursued has not yet been realized, it does not cease to have or to assume a great value in our consciousness. And yet it does not exist. Therefore values may remain unrealized and nevertheless be worth extraordinarily much. On the other hand, beings that are not valuable, endowed with full reality, abound. Hence it is possible to conclude that being and value determine, by their difference, two fields of distinct investigation: the ontological and the axiological sphere.

In our Latin American peoples (peoples by halves and civilization by quarters), justice frequently is not realized, is not, does not exist; but this does not mean that it is not valid; precisely because it does not exist and is valid, it is our duty to make it prevail in social relations, in the commerce of men. No political sophism can excuse us from acting toward the attainment of justice. It is clearly seen, then, how being and value are different things; and the sciences that study being differ in their object from those that study value.

Political Economy analyzes things that have a price. The other cultural sciences refer to intrinsic values. Good will is intrinsically good; as beauty is intrinsically beautiful; as truth or holiness. Economic values, on the other hand, are "instrumental" values; they serve as instruments for obtaining supreme goods. Wealth has no meaning in itself. It satisfies needs, but it must be subordinated to an ethical order of satisfaction of human needs. The rich man, the possessor of economic values, is subject to the good and the holy man, in the scale of values that gives meaning to action.

In this way economic science acquires its true cultural physiognomy. Integrated in the whole of the axiological sciences, it answers an undoubted demand of the spirit. The problem of the distribution of wealth makes clear how the categories of production and circulation are subordinated to the problem of distribution and consumption. Nature is a synthesis of force and matter. Economic production transforms these energies into "goods," into "useful things," that is, bearers of a value: economic value. Once produced, [p. 103] the goods, that is: the things that bear useful value, circulate; but production and circulation take on an accessible and human meaning insofar as what is distributed serves to satisfy the needs of consumption. Hence the economic value engendered in production and circulation is subordinated to the supreme values, to the justice that governs the problem of equitable distribution, and to the morality that determines the axiological conditions of consumption.

To produce wealth is an essentially human act, it is a phenomenon of culture, profoundly different from the material facts analyzed by physics and chemistry. What the economist wants is to achieve the greatest benefit with the least effort, and this is precisely the attitude of intelligence, which constantly tends to save effort and obtain the greatest possible yield. Therefore, what is at issue here and now is not material but profoundly spiritual, "intellectual" activities. In this way it is clearly, evidently seen how historical materialism, by itself alone, is false. The whole edifice of human culture is symbolized in the industry that manufactures, as Adam Smith would say, the tiny head of a pin. Historical intellectualism, complex and magnificent, if we are not mistaken!

Culture can be defined or characterized, at least, as the creation of values. Nothing is more perceptible than the creation of economic values. Labor is this agent producing wealth, engendering values. The worker acts upon the laws and phenomena of nature, turning things into bearers of value. Capital is nothing but the accumulation of the effort that has created values. Whence arises, beyond reply, the problem of the just distribution of the accumulated goods. If man did not always perceive the attainment of a just value, still nonexistent in the play of human activities, he would never propose to himself the problem of the equitable distribution of goods, of the useful objects accumulated by his effort; nor would the question of consumption, adequate to the wealth produced, occur to him.

Thus it is seen how the first categories of Political Economy refer to the engendering of economic values; while the last give meaning to production and circulation, subjecting their activity to the realization of the supreme values, intrinsically valuable. For what we crave is not to "produce" but to "consume," and we produce only in order to consume and for the sake of consuming. In this way historical intellectualism completes the investigations carried out by materialism, and impregnates with meaning the effort of the generations in their constant struggle for happiness.

#### II. LA CIENCIA SOCIAL, ECONOMÍA Y MARXISMO — II. SOCIAL SCIENCE, ECONOMICS AND MARXISM

[p. 104] The complexity of social facts defies all abstract schematism. Economic determinism is false, precisely because schematic, like historical intellectualism. Comte claims to ground social evolution in the development of the intellectual order, and Marx is bent on basing it upon the vicissitudes of economic factors. But the constant error of both thinkers lies in selecting a single social factor and claiming to elevate it to the rank of factotum. It is impossible to bend to a rigid model the manifold and constant variation of history.

To think that all social facts are related and correlated among themselves as phenomena of solidarity is to deny a priori all one-sided economic determinism. None of the various orders of the historical life of humanity can be declared "fundamental"; because all imply one another closely. At the center of economic life lies the intellectual factor: invention; just as social invention and imitation are made possible within the economic structure and the division of social labor.

Just as, in an organism, the life of relation is possible thanks to the support of vegetative life, so intellectual and moral life, in a society, implies the economic factors; but these cannot explain it as the fundamental structure of history, because, in turn, they imply the intellectual and moral characteristics of humanity. Producing wealth is not a primary act. If the purpose of a given production is suppressed, the production disappears.

Historical materialism supposes a basic structure (the economic one) and, above it, juridical, political, artistic, scientific, and religious superstructures. This proves to be notoriously false, because it denies the very concept of “structure” on which it claims to be founded. The so-called economic structure presupposes entire and full social life. Without language there is no economy, without invention there is no production, without moral and religious ideas to guide production, supply is not conceivable in itself. Without customs there is no exchange.

Economic values, which are means and not ends, cannot be conceived without ends. Wealth is not the determining being that the Marxists suppose, but a becoming determined by the complex of social life.

The categories of Political Economy are production, circulation, distribution, and consumption. The final cause of producing is [p. 105] consuming, because one does not produce for the sake of producing but in order to consume. Posita causa, ponitur effectus [once the cause is posited, the effect follows]. If the purpose of consuming a given wealth exists, the effort it cost to produce it is understandable. Therefore, becoming, the purpose, the purpose of consumption, is what explains production. It is not being that is the cause of becoming; rather becoming (the usefulness of the product) is the cause of being (the article produced).

Moreover, if the condition of demand is modified, the possibility of supply is likewise modified: Variante causa, variatur effectus [when the cause varies, the effect varies]. No one produces what no one desires. And in this subordination to social ends of consumption, all historical activities are conjoined: religious, ethical, aesthetic, scientific, juridical, and political. Once again it is confirmed how becoming, contrary to the opinion of Feuerbach and Engels, determines being.

Finally: suppress the purpose of a given production, and the production itself will disappear beyond remedy. Economic value, in this way, far from being subordinated to efficient causality alone, implies the order of desires and historical beliefs. It is something psychosocial, intrinsically. Sublata causa, tollitur effectus [with the cause removed, the effect is removed].

As regards production, can there be anything more intellectual than producing wealth? In our days, all production of wealth is superintellectualized. Pure muscular labor does not exist, because technology is scientific law embodied in machines. Industries are concentrated in formidable plants, which intelligence coordinates by means of “knowledge of utilization,” as Scheler would say. And if our century sees the apotheosis of action fulfilled, it is only because it constitutes, in the social sphere, the apotheosis of intelligence.

As regards circulation, nothing is more psychological and spiritual than exchange; nothing more “mental” than value and price. The characteristic of human Society, as opposed to communities, is precisely exchange; because value is not only labor, but the desirability of what labor engenders. It suffices to consider the exquisite phenomenon of credit to be convinced of the immaterial character of the category of exchange. Credit is faith. Charles Gide says in a magnificent lecture on “Materialism and Political Economy”: “first there was ‘land-wealth,’ then ‘labor-wealth,’ and, in the end, ‘faith-wealth,’ which today binds all the peoples of the world together in the dramatic episodes of the world economy.”

Finally, the distribution of human goods is not merely an economic question, as Marxism would have it, but an ethical and juridical one. The idea of the just is woven together with that of the useful in indissoluble union. In all societies, in all epochs of history, the work [p. 106] of the ideal of justice has been fulfilling itself. The “ideologies” of humanity (among them historical materialism itself, with a double ancestry in idealist philosophy and utopian socialism) are a preponderant cause of the social problem. Far from Political Economy being a justification and exponent of materialism, it is a clear and obvious transcript of idealism: Spirit in producing, spirit in circulating wealth and in consuming it!

It is clear that the extramental object, things, matter, being, are always there; but they do not become economic values unless they satisfy human ends, by means of the transformations that intelligence makes them undergo. “External goods receive their goodness (bonitatem) from their ends, that is, their dignity and validity derive from their services: What serves a determinate end, says a great philosopher of the Middle Ages, receives its goodness from that end.” It is necessary, therefore, that external wealth be a good of men, though not of the supreme kind, but of an inferior order; because the end is a good in itself, whereas the means is so according to how it is adapted to the end. There is no pure matter in Political Economy. Only through the mind does economic determinism make sense. For this reason the laws of Economics cannot be reduced to the type of simple natural laws. Efficient causality governs in the physical world; final causality, in the biological and the social world. History is the point at which both forms of causality converge, through the human mind. Values and ends complement each other; because if something is valuable, it is valuable for the attainment of an end, indisputably. Only through spirit does economic determinism make sense.

But the supreme reason that forbids disputing, with any chance of success, the thesis we maintain of Economics as a cultural and not a natural science is that the foundation of all economic categories is this: “the maximum production obtained with the minimum effort.” Now, the functioning of intelligence is stated in no other way. According to the empirio-criticism of Avenarius and Mach, this formulated law signifies the effort of human intelligence. To produce wealth is, therefore, to realize the intellectual order, economically. “Thus, as Professor Hearn, of the University of Melbourne, aptly described it, Political Economy (says Stanley Jevons) is the science of efforts to satisfy needs; it teaches us to find the shortest road to the realization of our desires. The object we propose is to attain the greatest number of goods at the cost of the least possible labor.” Identity of the economic principle and the logical law of the functioning of intelligence! ...

[p. 107] Historical materialism, in consecrating the preponderance of the economic order among all social orders, in declaring the latter epiphenomena and superstructures, does not and cannot go beyond the impassable limits of Spirit. Thus historical intellectualism and historical materialism are indissolubly linked; because human needs, felt as desires, can only be satisfied by intelligence. Matter is not manipulated socially except through culture. In this way it becomes wealth.

#### III. LA FILOSOFÍA SOCIAL Y EL MATERIALISMO HISTÓRICO — III. SOCIAL PHILOSOPHY AND HISTORICAL MATERIALISM

Historical materialism considers economic value preponderant. Here are some relevant texts, the work of Marx himself, which we prefer to the jumble of commentators, often devoid of culture, minuscule beings, without science or conscience, who prosper equally among European agitators and in the Indo-American slums. “In the social production of their existence, men enter into determinate relations, necessary and independent of their will. These relations of production correspond to a determinate degree of development of their material productive forces. The sum total of these relations of production constitutes the economic structure of society, the real basis on which a juridical and political superstructure arises, to which determinate social forms of consciousness correspond. The mode of production of material life conditions the social, political, and intellectual life process in general” (preface to the Critique of Classical Political Economy).

Let us concede, in the first place, that the determining action of the “economy” upon “ideology,” as the Marxists say, is unquestionable. But let us also demand the confession of the unquestionable determination of “the economic” by “the ideological.” For the Marxists, the unconscious determines the conscious. This is true; but not absolutely true; because the conscious also determines the unconscious. That is: human society, in its exquisite complexity, is the most notable example of reciprocal action. A acts upon B and B reacts upon A. Religion, for example, determines the economic: exchange from tribe to tribe, from one human group to another, is not practiced, at the beginning, because a set of prohibitions of a mythical order forbids it. Another example: religious things are placed outside commerce because a taboo, a stigma, protects them. In these, as in other innumerable examples, it is “ideology” that is the determining factor and the “economy” the determined.

[p. 108] But it is also true that the economic engenders historical aspects of religious evolution. Example: the preaching of indulgences in Germany was, indisputably, part of the cause of the Protestant movement. Another proof: the development of Christianity in decadent ancient society was due, in part, to the psychological and moral state of the classes oppressed by slavery. Had the mentality of the slaves and the dispossessed of fortune been different, the social environment would perhaps not have been so propitious to the rise of Christianity.

It is seen, therefore, that “the economic” explains, in part, the religious; just as it is observed that the religious “determines” aspects of “the economic.” That is: reciprocal action, not unilateral determination! Not “structure” and “superstructures,” but concatenation and social synthesis. Not economic causalism, but social determinism. Not historical materialism, but mutual and reciprocal action of the material upon the ideal and of the ideal upon the material; and, in all strictness, nothing is material, not even the economic order in itself, because it is impregnated with mentality.

The Egyptian myths are, in part, the flora and fauna of the country, divinized: the falcon, the ibis, the ox, the crocodile, the palm tree, the sycamore. Conversely, the opulence of the Jews in the Middle Ages was due, in part, to the fact that usury is not anathematized so severely in the law of Moses as in the Christian communion. In the case of the Egyptian myths, as Marx would say, “the mode of production of material life conditions the intellectual process”; and, in that of the Israelite bankers, moral law and religious creed acted upon the economic form; or, inverting, precisely, Marx’s formula: “the intellectual process (the ‘ideology,’ as a ‘creole Marxist’ would say) conditioned the mode of production of material life.” Therefore, in conclusion, to claim that the economic is fundamental and the religious “superstructural” is unacceptable. In the beginnings of social evolution, Religion plays a most important role; but so does Economics. In sum: There is no Oberbau nor Unterbau [superstructure nor base]. There is no “fundamental structure,” as Marxism would have it.

To insist on making human society an “organism of ideas,” without correlation with the physical environment, is impossible; but to claim that culture is something adjectival, and the economic and the technical something substantive, is false; because technology and economy are already spirit. Social forces of both kinds are always and everywhere conjoined, thus composing the essence of social life. Marxism is a false schematism of history, fortunately already superseded in contemporary speculation. In themselves, economic values lack meaning. Nothing is useful unless it is useful for something; nothing is wealth, unless it enriches [p. 109] for a purpose of pleasure or power; nothing is worth anything economically in itself. The valuable is an “ideological” category, by its essence, even when they do not believe it or pretend not to believe it, the partisans of outworn social systems.

Furthermore, the materialist conception of History, in the face of social philosophy, discards the individual factor. This is as absurd as claiming the elimination of historical contingencies. In this way, economic determinism becomes a lazy fatalism. Without the person of Jesus called Christ, Christianity is inconceivable. His divine example fills the Gospels. Suppress the “historical individuality” of the Messiah and you will annihilate Christianity. As Mohammedanism will be annihilated without Mohammed and Cartesianism without Descartes and Malebranche. Abstract from Marx, and Marxism, which for some reason is so called, will also be annihilated. How could he who principally and eminently engendered a social, moral, or religious doctrine be a mere accident in its evolution? This absurd disregard of the individual in the historical evolution of humanity will always invalidate the “collectivist” explanations of Marxism.

It will be answered: the individual is explained by his social environment. Yes, in part. Because he too constitutes an element of the causality of the social environment. Once again, reciprocal action! The Individual acts upon Society and Society reacts upon the Individual. Hegel, more profound than all the Marxists, pointed to the “Historical Individual” as one of the causes of History. Suppress Marius, Caesar, Pompey, Octavian, Antony, and explain, if you please, Roman History. Pascal maintains that had Cleopatra’s nose been shaped differently, the evolution of European history would perhaps have been other. In conclusion: the hero, of whom Gracián and Carlyle spoke, does not alone explain the evolution of culture; but without him, History is likewise in no way explicable.

The unilaterality of the Marxist conception, essentially hostile to the Individual and to Religion, disguises itself with the amphibological style of the celebrated revolutionary thinker.

The illustrious French logician Edmond Goblot says in this regard in his recent book “The Logic of Value Judgments”: “Marx is difficult to understand. Through our fault or his own? It is, according to his adepts, because his genius exceeds us. His thought rises to heights where average minds cannot follow him, or descends to depths where obscurity should not astonish us.”

“It is natural that common minds should be bewildered. But it might also happen that Marx’s thought were formless and incongruous [p. 110] in itself, to satisfy our reason. If I do not understand a page of Marx, I reread it slowly, attentively; I analyze it in its details. I ought then to find the thought that had been hidden from me; but, instead, I find only confusion of ideas and logical incoherence, which make me understand why I have not understood.”

This is addressed to the pedants and fanatics, as numerous in Mexico as in Europe, who have made a fetish of Marxism, and of Marx himself an implausible Totem; something like the sacrosanct Moloch of the messianism of the proletariat!

#### IV. LA FILOSOFÍA JURÍDICA Y EL MATERIALISMO HISTÓRICO — IV. JURIDICAL PHILOSOPHY AND HISTORICAL MATERIALISM

Like soul and body in the celebrated problem of the correlation of substances, so Law and Economics are closely concatenated in their development, without ever being reducible to one another. The whole work of the distinguished jurist and philosopher Rudolf Stammler, “is a reaction against the materialist conception of History,” says Rivera Pastor in his book Las Doctrinas del Derecho y del Estado.

According to the materialist conception of History, Law is a posteriori, effect and not cause, a late and derived exponent of the economic state that preceded it: epiphenomenon, superstructure, not an irreducible form of the social. Law is “an echo of the past that does not speak of the future.”

According to the thesis of Natural Law, Stammler reflects, the historical was opposed to the rational. The world of unconscious and unreflective impulse, chance, was opposed to the rational order, to natural law.

The juridical ideal, for Stammler, is not a rational code nor a program, because it lacks content. It is a form of reason, whose matter is history. From this the relation between Economics and Law can be inferred. They are incommensurable terms with respect to each other. The maximum of utility cannot found the minimum of justice. Just as it is not possible, by adding finite quantities, to reach the infinite. This, as Pascal would say, belongs to “another order.”

Thus the economic facts of universal history and their juridical forms have marched together; but this concatenation places us in the presence of concomitant and incommensurable magnitudes. It is useless to try to place under a common denominator what by its essence differs; what exhibits its radical heterogeneity with full evidence. The fundamentality of the economic cannot be admitted in the juridical. The contingencies of universal economic history cannot give rise to Law. That would be magic.

[p. 111] A positive social science, distinct from historical materialism, will never explain the juridical by the economic, nor the latter by the former. Social truth confirms the assiduous relation of both values, but does not strive to unify them. Correlation is truth; reduction, error. Scientific synthesis must not prefer one of the theses to the other. Knowledge that denies its own elements annihilates itself, because it lacks objectivity. Just as it is not possible to give a juridical theory of the economic, it is impossible to offer an economic theory of the juridical. And yet, on every page of History, the ever-changing, protean matter of Economics goes on bending itself to the juridical form! But not as a principle that necessarily engenders its consequence; rather as a liquid that fills its container, and molds itself ductilely to it. All law and all economy are “historical,” from one point of view; but juridical value expresses the ultimate purpose, and the economic is the matter that is informed by the design.

Before Marx, Universal History was studied without attending sufficiently to economic facts. A baneful error! It was an incomplete history. After Marx, everything tends to be explained “economically.” Another baneful error! The first point of view is as false as the second. The error is as false in its first as in its second manifestation. For true science recognizes the real causes and the limits of its explanatory reach. In upholding the hegemony of the economic, the autonomy of the non-economic is denied; and in ignoring the economic factor a positive cause of History is disregarded. Social life is subtler than the schematisms that claim to confine it.

“Within the bosom of society, says Stammler, or what is the same, in the midst of mass phenomena, aspirations arise that tend toward the transformation of the legal order in force. These aspirations clash with those that tend to conserve the established legal order. If the former triumph, the present legal order collapses, to give way to a new positive law. But, within this new ordering, new phenomena soon appear that represent the homogeneous development of the legal order. And, once again, those phenomena will provoke aspirations of transformation, which will end by prevailing, if they attain sufficient strength for it.”

The historical materialist G. Plekhanov, one of the intellectual pillars of Bolshevism, has written this brief formula, which condenses the vindication of Marx, Engels, and Feuerbach against the idealism of Hegel: “I am a partisan of the fundamental idea of historical materialism; it is not consciousness that determines being, but being that determines consciousness”: then, the economic is “being,” and the juridical, “consciousness”; [p. 112] but the truth is that true being is as much matter, material being, as consciousness, immaterial being. For this reason Law and Economics are integrated in a more truthful expression than Marx and his disciples think. Both are, and both are consciousness. The metaphysical error reacts upon the false conception of the relations that obtain between Economics and Law.

Inconceivable paradox! ... A social revolutionary, like the founder of the materialist conception of History, disdains, as Menger says, “the ideal orbit of the juridical.” Consequently, he tends to ignore, systematically, the relations between moral and economic values. Nevertheless, his own social vindication implies those juridical principles that he denies. For this reason he is urged to make of History a pure economic determinism, in which the ideal always appears as “effect” and not as “cause.” But science, never unilateral if it is to be true, must unite in its synthesis economic determinism and juridical idealism, thus answering the decisive urgencies of human consciousness, which make of the useful value a mere instrument of Civilization.

The pluralism of values renders intelligible the most complex course of History. By contrast, the baneful materialist theory, which subordinates as “epiphenomena” Politics, Law, Religion, and Culture to the economic phenomenon, fails to recognize the proper essence of each of these historical orders and, in failing to recognize them, claims to achieve an impossible knowledge. Nothing can be an object of knowledge without being respected in its integrity.

Philosophical thought does not consist in the absurd endeavor of unifying by disregarding, but in the respectful consideration of the different as different, the similar as similar, and the identical as identical in itself. Law and Economics affirmed their own being by showing themselves allied and diverse, within historical evolution and concatenation.

The materialists, like Marx, deny natural law and affirm only positive law, an emanation of the State. The partisans of natural law say: a law that does not conform to reason is not law, but simply a fact. The truth is that, since law is derived from traditions and customs, from beliefs and desires, as Tarde would say, it expresses at a given moment of social life that immanent ideal which constitutes the social essence of natural law, as well as the set of practices that is synthesized with beliefs and ideals. That is, natural law and positive law are two aspects [p. 113] ever linked of Law, which are incarnated in the inseparable facts of human solidarity.

#### V. HISTORIA O MATERIALISMO — V. HISTORY OR MATERIALISM

As one passes from the ontological to the social, from metaphysics to history, the conflict of “historical materialism” grows sharper. For a materialism can be sustained in itself; but it is impossible to affirm a “historical materialism” in the present state of philosophical thought. Only those who live hypnotized by the philosophy of the nineteenth century, which Ortega y Gasset has already declared “outworn,” can feel nostalgia for those systems, surpassed in the ceaseless effort of human intelligence to resolve the eternal metaphysical and moral unknowns. Only this species of romantics —the worst of all— who obstinately look backward, deaf to the clamor of the new theories, can today, like Lot’s wife, petrify themselves in the ecstatic contemplation of the errors into which Marx and his followers fell, unintentionally, as children of their century and actors of their historical moment. They were representatives of their age, of the century of the apotheosis of the natural sciences; for this reason they advocated an economic causalism of history, with a materialist foundation.

In that stage it was thought that the supreme model of the sciences was natural science. Just as in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries it was maintained, especially by the Cartesians, that mathematics was the type of universal science. Hence Marx and Engels pretended to give a “materialist theory of history.” Their endeavor consisted in referring history and its problems, by analogy, to natural science, erected in this way into the ideal and supreme model, not only of the scientific but also of everything comprised in the various cultural sectors. An unattainable endeavor! The historical materialists, like the Cartesian philosophers, failed to recognize the profound truth that lies in maintaining that not every method is suited to attain every kind of knowledge. Natural science confines itself within its sphere; there it is unassailable as science; because its object can be investigated by its method, the method of the sciences that study problems indifferent to values. In physics, chemistry, etc., it is not a matter of cultural problems, but of natural questions. In history, on the other hand, only cultural problems are at issue. The Universe fits within these two supreme significations: nature, culture. Now, culture is the same as “creation of values.” What values are created in the world of physical and organic forces?

[p. 114] Values occur in History, not in nature. The good, the holy, the useful, the beautiful, the bad, the profane, the useless, the ugly, are values, objects of knowledge of cultural science. Therefore, all naturalism (not merely materialism) is impossible. Just as physics cannot be studied as a problem of value, history cannot be analyzed as a physico-natural problem.

History possesses its own peculiarity, which sets it apart from the natural sciences. A historical materialism is yet another contradiction, in two words. If it is materialism it cannot be historical, and if it is historical it cannot be materialism. Economic history cannot be sustained as “materialist.” It implies spirit. It is as “mental” as the history of Art or of Religion. To create wealth is a cultural fact, not a natural one. To produce wealth implies human invention, language, desire, the division of labor, solidarity. If it were true (which in itself is absurd) that the economic factor preponderantly determines history, this alone would already have demonstrated the vacuity of all “historical materialism”; because economic laws are not natural laws, but social ones; because, as great contemporary sociologists have shown, the economic phenomenon is impregnated with culture, is culture, according to its own essence; not nature, not matter; but intellectuality, desirability, spirituality, mentality, in sum.

To maintain, on the one hand, a “historical materialism,” and to affirm on the other that such materialism considers the economic structure of societies fundamental, is to be the victim of a gross and momentous error. Economics, as a cultural and not a natural science, lives, like the other sciences of its kind, upon psychological investigations. “The error of the first architects of political economy, Tarde has said, consisted in persuading themselves that, in order to constitute the body of the science on which they speculate, the only means was to devote themselves to the material and external aspect of things, separated, as far as possible, from their intimate and spiritual aspect.”

This is also the error of Marxism. Economics studies values, not things. The sciences of nature analyze beings or things, not values. Now, what is the useful? What is wealth? What is exchange? What is price? ... Rent, wage, interest? ... Are they, by chance, beings, things, like the objects of natural science? No, certainly, they are values, like the other objects of cultural science, like all historical objects. Values, like good and evil, the ugly and the beautiful, the profane and the holy...

The theory of “historical materialism” turns out, then, to be inadmissible, superseded, “outworn.” It was estimable in its time; today it cannot [p. 115] be sustained in the face of the results of modern science, but can only crumble as something eaten away by time and sifted out by criticism. Only for the mystics devoted to Marxism does it remain unscathed. But this is belief, faith, religion, not science. We shall never discuss religious opinions or mystical attitudes; all respectable if they are sincere.

Considered from this peculiar point of view, Buddhism, Protestantism, Taoism, fetishism, and Marxism, communions of believers who swear by Buddha, Lao-tzu, Luther, or Marx, are unassailable. Let the believers officiate within their respective temples, protected by the public respect of those of us who do not have the honor of sharing their dogmas!

Only in one sense can we admit the truth of historical materialism. Here it is: materialism is a historical fruit, that is, something that is passing, or that has definitively passed into history. In this way, who could ever deny it? It is a fact that pertains to the past. To deny the past is a metaphysical impossibility. All materialism, that of Marx and Engels, like that of Democritus and Epicurus, or of the French philosophers of the eighteenth century, is “historical.” Human intelligence, in its perennial evolution, makes today’s truth the object of tomorrow’s criticism; and often turns back to the past, even the remote past, with its predilect attention; to make of the systems that prevailed centuries ago the precious and fecund nourishment of contemporary speculation. This constant rectification and ratification composes the rhythm of thought.

The great philosopher of history Oswald Spengler says: “Books like the Social Contract and the Communist Manifesto are powers of the first order in the hands of men of will who knew how to rise in party life and to form and utilize the conviction of the dominated masses. But the force of these abstract ideas does not extend beyond the two centuries that party politics lasts. In the end they are no longer refuted but tedious. Rousseau has long been boring. Marx will be so shortly.” To us, we confess it ingenuously, he has begun to cause tedium.

#### VI. EL DILEMA DEL SOCIALISMO MATERIALISTA — VI. THE DILEMMA OF MATERIALIST SOCIALISM
