One of the gravest errors of the theorists of socialism consists, in our view, in the pretension of tying the justice and truth of social claims to the materialist foundation, as a consequence is tied to a principle or a theorem to an axiom. Proceeding in this way, whatever there might be of reason and morality in the social theory that is upheld is subordinated to a metaphysical thesis already, happily, discredited [p. 116] in the philosophical field.

The foundation of a hypothesis communicates its value to the elaboration built upon it; so that, if the base is flimsy, the theory likewise shows itself flimsy; and if, on the contrary, it proves firm and tested, its evidence is communicated to the whole system of the edifice.

Science must be erected upon unquestionable foundations. All science is true science; its implicit ideal is to become so fully. Therefore, “scientific socialism,” as the theory of Marx and his adepts is called, must be founded upon unquestionable principles in order properly to deserve that name.

Now, nothing is more debatable than materialism as metaphysical truth; hence, nothing is more self-contradictory than a “scientific socialism” with a “materialist” foundation.

We are going to demonstrate, in this chapter, that either socialism ceases to be materialist in order to deserve the epithet “scientific,” or it is materialist in its genuine Marxist foundation, but then it cannot deserve the qualification “scientific.” In sum, we set against Marxism an inflexible dilemma: materialism or science; Marxist foundation or “scientific socialism.”

Materialism is still strongly accepted in various sectors, Messer affirms; but “we can say, without exaggeration, that all the serious representatives of present-day philosophy have recognized it as insufficient and have abandoned it as a conception of the world.” We should like to be given the name of a single great materialist thinker, a contemporary of ours, who could be placed beside Bergson, Husserl, Hartmann, Meyerson, etc.; although we already know in advance the insipid answer that is candidly wont to be given: “all these philosophers cited are representatives of the bourgeois mentality.” Sublime reason! Valiant folly! Logic is neither “bourgeois” nor “non-bourgeois.” It is Logic, plain and simple; without the restrictions of historical moment; even though Karl Marx busied himself writing a pamphlet on the “poverty of philosophy,” instead of having, like his master Proudhon, meditated on the “philosophy of poverty.”

Reality, what is, is material or non-material, ideal or non-ideal. That is: there are material realities, like stones; and immaterial realities, like thoughts. What is proper to the material is to be in space: or, what is the same, the material being possesses as its characteristic note to manifest itself in space; it is obvious that ideas do not manifest themselves in space, hence they are not matter; hence there are realities which, not having the notes that the material exhibits, are not material. [p. 117] I think the number 2. The number 2, as a thought, neither endures nor extends in space. This is absolutely incontrovertible. To deny material reality is as impossible as to deny immaterial reality. Thought is real. Matter is real. Both are so, but they are not the same form of reality, the identical reality.

Then it is clearly seen why materialism is an absurd metaphysical hypothesis. Its discredit stems from the fact that it denies a part of reality, ideal reality. Moreover, what is directly given to us is the idea; only through it do we go to material realities. Consciousness is consciousness of the real, and the real may be material, certainly; but, in itself, consciousness is immaterial. Materialism turns out to be a false simplification of the real; and, since every true philosophy must give an adequate notion of reality, materialism, being inadequate, has lost its prestige before contemporary philosophy; and this, equally for “bourgeois” and for “Bolsheviks,” because Logic bears upon all distinctions and is absolute in its evident principles. With reason it has been called “art of arts and science of sciences.”

If materialism suffers from the capital defect of failing to recognize forms of reality as notorious as the ideal ones (thoughts), then materialism is radically false. Moreover, every science either is true or is not science, because a false science implies contradiction. Therefore, either socialism is not grounded in materialism and, if it conforms to this condition, may deserve the label “scientific,” or it is grounded, as Marx and his followers would have it, on materialist ontology; but in that case it will rest on something false, it will no longer be “scientific,” which is, precisely, what we set out to demonstrate.

But it is not enough to prove that an opinion is false; we wish to carry our analysis as far as indicating the psychological origins of the error we combat. We do not merely show that the historical materialists are mistaken, but set out for them the history of their error, to see whether they may thus amend it. All consciousness is consciousness of something. If I will, I will something; if I love, I love something; if I think, I think of something; if I desire, I desire something; if I fear, I fear something. There is no will without an object of the will, no love without an object of love, no thought without an object of thought. Consciousness is this constant directing itself toward an object. When we are conscious of something we subordinate ourselves to the object of our will or of our thought.

Hence arises the fatal materialist error. Because we constantly handle material objects, we suffer the illusion of thinking that everything is material; and, when reading history, we believe that the Crusades and [p. 118] Protestantism are explained economically; it is obvious that there are material and economic elements in history; but we ought to reflect that our thoughts, our consciousness and our will are not material but, precisely, immaterial, even when they take as their object aspects of material reality. We ourselves are consciousness, thought and will, not the matter we make use of, but the immaterial cause thanks to which matter yields to intelligence and action. This is the origin of materialism, first as a psychological attitude and then as a metaphysical conception.

Deceptive illusion that forbids us to inquire into the structure of physical, intellectual and moral reality! Vain speculation that hides from us the world in all its most complex splendor! Sophism that belittles man and his history, with deplorable extensions toward the moral and the social! We think that socialism, in order to be “scientific” (a title with which it adorns itself), must now abdicate its materialist attitude. We do not live in the days of Marx and Engels. A century has passed since then; and, in the space of a century, ideas change, institutions change, customs are also transformed, to the point that today even conscious socialists turn their gaze toward the moral philosophy of value, and say with Radbruch: “The socialist professes socialism, not because he knows it to be inevitable, but because he perceives the contemporary social state as unjust, as exploitation and oppression; and, on the other hand, he contemplates the socialist order as a demand of justice”... Justice!.. One of these thoughts, one of these immaterial realities that, without occupying a place in space, move history through time! Materialism is intrinsically false; as indeed, according to the apt expression of one thinker, it is “the dunghill in which philosophy is fertilized.”

#### VII. LA CONTRADICCIÓN INTRÍNSECA DEL MARXISMO — VII. THE INTRINSIC CONTRADICTION OF MARXISM

In the preceding chapter we pointed out the flagrant contradiction between “scientific socialism” and its “materialist” foundation. The first contradiction alluded to is, then, between “Science” and “Materialism.” The second, which we shall analyze presently, proves still more serious than the first, because it is intrinsic. That is, it can be proved that, even admitting historical materialism as something unscientific, there is an evident opposition between the materialist principle of the system and its dialectical method. Considering the first contradiction noted, one might say: indeed, historical materialism is not “scientific”; but it subsists in its entirety as a speculation, as a creed. On the [p. 119] other hand, nothing could be alleged to dispel the second objection that we raise, here and now, against the Marxists.

Marx was an eminent disciple of Hegel. From that most illustrious philosopher he took the dialectical method, which Hegel had already applied, beforehand, to the understanding of History, in his celebrated lectures on the “Philosophy of Universal History.” The Hegelian dialectical method consists in the constant and evolving synthesis of thesis and antithesis. Every isolated idea, says Hegel, is false; it represents only a more or less considerable and imperfect part of reality; it demands its complement, it turns into its negation. Now, that two negations should coexist is impossible; every idea finds in a higher idea the affirmation of what it contains of truth and the negation of what it contains of falsehood. The synthesis is, at once, affirmation and negation of the thesis and the antithesis. But, in its turn, the synthesis—which is an idea like those very ones whose contradiction it resolved—has its antithesis in a new idea which, together with it, is resolved in a higher synthesis, from the idea of pure being, or abstract concept, up to the absolute idea, which embraces everything. Hegel could dare to construct the Universe dialectically because, in the sublime phrase of the philosopher, the “thought of existence” is “existence itself thinking in us.”

Philosophy, according to the great German idealist, has three moments: Logic, Philosophy of Nature and Philosophy of Spirit. Logic is the science of the idea in the abstract element of thought. It is the “fundamental Science” of the whole system of “absolute idealism.” In this system everything is ideal. Nothing can exist that is not idea. For this very reason it is called “absolute idealism.” To treat of realities different from the idea is, for Hegel, impossible. The world, universal existence, is immanent in the idea, and nothing can exist exterior to it, transcendent.

It is clearly understood how a dialectical method corresponds, with logical congruence, to an “absolute idealism.” If everything is idea, logic, dialectic—the science of the idea—will be the universal science. For this reason, too, Hegel’s system has been called “panlogism.”

Humanity admires, and will always admire, the celebrated thinker who, upholding idealism as his fundamental principle, taught the dialectical as his proper method. Here the metaphysical conception and its respective method accord with each other and imply each other. If we believe that everything is idea, the science of the idea must, logically, be the universal science; therefore Hegelian dialectic agrees, in serene harmony, with the principle whose truth it develops. An idealist dialectician is a [p. 120] fully capable philosopher, who may be disputed in his conclusions, but not as regards the profound unity of his creative thought.

On the other hand, what are we to think of a philosopher who accepts Hegelian dialectic, who assiduously deals with affirmation, negation and “the negation of the negation,” but who maintains materialism as his principle?... How will it be possible to join, without self-contradiction, the materialist foundation with idealist dialectic?... By what art of enchantment are we going to make matter, a reality distinct from the idea, dialecticize itself into thesis, antithesis and synthesis?... It seems impossible to assert it!

Well then, Marx is the author of this marvelous coupling, of this unintelligible cohabitation between materialist metaphysics, poor slave, and the noble Hegelian dialectic. Through Feuerbach he received the impossible double inheritance: dialectical-Materialism. Hence there originated the unresolved and unresolvable contradiction. Idealists can, without loss, spin out their metaphysical idealism in the subtle and elegant meshes of their fundamental logic. Materialists, on the other hand, will never be able to explain satisfactorily how matter is conjugated into thesis, antithesis and synthesis; that is, into ideas, at the spell of who knows what bewitched contrivance.

We could explain to ourselves, though not justify, the existence of an inductive materialism that should have recourse to the methods of the natural sciences and make an apotheosis of the procedures that have brought about the extraordinary flourishing of the physical, chemical and biological theories; because one might perhaps see some relation between the postulates of materialism and the experimental methods; but to pretend to unite in the close concatenation of a system the belief that matter is what constitutes universal being with the method of Plato and of Hegel is like maintaining an idealism that would be proved by the procedures of physical chemistry, on laboratory tables and in the bosom of retorts and crucibles. Therefore we conclude by affirming: materialism or dialectic; but not dialectical materialism. Behold the intrinsic contradiction in Marx’s thought!... Its mere statement ought to convince conscious Marxists of its falsity!

For every philosophical system exhibits the unity of a synthetic thought, if it be true that the fundamental conception and the method must imply each other. If, on the other hand, the conception and the method exclude each other, the system breaks, its unity is pulverized, and the ideas that formed it free themselves from the synthesis they sought to [p. 121] sustain, thus giving to the whole speculative construction the ruinous aspect of buildings erected on a pure sophism.

Let us now try to explain to ourselves the cause of the esteem that both Marx and Engels harbored for Hegelian dialectic. We believe we find it in the fact that dialectic is a rigorously deterministic and evolutionary method. Marx needed to demonstrate by means of his sociological theory that socialism is a necessity of universal history, “something that would have to be” as the inescapable product of the overcoming of capitalist society. Hence his enthusiasm for Hegel’s dialectic, which states, in logical terms, cosmic and cultural evolution. Socialism, collectivism, will not be, then, a pure dream of justice and humanity, but something rooted, dialectically, in the past of human collectivities. One goes to this organization as inescapably as a stone falls into a well or tinder burns with the spark from the steel.

Marx dared to put on the Pegasuses of Plato and of Hegel the crude bridle of his “historical materialism”; but the noble mythological steeds feel at once that an interested hand wished to govern them; and, conscious of their breed, they fling far from themselves the upstart rider who dreamed of taming them without being, like those illustrious philosophers, a dweller on Olympus. So it will always be whenever some materialist pretends to make use of idealism; because dialectic is the genuine method of pure thought, not the work of those who substitute war for justice, matter for the idea, and the fatal coercion of force for the pliant determinism of reason!

### II. EL MATERIALISMO Y LOS HECHOS PSICOLÓGICOS — II. MATERIALISM AND PSYCHOLOGICAL FACTS

#### I. EL CARÁCTER INESPACIAL DE LO PSÍQUICO — I. THE NON-SPATIAL CHARACTER OF THE PSYCHIC

[p. 125] “The assertion that certain psychic phenomena seem extended, rests manifestly on a confusion between physical and psychic phenomena.”

F. Brentano

Contemporary philosophy devotes its attention, very especially and preferentially, to marking out clearly the different spheres of the real. The empiricists, especially the English and French positivists of the end of the last century, committed several inadmissible confusions in characterizing the attributes of the psychic, above all as regards sensations. The great Brentano points out how these confusions can be dispelled by distinguishing between the sphere of the physical and that of the psychic properly speaking, bringing in “the idea of representation.”

Two great philosophers, perhaps the greatest philosophers the modern world has engendered, maintain the non-spatial character of the psychic. One is Descartes, the other Kant, “who, as Brentano himself says in his Psychology, considers space as the form of the intuition of external sense.” Spinoza, another of the supreme minds in the history of philosophical thought, corroborates the Cartesian point of view.

If, then, we except the group of the empiricists, such as Hartley, Brown, Stuart Mill and Spencer, the opinion of philosophers, even at the end of the last century, inclines toward granting to psychic experiences the mark of unextended phenomena.

Here is how a positivist as prominent as A. Bain expresses himself concerning all psychic facts other than sensations: “The sphere of the object or objective world [external] is circumscribed exactly by one property: extension. The world of subjective experience [the internal world] lacks this property. A tree or a stream is said to possess an extended magnitude. A pleasure has no length, breadth or thickness; in no respect is it an extended thing. A thought or an idea may refer to extended magnitudes; but it cannot be said of them that they have an extension in [p. 126] themselves. Nor can we say that a voluntary act, an appetite, a belief, fill a space in certain directions. Wherefore, whatever falls within the sphere of the subject is called unextended.”

It is interesting to know the opinion, no longer of the spiritualists, but of the agnostics, such as the illustrious English biologist Th. Huxley, who, referring precisely to sensations, states: “We have before had occasion to draw a distinction between material things or objects and sensations. A moment’s reflection suffices, moreover, to convince us that sensations are not material objects. An odor occupies no space and has no weight; to speak of a pound or a cubic meter of sound or of light would be absurd. By metaphor pleasure is said to be fugitive; but pleasure cannot be imagined as a thing in motion.” Nevertheless, the Mexican materialists, like the spiritualist-materialists, affirm that, like physical movements, psychic facts occur in space!

Not occupying a place in space, nor being able to occur in it in any form, is the universal negative character of everything psychic. Occurring in an I (which can never be conceived as situated in space) is the positive character of psychic phenomena, together with the third character that Brentano specified, taking from Scholastic philosophy the concept of “intentional inexistence”: reference to a content, direction toward an object: “Every psychic phenomenon contains within itself something as its object, though not all in the same way.” If I will, I will something; if I desire, I desire something; if I think, I think of something; if I feel, something is what is felt by me. This is what is called “the intentional object.”

The sphere of the reality of physical objects is characterized by a set of spatio-temporal marks. The sphere of psychic objects is characterized or distinguished, negatively, by not occurring in space, in any space, neither physical nor metaphysical, neither finite nor infinite, neither of one, nor of two, nor of three nor of n dimensions.

Fonsegrive says: “Physiological phenomena are distinguished from psychological ones by three principal differences: psychological phenomena are situated only in time. They have no dimension in space. They are unextended. Physiological phenomena have at once a situation in time and in space.” For example: a wound in a finger possesses length, breadth and depth, which is expressed in millimeters. The pain that results from the wound cannot be expressed thus. It would be ridiculous to say: “this pain has so many meters or millimeters of depth. However, the intensity as well as the duration of the phenomena of consciousness can be measured.” In sum: “they are unextended and intensive. Hence it results that only inner sense perceives them in a subject. [p. 127] Whereas physical or physiological phenomena can be perceived by several subjects at once, in space, by virtue of the fact that this is one of their characteristic attributes.”

Boirac and Magendie, corroborating the foregoing, teach: “the immaterial, the spiritual, is that which is of the same nature as thought.” Could it be said that thought is in reality contained in space, that it occupies in it a certain volume and draws a contour of its own? All these assertions are completely devoid of sense. In vain would one look in pleasure, pain, love, hatred, judgment, reasoning for the characteristic data of movement, that is, trajectory and velocity. Our inner states succeed one another in time and, from this point of view, we can reckon them and determine their duration; but in no way do they occur in space. It cannot be said of them that they fill or realize such and such lengths expressible in kilometers or millimeters. Doubtless one often speaks of the depth of an idea, of the velocity of thought; but who sees in this anything but purely metaphorical expressions?... The Mexican materialists and the spiritualist-materialists, even more contradictory than the former, are victims of the metaphors that Boirac and Magendie describe!... They think that space is the form of all reality. They confuse the object of sensation with sensation itself, the object of desire, of volition and of thought with desire, volition and thought. Through this lamentable confusion they suffer, as Husserl would say, “blindness to ideas.” They mutilate the broad field of reality, the magnificence of the diverse species of being, the vastness and complexity of the Universe; and their conception of the real is then something that offends against the harmonious diversity of existence!...

A. Jacques, J. Simon and E. Saisset teach: “I receive a wound in some spot of my body; once the flesh was cut, the little nerves that end in the injured part have shuddered in a particular manner; their movement has been communicated from one to another to the thicker ones, to which they are joined as branches to trunks; and, transmitted successively from these to the others, they arrive at last, after a more or less tortuous course, at the center where they gather and from which they depart: the brain. While all this takes place, I am suffering. The suffering I experience has surely as its occasion, or if you will its cause, the impression produced upon the organic apparatus. Nevertheless, it is distinct from it and does not resemble it at all. What analogy is there between a movement and a pain? The one takes place in extension, begins at one point and ends at another; it is swift [p. 128] or slow; its path is straight or curved and can be marked by a drawing. The other has only duration. There are long sufferings, but no extensions in them; there are strong and weak ones, but in them no curves or straight lines; in short, no sort of sensible image can represent pain”... But our critics, the Mexican materialists and the spiritualist-materialists, still more absurd, not only see space as a mark of the psychic, but thought itself, the non-spatial par excellence, is materialized in their concept, occurring in space. Señor don Eduardo Pallares has gone so far as to affirm that all states of consciousness occur in space!<sup>1</sup> It is no longer sensation, pleasure, pain that is declared spatial, but the idea. Now, to declare that ideas occur in space is to confess, without blushing, the most outlandish of absurdities, the most curious of antiphilosophical assertions!... A. Müller, a present-day representative of the latest currents of thought in contemporary psychology, writes: “Can pain not be extended, extend over the whole leg? Can cold not be extended and take possession of the whole back? But here it is necessary to distinguish carefully two things. What is extended in these cases is the physiological cause of the sensation, not the sensation itself. The latter is a sensation of something extended, but not an extended sensation. Can one, in effect, affirm: I have a sensation of cold of two thousand square centimeters in extension? We notice at once the senselessness of this assertion. But one can express what is meant in this way: I have a sensation of cold in a place on my body whose extension is two thousand square centimeters.”

<sup>1</sup> See the respective articles, published in the newspaper El Universal.

One must lack all mental finesse in order not to be able to distinguish properly between the intentional object of sensation and sensation itself. No one denies that the body and its organs are part of the cause in the phenomenon of sensation. But the extended body is one thing and the correlative sensation quite another. One can think a myriagon, even though this figure is not imaginable. But the thought of the myriagon is not the myriagon thought. The myriagon occurs in space, it is extended. The thought of such a geometrical figure does not occur in space, but in pure time; it does not extend, it cannot be drawn. Therefore the two orders of phenomena prove to be concomitant and irreducible; always concomitant and always irreducible!

The object of the problem we are discussing having been sufficiently elucidated, we can, thanks to the preceding reflections, corroborated by the authority of the great philosophers and psychologists mentioned, conclude:

[p. 129] The psychic has as its universal negative character this indisputable mark: It does not occur in space.

Contemporary philosophy goes further still. According to it, all things that exist occur in time. The physical, in time and in space. The psychic, in time alone. Other forms of reality, essences and values, are outside all time and all space. How are the Mexican materialists and their congeners the spiritualist-materialists going to accept this conclusion!...

Yet essential being, unlike existential being, is immutable, because if the essence of the straight line were to change, it would be at once straight and non-straight. The essence of the triangle is to be a figure enclosed by three lines; if this mark is suppressed, one is no longer in the presence of a triangle. Essence-being is indivisible. Moreover, essences are eternal. The right triangle will always be a triangle with a right angle; this excludes time. Therefore there are forms of reality that occur outside time and space. But..., how will those who, as Husserl says, “have blindness to ideas” and judge that thought is spatial be able to perceive this?... Impossible!

#### II. LAS FORMAS DE LA REALIDAD Y LA CONCEPCIÓN DEL UNIVERSO — II. THE FORMS OF REALITY AND THE CONCEPTION OF THE UNIVERSE

The constant error of all empiricist systems, especially of materialism, consists in thinking that only one form of reality is the one that exists. Whoever thinks thus, far from extending in its disconcerting richness the vastness of the world, simplifies it and schematizes it in a single direction, offering us, instead of the Universe, one and various, an unreal world in which, if there is unity, intrinsic diversity on the other hand appears eliminated forever.

From the constant dealings that man has with solid objects, which he ceaselessly handles in industry, from the daily commerce that intelligence establishes with realities that occupy a place in space, from this indispensable correlation, dictated by life (since to live is to adapt to a physical environment), proceeds the illusion into which some philosophers, devoid of mental finesse, fall. An illusion that consists in wishing to subject all forms of reality to a single form of reality!

Then the Universe is conceived, in its ample being, by detaching everything that is not “material,” in order to declare it “unreal.” The multiple and changing aspects of existence are disfigured thanks to the spirit of system, and non-physical realities, as indubitable as the physical ones, [p. 130] are expelled from the bosom of the Universe and declared senseless fantasies and vain objects of inadmissible speculation.

Consciousness is directed by preference, in the commerce of action, to solid bodies. They constitute the raw material with which the machines and utensils of human industry are made. The intentional object of the ideas that refer to technique acts by rebound upon the conception that intelligence creates with respect to all possible objects; in this way the fallacious and deceptive theory is inaugurated that simplifies, in an inadequate simplification, the opulence of attributes and forms of reality. Every “being,” within this position, is a “physical being”; everything fits within the characters of matter; everything occurs in the exclusive manner in which material reality occurs; everything is unified or identified with what occupies a place in space and an instant in time.

Empiricism engenders from itself its constant and inescapable fruit: materialism. Since it has not been possible to “see,” to intuit, thanks to an adequate method, the breadth of the diverse sectors or spheres of reality, it is obvious that whatever is an object of knowledge is made to fit, without consideration, within the one favored form. Thus Physics, the science of energy and matter, rises, irrefutably, to the post of honor, to the summit of knowledge. It takes the place of Metaphysics. Its reality is all reality; its method, every possible method; its doctrine, the universal doctrine; its scientific genius, every scientific genius..., and the conception of the Universe that results from this provokes in its adepts the false feeling that they have attained an “exact” conception of the real.

Newton said: “Physics, beware of Metaphysics.” We shall say: “Metaphysics, beware of Physics.” That is, let us never pretend to elaborate the whole of Philosophy with the laws and principles of Physics alone. The philosopher who insisted on constructing a fundamental Metaphysics without recourse to the data and conclusions of the physical sciences would already be, by this very fact, judged before the cultivated conscience of our century; but the thinker who wished to ground the entire edifice of Philosophy exclusively on the postulates and conclusions of Physics and Chemistry is likewise judged and rejected before the cultivated contemporary conscience.

What are the characters of “the physical”? Evidently physical reality occurs in time and space. That is: everything physical shows itself in two fundamental aspects: the spatial and the temporal. If space is suppressed, physical movement is impossible; if time is eliminated, movement likewise proves incongruous in itself. Physicists require [p. 131] for the construction of their illustrious science these two principles of the reality they investigate.

But when the knowing subject, instead of taking as intentional object physical, spatial and temporal reality, turns back upon himself, returns upon the very subject who investigates, he finds at once that consciousness, far from being like the “physical object,” something that extends in space, is a non-spatial “form of reality.” Consciousness endures, but does not extend; it breathes in time, not in space. Its form of reality is, then, essentially different from physical reality. And yet non-spatial consciousness is as real as extended physical reality.

From this it follows that, alongside physical realities, that is, alongside what occupies a place in space and an instant in time, there is that which, “enduring,” does not “extend”; and this is the spirit, consciousness, the psychic, however one wishes to call it. If I think, I think of something; but my thought of what occupies a place in space does not, likewise, occupy a place in space. Thinking can refer, not to a physical object, but to another thought; in this case, space will be absent, both from thought as subject and from thought as object.

In sum: there are forms of reality (sensations, ideas, feelings, volitions, memories, et cetera) that do not imply space in order to be. This is the conclusion reached by a mind disinterested in anything but truth. One understands with full intelligence in what the empiricist prejudice consists, which leads, without more, to materialism.

The fact is that the empiricists, with a single scientific scheme (the physical), try to clarify multiple and changing reality. The fact is that they do not look at what exists with the eyes of suitable investigators; rather, the scheme that once served them to understand the world they convert into the universal schematism. They project the nature of physical science upon universal knowledge; and what does not conform to their thought they declare nonexistent. They are “professional deformations,” as the agnostic Leibnizians would say, these materialist-spiritualists, who understand nothing!

Therefore, there exist two forms of reality, irreducible to each other: the physical and the psychic. But they are not the only ones. Above them hold sway other realities, for which time does not change, for which space does not extend. These forms of reality are essences and values.

Essences are immutable, indivisible, eternal. Plato called them Ideas. The essence of the straight line is an ideal object, which is never realized in space with absolute perfection. The same holds for the essence [p. 132] of the triangle or of the sphere. Triangularity is eternal, indivisible, immutable. The triangles we make in space are always imperfect, as are spheres, however burnished and exact we fashion them. By contrast, the paradigms, the essences, the ideas, have absolute intrinsic perfection. We know essences by approaching the phenomenon. For example: we see a sphere. From the sphere seen we rise to the idea of sphere. In the individual (the sight of an orange) we see the Universal: the sphere. No orange, as we know a priori, will reflect to us exactly the Platonic Idea of the sphere. But through the physical fact we intuit metaphysical being. If the orange of the experiment occurs in time and space, the essence occurs in our essential and not existential intuition, outside all time and all space.

Therefore there are ideal beings, non-spatial, immutable and eternal essences. That is: there are realities whose form of being, being purely ideal, exists outside all time and all space. As Hamlet said to Horatio: “There are more things, Horatio, in Heaven and Earth, than are dreamt of in your Philosophy”...

Lastly, values: the good, the evil, the true, the false, the beautiful, the ugly, the holy, the profane, the useful, the useless, positive and negative values, are, for some contemporary philosophers, realities like essences; only that their form of reality consists not in being, but in being worth.

The artist, the saint, the good man, the logician grasp positive values and, in this way, in the history of humanity, culture is elaborated, which is not, in the last analysis, anything but “creation of values.”

Compare this world, this broad Universe, full of distinct forms harmonized among themselves, with the meager, withered, unreal world of the materialists and the empiricists who, projecting, as we said before, their physical scheme upon being, deny it and falsify it beyond remedy. On one side are the diverse and congruent aspects of Nature and of History, as well as the sector of ideal objects which, linked with the phenomenon, explain it. On the other side only the “physical forms” show themselves, inexplicable in themselves.

To the first way of philosophizing belong the great personalities of a Husserl, a Max Scheler, a Hartmann. On the other side remains the constant rabble of those who, with “blindness to ideas,” can only conceive the Universe as a chaos of confused images which action orders imperfectly.

[p. 133] “We must now consider this neovitalism..., for Hans Driesch it is a question of a non-spatial factor, directive, according to an end.”

V. Viqueira

“The biological works of Driesch represent, in the history of science, an event of great significance, which deserves to be considered by all reflective minds.”

J. Maritain

Life is unintelligible without recourse to the idea of end. An organism always implies the subordination of a whole to its object. But not only does the organism presuppose the adaptation of which we speak. Each organ, each apparatus of the organism, expresses intrinsic finality. The eye has an end, to see. The ear, an end of its own, likewise, to hear; and so on successively. If the idea of end is set aside, the organism cannot be explained: not even its diverse organs and apparatuses, connected among themselves.

On the other hand, accept the idea of intrinsic finality, and the question to be resolved is lit up with intense clarity. The organs refer to their final cause; they are integrated in the organism by the subordination of some to others and, all of them, are related by virtue of the final end: the conservation of life, through time and space.

The two essential functions, reproduction and nutrition, in themselves have no meaning; but as the support of life in time and space, they take on a clear significance. Then, reproducing is congruent with the maintenance of life in the species; and nourishing oneself is likewise congruent with the maintenance of life in the individual.

For this reason Dastre could say that “protoplasm is an atavistic substance,” which we have not seen born and which we only see grow, thrive and reproduce.

In this way, individuals and species are nothing more than the most varied episodes in the resolution of a constant and universal problem: the success of Life, despite the negative conditions and the contrary causes it meets on its way in existence.

The same with the diverse senses. All of them are organic defenses that are explained by their finality; faculties that prepare the organism for triumph. Touch, hearing, sight are weapons arranged in obedience to, or in fulfillment of, the problem of living. Suppress all biological teleology [p. 134], eliminate the idea of finality, and the organism proves unknowable.

Moreover, the aesthetics of life is explained by recourse to the notion of finality. Organic symmetries are clarified if subordination to an intrinsic end is admitted; because what is subordinated to ends is in itself coordinated: now, coordination, referred to subordination, produces harmony.

Then the world of life exhibits in itself the cause of its beauty and symmetry. It is Cosmos, as the Greek said: that is, a world ordered to ends, a beautiful world, in sum.

Driesch adduces three proofs of his neovitalism: I. The autonomy of form (morphogenesis) is the first demonstration of vitalism, according to the celebrated German philosopher. He puts it thus in his Aberdeen “Lectures,” entitled, in the English edition, The Science and Philosophy of the Organism: “No kind of causality founded upon constellations of physical and chemical actions can explain organic individual development. No hypothesis that resorts to this kind of causality can account for such development. There exists, then, something else that must be regarded as the sufficient reason for the production of individual form.”

Life is an original and irreducible reality (eine Sache für sich), and Biology is a science that possesses its own independent principles.

The organism is a specific body, Driesch maintains, “constituted by a combination of different parts.” The very terms of this definition signify, implicitly, that the organism differs, not only from all combinations of crystals (dendrites, for example, which are formed by a characteristic arrangement of identical units). The nature of these combinations depends solely on the forces of each of the parts that constitute them. For this reason dendrites, despite their characteristic appearance, must be considered aggregates. On the contrary, the organism, even superficially examined, cannot be considered a mere aggregate. The organism is not always similar to itself. During the course of its individual life, the combination of the parts passes from a simple form to a complex one. They develop. There exists, during its development —to use W. Roux’s expression—, the “production of a visible diversity.”

This factor, which signifies the autonomy of biological phenomena, Driesch designates by the name of Entelechy. Nevertheless, he does not identify his doctrine with that of Aristotle. He employs the celebrated expression only as a sign of admiration for the Greek philosopher. The phenomena [p. 135] of life contain something “that bears its end within itself.” Colloidal matter is only the material basis, not the formal one, of life.

II. The second proof of vitalism refers to heredity, inexplicable mechanically. Heredity is clarified if, in the system of the special cells for reproduction, each part can perform all the functions, “occasionally.” A begonia leaf is such a system. The same holds of the reproductive organ in animals. Genes and chromosomes are only the material basis of reproduction.

III. Organic movement cannot be reduced to mechanics. Action is characterized, precisely, by being influenced by experience. That is, the past influences the present. The organism is not a machine that repeats, but a being that disposes of or takes advantage of experience in new combinations. Moreover, the organism is a transformer of sensations into movements. Mechanically it is incomprehensible how a machine can make use of the past and not repeat it. Phonographs only repeat; they do not elaborate action.

It is likewise incomprehensible to transform past sensations into movements, “two completely different phenomena.”

It is plain, therefore, how the eminent German biologist again walks in the footsteps of the Stagirite, to continue him by renewing him, in the face of the biological materialism of the present-day disciples of Democritus.

This is one of so many symptoms of the decline of the hypotheses that sought to deny the idea of end in the biological sciences. Now, in the ideas of the twentieth century, spiritualism returns to claim its empire, no longer merely in the field of the philosophy of the spirit, but in the interpretation of the results reached by the cosmological and biological sciences.

For this reason Ortega y Gasset has declared many of the ideas of the nineteenth century “obsolete.” Some, however, survive; because the conventional limits of time do not coincide with the evolutions of the human mind. But it is unquestionable that our age has already abandoned the empiricism of the past century, and new directions are marked out for independent speculation.

Vitalism, which was believed abandoned forever, displays, through the work of an illustrious biologist (who is also a great philosopher), the exercise of its action. The old hypothesis has been renewed at the very source of modern science. And today philosophy, in close partnership with scientific knowledge, makes of an Aristotelian idea the seed of new, plausible interpretations!

But our ingenuous materialists and our spiritualist-materialists, far from admitting Driesch’s non-spatial entelechy as an explanation [p. 136] of the arcanum of Life, pretend to reduce all movement to space; not only the biological, but the psychological as well. According to them, since space is “necessary,” everything must occur in it!... Admirable reasoning!

Life and spirit tend to make common cause around the idea of end. The contingency of physical law seems evident. The world is not, certainly, something that obeys a single law, but a harmonious system of laws that agree with one another. Through this extremely varied world there circulates the fundamental order that reveals itself to reason, the tireless elaborator of knowledge.

#### IV. DEVANEOS DE LOS IGNAROS SOBRE LA CUARTA DIMENSIÓN — IV. RAVINGS OF THE IGNORANT ON THE FOURTH DIMENSION

“We hold that philosophy should be a regulator of scientific theories, and that it ought not to march behind all the ‘theories,’ after the manner of a servant-girl, as Ancilla Theologiae did in earlier ages. Only philosophy, as the science of essences, should decide upon essential possibilities.”

Hans Driesch

The theory of generalized relativity, which has placed the name of Einstein beside those of Copernicus, Galileo and Newton, is one of the monuments of the human spirit. It has exercised a decisive influence on the physical sciences; and, like all great scientific hypotheses, it has philosophical significance. So it has been in the history of knowledge with other great theories, such as that of Kant-Laplace, concerning the origin of the worlds; such as that of Lamarck and Darwin on the origin of species and biological transformism. These ingenious conceptions of the physico-natural sciences pass beyond the properly scientific field into the philosophical; but, as Driesch says, philosophy must be a regulator of scientific theories and must not march behind all of them after the manner of a servant-girl; because, being the science of essences, it “should decide upon essential possibilities.”

Three great errors are often committed by those who, lacking properly philosophical competence, speculate or pretend to speculate, without rhyme or reason, on the theory of relativity.

I. The first error consists in thinking that the effort of the great German scholar has come to demolish the whole prior edifice of knowledge, “shaking it to its foundations,” and wrecking the secular [p. 137] and indestructible work of rational constructions. There are those who go so far as to say that the principles of common sense have been violated by Einstein and Poincaré! This is picturesque and absurd. Nothing can ever violate the rational criterion. The principles of reason are eternal, and the theory of relativity is founded, precisely, upon them. Were it to discard them, it would not in truth be a plausible and brilliant scientific theory, but a pure spawn of madness, unworthy of occupying the attention of any true thinker.

Scientific progress is a construction constantly rectified by itself. For this reason science is something essentially progressive. If the eternal principles of human reason: a is; a cannot be a and cease to be a at the same time; there is no middle between being a and not being a, were violated, the work of scientific knowledge would prove totally impossible. Grounded on these principles, and on that of “sufficient reason,” as Leibniz said, is how Einstein, together with other great investigators, has been able to bring to a successful conclusion his magnificent contribution to the effort of scientific knowledge. Eternal, impersonal reason is what assures the constant possibility of science; whoever denies this demonstrates by his attitude the slight consistency of his thought and the real background of snobbery that inspires it.

II. The second error lies in failing to grasp the proper scope of “the theory of relativity” as a scientific theory; because, without leaving the field of physical science, it is possible to indicate the limit of what is explicable by means of generalized relativity. Today’s physics describes only periodic phenomena. These phenomena are fundamental, and in all treatises on physics they are studied as the object of that discipline. Dr. Anglas says in his Essay entitled From Euclid to Einstein: “periodic are the movements of the stars, periodic, likewise, those of the electrons within atoms. In different orders of magnitude they are of the same nature as the movement of an oscillating pendulum. But whereas the latter, more or less hindered by the resistance of the air and the shaking of its support, dies away sooner or later, the course of the stars, like the intra-atomic whirl, continues forever identical, with a perfection confirmed by astronomical and physical observations.”

But, alongside periodic movements there are non-periodic phenomena. Periodic means the same as reversible; and irreversible phenomena are most numerous. A pendulum whose oscillations remain identical is the example of “reversible” movement, because it is the example of “periodic” movement; but there are other physical [p. 138] facts, “irreversible,” governed by the principle of the degradation of energy, the “true principle of irreversibility.”

The great law of Carnot and Clausius teaches that not all energy is equally capable of producing work. Energy is conserved in quantity, but not in quality. “In the domain of mechanics the degradation of energy is verified; therefore, in it reversibility cannot be absolute; and, all the more so, when it is a matter of thermodynamics; that is, of the relations of mechanical and caloric energy. Thermodynamics escapes, then, the physics of relativity and the representation of the Universe that the latter offers” (De Euclides a Einstein).

It is plain, from the foregoing, how, within Physics itself, the theory of relativity is confined within the principle of periodic movements; but the facts governed by the great law of Carnot and Clausius remain outside this sector. And, in passing from Physics to Chemistry, and from these sciences to Biology, one does nothing but mark the limits of the principle of relativity.

III. The third great error into which fall the partisans of extending beyond measure the field of application of the theory of relativity consists in trying to carry the theories of modern Physics over into the psychological. As Aloys Müller says in his Introduction to Philosophy: “There are two, and only two, kinds of physical objects. First of all, the electromagnetic field. This is a physical reality subsisting in itself, which has no need whatever of matter as a support. On the contrary, matter is nothing more than a product of the field. In the field there form, for reasons as yet unknown, knots of energy, places of condensation of the energy of electric tension or, according to the most recent interpretation, ‘wave packets.’ These are the ultimate constitutive elements of matter.”

Now, psychic facts cannot be referred to electromagnetic fields. Thoughts are not “wave packets.” Volitions and feelings cannot be explained by appealing to the characteristics of physical objects, which are always, according to their essence, “metric objects.” Therefore, in conclusion: Einstein has not come to invalidate, but to continue, within the measure of its possibility, the effort of human knowledge, basing himself, like all scientific investigators, on the eternal principles of reason and common sense. Furthermore, the theory of generalized relativity refers to the broad field of “periodic phenomena,” that is, “reversible” ones, but it cannot embrace all that refers to the sphere of irreversibility, ordered according to the great “law of the degradation of energy.” That is, Physics [p. 139] is as much the study of reversible phenomena as of irreversible ones; and the theory of relativity does not extend to the second sphere of physical knowledge. Finally, psychic facts remain outside the field of generalized relativity, because their properties do not correspond to the two metric objects of physical knowledge: the electromagnetic field and the gravitational field.

The foregoing reflections convince us of the picturesque and absurd attitude of those who, incapable of properly thinking philosophical problems, place in the fourth dimension all their ravings of the ignorant. In that region, which seems to them that of metaphysical delirium, they situate the claims of an incongruous philosophy! In that recondite field, they let their feeble fantasy fly. There, they say, all the principles of reason are shipwrecked and the values of common sense faint away. In that remote orbit, the impossible has its seat, madness its empire and negation its dominion!... It is because man, at bottom, also bears within himself a ballast of enmity against reason; an unconfessed desire for rebellion against the lasting principles of logic. In the fantastic field of the fourth dimension, some consciences, close to delirium, speculate on senseless dreams, on chimeras without substance!...

But what does the unintelligent use of the great scientific theories matter to those who know that, according to the profound thought of Pascal, “humanity is like a single man who constantly increases his knowledge and improves himself”?... Through all the deliriums, the endeavor of true thinkers continues, setting aside the snobbery of those who interpret scientific culture as if it were the handle of a phantasmagoria full of madness! As Driesch says: “Philosophy should be a regulator of scientific theories, and must not march behind all of them, after the manner of a servant-girl.” The supreme logical laws govern, by their own right, the universal field of knowledge.

#### V. EINSTEIN, MEYERSON Y LOS IGNAROS — V. EINSTEIN, MEYERSON AND THE IGNORANT

“How, can it be true that the demon of explanation, noted by me in Descartes and so many others, a demon that had seemed to me so strange, possesses me too?... Here is something I was very far from knowing. Nevertheless, I have read your book, and I confess that I am convinced.”

With these words full of good humor and replete with “meaning,” the great German physicist has rendered a cordial and profound homage to the [p. 140] illustrious French philosopher E. Meyerson. For the genius of explanation, the inspiring “demon” in question, is nothing less than the symbolic and poetic allegory of the epistemological theory fathered by Émile Meyerson.

This theory has been set forth in three celebrated books: Identity and Reality, Explanation in the Sciences and The Relativistic Deduction. Let us set forth, succinctly, the philosophical thesis to which we allude, a thesis which, naturally, the ignorant of Mexico, who elaborate fantasies on the four-dimensional Universe, ignore, as befits the ignorant, with unfathomable profundity. Yet Meyerson’s epistemological realism was acknowledged in the celebrated phrase that constitutes the epigraph of this chapter, a phrase dedicated to the philosopher by the distinguished German mathematician when, passing through Paris, Einstein saw fit to visit the theorist who, with H. Bergson, the thinker of “Creative Evolution,” constitutes today the highest exponent of French philosophy.

Let us set ourselves the problem of finding out what it is to explain. All science is an attempt at explanation. Universal science would be universal explanation. For it is obvious that the systematic endeavor of knowledge has no other end than this. Human thought carries out its scientific work in order to make explicit the causes of the different orders into which universal reality is divided.

Now, explanation reaches a successful conclusion, attains its final end, when the effect has been referred to its cause, when the phenomenon has been reduced to law. And how can the phenomenon be reduced to law and the effect to its cause? Meyerson answers: by virtue of an identification. To explain is to identify: to see the effect in the cause and the fact in the law. To reduce the diversity of what exists to a unitary principle. When, thanks to the scientific methods of investigation, we rise from the phenomenon to the law, from the fact to the cause, and from the law and the cause to the fundamental principle, we can declare that we have explained. Why?... Because we have identified, because we thus suppress the disconcerting and unintelligible diversity, reducing it to order, to unity.

Identification is the fundamental principle of intelligence, which says: A is A. This formula is clarity itself. There is nothing obscure or enigmatic. A is A; the same is the same. When our intelligence has succeeded in identifying things it did not identify before, it has understood them, has explained them. For example, in this syllogism: “Every diamond is carbon; all carbon is combustible, therefore every diamond is combustible.”

In declaring that every diamond is carbon, we have identified [p. 141] both terms. It will now be indifferent to call carbon by its own name or to call it combustible. And, in the end, from the structure of the syllogism we obtain that, the diamond “being” carbon, it “is” combustible. It is a matter, then, of a system of identifications combined by the intelligence, according to the principle of identity, which leads us to explanation. Electromagnetism came into being when, thanks to the efforts of physicists, electromagnetic identification was verified. Gravity was explained when it was reduced to universal gravitation.

But now a second problem arises, whose meditation constitutes the badge of glory of Meyerson’s epistemological essays: Is it possible to explain everything? We already possess, in advance, a part of the answer, because we know that to explain is to identify, neither more nor less than to identify; but we have not found out whether it is possible to identify everything. What we do not doubt is that, insofar as we can identify, we shall explain. It remains for us to find out whether the Universe, in its manifold and most varied manifestations, is capable of submitting to a radical identification. Meyerson holds that the entire history of the sciences shows us clearly how reluctant reality is to bend to our demand for identification. That is, the Universe might not be explicable as a whole, and offer us something in itself not identifiable, that is, irrational. The irrational would constitute the fixed limit of explanation, the barrier set against our intelligence, the elaborator of knowledge.

We begin with a deep faith in the possibility of reducing everything to unity, and unity is logic; but perhaps the real order does not correspond to the ideal logical order; perhaps there exists a reality irreducible to thought. If so, science will have a limit, an impassable limit, because the categories of our thought will no longer be applicable to investigation.

Now, the theory of generalized relativity, due to the genius of Einstein, is a new attempt at explanation in physical science. For this reason Einstein declares to Meyerson: “How, can it be true that the demon of explanation noted by me in Descartes and so many others, a demon that had seemed to me so strange, possesses me too?... Here is something I was very far from knowing.”

The total work of the extraordinary mathematician has consisted in a universal tendency to reduce physics to geometry and analysis. This is what constitutes his creative effort. From this results the four-dimensional Universe, the relativity of length, of duration, of mass, of form; because rigidity does not exist, as Gaston Moch says, except for bodies at one with the state of motion of an observer. And from the relativity of length and of time there derives [p. 142] that of velocity and of acceleration. “Length and time are quantities of the same kind: there exists a linear equivalent of time. Velocity is reduced to an abstract number. Consequently, energy is homogeneous with mass, from which it differs only by a numerical coefficient. There exists, then, no special difference between mass and energy.”

But Meyerson objects: “This tendency toward the assimilation of time and space (which is in reality a transformation of time into space) sometimes goes very far, certainly farther than we should be authorized to affirm it, not only by our immediate feeling, a consideration which the relativists, not without reason, font bon marché; but even by the best-confirmed facts, the most essential foundations of science. Can we really, as Minkowski postulates, fuse time into space? Is it exact, as Eddington formulates it, that the physical real, the continuum formed by space and imaginary time, is perfectly isotropic for all measurements, and that no direction essentially distinct from the others can be found there? It is clear, on the contrary, that, taken literally, these propositions are entirely extravagant, and have no relation to phenomena. The temporal dimension (we know it with certain and immediate knowledge, with a certitude such that any assault of reasoning, however seductive, would break without shaking it) is by essence different from the spatial dimensions.”

Our ingenuous ignorant, not content with the impassable limits that Meyerson shows between time and space, try to reduce them to unity, and declare, emphatically, that “ideas occupy a place in space.” No, they are given in “real duration,” as Bergson says, in “pure time,” the form of the universal intuition of the psychic, according to Kant; and, according to Descartes, “the essence of bodies lies in extension and that of spirits in thought.” Descartes, Kant and Bergson are the three philosophical columns on which the non-spatial character of the psychic rests.

Moreover, Einstein himself has written that “one cannot telegraph to the past.” This means that, whereas space can be traversed in all directions, forward and backward, time has to be lived only toward the future; for this reason we cannot telegraph to the past. And the principle of Carnot marks this action of time in the physical, formulating, as Meyerson says, a “law of irreversibility,” a history.

But our ignorant commentators on the fourth dimension imagine that the relativist explanation can be transferred to the field of the psychic. [p. 143] No, this is a physical explanation, and not the whole physical explanation. The real has not been able to be identified in its fullness. The “demon” of explanation, as Einstein says to Meyerson, has found its limit. How could one, without being utterly devoid of common sense, claim to raise generalized relativity, which is a physical theory, which interprets part of the physical, to the category of a psychological principle?...

In sum: identification, explanation, science is not reduced to the unity of generalized relativity. Philosophy has placed in its just scope and proportion the brilliant and plausible effort of Einstein and his disciples. As H. Driesch says: “the theory of relativity has been one of the great world fashions.” For us it is not a matter of fashion, but of truth; and we have said, without concern for fashion, what seems to us false and what seems to us true in Einstein’s great theoretical edifice. We know well that it is a rather thankless undertaking, today, that of the philosopher who wishes to act as logical judge. But philosophy is not, in our opinion, a thing of “today.”

The ignorant of Mexico select and understand nothing; they accept everything that is given them, because they lack critical spirit. But to philosophize is to criticize, it is to use one’s judgment, by virtue of which the false is separated from the true, alike in the great scientific theories and in moral or social problems.

#### VI. LOS IGNAROS A LAS PLANTAS DE LEIBNIZ — VI. THE IGNORANT AT THE FEET OF LEIBNIZ

“We see, then, how the great philosopher Leibniz affirmed categorically the metaphysical nature of space, which he then calls immensity.”

Eduardo Pallares

“It has been attempted to evade the difficulty by saying that space is a property of God. To this it has been answered that immensity is the property of God; but that space, which is often commensurable with bodies, and the immensity of God, are not the same thing.”

Leibniz

The evident contradiction between these two assertions would suffice: “Space and the immensity of God are not the same thing,” and “Leibniz affirmed categorically the metaphysical nature of space, which he then [p. 144] calls immensity,” to spare us the object of this chapter; because one cannot, logically, call immensity that space which, according to the German philosopher, is not immensity. Hence, “The Immensity of Space” is refuted in two words. Nevertheless, let the curious reader read what follows, and judge definitively.

I. It seems incredible that, across the centuries, the ancient dispute between Clarke and Leibniz should still be renewed; but stranger still that, wishing to uphold the thesis of Clarke, someone should declare himself a partisan of Leibniz’s. This fills us with amazement and consternation! Whoever knows, even if only in some epitome, the terms of the most famous discussion will understand how the opinion of Clarke, analyzed by Leibniz, is today, more than ever, untenable. According to the English thinker, immensity, which in his conception is nothing but limitless extension, constitutes an attribute of the divine essence. An opinion that must undoubtedly be rejected, because, to admit it, there follows with intellectual evidence the absurdity of denying to God unity and simplicity. Real space is always limited and divisible. Imaginary or ideal space, also called absolute, is the indefinite possibility of extension.

All space is indefinitely divisible. Therefore, whoever makes of immensity limitless extension will always be able to divide and subdivide it. Now, the divisible in itself is opposed to the one, to the simple. For this reason Leibniz begins his Monadology by maintaining that the monad is a simple substance, that is, one without parts. Where there are no parts, there can be neither extension nor figure nor possible divisibility. Every being, every simple monad, lacks parts; and if it were spatial, it would be divisible into parts. God is a monad, “the monad of monads.” Hence God, like any other simple substance, in Leibniz’s monadological system, is non-spatial. Were he spatial, he would be divisible, that is, he would not be simple, because the composite is what has parts, and the simple what does not possess them. In this system, space can refer only to an order; substantially all beings are non-spatial, because they are of a spiritual nature. Hence the name absolute spiritualism, given to the Leibnizian conception. “There is no need to fear dissolution, says the great German spiritualist; for there is no conceivable way in which a simple substance can perish naturally.”

Theophilus (the character who expounds the spiritualist ideas in the celebrated dialogues on “human understanding,” which their author composed with the purpose of discussing and assaying the thought of the illustrious English empiricist, John Locke), affirms not, certainly, the theory of [p. 145] Clarke that immensity is a limitless extension and an attribute of the divine essence, thus spatialized; but only that the idea of the absolute, in relation to space, leads or conducts us to that of immensity. He deceives himself who would imagine an absolute space that is an infinity of parts. Immensity is, therefore, something different from space. And the ideas, the volitions, the feelings of the human soul are not given in immensity; rather, this divine quality is a pure attribute of the supreme monad, distinct from space. Just as neither are our thoughts, nor our sensations, nor our human feelings, “all too human,” given in eternity; because eternity is another absolute or limitless attribute, which belongs to God alone.

Boethius said elegantly and profoundly, many centuries before the German mathematician: Interminabilis vitae tota simul et perfecta possessio [the whole, simultaneous and perfect possession of unending life]. In contingent beings, change implies different moments. The necessary being of God is simultaneously, sempiternally all that he is. For us, contingent and ephemeral beings, time and space; for God, eternity and immensity.

When Philalethes declares: “We do not know what relations spirits have with space nor how they participate in it, but we know that they participate in duration,” he affirms what we have always maintained, that spiritual experiences are given in duration, that is, in pure time. When Theophilus replies that “all finite spirits are always united to organic bodies, and represent the others to themselves in relation to this union,” he expresses with perfect clarity what the relation of spirit to space can be; it is only a relation of representation, an idea. “In this way —he adds—, their relation to space is as clear as that of bodies.” Which means that finite spirits maintain with space only the relation of “representation.” A representation is a psychic act, not a spatial phenomenon; but the spiritualist-materialists interpret the ideal, immaterial, monadic relation as if it were a spatial relation. Sublime!

Furthermore, “God is the source of essences and of existences.” Essences are given in the divine understanding; and in the will of God, Leibniz explains, “lies the reason of existences.” From which it is correctly inferred that, as essences, as possibilities, time and space have their reality only in God. But only one who does not know how to distinguish essence from existence will confuse the work of the divine will (existences) with that of the understanding (essences). “If God were extended, says Leibniz, he would be formed of parts”; it is [p. 146] so that he is not extended, therefore he can be thought in his non-spatial simplicity.

II. Here is the true doctrine of Leibniz on space and time. To it refer the two celebrated formulas: Space: ordre des situations, Time: ordre des positions successives [the order of situations; the order of successive positions]. In Boutroux’s conception, one could not consider space and time, within Leibniz’s system, as realities distinct from immaterial substances.

If one abstracts the things situated in space or in time, Boutroux goes on to say, “only numerical differences remain”; that is, abstract differences, “null from the standpoint of the will.” “Suppose that space is a substance, you could not then say why God created the world at such or such a moment of time or place in space, rather than at another time or in a different place.” Leibniz’s thought, clearly set forth by Boutroux, constitutes the proximate antecedent of Kant’s theses on the ideality of time and space. The former, according to Leibnizian thought, is only the order of possible coexistences; and time, the order of inconstant possibilities that maintain connection among themselves.

The eternity of God is his immutability opposed to the variations of time. His immensity is his own singular unity opposed to the limitations of space. God exists outside space because he is superior to space; he exists outside time, because he is also superior to it. To occupy a place one must be limited. God is infinite and unique. If time elapsed for God, both would change together. God neither passes nor is limited, he is, in his perfect aseity, in his absolute immensity, in his unfailing eternity. This is the pure and genuine Leibnizian doctrine; not the one lent to it by his absurd disciples.

In sum: psychic experiences are not given in space; in no space. Neither in the physical, nor the mathematical, nor the imaginary, nor the absolute, nor in that of one, nor of two, nor of three, nor of n dimensions; nor in the immensity of God!... Quod erat demonstrandum.

Thus the ignorant are left at the feet of Leibniz!

### III. LA CULTURA UNIVERSITARIA Y EL MATERIALISMO HISTÓRICO — III. UNIVERSITY CULTURE AND HISTORICAL MATERIALISM

#### I. LAS DOS ILUSTRES GEMELAS — I. THE TWO ILLUSTRIOUS TWINS

[p. 149] “Nothing is destroyed except what is replaced.” —Auguste Comte

By royal decree issued at Valladolid on the 12th of May, 1551, the Emperor Charles, in his own name and in that of his mother Queen Joanna, created the Royal University of Lima, with the privileges and liberties of the University of Salamanca.

In the Recopilación de las Leyes de Indias there is reproduced the decree signed at Valladolid by the Queen of Bohemia, Governess of Castile, and dated the 21st of September, 1551: “To serve God and the public good of our kingdoms, it is fitting that our vassals and natural subjects should have in them Universities and General Studies where they may be instructed in all the sciences and Faculties, and for the great love and will we have to favor and honor those of our Indies and to banish from them the darkness of ignorance, we found and establish in the city of Lima, in the kingdoms of Peru, and in the city of Mexico, in New Spain, Universities and General Studies, and we hold it good and concede to all persons who in said Universities shall be graduated, that they enjoy in our Indies, Islands and Mainland of the Ocean Sea the liberties and franchises which those graduated in the University and studies of Salamanca enjoy in other kingdoms.”

Such was the common origin of the two illustrious twins: the University of Lima and that of Mexico. San Marcos has lived, across the centuries, its glorious academic life. It received Bolívar within its walls and confirmed, at the appointed hour, the autonomy of Peru.

Our University, by contrast, suppressed by the government of don Benito Juárez, restored by don Justo Sierra during the administration of don Porfirio Díaz, has suffered the tragic destiny of our history. Today it is about to complete only twenty-five years since its restoration.

The illustrious master don Justo Sierra said, on the day the University was reborn: “Do we have a history? No. The Mexican University that is born today has no family tree”... “If it has no forebears, if it has no grandfathers, our University has precursors: the guild and the cloister of the Royal and Pontifical University of Mexico are not for us the ancestor, they are the past.”

[p. 150] On this point we differ from the opinion of our Master. The past is always the ancestor. It is impossible to deny the action of time, which organizes and unites the secular work of the generations. Our precursors are our grandfathers. But, of course, the work of the centuries is modified and transformed by the unanimous effort of the generations! ... The Sorbonne is the medieval work of the grandfathers, the supreme grandeur of Paris as the theological and philosophical capital of Catholic Europe; yet, across the centuries, this eminent Institution is the Alma Mater of modern scientific and philosophical daring. Oxford and Cambridge constitute the intellectual and moral reliquary of an England Catholic, Protestant, and freethinking. The past, according to Leibniz, is “pregnant with the future.”

Tradition is invention and repetition. Culture is unity of style. To disown the past is impossible, because the law of universal causation sets its seal upon the organic effort of the generations. What has no style is barbarism. Whence come the letters of our alphabet? Carlyle asks himself; whence our very word? Whence the cooking that feeds us and the building that shelters us? ..., and the British thinker answers gallantly: “You will find the fibrous roots of contemporary events in the dust of Cadmus and of Trismegistus, of Tubal-cain and Triptolemus. Their first roots lie with father Adam himself or in the ashes of the first fire that Eve kindled.”

Mexico’s error has consisted in disowning its traditions. This is the same as losing “acquired velocity,” as the mathematicians say. But we want our thought to be interpreted with good sense. To conserve is not merely to repeat; it is also to create; to create without destroying what can be put to use for progress.

The University of Mexico ought to have preserved, like that of Lima, its illustrious lineage. Not in order to stand still or petrify; but in order to combine, in the effort of the present, the work of the past with that of the future. To create always; to destroy what falls and dies beyond remedy; but to know that “one destroys only what one replaces,” as Auguste Comte used to say. In this way one is great. Thus victory is reached and affirmed.

Our time, an epoch of social claims, an age of profound criticism, is also a century that pays its most devoted homage to History. The historical sense is “a form of universal sympathy”; for this reason it can and must be reconciled with criticism. Without the historical sense, nothing is understood; without judgment, nothing is integrated. The supreme integration is always a synthesis of thought and sympathy. To comprehend [p. 151] is as much to understand as to love. Woe to those who neither love nor understand!

Painful days are passing today for Culture and the University. All the passions gather around our Alma Mater! The insane hatred of those who never understood the mission of higher education; the ambition of those who cannot wait; the greed and dissimulation of those who long to set a limit to the noble and free expression of thought; the abandonment by the powerful, who could, if they wished, gallantly fulfil their duty; the vanity swollen with pride that boasts of being apostolic, et cetera.

Our house of studies staggers in the midst of the violence of the storm; poor, forsaken, anguished, convulsed, its breast and face spattered with blood by the work of petty politicians, it seems close to succumbing in its undertaking. It was about to celebrate its anniversary when the unleashed forces of Evil mock it! Nevertheless, it must triumph, drawing strength from its anguish. For its cause is not transitory nor its undertaking futile. It has in its favor the secret of its destiny: “to realize in its supreme forms the work of national education,” as the fundamental article of its Statute says. It must conquer, in the end. Let us believe firmly in its triumph!

Freedom of teaching cannot be suppressed at the hands of the officiants of any cult. Freedom is thought. Thought is freedom. Autonomy lies in the essence of thinking. Heteronomy of philosophical or scientific speculation amounts to the annihilation of speculation. Neither in Rome nor in Moscow has anyone reached the absurdity of declaring a priori a standard for philosophizing or speculating scientifically; for such a limit would prevent the very act of thought.

The “exact conception of the Universe” that some advocate is, of course, impossible; because exactness does not belong to all branches of knowledge, but only to the mathematical sciences; but if it were achieved in those sectors of knowledge where it is possible, it would be so only thanks to freedom of inquiry and the autonomy of philosophical judgment. We cannot go beyond the examples that Europe offers us. Neither Bolsheviks nor Fascists have dared to declare that Universities must be confined within intangible dogmas!

At this very moment the Pan-American Congress is being held. Scholars from all the countries of the Continent have gathered in our capital. They observe our acts attentively. They will return to their respective peoples to declare what they witnessed in favor of Mexican civilization. Shall we go on offering them the spectacle of derision of an illustrious University torn apart by the criminal effort of those who put the idea in irons? ... Mexican society [p. 152] and the Government of the Republic have the strict duty to intervene in something that forms part of the good reputation of the Fatherland.

The author of this article, a son of the University of Mexico, but honored also by the Cloister of San Marcos of Lima with his incorporation as Doctor honoris causa, wishes to offer, in these mournful moments, all the bitterness of his filial feelings to that glorious University, twin of his Alma Mater, as a sincere homage of love and veneration.
