Of all that is ours, perhaps it is pain that most deeply formulates and expresses the irreducible intimacy of one’s own being.

#### II. ¡CONSUMATUM EST!

In our previous chapter we said, referring to the foundation of both Universities, of Lima and of Mexico: “San Marcos has lived, across the centuries, its glorious academic life. It received Bolívar within its walls and confirmed, at the appointed hour, the autonomy of Peru.”

“Our University, by contrast, suppressed by the government of don Benito Juárez, restored by don Justo Sierra during the administration of don Porfirio Díaz, has suffered the tragic destiny of our history. Today it is about to complete only twenty-five years since its restoration” ...

We did not expect that the tragedy of our transformation, always irregular, would be confirmed so soon by a new, painful, inconceivable episode. The Autonomous University is dead. The institution may, to be sure, go on living, but not without heteronomy. The State indicates the form of life of the new institute. Freedom of teaching, united with the idea of autonomy, will disappear, will be constrained, will be modulated in a form that answers to the pedagogical aims of national policy. That is, the University will not be able to assume its autonomy, the autonomy of its own thought. A thought external to it restrains it by ministry of the law. Freedom of teaching should have no other limits than those laid down by the norms concerning political order and morality.

There is no sufficient reason for the State to intervene in higher education. For this reason the Spanish Constitution contains in its Article 48: “Freedom of teaching is recognized and guaranteed.” And don Nicolás Pérez Serrano, commenting on the cited article, says: “The supreme dignity of the function requires that teachers and disciples divest themselves, in the classroom, of whatever is not objective serenity and sincere devotion to the one truth. When the chair ceases to be a communion of devotees who believe in science and becomes a platform for [p. 153] one-sided and wholly unscientific propaganda, freedom has been prostituted.” Therefore it is beyond doubt that, once university teaching is subjected to political propaganda, it will cease to be autonomous, and if teaching ceases to be autonomous, the University itself will lose its autonomy. Hence the Autonomous University of Mexico has ceased to exist. A new institute supplants it. We voiced these same ideas in October 1933, upholding the indispensability of the principle, which today is flouted by the new political-economic organization of what was our Alma Mater.

For, in what concerns the life of the spirit, what matters is not the exteriority of the manifestation but the interiority, the intimacy of thought. Outwardly, everything is preserved. They are the same buildings, the same libraries, identical laboratories; only a few professors no longer occupy their chairs; but inwardly, in the intimacy of the university conscience, the Alma Mater is no longer the same. Before, it was autonomous and now it is heteronomous. Before, it was poor; it is going to be rich.

Let us analyze the work of the chair in order to show that the essence of university teaching lies in freedom.

A chair is a colloquy between teachers and disciples. Its essence is dialogue, dialectic. The Platonic academy is the model of university chairs. Aristotle discoursed peripatetically with his students on themes of science and philosophy. The chair is a mutual and reciprocal exchange between the professor and the students. The teacher is only a student who has gained, through his age and his knowledge, some advantage over his disciples, which his years lend him. The chair obeys an order; everything that obeys an order answers to an end; the “final end” of the chair, of academic discussion, is the attainment of truth. Children may be guided in the primary school according to the aims of the State, in keeping with our legislation; but the university adults fill the lecture halls with the purpose of knowing. “To know in order to foresee, to foresee in order to act,” as positivism taught. What is there to discuss, to treat dialectically, or to know, if a dogma is imposed on us beforehand? Why know if one already knows? Why inquire, if an article of the Law imposes the truth? ... The intrusion of public power, which claims to interrupt the Platonic dialogue, is a sophism, something more, an absurdity!

These are not sporadic considerations nor pleadings on behalf of prejudices. What we maintain constitutes the summary of what the science called by Scheler the “sociology of knowledge” teaches in this regard. Culture cannot be dogmatically defined, because its essence is [p. 154] creation, “creation of values.” By virtue of this, every dogma, every assertion, unless it is a question of a principle absolutely self-evident (and this is not the case), will prove too narrow to formulate the effort of the generations that discover truth. So that, in suppressing the autonomy of university teaching, one defrauds not only the men of the present but the youth of the future. The sociology of knowledge proves effectively that every present determination or constraint will redound to the detriment of posterity. To make slaves today will act as a negative condition, as a counteracting cause, upon the freedom of the future; and since thought is perfectly free, the organization of the new Institute, which supplants the old Autonomous University of Mexico, constitutes from now on a drag upon Mexican civilization. Under these bad auspices, with this retroactive sign, the new house of studies is inaugurated!

Moreover, not only is there an offense against pure reason, a denial of the postulates of universal academic life; the announced yoke also breaks a venerable tradition in Mexico. For the principle of freedom of teaching has always ruled in the Mexican University, as in the National Preparatory School, founded by don Gabino Barreda. The famous founder of the Preparatory School did not hesitate to seat Father Pascua in the chair of Physics at San Ildefonso. Don Rafael Ángel de la Peña, an illustrious humanist and mathematician, who professed Catholicism, taught Geometry and Castilian Grammar in those very classrooms. Canon Labastida was a disciple of the great Mexican educator; and the humble author of this article had the singular honor that the Lectures he once gave on the critical history of Positivism were presided over by the illustrious philosopher Dr. don Porfirio Parra, heir of Barreda in the direction of the Mexican positivist school.

But then there was more love, more kindness, more nobility of spirit in the conscience of Mexicans. Then the passions did not poison people’s minds. Everyone respected the work of others. Today it is not so. Unfortunately, “truth” is not the supreme value that inspires the claims that people wish to undertake. A breath of ill will poisons the attitude of some Mexicans toward the others. We are all enemies of all. The Mexican neither believes nor loves; for this reason he hopes for nothing. Unbridled passions rear up under the wicked lash of evil and rancor. No lasting work can be born of this monstrous moral agitation.

The University is Culture, Reason, and Tradition at one and the same time. In the vortex of the sad historical moment we have reached, the old House of Studies went under. The Spirit will not speak through the race of professors [p. 155] and students. Now the word belongs to political force! It reigns with a conscience clouded by hatred and a purse brimming with gold. Historical materialism is delighted; but it is not good to triumph in this way. To know how the materialist doctrine rules when it rules, one need only wait a few days, a few hours...

¡Consumatum est!

#### III. LAS HUMANIDADES Y LA POLÍTICA — III. THE HUMANITIES AND POLITICS

As the Latin classic said, nothing human is “foreign” to the humanist. One who devotes his activity to the vigor of thought in its literary and philosophical expression cannot consider as alien to his action the spheres in which the secular effort of the generations unfolds, nor rich and complex contemporary life. To witness in history the evolution of moral doctrines is the best incentive to hope that they will be realized, lending nuance to action. Thought that does not aspire to fulfil itself is mere play, a meaningless idle fancy.

The unity of the human spirit makes of ideas conatus, that is, principles of action. It is impossible that the will, inspired by feeling and by philosophical judgment, should not tend to crystallize into ostensible, indubitable acts. He who thinks one way and acts in another is not a man but a monster; though in our days there are many adepts of monstrosities and crimes. This intimate connection in the structure of the spirit constantly ensures the growth of culture, because God is the personality in which all values and all virtues are harmonized; and man, a divine likeness, aspires to be, like God, the unity in which values and virtues are integrated.

The University of Mexico has made its autonomy the law of its action; and the principle of freedom of teaching, in close solidarity with the idea of university autonomy, acts preponderantly, orienting the efforts of university people toward the attainment of their high destinies.

Professors and students, humanists inspired by philosophy, art, science, and history, tend, by reason of their culture, to externalize the Platonic ideas of knowledge in concordant works, in actions congruent with the same ideal. For this they throw themselves into political struggles and seek to make of the State, of the City, realities that answer to the concept of justice, to the paradigm of virtue. What is strange, then, [p. 156] if in Monterrey and in Tabasco they struggle to achieve the expressions of their philosophical thought? ...

The concept of politics comes from the noble Platonic and Aristotelian tradition, which opposed the effort of the sophists. Socrates taught with his life, sanctioned with his martyrdom, the ethical ideal of the just City, that is, of human coexistence in accordance with the imperatives of reason. University students cannot but strive for the realization of the just City, of society organized in accordance with incontrovertible rational principles. For this they throw themselves into political struggles and, without fear of the consequences of their thought, aspire only to see it realized in life; because any reality incongruent with the ideal is not, in truth, an argument against the ideal itself, but against the facts that do not fulfil it and the situations that deny it.

This politics is what Plato and Aristotle taught to the humanists of all times. According to them, the City is the way to realize Man fully. The virtues of the intelligence are signified in a single name: prudence; the virtues of the heart, in one other name alone: courage; the virtues of conduct all fit within a single denomination: temperance. But there is, according to Plato, something superior to prudence, courage, and temperance; this new paradigm, this eternal model that ensures the equilibrium of all human virtues, is justice. And justice can be realized only in the State, in the City. How could it be possible that university students, nourished on classical teaching, should cease to aspire to see it fulfilled in the Republic? How could those who “know,” that is, those who have incorporated into their mental being the eternal principles of morality, not wish to see them externalized in social life? How could those who proclaim the categorical imperative in the lecture hall turn away from the idea of a just State? ...

Hence the grandeur of the University Institution. At this moment it marks out for the Mexican State the unmistakable directions of moral action. The University is the reliquary of culture, the inner voice of conscience, the exceptionless principle of reason. It represents the eternal against the transitory, the necessary against the contingent, the ideal against the real. For this it is great.

We university people are not against this or that regime set up by politicians of a few years, a few days, or a few hours. We are against no one. We are fulfilling our duty: that is all. And if the politics of the University is not condemnable, it is because it opposes only the counteracting causes of the ideal and the negative conditions of duty.

Before long our Institution will complete five lustra of life. [p. 157] Before its rebirth, the Schools and Faculties were only the membra disjecta of a nonexistent being. Thanks to don Justo Sierra, the university organism was reborn; but there was no unity of action, no sympathy among the groups, no integrity and unity of thought. Each School or Faculty lived apart, without thinking that the organic and creative effort could proceed only from union. Afterwards the Institution has been attacked; but everything that is attacked becomes integrated within. The need for defense creates the warlike organization. The forces that act in synergy in the social order are always human needs, that is, human sufferings, because every need, in Schopenhauer’s words, “is a pain to which its satisfaction puts an end.” Civilization is a consequence of social struggles. Every belief proceeds from an externalization of the soul, which applies itself insistently to what incites, astonishes, or moves it. In this way, by being attacked, the University has flourished, not only in a harvest of thoughts, but in a synthesis of heroic actions. For our young heroes, we university people reserve the most prized flowers of our moral conscience! May these sentiments, devoutly consecrated, be their reward!

But today the University of Mexico is already adult. The culture of the humanists has borne its fruit. The young raise their heads from the attentive reading of the books of the humanities, cease their metaphysical speculation, and inquire whether social life in its manifold manifestations may perchance accord with the text of the books, with the inspirations of the geniuses, with the affirmations of the ideal! And since they cannot remain indifferent to the distance between what is thought and what is lived, they wish to compel reality to bend to the decisive urgencies of the law. It would be impossible to pretend to hold them back in their attitude; for that would be as much as to annihilate the spirit in itself! For the will is informed by reason, as reason is externalized in the act.

The dramatic moments now passing strengthen the university conscience and demonstrate, no longer only to Mexico but to the whole world and to Latin America (which closely attends to our effort), its unmistakable vitality, its constant and perfect spirit of justice. We have faith in the attainment of our destinies, because we have love; for this very reason we harbor hope. Faith is belief in the invisible, based on love and sustained by hope!

And so the divine virtues accompany the human ones. “Love without sacrifice is vanity,” as one of the Italian heroes said as he died in the last European war. University students, far from all vanity, know how to sacrifice themselves on the altar of a pure and upright love. Thus they “make a fatherland,” incorporating in a page of undoubted nobility, [p. 158] in the body of our Institution, the crowning of the divine virtues, which complete the picture of the great pagan virtues: justice, courage, temperance, and prudence.

Reverently, and with love too, we would wish to put into these lines the sense of our most devoted admiration for the noble, the quixotic servants of eternal Truth!

#### IV. LAS BASES DE LA UNIVERSIDAD AUTÓNOMA — IV. THE FOUNDATIONS OF THE AUTONOMOUS UNIVERSITY

“The sophists of all the tunics.”

Justo Sierra

The fundamental principles of the National Autonomous University are:

1. Autonomy.

2. Freedom of teaching.

3. The economic subsidy.

These three principles are irreducible to one another. That is: given the autonomy of the Institution, freedom of teaching cannot be reduced to it, nor vice versa. And neither of the two principles can be reduced to the economic question, nor the latter to them. Therefore each of the three postulates possesses its own characteristic and substantial integrity, as we shall now demonstrate.

As regards the idea of university autonomy, it is obvious that the essence of autonomy lies in not receiving the law from elsewhere, but in giving it to oneself, by virtue of one’s own sovereign principle. The only thing that can be admitted as a restriction is the form of autonomy declared by the Law. But the State has provided for autonomy by creating, by the design and will of the legislator, the organism of the University as a Moral Person sui juris.

It is seen, from the foregoing, that the idea of an autonomous University is not the idea of freedom of teaching. A university can be autonomous and not advocate the principle of freedom of teaching, because this implies no contradiction; and, vice versa, a university can be heteronomous and advocate the principle of freedom of teaching.

As an example of the former one may think of the various confessional universities. The Catholic University of Louvain does not uphold the principle of freedom of teaching and is, nevertheless, as a University, fully autonomous. The University of Georgetown, in Washington, [p. 159] is autonomous, but teaching is subordinated to the confessional essence of the institution. It is therefore proved that the autonomy of the university organism is irreducible to the affirmation of the principle of freedom of teaching.

On the other hand, the University of Mexico, in the period when it depended on the Mexican State as a wholly subordinate organism, was heteronomous and, nonetheless, freedom of teaching prevailed in it with full breadth and rigor. Hence, in this case too, it is confirmed that the affirmation of university autonomy is irreducible to that of the principle, so often cited, of freedom of instruction.

If, then, the two postulates are irreducible to each other, it is indispensable to affirm them separately; and the attitude of those who claim that, in affirming autonomy, the thesis of freedom of teaching is already implied is sophistical and an outrage. This conduct, sophistical and outrageous, can lead to the flat denial of the principle of freedom of instruction; because, once university autonomy is granted, all freedom in research may nevertheless be violated. It is, therefore, indispensable to affirm both distinct principles in detail, and not to claim that freedom of teaching is a “form without content”; because the content of such a form is supplied by the chair itself, which can never be empty of concrete content, according to the subject at issue: scientific, philosophical, historical, linguistic, et cetera.

A chair always has content or it is not a chair. The content, as we have just declared, is the object-matter of teaching; therefore the principle of freedom of teaching is not a “form without content,” as the tendentious and shamefaced sophists declare, but form and content; form as regards freedom, and content as regards the chair. Only those chairs will be without content that are served by inept professors; and even these will have content, only deficient, frustrating the purpose attached to teaching.

But there are persons who think only of “contents” and disown “forms.” Now, the formless (without form) in itself is the unthinkable. Matter, pure content without form, is non-being itself, the unknowable in itself, the nonexistent in person. Therefore, in conclusion, we affirm and prove that freedom of teaching is form and content, indissolubly bound to each other; form as regards freedom of research and content as regards the object of research. Quod erat demonstrandum.

The last of the theses or unwaivable foundations refers to the economic. The economic is nothing but an instrumental means, yet a necessary one. Whoever wants to attain an end must want the means to [p. 160] attain it, if he wants it in earnest; and an Autonomous University that advocates the principle of freedom of teaching, if it does not possess economic means sufficient for the attainment of its designs, can be only a project to be realized, forever unrealizable. Therefore: either one has the laboratories, libraries, endowments, and emoluments that suffice, or one falls into a perpetual state of incompetence to achieve the very ends of the Institution.

“The New University, whose outlines are little by little being drawn in our time, tends to be—as the distinguished Spanish master don Francisco Giner declares—a microcosm. It embraces every kind of teaching; it is the highest institute of cooperative scientific research; it prepares not only for the various social professions but for life, in its infinite complexity and richness. It stimulates at once, along with the vocation to knowledge, intellectual reflection and the search for truth in knowledge, the development of bodily energy, the impulse of the will, pure habits, the joy of living, moral character, healthy tastes, the cult of the ideal, and a social, practical, and discreet sense in conduct.”

The foregoing considerations prove sufficiently that whatever may be alleged against this is dictated neither by pure reason nor suggested by prudence, but by the prevailing spirit of sophistry, which has been working so many evils against the true interests of national culture. But since the transitory obstacles that interpose themselves in the march of human societies, far from triumphing definitively by extinguishing action, serve only as an undoubted incentive to the attainment of high moral destinies, we believe that the University of Mexico, if it does not capitulate and sink, will confound with its truth and its nobility “the sophists of all the tunics,” who at times dare to allege their feeble arguments in the very benches of the Cloister and even from the very seats of the Honorable Council of the illustrious and battered Institution.

#### V. PEDAGOGÍA UNIVERSITARIA — V. UNIVERSITY PEDAGOGY

Amicus Plato, sed magis amica Veritas

Nothing that concerns man can be foreign to the humanist, as a celebrated verse of Terence taught people. For in the world, according to Sir William Hamilton, “only the human being is great, and in the human being only the spirit is great.”

University Pedagogy is not something yet to be made; it is an explored and scientific field, which rests, as don Francisco Giner de los Ríos has shown, on the conclusions of contemporary social science, [p. 161] one of whose final and fundamental chapters is what Scheler calls the “Sociology of Knowledge.”

The human is the world of values, enclosed in the circle of Culture and opened wide to the inspirations of the Infinite. There, in that magic circle, find room the religions and the philosophies, the arts and the sciences, Economics and Law, the technique that teaches how to apply and the practice that orders living. Nothing is foreign to the humanist, to the university man: universitas studiorum.

Only the naive, who think in their ignorance that the world was formed with them and that the universal forms of knowledge arose palpitating out of nothing, can dare to proclaim that university pedagogy remains to be created or wholly renewed. Everything that has been engendered in History is continuity and tradition. Everything is the work of a cause, the effect of a past, a moment of an evolution, an episode in an incomparable drama: the march of the Spirit across the centuries and the generations.

But such naivety implies arrogance. Such ignorance bespeaks a ridiculous self-sufficiency. How can one interrupt, drawing out of nothing the “new knowledge” and the “new culture”? How disown what informs the very creative breath? How make of a Newton or an Einstein absolute values, emancipated from the cultural tradition of the human race? ...

Newton is the debtor of Kepler, the debtor of Galileo, the debtor of Copernicus. He represents the synthesis, the hour of synthesis, after the centuries of analysis. The same with Einstein. His genius is indisputable, his creation astonishing, but his tradition unavoidable. He himself has written: “the theory of relativity of Lorentz.” And rightly so. It is Einstein’s as much as Lorentz’s. It is the social, unanimous work, which is integrated with Genius and by it perfected and formulated. But without society there is no possible culture. Values are reflected in the life of human collectivities. Hence the “sociology of knowledge,” as Scheler calls it.

And against this sociology of knowledge rebel, in their naivety and arrogance, those who make of their own ignorance a magical fiat. No, the world was not made yesterday. And everything is conjugated in Nature as in History; “everything conspires,” as Leibniz gallantly said.

When one attends the University according to some, one loses personality, creative power, inventive naivety, in order to join the scholastic tradition, the collective deformation, the impersonality of that pedagogical environment.

What an absurdity! ... What is lost, this indeed beyond remedy, is the ignorant personality; what is acquired is the cultivated personality. Does the change not seem beneficial to you? ...

[p. 162] But the most baneful error is the one that makes people think there can be culture without cultural summits and good technical schools without suitable Universities. Technique is the derived and science the fundamental, as the consequence follows the principle and the corollary the axiom. Culture is, in its form, pyramidal, not spherical. The pyramid raises its apex ever higher; it is a golden antenna in which the “cosmic rays” and creative thoughts are reflected and conjugated. Technique without science is impossible; science without laboratories, libraries, and universities is absurd.

The two twin prejudices run counter to the essence of university pedagogy: the prejudice against manual labor, which characterized ancient societies, and the prejudice against intellectual labor, which arises in modern ones.

Intellectual workers are, in the mutual and reciprocal effort of the division of social labor, like their brothers the manual workers, absolutely necessary. Labor is never solely manual or solely intellectual. The hand and the intelligence cannot be enemies.

One element unites them: the tool; from the earthen pot to the locomotive and from the “equipment” to the linotype. The tool is technique, intermediary between Science and action; as intelligence is intermediate between felt need and satisfied will.

It is impossible to pretend to supplant Universities and Academies with Polytechnic Schools. The Academy was first and the Workshop last: “To know in order to foresee; to foresee in order to act.” There is no help for it! Why persist, maliciously, in sowing enmity and bewilderment where logic and history together suggest the harmony of human effort? ...

And in all these useless debates, in all this political activity, in all this public disarray, with passions run wild and hatreds fostered and spread, and incongruous ideals, and sterile struggles, an evident truth reveals itself, which Tyrians and Trojans alike would try in vain to hide with their unpatriotic rancor: Mexico is wasting its time, Mexico argues over and shows disrespect to the supreme values of culture. For this reason the Nation is going to be defeated, in the face of other, happier peoples, who think and act, create and educate, know and love. The University and the Polytechnic cannot quarrel, because both are Culture, organic and human culture. And the State has the duty to be university-minded and polytechnic, intellectual and practical, active and wise. The time that nations squander finally turns into humiliation for all. For this reason Don Francisco Giner de los Ríos, the great lay mystic who, with Unamuno, was the intellectual and moral author of modern [p. 163] Spain, says: “The new University directs toward a type of life ever more complete, not the closed training of a presumptuous, narrow and governing minority, but an education open to all the horizons of the spirit, which reaches all classes and radiates its vital action in every direction, not only of knowledge, let alone of mere instruction, but of ennoblement, of dignification, of art, of culture and of enjoyment. May that University, with its popular extension, which gives it as students all ages and classes, the rural colony and the urban one, the canteen, the baths, mountaineering, musical audition, games and sports, the newspaper, the book, the circulating library, excursions to the countryside, to the farm, to the museum, to the mine, to the monument, to the workshop, and so many other avenues of infiltration, deepening the unity of the national soul, diffuse in good hour throughout all its domains the pious longing for a society and a life ever more human.”

Yes: “a society and a life ever more human,” that is, more cultured. “In the world only man is great; in man only the Spirit is great.” Here is a truth that demolishes in advance all the futile allegations of historical materialism!

We Mexican university men are “friends of Plato, but more friends of Truth.” We go against no one; our teaching is universal, as is our Institution. The Polytechnic and the University do not exclude each other. They can be enemies only if they try to supplant each other. Each organ for each function. And the State, if it is just, must set itself up as a State of Culture, friend of the Polytechnics, as we are of Plato; but more a friend of Truth!

In the unfathomable generosity of thought, all the hatreds of the bell tower are annihilated!

#### VI. PEDAGOGÍA POLITÉCNICA — VI. POLYTECHNIC PEDAGOGY

#### Amicus Plato, sed magis amica Veritas

“If you let the specialists work on one side, and the politicians on the other, they will soon have cut the roots of the tree of Science, in order to gather only the fruits that can be consumed immediately.”

A. Fouillée

Once Pharaoh, great patron of the scholars of the Museum of Alexandria, conversing with one of the most illustrious geometers on matters [p. 164] of geometry, after having received from the mathematician a detailed and exact explanation of the proofs of the theorems and the solution of the geometrical problems, impatient to reach the desired result as soon as possible, without having to rely on the long chains of reasoning of geometrical proof, said to his distinguished interlocutor: “There must exist in Mathematics a swifter and easier road for kings to follow.” And the interlocutor replied: “Majesty: in Mathematics kings have to follow the path of everyone; there is no royal road for monarchs in demonstrated science.”

The anecdote comes in handy today, but not in regard to kings; for the majesty that has replaced monarchs in our time is the people or, as is so often said: “the masses.” Well then, there is no royal road for kings or for masses. Science cannot be demonstrated except by the order of the foundations that logic studies. And Pharaoh, like the people and the pharaonic masses, must follow the rigorous ordering of geometrical foundations or they will never know geometry. Dura lex!

This means that it is absolutely impossible to persist in the absurd problem of constructing “popular science.” Science, especially mathematical science, will always remain foreign to unprepared persons. Newton’s binomial is unintelligible without knowledge of the theory of the combinations that can be made with “m” letters, taking them successively one by one, two by two, three by three, and so on. And whoever is ignorant of the theory of combinations will never understand the problem of the coefficients in Newton’s celebrated formula. On the other hand, whoever knows the implicit theory will easily understand the Newtonian expression; there is no royal road for “masses” in Algebra, just as there was no practicable private route for Pharaoh in Geometry! ... Science is the organic effort of demonstration and foundation, which obeys an unexceptionable theory: Logic.

Therefore, the prejudice against “pure science,” against the Academy and the University, is at least as absurd as its twin: the prejudice against “manual labor,” which formerly prevailed, naming certain human tasks “servile” and other activities “liberal.” All work is produced around the supreme end: Culture.

Without industrial technicians peoples decay and become slaves of other, better-equipped peoples; but without the systematic teaching of pure Science, of knowing for the sake of knowing, all industry and all technique are works of vanity, illusion or routine. How many purely theoretical discoveries, for which no practical application is found at first, are later the foundation of machines and instruments [p. 165] most useful in the development of industry and technique!

For example: the discovery of electromagnetism by Oersted, Ampère, Arago, et cetera, becomes, shortly afterward, the invention of the dynamo; and this invention has made modern industry possible, in large part. What would the great factories be without the electromagnet? Without Galvani’s famous and primordial experiment, how would electricity have become “domestic”?

Therefore one must look, at the bottom of every industrial renewal, for pure scientific work. Technique is the consequence, Science is the principle; industry is the corollary, research the axiom. Amicus Plato, sed magis amica veritas! Or as it is said, gallantly, in Spanish: “valor does not exclude courtesy.” The valor of Industry and Technique must not set aside the supreme courtesy of Science and the Humanities. “This ought to be done and that not left undone,” as the Gospel puts it.

Once opulent Polytechnics have been created beside a lifeless University, the result will be a purely superficial and retroactive practice, without theoretical roots. An overpopulation of literati and poets is a sheer absurdity among neurasthenic peoples; but a technified mass, without Institutes of higher culture in the Nation, is only technified barbarism. And barbarism must always be excluded, technified or not!

If the liberal professions do not fulfill their social task of elaborating the ideas of the community; but rather, far from it, are no more than a burden on the realization of the life of all; if lawyers are produced wholesale, and each represents a scant contribution of morality, science and culture; if physicians crowd the great cities where they offer their useless services, and refuse to lend their contribution of labor in the countryside or the small towns, where they would obtain ample profits; if engineers are not found at the head of industrial or agricultural enterprises, but fill the public offices to the detriment of their own professional prestige, it is no wonder that the working classes refuse their deference to the liberal professionals, unworthy of their social task.

A people of deficient intellectual workers must be a people of beggars. But how can a University without resources or capital create from within itself fit representatives of the culture it imparts or ought to impart? What sufficient reason or justice is there to deny the University a decorous subsidy from the State?

According to some contemporary philosophers, what is characteristically human would consist in man’s being a worker, homo faber. The homo faber, the working animal, constantly shapes the conditions of collective [p. 166] industry and thus brings about the concomitant transformation of society. As long as we Mexicans devote ourselves by preference to politics and war, our country will suffer from its everlasting ills.

Politics, war and idleness have always been the favorite occupations of Mexicans. This was possible in the nations of ancient history, dominated by the prejudice against technique, against manual labor, which was called “servile”; because those peoples subsisted upon masses of wretched slaves, private and public. Today slavery is impossible. Workers and peasants do not wish to be slaves, can no longer be so. Since the social composition of nations has been radically modified, war and politics are determined by science, industry, agriculture and commerce. In this way intellectualism and historical materialism turn out to be two aspects, intimately linked and connected, of social truth.

Man, we have said before, is a maker. Why does he make? To remedy his needs. Industry rests, precisely, on this urge. Without good technical schools, national industry will always be deficient. But industry is based on science: and without good Universities, the harmonious functioning of industrial activities can never be achieved. We shall always be a social organism of deficient culture, with its mind and its sensorium outside the nation itself! Other peoples will always give us the truth we shall have to apply and the technical norm we shall have to follow!

“It is important above all —says Alfred Fouillée— that the spirit of disinterested speculation subsist in Science. At bottom, this spirit is the philosophical one. All the great scholars were true philosophers, full of universality, enthused by the great conceptions of the Universe. If you let the specialists work on one side, and the politicians on the other, they will soon have cut the roots of the tree of Science, in order to gather only the fruits that can be consumed immediately.”

So that, in conclusion, as the illustrious founder of the University of Mexico thought, we must “Mexicanize science and nationalize knowledge.” To this end, let us endow with equal generosity, with the same predilection, the cultivators of pure science and the technicians who apply it. The University and the Polytechnic were born together in the work of humanity, and together they will prosper or decline: because they are bound in the terms of an irrefutable syllogism, as the two irreplaceable pedagogical premises of the single, civilizing conclusion. The homo faber presupposes the homo sapiens.

#### VII. LA CIENCIA Y LA TÉCNICA — VII. SCIENCE AND TECHNIQUE

[p. 167] “Only by starting from the soul can the significance of technique be discovered.”

Oswald Spengler

One of the best arguments regarding the problem of the relations between Science and Technique is furnished by the theory of the great contemporary logician Edmund Husserl. Whoever clearly understands this theory knows precisely the unavoidable relations that follow from the fact that all technique, all norm, is founded on pure Science. So that, without pure science, without Academy, without University, there can be no technological Institutes.

“It is easy to see —says Husserl— that every normative discipline, and a fortiori every practical discipline, presupposes as its foundation one or several theoretical disciplines, in the sense of possessing a theoretical content independent of all normation, which will have its natural seat in some theoretical science or sciences, already constituted or yet to be constituted” (Investigaciones lógicas, p. 63 of the Spanish version by Morente and Gaos).

To every scientific truth, whatever it may be, the idea of normation can be added; and then, thanks to this addition, it becomes normative; but the norm that results from adding to the truth the idea of normation is one thing, and the truth itself, which in itself is not normative, is another. Therefore the culture of a people demands, along with studies of theoretical science, knowledge of science applied to action.

For example: it is a geometrical truth that the square constructed on the hypotenuse is equal to the sum of the squares constructed on the legs. Observe, then, how this geometrical theorem is absolutely independent of every idea of norm, of every technical principle. It teaches how to do nothing. It simply sets forth a characteristic property of the triangle.

Let us now add “the idea of normation,” as Husserl says, to the theorem in question, and we shall obtain, as a consequence: to form the square of the hypotenuse, add the squares of the legs; or else: the squares of the legs are equivalent to the square constructed on the hypotenuse. It is clear how every theoretical principle can engender from itself a technical formula.

And since what is said of the theorem cited can be said of every theoretical truth, every theoretical truth is capable of being stated as a rule [p. 168] of action. For this reason, the techniques of calculating and of drawing have been based on Algebra and Geometry since antiquity.

A technical School of mathematics must necessarily be erected upon pure scientific knowledge. A technical school of mechanics is likewise inconceivable without pure mechanical theory. The same holds for the techniques derived from physics and chemistry, from physiology and the natural sciences. For this reason, too, the great cultured peoples who elaborate pure science are the very nations that march in the vanguard of technical progress. To add the idea of normation to a principle is relatively easy and secondary. The important thing is to discover it. Practical applications will come afterward, logically.

The great Pasteur, one of the most luminous investigators in the history of Science, in discussing experimentally the problem of spontaneous generation, was able to provide the basis for admirable technical procedures, which Medicine has elaborated and re-elaborated endlessly. The problem under debate consisted in ascertaining whether the spontaneous generation of Life is admissible. Pasteur’s experiments proved that what was taken for “spontaneously created” life was nothing but already “preexisting” life. The germs preexisted. Hence they later developed under suitable conditions.

But this theoretical debate at once provoked, with incalculable consequence, manifold and generous technical discoveries. The same great French investigator was able to cure rabies by means of his extraordinary discovery. Then the world was moved with amazement and it became possible to establish the “Pasteur Institute,” academy and laboratory, theory and action, science and technique of healing. Such is the marvelous effect of adding “the idea of normation,” as Husserl calls it, to the principles of pure science!

Who was to think of the subsequent effects of a doctrinal speculation on spontaneous generation? Who could have foreseen that from the debate on a theoretical question such a heap of benefits was to spring for humanity? How could what was directed to the metaphysical discussion of the generation of Life be resolved into admirable techniques of healing?

Which proves that science and technique cannot be divorced from each other. Just as the Intelligence that knows and the Will that wills cannot be separated. Bacon said it already, from the Renaissance: “Man, servant and interpreter of Nature, governs it only insofar as he understands it. For whoever does not know the cause cannot produce the effect.”

[p. 169] Let us abandon the ignoble prejudice against pure science, as an unworthy obstacle to the march of civilization. Let us not attempt to break the august harmony of the human soul, which develops in history, engendering from itself that marvelous and constant flowering of knowing into doing, of understanding into building, of philosophizing into living. Nothing can dissociate the synthesis of the University and the Polytechnic. Do we want a fit Polytechnic? Then we aspire, eo ipso, to an illustrious University. Both correlative terms imply each other mutually. And let us not attempt to regulate, to mold in advance, the generous and unforeseeable movement of the investigator. Let us leave him abandoned to the resources of his research; responsible before his problem. He will find the way, no doubt. It would be presumption to show it to him. The very sequence of his activity places him in the presence of the canon he must follow.

It is not possible to define a priori the activity of genius, which is woven of unheard-of episodes, of magnificent associations of most personal ideas. The norms and canons will be produced a posteriori. Science founds and technique realizes. Science creates and art applies. Everything is bound together, everything sustains everything else, reciprocally. No one will accept the sad role of showing himself an intruder in the very reliquary of scientific intimacy. To doubt can be more useful, infinitely more, than to define in advance, in a dead precept, the generous impulse of the spirit eager to know.

“To understand the essence of technique —says Oswald Spengler— one must not start from machine technique, still less from the deceptive idea that the construction of machines and tools is the end of technique. In reality, technique is most ancient. It is not a historical peculiarity, but something enormously universal. It transcends man and penetrates the life of animals, of all animals. To the type of life that the animal represents, as distinct from that which the plant represents, there corresponds free mobility in space, relative freedom and independence with respect to all the rest of nature and, therefore, the need to assert oneself against it, to give one’s own existence a kind of meaning, content and superiority. Only by starting from the soul can the significance of technique be discovered.”

If such is the truth, how are we to fall perplexed with admiration before the mere exteriority of machinery and tools? These machines refer to a soul. These tools confess a spirit. One must seek, as the economist said, “behind what is seen, what is not seen.” And this last is the soul of modern culture. At the bottom of all industrialism lie the will and the thought of our historical moment. Hence, if technique means intelligence and consciousness, [p. 170] the Polytechnic must also confess it. It implies the university institution, symbol of pure science. The Sorbonne, Oxford, Berlin, Harvard possess the secret of the technical development of the greatest nations. It is the same universal genius that establishes the value of the autonomous chair and the significance of modern industry.

Moreover, which of us who speak Spanish, here in America or in Europe, does not remember that a professor of humanities of the University of Salamanca, Don Miguel de Unamuno, overthrew “the secular throne of Recared”; and another lay mystic, Don Francisco Giner de los Ríos, cultivator of the techniques of the Science of Education, formed in the classrooms of the “Free Institution of Teaching” the select group of teachers of the Spanish Republic?

#### VIII. LA ESENCIA DEL ESTATUTO UNIVERSITARIO — VIII. THE ESSENCE OF THE UNIVERSITY STATUTE

“The University is something entirely different from a simple juxtaposition of the Faculties and Schools that compose it. It is a natural whole. Just as science is one, despite the diversity of the particular sciences, there exists among the schools in which the different human sciences are taught a unity of aspirations and a solidarity of interests. This unity and this solidarity are what the University expresses. It possesses, then, a function of its own, different from those that fall to the schools of which it is composed... It is held that, in principle, the fundamental role of the University is rather the disinterested cultivation of science than the investigation of its practical consequences.”

Émile Durkheim

During the rectorship of Don Manuel Gómez Morín, care was taken to draw up a university Statute, which we all thought would be lasting. Not because we had a lively intellectual sympathy for the regime that then held sway, but because in that Statute the true essence of the university Institution was reflected. The defects of the academic Constitution were slight. Everything could have survived, had there not come, as always among us, the absurd onslaughts of unrestraint and incomprehension.

The essence of university life lies in the purity of the [p. 171] Faculty of Philosophy and Letters, as well as in the prominence given to the Faculty of Sciences.

If the Faculty of Philosophy is modified, the University survives; but if the Faculty is corrupted or annihilated, the University is annihilated with it. It constitutes its essence.

In the Middle Ages, the Universities of Paris and Oxford were the seat of philosophical thought, as Bologna and Montpellier were the emporia of Jurisprudence and Medicine. In modern times, the essential Faculty had at last to be divided into a Faculty of Philosophy, History and Letters, and a Faculty of Sciences.

Modern science is one of the “humanities” which, by its great development and notable importance, implies the correlative autonomy of its adequate Organ. Because of this development of scientific knowledge the creation of a Faculty of Sciences has been necessary, whose establishment clearly expresses the importance of the scientific disciplines and the eminent regard in which they are held today in all sectors of human culture.

Therefore the Faculty of Philosophy, together with that of Sciences, forms the nucleus of the teaching activities in every University truly worthy of the name.

These two being integrated and set in their place of honor, as the synthesis of academic effort, the other traditional Faculties, of Law and Medicine, constitute the organic complement of the University. Since, according to our traditions, the School of Engineers has always belonged to the higher culture of Mexico, nothing is more equitable than to refer this school, of ancient and illustrious lineage (which already in his century Baron von Humboldt extolled), to the Faculty of Sciences.

What does not seem acceptable, in the Statute of the University in force until these last days, is the inclusion, within the Faculty of Philosophy, of the group of schools whose proper mission is the cultivation of the Fine Arts.

The University of Mexico ought to have an Institute of Art, since the prestigious and meritorious Academy of San Carlos, also of ancient and distinguished lineage like that of Mines, as well as the School of Plastic Arts and that of Music, are integral parts of the corporation. With these three organisms of culture, the Institute of Arts or of Fine Arts could be formed, leaving to the Faculty of Philosophy its traditional name, and not calling it, absurdly, “Faculty of Philosophy and Fine Arts.”

Beside the Faculties, the Statute indicates very clearly the [p. 172] functioning of the Institutes for research, whose life is so essential to the development of university culture.

As can be seen, nothing remains to be created within the old organization.

Today a new Plan, a new system, is presented to us. We shall not enter into a detailed discussion of its chapters and sections, because we are persuaded of the intrinsic goodness of the previous regime. We do not think that a Statute that could not even govern should be modified or abolished, unless opportunity and adequate time are given for its functioning, so that its defects and advantages may thus be weighed.

If one does not act in this manner and acts out of passion, without being able to exhibit a single reason sufficient to justify the new course of conduct that is to be undertaken, one will proceed without tact or good sense.

Every University must tend toward the realization or attainment of two ends: the increase of knowledge and its diffusion at the university level. But contemporary needs imply a new eminent purpose: university extension. If an academic organism diffuses, in the order that falls to it, higher culture, it collaborates, to the measure of its essence and possibilities, in its increase and development and, finally, extends the university work so that those individuals who are not qualified, for obvious reasons, to follow the regular academic courses may take part in the effort of the House of Studies. The University fulfills the essential ends of its Institute: it creates culture, diffuses it and “extends” it.

It is seen, therefore, that the good road does not lie in devising new forms of university organization that come to supplant the traditional order, already tested in the episodes of universal history and the joint work of the generations. We are, fortunately, not concerned with creating the world; we are not the dispensers of human laws nor the wonder-workers of the sociology of culture. Let us be content with our humble role. Let us be respectful disciples of venerable currents of human thought, which have been subsisting through the centuries and which come from Athens and Alexandria, from Paris and Oxford, from Salamanca and Berlin.

Our mission does not require that we constitute ourselves legislators of genius, capable of renouncing the past, in order to establish the future upon our insignificance. What we need is not to create, always a difficult thing, not to say unusual or unheard of, but to order, to work, above all to work, to investigate and to teach, so acting that the fruits of our work, our teaching and our research may be poured out upon the masses of the people, to the measure of our possibilities; because no one is obliged to the impossible, as the adage says; and [p. 173] the University of Mexico cannot by itself bring about the Mexican palingenesis. We cannot offer the universal redemption of our fellow citizens, nor undo by our own doing all the obstacles that stand in the way of the happiness of the Republic.

Above all, let us never deceive. Let us tell the State what it can loyally expect of us. Let us form a University with university men, that is, with persons devoted to study. The University is not a Church, nor a Union, nor a Brotherhood. It possesses an end in itself: the increase and diffusion of human culture.

The Family, the School, the Workshop, the State and the Temple sum up the associations of higher culture. The University is not the State, nor the Family, nor the Workshop, nor the Temple; but if it declines, the other organisms are depressed and disordered. Let us not accept all the responsibilities of the Republic, for such acceptance neither falls to us nor is within our competence; let us know how to fulfill our duty modestly, as Nelson counseled the British sailors at Trafalgar (this is already profoundly difficult); and let us prepare the new generations, so that in the coming hour of manhood they may attain their reward; so that the young men of today may be, tomorrow itself, capable citizens, beings worthy of deserving the name of men.

### IV. LA DECLINACIÓN DE LA CULTURA — IV. THE DECLINE OF CULTURE

#### I. EL PROBLEMA DE LA DECLINACIÓN DE LA CULTURA — I. THE PROBLEM OF THE DECLINE OF CULTURE

[p. 177] According to some thinkers, we would find ourselves in the very throes of decadence. This is the opinion held by Oswald Spengler. According to others, progress, which was believed necessary in the conception of the philosophers of the nineteenth century, could be seriously threatened by opposing causes of a political and military order. This is the thesis sponsored by Meyerson. According to Branly, man could be reduced to the proscription of intelligence, and would return to the state of the sociable ant, but would remain a stranger to all progress. According to Lothrop Stoddard, “the revolt against civilization” is due, above all, to the fact that “the inferior survives and multiplies, because he has already been freed from the effects of natural selection. Civilized society, instead of diminishing at its base and growing at its summit, has come to grief at the summit, while expanding below. The result of this double process was, naturally, as disastrous as it was inevitable. Lacking superior individuals, and saturated, on the other hand, with the stupid and the degenerate, the race cannot bear, for very long, its own civilization.”

Berdyaev prophesies a new Middle Ages, because we happen to live in a state comparable to that of the fall of the Roman Empire and of the ancient civilization of the third century, “when only Christianity spiritually saved the world from definitive ruin and decomposition.” Lastly, Meyerson maintains that “the moral isolation and growing hatred of peoples, as well as the power of the means of destruction, which rapidly increases,” make possible the disappearance of a great part of the immense capital accumulated by culture.

Since we are in the presence of exceptional individuals, endowed with profound understanding and ample information in the various orders of knowledge (sociologists, anthropologists, physicists, philosophers and moralists), the debate on the problem acquires the most considerable proportions it could assume. Moreover, the very fact that this time it is not a matter of intellectuals of a single specialty, but that the coincidence on the possibility, and even the probability, of the decline of Culture gathers votes of such diverse provenance, ought to make every thinking man ponder, anxious as he is for the happiness of his fellows.

[p. 178] At the end of the celebrated book on The Relativist Deduction, the French philosopher studies, in brief but unforgettable reflections, the possibility that the means of destruction at the disposal of civilized humanity today, urged on by the action of hatred among nations, may ruin Europe’s centuries-long effort toward the realization of the most illustrious Culture in History.

Three cases of the violent destruction of a civilization present themselves to our understanding. The first and oldest of all, so old that we can scarcely compute the number of centuries that separate us from the event, is that of the failure of Quaternary culture. J. de Morgan, in his Humanidad Prehistórica, asserts that “with the end of the Magdalenian industry, the arts suddenly disappeared, without our being able to know the causes of this event. But, in any case, the disappearance of Magdalenian art was an unspeakable calamity; because perhaps man, had the disaster not occurred in those remote centuries, would have progressed rapidly, and the beautiful period of the age of Pericles would have been anticipated by some thousands of years.” What was the cause of the catastrophe? Can it have been due to reasons of a geological nature, to natural cataclysms, or else did wars and invasions destroy the marvelous art of the individuals of the Magdalenian Epoch?

The second historical disaster is as surprising as, or more than, that of the great Quaternary culture. Meyerson refers to the end of the Aegean civilization. Historians know today that on the island of Crete there flourished a stupendous culture, which built magnificent palaces, whose ruins reveal an artistic power as great as that of the Quaternary peoples, extolled by J. de Morgan. This culture spread beyond Crete, and was expressed in the Cretan-Mycenaean elaboration, whose splendor is proclaimed, among other testimonies, by the celebrated “Lion Gate” at Mycenae. The excellence the Aegeans attained in architecture, decoration and ornament is not inferior in importance to the most celebrated specimens of Egypt or Babylon. The very Greeks of the classical age, Meyerson notes, retained fewer precise memories of Cretan civilization than the Middle Ages have bequeathed to us regarding classical culture. Here is an example of how a civilization can perish by violent death... Will the near future reserve so tragic an end for the Culture of which Europe now boasts and makes a show?

But the most notable and unforgettable illustration of all is the Middle Ages themselves. The invasion of the barbarian peoples, during the first centuries of the Middle Ages, obscured the development of Civilization; it seemed to ruin all that the Greco-Latin genius had elaborated [p. 179] as the supreme jewel of the diadem of the human spirit. In the year one thousand, Christendom awaited the apocalypse of the ages. The disaster of the world was believed to be near. How was human culture saved from that inevitable collapse? It is that, perhaps —we reflect— an institution made possible the continuity of the progressive effort of peoples. It was necessary that, in some community of a universal nature, the synthesis be effected of the expiring ancient world with the new vital forms brought by the Germanic races. This institution, medieval par excellence, was the Church. In it were united the philosophical forms of Greek thought —which constitute the universal element of European culture, impersonal and supreme reason, the Anaxagorean and Platonic nous— with the Roman political and juridical forms, which the Greeks would have been incapable of constituting. And to these two sublime elements, the philosophical reason of Greece and Roman Law, the supreme Medieval Institution was able to add the spirit of Christianity. Only because the Church succeeded in synthesizing Hellenic catholicity, universal reason, the philosophy of Plato and Aristotle, with the Roman political element and the action of evangelical morality, was it possible not only to contain the invading barbarism, but to make it serve the ends of European culture.

“If something similar (declares Meyerson with reference to the medieval catastrophes) were to occur, even from afar, to these tragic adventures; if thought, which we have seen constantly increase its empire during a series of centuries, were to lose its vigor, would not this fact suffice to put an end to the period of development of the mathematical sciences, which seems to coincide, precisely, in antiquity as in modern times, with true human progress?”

What remains proven, according to the philosopher, is that progress is not necessary, as the men of the nineteenth century thought, who believed the flourishing of culture to be secured against all the negative conditions that might supervene in the future. When we observe how the nations arm themselves against one another and revile one another with undoubted cordiality, precisely now, when they have accumulated incomparable means of destruction, it is possible to think of the disaster of civilization or, at the least, as Berdiaeff says, of the imminence of “a new Middle Ages.”

In sum, the induction practiced by Meyerson refers to the examples of Prehistory and History, which allow us to witness the violent death of civilizations such as the Magdalenian or the Cretan, as well as that of classical culture, which was on the point of being consummated by the invasion of the barbarians. It will be said that these examples cannot be likened to the [p. 180] case of European civilization; because they are confined to partial disasters and not to the ecumenical catastrophe that the end of Europe would signify. But, on the other hand, it may be alleged (since the war of the nations has made us fear it in its apocalyptic reiteration, on the one hand); and, on the other, given the present state of the powers, ever less propitious to universal peace, that the means of destruction at the disposal of the men of other ages can in no way be compared to the formidable ones accumulated by contemporary culture.

It would be, in the unhappy event of its happening, the most contradictory occurrence of all times. Culture negated by its own “creations of values”; technique annihilating intelligence, and industry extinguishing the lamp of science! That is to say, as a Hegelian would put it: the dialectic of History “lapsing into decline” the forms of History.

#### II. LA FUERZA DEL SINO CULTURAL — II. THE FORCE OF CULTURAL DESTINY

Our time, which witnesses so many resurrections, and which beholds in full decadence so many institutions once beyond dispute (such as parliamentary democracy), has seen the type of the “prophet” reborn. That is, the inspired man who foresees and defines the future, who makes signs to what is to come from his consciousness as a wonder-worker. A being of intuition, whose apotheosis he undertakes in his doctrine, a thinker who subordinates understanding to faith. The prophet Oswald Spengler, author of one of the most celebrated books of the century, announces to us not merely the decline of Culture, but “the decline of the West.”

Only the modern prophet, like Nicolas Berdiaeff or Spengler himself, is not, like Ezekiel or Isaiah, a magnificent solitary who takes his own apocalyptic visions for things actually seen, but a profound scholar, nourished in the knowledge of the humanities and the sciences, who intuits the cycle of the development of societies as though they were organisms; and, by systematic comparison and circumstantial equation with civilizations already past in history, shrewdly divines that our time is the precursor of an irremediable cultural decline.

Today prophecy results from elaborating, in a system of intuition, enormous contingents of facts. Formerly, the prophetic man set forth, in an aphoristic style, the sudden illuminations of his consciousness. Today it is not so. Inspiration joins itself to rational knowledge and, in this way, prophecy, impregnated with science, is perhaps less pure as aesthetic and mystical elaboration, but more disturbing and synthetic, [p. 181] since it has recourse, in order to be formulated, to human reason, the impartial elaborator of laws and systems.

Spengler, as he himself notes in the first pages of his celebrated book, has made the thought of Goethe his motto: “divinity is active in the living and not in the dead.” That is, the world that reason sounds out is the object, already elaborated, already made (tout fait, as Bergson says); the reality that intuition glimpses, by contrast, is something in the process of being formed, something not crystallized, not solid, not defined, but still to be defined in its characteristic fluidity (the world en train de se faire, according to the other expression proper to Bergsonism). Spengler, in the diverse episodes of the development of a culture, aims to inquire intuitively into the secret of this essential and essentially creative flowing. Beneath the phenomenon, he skirts destiny; at the bottom of social evolution, he unravels with his genius the very meaning of organic development. There divinity is active, according to Goethe’s phrase. Civilizations that pass in history, peoples that appear and disappear, wars, revolutions, legislations, arts, sciences and States, are not the essential nor the primordial. The primordial is destiny, the inner law of development. There God works.

Nietzsche supplies the prophet with the distinction between Culture and Civilization. Culture is the creation of values, civilization the diffusion of what has been created. Culture invents and civilization diffuses. Culture is daring; Civilization, imitation and development. When cultures turn into civilizations, when the creative work becomes universal repetition, it is because its end is now arriving. Our century is an epoch of decadence and civilization, of development and routine, rather than an age of invention and surpassing. Man has not been able to elaborate, in the course of his history, more than a few cultures. All of them have their springtime age, their summer of maturity, their autumn of decline and their winter of decadence. This is the fatal cycle imposed by destiny. To be born, to grow, to spread and to die. The nineteenth century and the twentieth are declines of modern culture. It is already autumn; before long, the winter of decadence will be the precursor of death.

At the bottom of the Spenglerian conception, full of grandeur, and inspired unquestionably by Goethe, Nietzsche and Bergson, there lies an inadmissible naturalism, an organicism. Spengler assimilates wholly to living organisms the cultures themselves, and not merely simple societies or nations, as Comte and Spencer did.

“Cultures,” he declares, “are organisms; a culture is born when a great soul awakens from the primary state and detaches itself from the eternal infantilism of mankind; when a form arises from the formless, [p. 182] when something limited and ephemeral emerges from the limitless and enduring; it then flourishes upon the soil of a region to which it remains attached like a plant. A culture dies when that soul has developed the sum of its possibilities in languages, dogmas, arts, States, sciences, and returns to submerge itself in primitive spirituality. When the term has been reached, when the idea, the multitude of inner possibilities, has been developed outwardly, then, suddenly, the culture becomes ossified and dies; its blood congeals, its forces are exhausted; that is, it is transformed into civilization. This is what we feel and understand in the words Egyptianism, Byzantinism, mandarinism. And the gigantic corpse, a dried-up trunk without sap, can remain in the forest for centuries and centuries, raising its dead branches to the sky.”

And the prophet adds, profoundly convinced of his dogma: “Such is the case of China, of India, of Islam. This is the meaning of all the decadences of History, an inner and outer fulfillment, a consummation that inevitably overtakes every living culture. The one with the cleanest contours lies before our eyes: it is the decadence of antiquity.” That is to say, for Spengler there is no constant progress; rather each great culture among those elaborated by humanity is like a plant rooted in its soil, which thrives and then dies. The end of each culture is immanent. Europe is in the throes of decadence. It has already yielded the greater part of its precious fruits. Imperialism, socialism, and so on, mark the evening twilight of Western civilization. It will still be long centuries in dying, because a century is scarcely a short span of time in the life of a culture. But already the possibilities of the West are being exhausted. Death already begins. Our culture is a culture of decadence. The great contemporary minds in art, science and philosophy are geniuses of decadence. The prophet, in intuiting destiny, knows that the end is about to be reached. The blood congeals, the forces are exhausted. In the future there will remain the “dried-up trunk without sap raising its dead branches to the sky.”

Spenglerian naturalism, like the conclusions of Meyerson to which we referred in an earlier chapter, is thus apocalyptic. The one, from the standpoint of the memorable examples of cultural destruction represented by the end of the Cretan-Mycenaean culture and that of the Magdalenian, taking into account the immense power of destruction accumulated by contemporary civilization, believes its early end possible; the other, intuiting the internal development of Western culture, thinks [p. 183] that the force of its destiny condemns it to disaster, because it is exhausting its possibilities of action and development. Both agree in a pejorative affirmation. Both fear the end.

For Lothrop Stoddard, it is not a matter of destinies nor of violent catastrophes, provoked by the unusual accumulation of means of destruction amassed by technique inspired by science; but of the degeneration produced by miscegenation, which goes on developing the multiplication of the inferior and preventing the tone of culture, which is due to the quality of the superior men. Our contemporary civilization would be explained by a progressive quantum and not by a quale that is, rather or possibly, regressive. Number, multiplying without restraint, brings with it, in Western civilization, the lack of intellectual and moral superiorities. The “decline of the West” is, then, a racial, ethnographic phenomenon. To the examination of the theses that Stoddard advances we shall devote our next reflections.

#### III. LA MORFOLOGÍA DE LA HISTORIA UNIVERSAL — III. THE MORPHOLOGY OF UNIVERSAL HISTORY

The affirmation of “the decline of the West,” obtained as the conclusion of Spengler’s book, is the fruit of a method of interpreting History, whose foundations were set forth in treating of the force of cultural destiny. The method itself and the conception imply each other within the thought of a morphology of human developments.

Let us say what Spengler understands by the “morphology” of History: “All the ways of conceiving the world can, in the last instance, be designated by the word morphology. The morphology of the mechanical, of the extended, the science that discovers and orders the natural sciences and causal nexuses, is called systematic. The morphology of the organic, of history and of life, of everything that possesses direction and destiny, is called physiognomic.”

Spengler is bent on achieving the physiognomics of humanity. How will he proceed? Of course, it is obvious that the task cannot be accomplished by a single investigator. For this reason, the prophet of the decline of the West entitles his book Outline of a Morphology of History. The physiognomics of humanity cannot be elaborated at one stroke, in a single effort, but will have to be carried out through the accumulation of innumerable investigations. Nevertheless, the fundamental outline can indeed be achieved. It is a work that is distinguished from the natural sciences in that it does not try to discover and order laws, nor relations of cause and effect (this is what is systematic); but to intuit the direction and destiny of the life of humanity. “The historian comprehends and penetrates [p. 184] men and things at a single stroke, guided by a feeling that cannot be learned, that eludes all premeditated intervention, and that enjoys the fullness of itself in exceedingly rare moments. To decompose, define, order, circumscribe effects and causes, can always be done whenever one can. It is labor. The other, by contrast, is creation” (this is the physiognomic).

Physiognomy and the symbol are linked with metaphor. Law, concept and formula are also related among themselves. The former are intuitive expression, which inserts itself into organic development without stopping it, without halting it, without dissolving it, without killing it. The latter constitute the analysis that makes of the known a rigid object, which can be measured and divided. The intellect suspends the vital in knowing. Intuition vivifies it in investigating (at this point the influence of Bergson is noted. It is the same distinction that the French philosopher establishes between rational knowledge and intuitive knowledge: the latter, in Spengler, becomes “physiognomic”).

Since each culture is an enormous living organism, and every organism is born, grows, reproduces in its development, and dies, so likewise the few great cultures elaborated by man, in his history, possess, each of them, an epoch of initiation, another of development and maturity, and another, finally, of decadence. Spring, summer, autumn and winter. When a culture has exhausted its possibilities of development, it becomes civilization. Civilization is, therefore, the decadence of culture. Then it is possible, by the intuition of destiny, to foresee, thanks to the comparison of cultures among themselves, and of their diverse great stages of unfolding, the part or epoch that has not yet been realized in history, but that will surely be fulfilled, ordained by the force of destiny. The European West has not yet finished “declining”; but, thanks to the systematic equation of the seasons of Western culture with the stages completed in the evolution of other different cultures, already past, it is possible to ascertain, in its broad outlines, the final stage of European culture.

The pre-Socratic philosophers, at the antique beginning, correspond to Galileo, Bacon, Descartes, Leibniz, in Western culture. This is “the summer” of the European world; just as the religion of Dionysus “corresponds” to Luther and Savonarola. Both epochs are equated, in Greece and in the Renaissance. The Sophists of the fifth century and Socrates “correspond,” likewise, to the English sensationalists and the French encyclopedists. This would be “the autumn,” in Greece as in Europe. Autumn shows the apotheosis of urban intelligence, the culmination of the [p. 185] spiritual efforts, faith in the omnipotence of the intellect; the cult of Nature and rational religion. And just as Plato and Aristotle create the great “final systems” of the Greek autumn (346 and 322 B.C.), Kant, Goethe and German idealism signify “the end” of the European autumn.

With Hellenism, with the Epicureans, the Skeptics and the Stoics, urban, cosmopolitan civilization begins in ancient culture; but it is because the creative force of the spirit has already been extinguished. Humanity becomes, within its cosmopolitanism, irreligious. It possesses a materialist conception of the Universe; it pays homage to the sciences and to utility. In Western culture there flourish Comte, Darwin, Spencer and Marx. It is the epoch of socialism, positivism, pessimism and anarchism. Music has Berlioz and Wagner as its gods; and as the piling up of the orders of Architecture is produced in the Roman coliseums, theaters and forums, so American architecture develops in Western culture. It is the full decadence of the West. Spengler prophesies an epoch of dictators in the evolution of the modern State. Money predominates, economic powers penetrate the political forms, and these go on taking an ever more primitive and despotic character. Civilization is essentially warlike, from Bonaparte on. Destructive wars follow one another, and imperialism and socialism, as fruits of the very cultural action that is declining, dissolve the body politic into shapeless masses. Then the fourth class rises to the foreground. It is the inorganic and cosmopolitan mass. In this way European civilization will develop, until the year 2000, and beyond; comparable, in its decline, to the society ruled by the Roman Caesars.

Therefore, in conclusion, Spengler warns that an organic fatality leads or conducts European civilization to the triumph of socialism and imperialism. For this reason the socialists, who also believe, in upholding the theses of historical materialism, in the fatality of the advent of communist society, have celebrated the book of the German prophet (notwithstanding that its author declares that “Capital” is beginning to hold no more surprises or lessons, just as “The Social Contract” of Rousseau holds none). Both, Spengler and the Bolsheviks, pay tribute not to determinism but to historical fatalism.

In this way, the force of cultural destiny explains contemporary history as the evening twilight of a great secular and organic movement... At this moment, equated with Greco-Latin decadence, Spengler’s prophecy is completed by the thought of a new Middle Ages, which Berdiaeff foresees.

#### IV. LA NÉMESIS DEL INFRAHOMBRE — IV. THE NEMESIS OF THE SUBMAN

[p. 186] “Marxism, more shattered today than ever by criticism, is ceremoniously exalted to the rank of dogma of great empire.”

Max Scheler

“Congenitally incapable of adapting himself to a higher social order, the subman inevitably becomes its enemy.”

L. Stoddard

For Lothrop Stoddard, the problem of the decline of Culture does not obey any “historical morphology,” it is not the work of an esoteric “destiny” acting mysteriously. It is a biological, racial, anthropological problem or, better said, a question of social hygiene.

It happens that the peoples who create culture are sometimes unable to sustain it in its development; for it costs as much effort to form as to conserve; the capacity to build the new matters as much as the reiterated effort of sustaining it.
