It is impossible for culture to remain unimpaired, to intensify and spread, if the race that formed it deteriorates. To create and to sustain are, in sum, the same. The sustaining of a culture is achieved only by new creations; these demand of the race the constant capacity for surpassing, endorsed by the superior quality of the spirit.

For this reason, the problem of culture is, at bottom, a biological work of conserving the superior type that elaborates it. If man multiplies in his quantitative development, but the quantum is not refined, he will never succeed in engendering what quality can attain. And race is not quantity, but pure quality; it is not number, but fitness; it is not confused and innumerable reiteration, but genius and an intrinsic property of constant surpassing.

Here is how Stoddard combats the idea of the cultural cycle, in face of Spengler: “certain thinkers invented the law of civilization and decadence. This fatalistic theory affirms that civilizations, like individuals, have their cycle of youth, maturity, decrepitude and death. But what is that cycle? There have been civilizations such as Egypt and China, which lasted thousands of years; others that lasted only centuries, others, in short, only brief generations. It is evident that a statistical curve could not be drawn; and the idea ended by discrediting itself. Naturally, theories have not been lacking. The ruin of civilizations has been attributed, variously, to luxury, to vice, to city life, to the lack of religion and to [p. 187] an infinity of things. But these theories are not satisfactory either. They may be pointed to as particular causes; but in no way can the phenomenon of the decadence of civilization be attributed to them in general.”

The Saxon, great selector of privileged races among animal species, great cultivator —culture, cultivation— thinks that the secret of history is not an oriental fatalism, but a biological question: “All this vast accumulation of ideas and materials which, heaped up and joined in marvelous continuation, go on growing harmoniously in splendor and majesty, rests upon living foundations. Upon the man and the woman who are its creators and upholders. So long as man and woman are capable of bearing its weight, the construction will grow solid and secure; but, at the moment their vital bases abandon it, the most powerful civilization will falter and will founder, at last, in chaos.”

Now, a race is not superior because all its components are superior. There has always existed a formidable disproportion between the superior and the rest of men. In glorious Athens itself “rascals, the stupid and the mad abounded, as is vividly shown in the immortal satires of Aristophanes”; but what always matters is to conserve the “élite,” the leaven of the human group.

Hence the falsity of “the law of civilization and decadence.” Hence also the nonexistence of cultural “destiny.” Civilizations, unlike living organisms, cannot possess a determinate cycle of life and death. If there is the possibility of adequately selecting superior individualities, any civilization can be immortal.

But against the persistence of the reserve of superiority three horrible Nemeses act:

1. The tendency to overload the structure of civilization.

2. The tendency to biological regression.

3. The tendency to atavistic rebellion.

The three Nemeses are the true obstacle to racial surpassing. Against them, contemporary biological science proves that the motor of evolution is not a mythological adaptation to the environment, as the Lamarckians think, but, precisely, the atavistic quality of the race. And, while the Bolsheviks deny the value of the “élites,” they go against the essence of human surpassing and exalt the subman.

Nietzsche’s superman is not admissible; but the biological, eugenic creation of new aristocracies is. The only thing that can defend [p. 188] humanity from the ravages caused by the terrible Nemeses is the eugenic ideal: “a super-race in constant process of perfecting. Not the Nietzschean superman, that brilliant and sad vision of a dominating caste, flourishing like a splendid parasitic orchid upon a trunk growing more rotten each day, of servile degradation; but a race wholly clean through the elimination of its defects, and wholly higher through the cultivation of its qualities.”

Civilization tends to decline from two causes: its own growing weight and the impoverishment of its human base. But there is another contrary cause, perhaps the most fearful of the Nemeses: “atavistic rebellion”; because, as a culture is elaborated, it leaves behind it a human multitude incapable of keeping its pace. These stragglers of civilization are the congenital barbarians. Primitives incapable of molding themselves within culture. But there also exist the degenerates, the imbeciles, the neurotics. These beings, in the state of nature, would perish (the phenomenon of degeneration is common to the human species and the other zoological species); but, in the state of social life, they are preserved and propagate.

Alongside the primitives and the degenerates, there is the numerous group of the infra-men or submen. These are “the individuals who are found below the type of capacity and adaptability imposed by the social order.” The subman is the worst enemy of culture. As he is a misfit through organic defect, he is a perennial malcontent and a perpetual dreamer. He will follow those who deny the effective discipline of the social order. He will be the rebel, the impassioned hater.

The world is today the battlefield of a struggle of titans, a struggle that has long been threatening. Now we have it upon us and it must be remedied. No country is immune. And Stoddard takes pleasure in reproducing the terrible dictum of Le Bon: “The Bolshevik mentality is as old as history. Cain, in the Old Testament, had a Bolshevik spirit. But only in our days has that old mentality allied itself with a political doctrine to justify it. And in this is shown the reason for its propagation, so rapid that it has undermined the old social scaffolding.”

In sum: Stoddard opposes to the cyclical and mystical theories of Spengler the results of biological science; and to the Marxist exaltation of a social class, the old notion of race. So that it might be said, in conclusion, that just as the theorists of social revolution rest upon the concept of the “class struggle,” the philosophers who vindicate culture are faithful adherents of “aristocratic selection.”

[p. 189] In the next chapter we shall verify how Nicolas Berdiaeff, another mystic, another prophet of genius like Spengler, synthesizes Christian thought with the very idea of aristocratic selection.

#### V. NICOLÁS BERDIAEFF — V. NICOLAS BERDIAEFF

In earlier chapters we have referred to the fearful problem of the decline of Culture. Meyerson, Spengler, Lothrop Stoddard have been, in their respective conclusions, the object of our analysis. Today we continue the investigation by studying, if only cursorily, the cultural and political thought of another prophetic mind like that of Oswald Spengler: Nicolas Berdiaeff.

Berdiaeff prophesies “a new Middle Ages.” Why? “Because we live,” he says, “in a state comparable to that of the fall of the Roman Empire and of the ancient civilization of the third century, when only Christianity spiritually saved the world from ruin and definitive decomposition.”

“In that epoch, the elements of barbarism were penetrating the failing and wavering Culture. Today, likewise, the aristocratic bases of Culture have been shaken. And a new assimilation of the barbarian element has been necessary: a genesis of light in the darkness.”

“Bolshevism cannot be liquidated with a good organization of cavalry divisions. Cavalry divisions can only increase chaos and decomposition. They sustain that abnormal and dangerous state of affairs, wherever power, which has passed into the hands of the soldiers, is constituted of nothing but external force. Thus it was that the Roman Empire perished.”

In the transcribed paragraphs, several ideas are combined that must be set forth separately: it begins by equating the state of the present world with the civilization of the third century; because, today as then, the elements of non-culture visibly penetrate into the bosom of the wavering civilization. Next it is affirmed that, in notable corroboration, today as then, the aristocratic bases of human culture are being shaken. Therefore, adds Berdiaeff, the assimilation of the barbarian element is required. But this element of non-culture (Bolshevism) cannot be vanquished by force. Military force, acting from the outside inward, will only increase chaos and decomposition. Power in the hands of soldiers is only “external force.” So Rome also perished. The remedy must be interior and not exterior. Christianity spiritually saved the world in the catastrophes of the third century, constituting an internal principle [p. 190] of moral and religious culture. Today too it constitutes in fact, according to the philosopher, the only regenerating spiritual principle: “the genesis of light in the darkness.”

We may ask ourselves: is the equation of the state of the contemporary world with decadent Roman society plausible? Many great thinkers agree to see in the characteristics of our epoch symptoms of undoubted decline. Spengler indicates that contemporary socialism corresponds, in the “synchronisms” of the development of the various Cultures, to Epicureanism, Stoicism and skepticism of classical civilization; to Buddhism in the history of Indian development. They are signal manifestations of decomposition and decadence.

Is it plausible, likewise, to deny force as a coercive element inadequate for the sustaining of civilization? Would military force be incompetent to prevail in the undertaking? Could the moral vigor of Christianity yield the fortress that “the cavalry divisions” cannot reduce?

Berdiaeff, as we see, is adverse to Fascism. He does not think that the work of the Duce is apt to resolve the social problem in contemporary culture. Nevertheless, “we live,” he says, “in an epoch of Caesarism.” On this point our philosopher coincides with Spengler. Both prophets of history augur a century of Caesars, which has scarcely begun in Europe: Caesar Stalin, Caesar Hitler, Caesar Mussolini. Who will be the next Emperor in Europe or America? Will he arise tomorrow in Paris? Perhaps in London? Perhaps in New York?, after Roosevelt.

Nor is the philosopher a pacifist: “There are epochs,” he adds, “when it is fitting to rise up against the pretension of submitting historical destinies exclusively to military force. Russia has succumbed through having transformed the people into an army. The army has destroyed the State.”

Concomitantly, interstate war becomes intrastate strife (if both terms may be permitted us). War becomes Revolution. It is no longer the struggle of nations among themselves, but the rabble against the State. Diocletian sought to halt the fall of the Empire and the complete destruction of the ancient world, deploying great energy in his attempts; but Saint Augustine, was he perhaps less active than Diocletian? And does he not occupy a more considerable place in world history? “Our epoch demands above all works like those of Saint Augustine. We need faith and the idea.”

The Diocletians of today are the Roosevelts, the Hitlers, the Mussolinis, the Stalins; where will the Saint Augustine be, that is, the reformer through the [p. 191] spirit, the Christian active through conviction and disinterestedness, who may be the moral leaven of a new human stage? They are reactionaries, adds Berdiaeff, “backward people who wish to sustain themselves on the basis of principles of modern history, and to return to the ideas of the nineteenth century. The true reform must reject three ideas of the faltering European mentality: “substanceless liberalism, individualism and juridical formalism.”

The barbarians were held back, for centuries, by the majesty of Rome; until the decomposition of the Roman aristocracy and of the Empire produced the catastrophe. Today it is not the barbarians who push toward disaster. No one invades the frontiers of Europe. The danger of cultural eclipse is internal. The idea of a catastrophe provoked from within is what comes to mind in considering the episodes of the Communist Revolution. Now the problem is not transcendent, but immanent. It does not come from outside; it is within; it coexists with the pulsations of modern economic and political life. It is incorporated in the very substance of society. It is flesh and blood of the collective organism.

For communism does not invoke justice, but inescapable necessity. Historical materialism, in its dialectical process, is not grounded upon juridical ideals, but upon economic necessities. For it, moral ideas are epiphenomena. Culture has a basis that is of a different order from that invoked by “utopian socialism.” The Russian regime has been arrived at by an organic reason, by virtue of a dialectical development, in accordance with the intrinsic logic of history.

And in the face of the contemporary catastrophe, Berdiaeff vindicates two essential principles: one moral, Christianity; and the other political: the aristocratic bases of human culture.

All the errors of socialism, which calls itself “scientific,” stem from the negation of God and man. Marx believes that the good is to be realized by violence, that is, by evil. This is what the “dialectic” of the system implies. And this logic is combined with the messianic idea, with the prophecy of the “final triumph.” “In this way we see how the Five-Year Plan which, objectively considered, is not socialism but State capitalism, is carried out in an atmosphere of religious effusion.” Hence the communist adoration of technique of capitalist origin. Hence the attempt to subject Science to Technique and the University to the Workshop.

What Marxist socialism cannot resolve, being the unilateral doctrine that it is, and what Christianity can indeed construct, is the synthesis of personality and society. Messianic communism sacrifices personality to society, the individual to the mass, aristocracy [p. 192] to democracy. Outside the Christian spirit, aristocracies persecute and oppress democracies, as in ancient societies; or else, as in Bolshevism, the human personality is eclipsed before the omnipotence of the soviet.

But, according to true morality, in the Christian’s conception, there is a ground of undoubted justice in the communist claims against absurd liberalism. “Be that as it may, the future belongs to the workers, to the working masses; this is an inescapable and just fact. Everything depends on ascertaining which principle will found the new life.”

The human individual does not fit within the limits of society and of the fatherland; beyond humanity lies his infinite aspiration. All the values of Culture, which each human personality integrates in the measure of its possibilities, are integrated in God essentially. For God is the highest truth, the highest beauty, the highest goodness, the highest justice. Hence human societies must always respect individual values, and above collectivism and individualism each personal consciousness aspires to something absolute, infinite, eternal...

At all events, “the Russian people, with its Revolution, has clearly posed the problem to all peoples.”

Nicolas Berdiaeff, like Tolstoy, like Soloviev, like Dostoevsky, is a Christian mystic, in whom philosophy is integrated with the religious and secular tradition of his race!

#### VI. RENAN Y BERDIAEFF — VI. RENAN AND BERDIAEFF

“Le socialisme peut amener, par la complicité du catholicisme, un nouveau Moyen Âge.” [Socialism, through the complicity of Catholicism, can bring about a new Middle Ages.]

Renan

“All the signs, all the proofs attest that we have come out of a mute era to enter a nocturnal era. It is the thought of another world that is beginning, which might be a new Middle Ages.”

It is obvious that, if the history of ideas is undertaken without passion or prejudice, with the sole aim of finding the truth, one always finds an antecedent of the thought that seems newest, of the theory that, at first sight, would be said to lack history. Pascal already thought that Humanity is like a single man who constantly instructs and improves himself. Few of the books published in our days will have [p. 193] stirred universal public opinion like A New Middle Ages, by Berdiaeff. In its pages the religious spirit is opposed to the work of Russian communism.

A New Middle Ages has its antecedent, however, in the final chapter of Renan’s celebrated book History of the People of Israel. That chapter, the sixteenth of book X, is entitled: “Finito libro, sit laus et gloria Christo.” [The book being finished, let praise and glory be to Christ.] In it the celebrated French ideologue develops the thought that regards Christianity as the culmination, end, or final cause of Judaism: “Once Christianity was produced, Judaism continues, still; but as a dried-up trunk beside a single fruitful branch. Henceforth, life departed from it. Its history, though still very interesting, takes on only secondary importance, from the general point of view. Christianity is the masterpiece of Judaism, its glory, the summary of its evolution.”

Judea and the Greco-Roman world were like two universes revolving side by side, under the action of opposing influences. “Until 1848, socialism dug its mine galleries beneath a soil whose surface showed no tremor whatever. It basked in the sun, without sensing the work being carried out in its entrails.”

But in the Revolution of 1848 the work of vindication came to light. From then on, the problem of the just distribution of social wealth was named “the social problem,” par excellence. And all the institutions of Culture suffered, unquestionably, the formidable and unique action of that tremendous subterranean labor. Like all events that are prepared slowly in the shadows, the rise of socialism, on coming to light, was already ripe and unimpeded, ready to develop in unsuspected forms, modifying, by virtue of its powerful action, the structure of human society; at least, the traditional forms of European social life. The work of the social vindicators has been, since the middle of the nineteenth century, the great human problem, the constant concern of philosophers, moralists and sociologists.

“The immediate future is dark. It is not true that it is favorable to the work of light. Credulity has deep roots. Socialism, through its complicity with Catholicism, may bring about a new Middle Ages, with its barbarians, its Churches, its eclipses of individuality and of liberty, of Civilization, in a word”...

In this astonishing paragraph, the illustrious French thinker seems to have foreseen the extraordinary content of Berdiaeff’s book; not only its content, but also the very title and spirit of contemporary production.

Renan fears that the Jewish work may overshadow the Greco-Roman work; that it may [p. 194] overshadow it, but not that it may destroy it. What he fears is an eclipse, but not the extinction of the classical genius. “The Greek work, that is, Science, rational, experimental Civilization, without charlatanism, without revelation, founded upon reason and liberty, will continue without end.” In sum, Renan is anguished by the nearness of a new Middle Ages... Here are Berdiaeff’s words: “The spiritual center, in an imminent future, will be, as in the Middle Ages, solely the Church. For today the life of the Church develops along paths that are not official, but invisible from the outside. The frontiers of the Church do not appear clearly: they cannot be pointed out with a finger like a natural object. The life of the Church is mysterious and its paths are not comprehensible to reason. The spirit blows where it will... The new Middle Ages will triumph over the atomism of modern history. This atomism is overcome, falsely, by communism; truly, by the Church and the ecumenical spirit.”

“All the social utopias of the nineteenth century could only be elaborated in a Christian environment; for all of them exhibit a transposition to the European social milieu of the notion of the Kingdom of God. The very Marxist idea of the messianism of the proletariat has a religious origin, even if it is more Jewish than Christian. Greco-Roman civilization, aristocratic by principle, despised labor and considered it the patrimony of slaves.”

Could a fuller confirmation of Renan’s profound phrase be given? The immediate future is dark. It is not true that it is favorable to the work of light. Credulity has deep roots. Socialism, through its complicity with Catholicism, may bring about a new Middle Ages, with its barbarians, its Churches, its eclipses of individuality and of liberty, of civilization, in a word.

For Berdiaeff, the new Middle Ages will be despotic, not democratic. “From today on, the working masses and the popular strata play a great role in the destinies of government. All the politics of the future will have to reckon with this, seeking the means that may allow us to limit the dangers which the power of the multitudes brings upon qualitative culture.”

This is what moves Renan: the synthesis of the Jewish spirit and the proletarian vindication, because Israel “was the first to give a form to the cry of the people, to the complaints of the poor, to the obstinate demand of those who thirst for justice.” In this way, Judaism and Christianity signify, for Antiquity, what socialism signifies for modern times.

Berdiaeff’s book condemns the work of the European men of the Renaissance as a deviation from the organic sense of Western culture. Everything that [p. 195] the Modern Age has engendered results from the Renaissance: the Reformation, the French Revolution, individualism, irreligion, the positivism of the nineteenth century, socialism and anarchism! Everything came, in the end, from the “decomposition” of the Renaissance.

The complicity of the two internationals, the Catholic and the Bolshevik, denying individual values in order to subject intelligences to untenable dogmas, is what, according to Renan, will imperil the foundations of European culture. Dogmatism in thought is fanaticism in feeling and tyranny in action. That which cannot be discussed, that which must be adored and that which is imposed on us to do, without freedom of criticism, is what will engender the new Middle Ages!

The President of the United States, Mr. Franklin D. Roosevelt, on being named Doctor of Laws by Temple University, has just uttered these noble words, which indicate that the medieval era, for the great neighboring nation, has not yet begun: No group, no government, can prescribe, precisely, in what knowledge should consist. Truth is found only when men have freedom to seek it. Glorious Nation and distinguished Chief Executive, who take the side of the human spirit against the white and red fanaticisms that besiege us! On this model should be traced the respectful attitude toward thought which the fanatics of both camps seek to wreck in Mexico!

Disdain for humanism may turn into the negation of Humanity; for it already signifies the abomination of Culture. The freedom of individual conscience, the right to the autonomy of thought, will have been abolished... But it will not be forever!

Individualism and liberty are Culture itself; the new Middle Ages will pass, will be only an episode in the course of the centuries; and the eternal spirit of Greece “will continue without end.”

Be that as it may, Renan and Berdiaeff agree on one point: the imminence of another Middle Ages. For those of us who witness the twilight of the Modern Age, and were educated in the forms of the European tradition that today declare themselves failing, no problem can stir us more. Heirs of a vanishing century, full of curiosity and fear, we witness the renewal of the world. One would say that we have reached the apocalypse of the ages. Who will conquer in the end, the Greco-Latin genius or the spirit of Israel?

[p. 196] “In this sense the theorists of Culture are right when they affirm that Western culture is at present in the evolutionary (advanced) phase of civilization: under the sign of Americanism, with the consequent predominance of economy and technique, and the relegation to a second plane of the ancient primordial factors of Culture: humanity and instruction.”

W. Sauer

The field of scientific investigation is divided into two distinct realms: Nature and Culture. On the one hand, the sciences of nature; on the other, the sciences of moral life, reflected in History. As Rickert rightly maintains, the designation “sciences of the spirit” is a most deficient characterization of the particular non-naturalistic disciplines. Recently, the term “cultural science” has come into ever more frequent use.

What is nature? Natural products spring spontaneously from the earth; the products of culture presuppose some value, which human intelligence recognizes. Hence that most ancient form of human culture called agriculture, that is: the cultivation of the natural fruits of the earth, adapted to human ends, that is, to “values.”

The natural is a process not thought of as good. If, then, the notion of good or of value is removed, everything reduces to the spontaneity of nature. Culture is opposed, logically, to Nature. The sciences differ radically in their object, according to whether they devote themselves to the study of natural phenomena or to the investigation of moral, historical facts, unintelligible without the notion of good, of value. And Rickert enumerates, among the great syntheses of moral phenomena, these constant fruits of human coexistence: religion, law, economy, the State, customs, science, literature and art.

Now, the great majority of philosophers see in Culture the meaning of Life. Only some thinkers, who form the group of the naturalist philosophers, would subordinate Culture to Life. The problem is disquieting and profound. It can be formulated in these terms: for man, who constantly develops through time and space, life seems to be the ultimate end. Must not, therefore, everything that the unanimous effort of the generations does and attains be subordinated to life itself?

[p. 197] No, because that would be as much as declaring that being is something valuable, and so valuable that it surpasses ought-to-be. If, on the other hand, being is worth something only insofar as it is subordinated and submitted to the ideal, life itself acquires meaning by virtue of this subordination. What is valuable is not life, but noble, cultivated, holy, superior life.

Philosophical naturalism, animated by an inconsistent vitalism which sets itself up as the end of the world, counsels, in the work of Rousseau, of Tolstoy, of Nietzsche, that “return to nature” which lacks meaning.

What happens is that, at times, the stage of cultured life is so distilled, so corrupted and prostituted, that men clamor for a return to a greater simplicity, to a purer style, to an easier organization, to a more wholesome coexistence.

But it is obvious that this apology for nature constitutes, consciously, an apology for culture. What is detested, partly with justification, is a heavy and murky cultural form or stage, clumsy and confused; and then what is expressed by the phrase “return to nature” is not precisely such a return, but a more rational, purer, more even-tempered form of life, from which unnecessary complication and sterile bustle would be expelled.

This is what Rousseau maintained in his “Discourse on the Sciences and the Arts.” Voltaire, after reading the allegations of his illustrious rival, recognized, in part, their justification and, in part, rejected the excessive and intrepid spirit of the naturalist philosopher, writing to him, as a comment on his celebrated “Discourse”: “after reading you, sir, one feels a lively desire to go about on all fours.” Life for culture is action for the purpose and the longing, movement toward the ideal; the attitude that fills itself with value. Culture for life is human invention serving crime, war, plunder, or simply versatility and inconstancy. If nothing is higher than life, nothing exists superior to desire and passion. If life is the supreme value, one would never have to deny it. And heroism and holiness are condemned in advance. Values truly loftier than mere living, supreme ideals, before which nature must bow without remedy or appeal, in order to fulfill man completely; to crystallize in an act, in an existence, the formless sketch that each individual is!

But the same value has not always come to the foreground of culture. Antiquity placed in the first rank, above all Classical Antiquity, animated by Greece, the aesthetic value. The morality of Aristotle, supreme compendium of Hellenic customs, places in the first [p. 198] rank Beauty, “the golden mean.” Beautiful truth, generosity, magnanimity. All that civilization rests upon the beautiful ideal. Geometry, art, science, morality, everything obeys the sublime canon of static order, and is interpenetrated with it, with lofty disinterestedness, with a generous spirit of surpassing and equilibrium. The perfect is the ideal pursued, the harmonious is the circle of national action. The “Ancient City” has the charm of the sculptures of Phidias, the theorems of Euclid and the dialogues of Plato.

Christian civilization, derived in part from the ancient, breaks the circle of classical elegance and proportion, to give us an interiority of higher life. The moral value leaps to the foreground. It is a culture eminently ethical and religious: “You shall love God above all things and your neighbor as yourself.” And for the “Ancient City” there is substituted, by the greatest philosopher of Christianity, the “City of God”: “Two loves built two cities: love of self even to contempt of God, the earthly City; and love of God even to contempt of self, the heavenly City. The one glories in itself; the other in the Lord. The first lavishes its glory upon men, the second places its best glory in God, witness of its conscience.”

Contemporary civilization inclines to place technique and money above all values. Never did both hold such sway. But if industries proceed from science and money is only an instrument of exchange and of power, such disordered love of the extrinsic must put an end to the civilizing movement. The “will to power,” as Nietzsche called it, is the old diabolical spirit of domination; and a culture that subordinates itself to money and technique is a diabolical culture, which almost accomplishes the apotheosis of Life without scruples, complicating it needlessly and fatally. Hence the wish to “return to Nature.”

Such a return is impossible. The immense cycle cannot be closed; and in this universal exercise, in this reiterated longing for technical and practical surpassing, the meaning of the greatest cultural values may be lost, whose apotheosis Aesthetic Paganism and Moral Christianity undertook. Hence the decline of Culture is declared in various tones; hence the beginning of the end is seen in certain aspects of contemporary history. The Apocalypse will come from the absurd spirit of domination; and, if the modern world is to be saved, it will be only by instructing and edifying itself anew in the pages of Plato’s Symposium.

Beside the Symposium, the Supper; beside Plato, the Gospel. After Phaedrus, in that sublime Dialogue, discourses on the effects of Love, [p. 199] and Pausanias on its various kinds, and Aristophanes on its origin, and Agathon and Socrates on its subtle essence, declaring that “Love is a mean between good and evil, the beautiful and the ugly,” and that the proper object of Love is Beauty, Christ appears to us at the Supper teaching another kind of love, which paganism did not know: Charity, that is, “love of neighbor as of oneself, and love of God above all things.” Faith, Hope, Charity; but, as Saint Paul says, the greatest of the three virtues is the last.

We are, as Nietzsche affirms, “human, all too human.” Nevertheless, the high values of History must be confessed by the centuries or the collective constructions will decay and will perhaps be annihilated in an evening twilight of abomination. Means are not ends. Technique and gold take on meaning only through Truth, Justice, Beauty and Holiness. This is what the philosophical consideration of the various historical cultures shows.

## APÉNDICE — APPENDIX

### TESIS — THESIS

[p. 203] PRESENTED IN THE OPEN COMPETITION TO FILL THE POST OF ADJUNCT PROFESSOR OF GENERAL HISTORY, AT THE NATIONAL PREPARATORY SCHOOL, BY ANTONIO CASO.

Mexico, April 19, 1906

CONTENTS

[p. 205] 1. THE KAMO-SEMITIC CIVILIZATION

a. Origin b. Development

2. THE ARYANS

a. The Indo-European languages and the Aryan people b. Civilization of the primitive Aryans

3. THE IRANIANS. THEIR HISTORY

a. Iran b. The Medes c. The Median Empire: Deioces, Phraortes, Cyaxares d. Persia and its inhabitants e. Cyrus and the founding of the Empire f. Cambyses and the conquest of Egypt g. Darius. The bas-relief of Behistun h. Extent of the Persian Empire in the time of Darius i. The expedition to Europe j. Death of Darius

4. THE PERSIAN CIVILIZATION

a. Religion b. Political and social organization c. Plastic arts

5. CONCLUSION

#### INTRODUCCIÓN — INTRODUCTION

[p. 207] The present study has been written in an effort to draw out of the ancient history of Persia the general principles, the “guiding ideas” that channeled and sustained the sum of minute details, magnificently set down in other works such as those of Maspero and Lenormant, but which could not fit into a simple essay, conceived rather in an educational sense than in an archaeological direction.

What was it that made up the historical legacy inherited by the Persians? How did that great people increase the said legacy? In what manner were the attributes of the earlier civilizations modified in passing through the soul of the Iranian nations? In what does the Persians’ bequest to later civilization consist? Here, enclosed in these four questions, is the design that presided over the conception of this work.

In proceeding thus, the undersigned believes he has complied with the prescriptions of the law.

A truth demonstrated by experience is that civilization shifts its seat, and that the most illustrious peoples and the most advanced societies succumb in the end like every organism, leaving to other societies and other peoples the treasure of their conquests. At the same time that these successive displacements occur, new elements come to complicate social development and bring new causes of evolution.

The Iranians (Medes and Persians) appear on the field of history as one of those elements that render the development of civilization more complex. When they invade the Orient, they represent a new ethnic layer that combines with the races that previously peopled the country, grafting onto the trunk of the Kamo-Semitic elaboration a new germ of physical and moral vigor, which, mingling with the earlier components, drives progress forward, grouping human efforts in a higher synthesis, more heterogeneous and more luminous.

#### I. LA CIVILIZACIÓN KAMO-SEMÍTICA — I. THE KAMO-SEMITIC CIVILIZATION

a. Origin

When the ancient world was still a victim of the atrocities of primordial savagery, when only a few peoples had managed [p. 208] to accomplish the first stages of barbarism, when neither the Chinese center of culture in the valley of the Hwang-ho nor that of Aryan civilization in the basin of the Oxus had yet been born, already in the land of the Nile and in that of the Euphrates two powerful nuclei of progress were winning separate conquests that were to be transformed, in the course of time, into the ample foundations of general evolution.

The two beacons of the most distant times were the Egyptian and the Chaldean; the first radiated from the Nile, the second from the Euphrates. While nomadism and its social corollaries were the characteristics of the scattered multitudes, while rudimentary political organization, embodied in the republican tribe (Letourneau), constituted the most advanced stage of the evolution of government in the better part of human groups, while initial fetishism reigned in the consciences of the enormous majority of the planet’s inhabitants, the two most ancient civilizations of the Orient already formed great barbarian monarchies, strongly centralized, admirable political organisms, higher on the scale of social perfection than the similar organizations which, in the new continent, and in comparatively recent times, the people of the Mexicas and that of the Peruvians fostered. Religion had advanced in Egypt to the point that at Memphis they already adored the god “creator and benefactor who knows all and exists from the beginning,” and in Chaldea the Sumerians and the Accadians of Ur and of Bal-ilá knew the agricultural art so perfectly that under the serene and diaphanous sky of the country between the two rivers (Euphrates and Tigris) the most opulent harvests in the world were reaped. With reason, and without hyperbole, the erudite historiographer Frédéric Loliée has said that, “at the origin of time, Egypt and Chaldea appear alone, like planets gravitating in space amid the universal darkness.”

b. Development

In the course of the centuries, Chaldeans and Egyptians developed their respective civilizations and reflected them in the spirit of the neighboring Semitic groups, who modeled their most interesting subcultures on the two primitive foci, engendering the sublime religious formation of Israel and the ductile mercantile civilization of the Phoenicians.

There were two principal causes why, before the Persian hegemony, a nearly general type of progress was created in Asia Minor: first, the overland commerce of the Phoenicians and, second, the conquests or raids of the Assyrians in the time of the Sargonid emperors. War and commerce, the two perennial vehicles of advancement, engendered then (as always) that exchange of ideas, traditions and customs which draws men together and which effaces, on the [p. 209] battlefield or in peaceful transaction, the differences and the age-old hatreds imposed by race or by the exigencies of the environment.

“The Phoenicians [says a historian] went to Arabia, whence they returned with gold, agate, onyx, incense, myrrh, pearls, ivory, ebony, ostrich feathers and monkeys from India, or took the route of Assyria in search of cotton and linen cloths; or that of the Black Sea in pursuit of horses and slaves, or of copper vessels chased by the mountaineers of the Caucasus.”

Assyrian and Egyptian industry and merchandise went, together with the idols and the arts of these peoples, to the most remote regions, and, in undertaking their pilgrimage led by the Phoenician caravans, they brought closer the variegated nations of the classical Orient.

The other factor of the progressive similarity was, as we have said, war, above all the wars of the Assyrians, and among these, those undertaken by the most warlike descendants of Sargon, Sennacherib, Esarhaddon, Ashurbanipal, stirred the Asiatic world; thus one of these Emperors boasts of his exploits: “I passed like a devastating hurricane. The harnesses and the arms floated on the earth in the blood of the enemies, as on a river.” “I heaped up as trophies the corpses of their soldiers and cut off their extremities.” “Those I took alive I mutilated and as punishment deprived of their hands.” The empire of Nineveh then reached the summit of its grandeur (under Ashurbanipal), extending from Iran to the Aegean and from the Caspian to the sea of the Indies... Colossal power, which very soon dissolved like smoke, but which, in spreading like a “devastating hurricane,” scattered Chaldean thought and art throughout the reaches of Asia! Precisely at that time the Iranians announce themselves on the field of history.

#### II. LOS ARYAS — II. THE ARYANS

a. The Indo-European languages and the Aryan people

The scientific theory that affirms the existence of the Aryan people as the prolific germ of the ancient and modern Japhetic races is one of the most beautiful monuments of philology.

The comparative study of languages has inspired scholars with the notion of the existence of such a people, a notion that we should surely not possess through history, nor through tradition, nor through the analysis of ethnographic attributes, which have been profoundly modified by the influences of the various climates and the crossings that the Indo-European group has undergone since its dispersion.

The analogies of grammatical turns and the community of roots among Sanskrit, Avestan, Greek, Latin, German, English, Russian and the [p. 210] Romance languages, analogies and community difficult to perceive amid the innumerable set of qualities that characterize those tongues, nevertheless exist. Many Sanskrit radical words agree with the Greco-Latin and Germanic ones, as, of course, with the derivations that the Romance languages have made from Latin. Moreover, the divergences between the Aryan roots and those of the other languages (Hamitic or Semitic) are enormous, so that a defined group of sister languages is marked out, proceeding from a common center. From such inductions the linguists have derived the idea that Indians, Persians, Greeks, Latins, Celts, Germans and Slavs once spoke the same language, thereby forming a single people.

These arguments are confirmed by some historical data: the Indo-Iranians recall in their sacred hymns their Aryan provenance. The Medes (says Maspero) vaguely preserved the memory of the time when, united with other nations of the same ethnic stock, they wandered through Airyanem-vaêjo (—the land of the Aryans—) and, even though it is true that the Germans, the Latins, the Slavs and the Greeks forgot their origin (Seignobos), such a reason cannot be deemed contrary to the thesis that admits the real existence of the Aryan people, since these four peoples are those that settled farthest, and at a later time, from the place where the split occurred.

In the moral, the religious, the philosophical, the social order, Indians, Persians, Greeks, Romans and Germans present features peculiar to their original identity.

The speculations of Renan (in The Future of Science) on the ineptitude of the Indo-Europeans for constituting monotheistic religions; the rapid evolution of the sentiments of honor and duty (altruistic sentiments) among the Persians, Greeks, Romans and Germans (the morality of the Ramayana, of the Zend-Avesta, Socratic philosophy, Stoicism); the great philosophical faculty made manifest by the Buddhas, the Platos, the Zarathustras and the Kants; the aesthetic sentiment aroused by the contemplation of nature that shines in the great works of the Indo-European literatures; the social and political institutions common to all these peoples (Letourneau): all increase, in the mental order, the probability of the hypothesis that affirms the remote existence of the great initial nucleus: the Aryan people.

b. Civilization of the primitive Aryans

“When the Aryans came down through the gorges of the Kabul to settle in the Punjab, they resembled in many respects the Persians as Herodotus describes them and the Germans as Tacitus paints them.” “They were tribes that lived on the borders of sedentary life and nomadism, whose principal wealth consisted in great herds of cattle...” “Each tribe was guided by a kind of king or chief of arms”... “They had [p. 211] no castes or clerical corporations; each father of a family was a sacrificing priest within his own house. Their customs were wholesome, simple and free”... “They did not suffer mystical or morbid dreams; on the contrary, their sentiments were manly and honest, and they addressed their gods in their prayers to ask of them strength, glory, triumph and booty” (Taine, Nuevos ensayos de Crítica y de Historia).

When one thinks that from that warlike and nomadic people, organized patriarchally, there have sprung, in the course of time, the most complex of civilizations (the modern) and the Indian, Persian and Greco-Latin cultures, one cannot help being surprised at the ever-growing heterogeneity of progress. Facts of the present category, at every instant recorded by history, prove that the most penetrating intelligence is incompetent to foresee the innumerable consequences of the most complicated social phenomena, in which physical, biological and psychological causes are summed and linked in admirable marriage.

#### III. LOS IRANITAS — III. THE IRANIANS

a. Iran

“Between the Tigris and the Indus, the Caspian Sea and the Persian Gulf, there rises the region of Iran, which embraces an extensive territory, in part sterile. It is composed of burning deserts of sand and frozen plateaus crossed by deep and mountainous valleys. The climate is harsh and uneven, hot in summer and glacial in winter; at some points one passes from two degrees above zero to forty below, from the heat of Senegal to the cold of Siberia. Impetuous winds reign there, cutting like swords, but in the valleys along the rivers the land is fertile.” “From that land of fruits and of orchards come the peach and the cherry” (Seignobos).

b. The Medes

Iran was inhabited by various tribes, two of which have been famous: the Medes and the Persians. The Medes, tributaries of Nineveh since Sargon, were the first to become civilized, but also the ones who most quickly lost their virile and warlike attributes, becoming effeminate through contact with the Orient and ending by wearing ample flowing tunics instead of the body-fitting skins which they had at first used to shield themselves from the cold of their country, and by accepting the religious practices of the degenerate Assyrians and the idle and soft life of corrupted peoples.

[p. 212] c. The Median Empire

Deioces. The legend, related by Herodotus, has “embroidered the theme” of the history of the Medes, presenting in Deioces (builder of Agbatana) the type of a just and wise monarch. Highly esteemed in his tribe, Deioces administered justice with such discretion that the inhabitants of his district chose him as chief. Little by little his fame invaded the other tribes and the Medes came to his tribunal in search of justice. Later the venerable magistrate renounced his supreme office, and banditry and anarchy fell upon Media. Then (says Maspero) the Medes assembled, perhaps to obtain a resolution in favor of public affairs, and the friends of Deioces spoke in this way: “This life is intolerable to us and we shall have to abandon the country unless we choose a king who will keep the peace among us, for then, governed by equitable laws, we shall be able to cultivate our fields without anxiety, without fear that violence and injustice will drive us out of them.” Deioces was chosen king and the Medes, obeying the orders of their monarch, built the seven-times-walled City of Agbatana. He died after having governed fifty-three years, and was succeeded by his son Phraortes.

There are three historical truths that the legend contains, namely: the real existence of Deioces (the Diyaukku of the Assyrian texts); the creation of a Median principality; and the founding of Agbatana, a capital which centralized some of the scattered warlike tribes.

Phraortes. Phraortes the conqueror succeeded Deioces the peaceful. He attacked the Persians and subjugated them. Once master of both nations, he subdued other peoples of Asia, and from conquest to conquest launched himself against the Assyrians of Nineveh. The Ninevites triumphed over the Medes and the king fell mortally wounded together with the greater part of his army, after reigning for twenty-two years.

Cyaxares. Cyaxares, son of Phraortes, continued his father’s warlike policy; but before taking the offensive, he organized his army, classifying the contingents of the tribes into various military orders, which gave consistency and power to the hordes that Phraortes had led.

He undertook an attack on Nineveh to avenge his father’s death; then he repelled the invasion of the Scythians, driving them out of Asia, and, united with Nabopolassar, lieutenant of Asuredililani, destroyed Nineveh, “the City full of frauds and of iniquities,” the “City full of rapine and of blood,” according to the tremendous imprecations of the prophet Nahum.

After the fall of Nineveh, Cyaxares subjugated northern Mesopotamia and then engaged in a long struggle against the Lydians. He died leaving the throne to his son Astyages and an empire that extended from the Halys to the great desert of Iran. The warlike civilization of the Assyrians collapsed [p. 213] a victim of the combined efforts of the Babylonian and the Mede. Nabopolassar respected the god Ashur and the goddess Ishtar (Babylon was after all an offshoot of Nineveh), but the Mede razed the sanctuaries, destroyed the sacred books and set out to annihilate the immense empire. This victory is symbolic; the world henceforth will be ruled by the race of Cyaxares, the best Kamo-Semitic civilization will be refined in the vaster mind of the Indo-European, favorite son of intelligence and of light.

d. Persia and its inhabitants

Persia is situated to the southwest of the Iranian plateau and extends between the Persian Gulf and Media. It is burning and sandy in the maritime region, cold and sterile in the mountainous one; in the center of the country fertile valleys are to be admired, such as that of Shiraz.

If Herodotus is to be believed, the Persians comprised ten autonomous tribes confederated under the presidency of the Pasargadae, whose principal family was called that of the Achaemenids. The country was poor and mountainous, but the tribes (independent until Phraortes, as we have already stated) preserved indelibly their amiable beliefs and the roughness and simplicity of their customs. Until the age of ten, says Herodotus, the Persians teach their sons only three things: to ride a horse, to shoot the bow and to tell the truth.

e. Cyrus and the Founding of the Persian Empire

The childhood of Cyrus and the causes that impelled him to rouse his people against the Medes are enveloped in the penumbras of myth. The illustrious Greek historian so often cited is the delightful storyteller who instructs us about that fable. Rather than relate it, it seems to us fitting to summarize briefly what modern science has clarified regarding so powerful and magnanimous a prince.

Kurus, or Cyrus, belonged to the royal dynasty of the Achaemenids, and before launching himself into war he restored the cult of Zarathustra in all its purity. Aided by Tigranes, king of Armenia, he defeated Astyages (thanks to the treachery of the Mede Harpagus) and all of Media accepted his dominion (549 B.C.). Croesus, king of Lydia, concerned at the disaster suffered by Astyages, formed a powerful coalition against the Persian, of which the Babylonians, Egyptians and Lacedaemonians were members; but having entered the fray before the reinforcements of the allies could arrive, he was routed at Thymbra and captured at Sardis, the magnificent capital of the Lydian kingdom. All the states of Croesus and the Greek colonies on the shores of the Aegean fell into the power of Cyrus.

Then the conqueror set out for the west and achieved such successes that Herodotus sums up his work in these eloquent and picturesque terms: “He brought all of upper Asia under his hand, without excepting a single region.”

[p. 214] Now that only Babylon remains standing, face to face with the Persian empire, the king advances to conquer it; but since it was impossible for him to force its walls, he resorts to cunning to achieve his ends; and, when all the inhabitants were given over to the revels and rejoicings of a great festival, the Persians seized the wealthy city, in the year 538 B.C.

All the provinces yielded, Phoenicia included, and the Hebrews greeted their liberator with enthusiasm, returning to their ancient homes after the long and famous captivity of Babylon.

Nothing certain is known concerning the last years of Cyrus. The Persian empire, at the disappearance of its founder, had as its limits: on the west, the Aegean, the Mediterranean and the Arabian Gulf; on the east, the Indus and Mount Emaus, the desert of Arabia and the Erythraean Sea; on the south and north, the Pontus Euxinus, the Caucasus, the Caspian and the Jaxartes.

Babylon, the last and splendid bulwark of ancient domination, fell into the hands of Cyrus, as bloody Nineveh had fallen into the power of Cyaxares. Decisively, the ancient world was yielding before the victorious might of the Aryans.

#### f. Cambises y la Conquista de Egipto — f. Cambyses and the Conquest of Egypt

Cyrus bequeathed the empire to his firstborn Cambyses (Cambusio), leaving to Smerdis (Bardiya), his second son, the government of some provinces. Cambyses, symbol of conquering wickedness, killed his brother to inaugurate his reign, and employed so much secrecy in doing so that the court and the people imagined he had shut him up in a castle. Soon afterward he marched on Egypt, which surrendered to him in a single great battle (525), and he terrified the nation of the pharaohs with his impieties and his follies.

A rebellion of the magi, who during his absence placed Gaumata on the throne (saying that he was Smerdis, whom he did in fact resemble), summoned him back to Persia; but once the imposture was elucidated, seven Persian nobles killed Gaumata and the magi who supported him, and Darius, of the noble Achaemenid line, was elected “great king.”

#### g. Darío. Bajo relieve de Behistún — g. Darius. The Bas-relief of Behistun

Darius recounts these events in the bas-relief of Behistun, “an enormous rock 450 meters high, worked with the pick.” So says the inscription in three different languages. It is Darius who speaks; here is what he says before being king: “Cambyses, son of Cyrus, reigned here before I reigned.” “Cambyses had a brother Smerdis, of the same father and the same mother.” “When Cambyses killed Smerdis, the people did not know of the deed.” “Afterward he went to Egypt and, while he was there, the people became rebellious.” “Then lying was frequent in the country, in Media and in the [p. 215] remaining provinces.” “There was a magus, Gaumata, who deceived the people by telling them: ‘I am Smerdis, son of Cyrus.’” “The people rose and gathered around him, abandoning Cambyses”... “He died wounding himself.” “When Gaumata, the magus, had taken Persia, Media and the other countries from Cambyses, he did in them his own will, he was king.” “The people feared him for his cruelty, he would have been capable of making an end of the people, so that it might not learn that Smerdis was not the son of Cyrus.” “Darius, the king, declares it, there was not in Persia or in Media a man who would have dared to snatch the crown from that Gaumata, the magus.” “Then I dared.” “I asked protection of Ahuramazda, and Ahuramazda granted it to me.” “With the company of a few men devoted to my person I killed Gaumata and his accomplices and I became king by the protection of Ahuramazda.” “Thus I restored the empire that had been taken from our race.” Afterward Darius, “the conqueror of nine kings,” the illustrious man of the younger branch of the Achaemenids, enlarged the empire by conquering Thrace and carrying his victorious arms as far as the land of the Indus.

#### h. Extensión del Imperio Persa — h. Extent of the Persian Empire

Under Darius, the immense agglomeration produced by the successive conquests of Cyrus, Cambyses and Darius brought together in its bosom all the inhabitants of the East: Medes, Persians, Assyrians, Chaldeans, Egyptians, Phoenicians, Jews, Syrians, Lydians and Indians, scattered over all the countries, from the Danube to the Indus and from the Caspian to the cataracts of the Nile. All the traditions and all the languages, all the religions and all the customs fitted within so enormous a territory. There rose the classical cities of antiquity, Jerusalem and Tyre, Babylon, Memphis and Thebes. There was compendiously gathered all that was most glorious that man had created up to that epoch, and Darius was hailed as a universal monarch. Never had the East seen so overwhelming a power; never could it have been suspected that the humble, disorganized tribes of the age before Deioces could have raised themselves to be mistresses of the world; nor, likewise, that the high-born Achaemenids who preceded Cyrus would on some occasion wear the richest and most prestigious of all diadems.

#### i. Darío emprende su expedición a Europa — i. Darius Undertakes His Expedition to Europe

Who would be capable of checking the boldness of so powerful a majesty? What empire, however vast, could halt the advance of the victorious Persians? Before what nation would that Darius, king of kings, bow his neck? ... It was not an empire that stopped that empire, it was not a despot who defeated that despot. There is something more powerful than force, something more resistant than many armies, something more immaterial and more sublime: the consciousness of freedom and the fullness of right.

[p. 216] A handful of free men, a small number of citizens, are worth more than many millions of slaves and subjects. A Greece in love with its independence and possessed of its virtues, an Athens that organizes victory, a general who was called Miltiades, that is what suffices to confront the colossus. Greece is young and energetic, she breathes with ample lungs the clement and serene atmosphere of the peoples whom a propitious future kisses. She is not on the road to dissolution like the Ninevites whom Cyaxares annihilated, or the Babylonians whom Kurus undid. The Hellenes, our august fathers, the pagan fathers of modern civilization, have longing and firmness in abundance. Against the broad and muscular breasts of the Greek hoplites oriental barbarism will dash itself, as the restless mass of water of the torrent dashes itself against the unshakable resistance of the rocks. With reason someone has thought that the epic of Marathon and Salamis is the culminating event of History.

After an expedition against the Scythians to prevent their formidable depredations (an expedition that turned out to be a disaster), Darius, fascinated by the Mediterranean, that “hypnotic sea” for all Asiatic warriors, hurled himself against the Greeks. Let us set out in detail the Hellenic-Persian struggle as far as the battle of Marathon.

Cyrus (a historian teaches) founded the Persian state, Cambyses compromised it in his mad adventures, Darius was the second founder and, what is more, the organizer of the empire.

On the coins called darics, the autocrat is seen with the scepter in his right hand and the bow in his left, traversing at a rapid pace the districts of his fatherland, a living symbol of the national unity that rested upon his person and of his sovereignty present everywhere.

The causes of the rupture of the Greeks with the Persians were several. Democedes, a Greek physician in the service of Darius, led an embassy of the “great king” to Magna Graecia, but on arriving at Croton he escaped. The embassy, after vainly demanding the fugitive, returned to Persia through great hardships. Thus the dignity of the master of the Medes was slighted, and the first motive for displeasure arose. Hippias, driven out of Athens, came to Asia Minor imploring the aid of the satrap Artaphernes, governor of Sardis and brother of Darius. Finally, a revolution broke out in Ionia, the determining and decisive cause of the conflict.

Aristagoras of Miletus, aided by the Athenians, took, occupied and burned Sardis (500); Darius prepared for punishment: the population of Miletus was deported and Ionia reduced to vexatious slavery. The focus of Hellenic radiation passes from now on to Europe. The capture of Miletus aroused an intense grief among the Greeks; the brothers from beyond the Aegean had succumbed; oh misfortune! and, when in the year following the disaster the poet Phrynichus composed and had performed a drama entitled “The Capture of Miletus,” cries and sobs rose from all parts of the theater and the poet was [p. 217] condemned to a fine for having made manifest the bloody misfortunes of the race. The Ionians subdued, it came the turn of the Greeks of Europe. The first armada was destroyed by the storm as it rounded the promontory of Athos (492). Two years later, insolent Persian heralds arrived at the Greek cities demanding water and earth, signs of submission; but when the barbarians reached Athens they were put to death, and the same fate befell them on presenting themselves before Sparta, in an attack upon the proud Lacedaemonian sovereignty. Then, from the coasts of Cilicia, there set out, under the command of Datis and Artaphernes, a fleet of ships that punished Eretria in Euboea, and later, on the advice of Hippias, went to disembark on the plains of Marathon, seven leagues (counting toward the east) from Athens. The full moon prevented the participation of the Spartans; no more than 10,000 Athenians and 1,000 natives of Plataea went out to meet the Persians. There the illustrious battle was joined; Miltiades, tyrant of the Chersonese of Thrace, was one of the ten strategoi who commanded the Athenians. The clash was terrible and the victory complete. The later vicissitudes of the war, down to the peace of Cimon, would take it upon themselves to prove that the world would not be Persian. Marathon was a sacred place where for centuries sacrifices were celebrated to the propitious Olympian divinities, and “Marathon!” was also the national war cry.

#### j. Muerte de Darío — j. Death of Darius

Darius died in 485, afflicted by the disturbances of the satraps.

#### IV. LA CIVILIZACIÓN PERSA — IV. PERSIAN CIVILIZATION

#### a. La religión — a. Religion

Persia —says Michelet— lacks temples and ceremonies; it has no other cult than prayer and the word; the texts, though confused, always distinguish three ages: the patriarchal, the sacerdotal, and that of the Medo-Chaldean magism.”

“Magism did not come to be organized until after the conquest of Babylon. The Greeks did not know Persia except in its epoch of decline.”

Persian civilization, tributary and heir of the Hamito-Semitic elaboration (as we set forth at the beginning of this study), is not original in the arts or in letters; but in metaphysico-religious formation, Persia is one of the great original peoples. Ahuramazda and Angromainus represent two stupendous theological-philosophical conceptions, and Zarathustra (the semi-legendary prophet) is a personage as worthy of memory as Moses, the most celebrated Jewish lawgiver.

Mazdaism arose probably in the Airyanem Vaedjo (according to another opinion it developed in Media almost at the end of the migrations

[p. 218] The most ancient tradition places the birth of the admirable apostle at Raghaz, in Media, and his life, during the first times of the Iranian race, when the tribes were still encamped in Bactria. Zarathustra (about whom one of the most illustrious representatives of German thought after Schopenhauer, F. Nietzsche, has elaborated a vigorous and triumphant poem) was of royal origin, and was chosen by God to regenerate the world. His infancy and youth constituted a long and dramatic struggle against the demons. It is to be noted that the devil always intervenes in the life of religious reformers. Jesus was tempted by him; Luther, shut up in the Wartburg, was also a victim of his attacks; it seems that the mystic, in the deliriums of his solitudes, is very prone to personify the malevolent spirit, suffering in consequence most painful anguish, bitter fruits of his faculty of autosuggestion.

When Zarathustra reached the age of thirty, a superior genius appeared to him and led him before Ahuramazda. Invited to question the god, he asked him who was the best of creatures. Ormuzd answered that the best among men was the purest. Afterward he wished to know the name and mission of the Angels, as well as the nature and attributes of the principle of evil. He crossed a mountain of flames without experiencing any harm, allowed his belly to be opened and molten metals to be poured into it, without uttering a complaint, and after such great trials he received from the hands of the god the Zend-Avesta, and descended to earth to preach what he had learned (Maspero).

The legend relates that he died struck by lightning, after his proselytes had increased considerably in Bactria.

Was Zarathustra a real person? Was he a fictitious being imagined by the Iranian people? It is not possible to decide such a question. Zarathustra is a Homer who, like the eminent singer of the Iliad, can serve as a theme for the fierce battles that the contradictory arguments of scholars are wont to wage among themselves.

No book of Zarathustra is preserved, but the doctrine is set forth in the Zend-Avesta (law and reform), sacred book of the Persians. According to the legend the Zend was divided into 21 books, written on 12,000 cowhides joined together by means of golden threads. The Muslims destroyed it on invading Persia; but some families faithful to the Zarathustrian doctrine took refuge in India and there, under the name of Parsis, settled and preserved one entire book of the Avesta and fragments of two others (Seignobos).

Ahuramazda, “the sovereign who knows all,” created the world; as he is perfectly good, he could not have created evil. Whatever evil there is, is the work of Angromainus, “the spirit of suffering.” Ahuramazda is luminous, resplendent, very pure, he possesses knowledge, the wellspring of pleasure. The soldiers of Ahuramazda are angels (yazatas), those of Angromainus, demons (devas); the former dwell in the east, the latter in the west, in the darkness of night and the half-shadows of twilight.

[p. 219] Ahuramazda and his army strive to preserve men and make them happy; Angromainus and his perverse spirits are bent on destroying and saddening men.

All that is beautiful and good is the work of Ahuramazda: water, seeds, fire, light, the domestic animals, and among them the dog, the birds, and among all of them the cock, that announcer of the dawn.

Whatever is ugly and evil proceeds from Angromainus: night, drought, cold, the desert, poisonous plants, serpents, parasites and the brutes that dwell in abject lairs, toads, lizards, rats and vermin.

Life, purity, work, truth, are the blessings of Ahuramazda.

Death, crime, sloth and lying, the scourges that Angromainus has in store for us.

The cult is reduced to reciting sacred hymns before the fire that burns on the mountains. Ahuramazda manifests himself only under the form of fire or of the sun. According to Herodotus, the Persians called those men senseless who raise statues and temples to the gods.

Conduct must tend to aid Ahuramazda in his work of destroying Angromainus; humanity is one of the armies of love. Such, in briefest compendium, is the magnificent religious conception of the Persians.

As is seen, such a religion is a dualism that explains all existences and all attributes by the principles destructive and evil, conservative and good; a dualism that engenders a pure morality, very different from and superior to those the East had known until then. The dualistic solution given by Mazdaism to the problem of the existence of evil in the world greatly honors the philosophical capacity of the Persian people, and the norms of conduct derived from the theological canons show that the Persians of the golden age were good men, who already knew, in such remote ages, that the essential end of all theological elaboration is the good.

#### b. Organización Política y Social — b. Political and Social Organization

The fable of Deioces (related when we dealt with the Medes) clearly proves how the evolution of the primitive, unconnected tribes toward royal government took place. The supreme judge who is transformed into a king is a great political truth, adorned by myth but highly symbolic. The laws of the transformation of governments are identical in all nations, in the ancient continent just as in the new. From the republican tribe to the small monarchical state, and from there, through conquest, to the great barbarian monarchy. Phraortes and Cyaxares are the founders of the great monarchy, just as Deioces was the creator of the small one.

The patriarchal customs of the peoples of the Aryan race (beautifully described by the Herodotuses and the Tacituses), those virile virtues, those desires for glory and booty, healthy and strong, disappear slowly, while monarchical absolutism increases.

[p. 220] When Deioces had built the sevenfold-walled city of Ecbatana, he hid himself from the profane gaze of his subjects and there, in the most fortified place, lived with his treasure and his seraglio. To laugh or spit in his presence was a grave crime; to adore him as a god, the duty of every Mede; Deioces inaugurated the era of despotism. With Phraortes and Cyaxares, oriental contact and conquest hasten the centralizing and tyrannical evolution; both monarchs, above all the second, after the fall of Nineveh, were veritable gods who imposed their caprices upon their people.

Among the Persians, Cyrus, who drew his nation out of the barbarous or semi-barbarous state in which it found itself, awakening its ambitions and animating its warlike spirit, the man superior to all his compatriots in political tact, wisdom and genius, was at once sovereign and educator, this last quality (says an author) being the one that most excited in the heart of his vassals the sentiments of love and profound respect toward his person. It was he who inaugurated the great Persian might and the dazzling opulence of his empire. Aristotle affirms: “The magnificence of the Persian kings reached as far as majesty and grandeur can go.” Cambyses and Darius, both conquerors, scaled the pinnacle of despotism, living in Susa or Ecbatana, within a marvelous castle closed with bronze gates and fortified with high walls. We, the modern Westerners, cannot have an exact idea of the exorbitant luxury of the East, nor is it easy for us to form a complete conception of the powerful tyranny of one of those criminal demigods, Cambyses or Xerxes. “Not a god himself could aim better,” said the courtier Prexaspes to the demented Cambyses, when the king had just split the heart of Prexaspes’ son with an arrow-shot as accurate as it was unjust. Life and fortune and family, all belonged to the “Great king.” All had to be placed at the unconditional disposal of the man raised to the category of a divine being.

Darius, the organizer of the empire, was the first Asiatic prince who understood, if only imperfectly, this elementary political truth: to govern the conquered with profit, it is necessary to leave them a certain autonomy. He divided his dominions into provinces (Satrapies), and the ancient usages, laws, languages and religions continued to subsist.

The satraps were men of careful political education, who maintained armies of their own, inhabited rich palaces and possessed a bodyguard, harem and court. Each satrap had to collect the tax, partly in metal (gold and silver) and partly in kind (wheat, horses, ivory), sending it to the emperor. Out of the great sum collected, the satraps, the scribes and the army were paid; the remainder, gathered into enormous ingots, was accumulated in the royal coffers, feeding an immense treasure. The administration of justice was also established at that time more firmly and regularly than before. History must regard Darius as an illustrious ruler, and, in sum, may accept as sincere these words in which the [p. 221] autocrat singled out his goodness: “Ahuramazda has favored me because I never committed cruelties or injustices, or oppressions.”

The Persian empire, historians have affirmed, was a great period of peace; the Iranian warriors rendered a great benefit by subjecting all peoples to the same power, preventing them from destroying one another. War became somewhat humanized, and the oriental world in the epoch of the Achaemenids, like the occidental in that of the Roman Caesars, marched along a less stormy course, ever further from the bloody outrages that characterized past times.

#### c. Artes Plásticas — c. Plastic Arts

The Persian architects, says Seignobos, copied the Assyrian palaces. At Persepolis one observes the flat roofs in the form of terraces, the doors guarded by stone mastiffs, the bas-reliefs representing hunts and ceremonies. Nevertheless, the Persians introduced three points of improvement: they used marble and stone instead of brick, made in their halls roofs of painted wood, and built slender columns that are the most graceful known.

To this it is due that their architecture is more elegant and pleasing than that of Assyria.

#### V. CONCLUSIÓN — V. CONCLUSION

At the beginning of the present work we wrote:

“A truth demonstrated by experience is that civilization is displaced, and that the most illustrious peoples and the most advanced societies die in the end like every organism, bequeathing to other societies and other peoples the treasure of their conquests.”

Heirs of the civilizing tradition of the ancient peoples belonging to the Hamito-Semitic race, the Medes and the Persians succeeded in constituting “the vastest and least oppressive empire the East ever knew.” If they did not introduce eminent advances in the sciences and the arts, on the other hand their religion and their elevated morality are two titles of glory that many peoples would envy. If at the touch of the general weakness they weakened, if living in the decadence of Asia Minor they declined, they never entirely abandoned the worthy mental qualities of their race, never showed themselves enemies of progress, never lost the vivifying blessing of Ahuramazda.

When the irremediable discouragement set in, Alexander, the Macedonian hero, shattered their empire in a series of sublime triumphs, and, sick from then on with that deadly inertia that always welcomes the sunsets of ancient civilizations, they set themselves to vegetate in the shadow. History turns its eyes away from them and fixes them on the Greco-Latin West. There resides the splendid future.

### LA AUTONOMÍA UNIVERSITARIA — UNIVERSITY AUTONOMY

[p. 223] The State must claim for itself, as an indisputable corollary of its sovereignty, action and predominance in the primary education of the Republic. No one will dispute this right, which constitutes one of the essential prerogatives of every democracy. But, as education rises in grade, the undisputed and indisputable predominance of the political person of the Nation diminishes concomitantly, in order to leave ample range of activity to private association. That is to say, if the primary grade of instruction belongs of full right to the Government, the secondary and university grades not only tolerate but demand the restriction of the orbit peculiar to the public thing, to the benefit not only of political and social institutions, but also of the efficacy of culture and its growth through individual and collective effort.

These are the principles embraced in their enunciation by the problem of university autonomy. In order to discuss the matter in an orderly fashion, let us divide it into two great different questions, bound together, nevertheless, within the intimate connection demanded by the very concept of freedom or autonomy. The teaching question and the economic one.

Autonomous means that which orders and commands by itself. From the Greek autos, oneself, and nomos, law. To the word autonomous is opposed the term heteronomous. From the Greek heteros, other, and nomos, rule, law. The University of Mexico is heteronomous, in the sense that it does not govern itself, but is commanded and ordered, at their pleasure, by the gentlemen secretaries of Public Education. Now then, public opinion demands, because it has verified the gravest defects of the present system, that our highest teaching institution break its traditional subjection and realize its freedom.

IV. On the point of teaching, it is clear that no Minister of Public Instruction, however wise and capable he may be supposed, possesses gifts sufficient to [p. 224] set himself up as legislator and teacher, still less as despot or tyrant, of an Academy that embraces, in its higher grade and by its very essence, the universality of human studies: Universitas studiorum. Neither Pico della Mirandola, nor the very Aristotle of Stagira, nor any man whatever, unless he were omniscience itself, would be bold enough to set the standard and carry out the ordering of human knowledge! In a century like ours, in which the division of scientific labor has reached the maximum of development and power, intellectual dictatorships, far from being possible, could fit only in the clouded mind of slaves or madmen. If Erasmus and Voltaire were the oracle of their centuries, in the twentieth, which we have reached, of the Christian Era, there are no oracles. The work of individual and collective culture smiles at them without pity. Therefore every discreet man who occupies the Secretariat of Public Education must strive to cast far from himself the terrible and ponderous burden of university mental dictatorship.

V. Instead of a ridiculous autocrat, the teaching labor of a University is distributed, democratically, among all the members of the institution: students, professors, Academies of the Faculties, University Council or Senate, and Rector, or, as this personage is called in the Universities of the United States, “President of the University.”

VI. But if, in the intellectual sphere, the reasons for autonomy are incontrovertible, and it is an act of barbarism to deny them, in the moral sphere the arguments that occur in favor of the thesis are no less brilliant. On various occasions, some gentlemen secretaries of State have cast a stigma upon the professors and the students. They have said that, in times of tyranny, the students and professors did not behave as truly free men, but as mere instruments of despotism, going to the extreme of tolerating the imperfect militarization of the schools. When General Huerta ordered the “militarization” of the Preparatory College of Mexico, we had the honor of saying, from the rostrum of the chair, to our students of logic and history of philosophy that “it was preferable to open schools in the barracks and not to turn the schools into barracks.” It is true that university professors have not always been at the moral height of their mission; but this is due to their not having been free, to the University of Mexico’s not having achieved its desired autonomy; to our rulers’ wishing to dispose, at their pleasure, of the university chairs, in order to grant them to their favorites; to the fact that, in sum, to be fully worthy, one must depend only on justice and reason, and not on political conveniences, always contemptible, of the passing minute. Autonomous was the old University of Mexico, founded by decree of the Caesar Charles V; and the Republican [p. 225] University, its heir, has only been a slave! How many times the prudent historian finds, in the history of national traditions, freedom and justice in the Spanish colonial regime, and slavery and injustice in Mexican democracy!
