As regards the economic question, it must be recalled that the greater part of the foundations and endowments of our university schools did not come from the National Government, but from various associations. The College of San Ildefonso, with its magnificent building, was the work of the Society of Jesus; some wise and self-sacrificing physicians, who are the lasting honor of national science, created the Faculty of Medicine; and the Palace of Mining, which Mexican governments have been using to house various Ministries and offices, was the creation of the miners and not of the State. What would be strange if the Mexican Nation, convinced of the justice that supports the partisans of university autonomy, were to return, in an act of equity, to their legitimate owners, the capital of which it has for so long enjoyed the usufruct. Moreover, the Stadium, recently built, was so built, in good part, thanks to the school fees paid, in times of general penury, by the university students, and thanks also to the generosity of their teachers, who gave a day of their pay to help defray the expenses incurred

If, according to the constitution of the Republic, moral persons cannot hold every kind of real property for themselves, as happens in other countries, the National Representation may deliver, each year, the sum that the budget of the University amounts to, to the Rector and the Council, and these respond, suitably, before the authorities of the Republic.

In sum, absolute freedom in teaching. Appointments of the Rector and of the professors by the university institution itself, and effective and efficacious economic autonomy. If General don Álvaro Obregón and Doctor Gastélum, Secretary of Public Education, should accomplish the desired work, or begin, at least, to fulfill it, they will deserve well of their grateful fellow citizens; and let them not lend ear to the arguments of the sophists who avail themselves of minuscule contemporary incidents to deny the effort that is asked of them. Autonomy is so great that, at the end of a few years, it will have been able to erase even the vestiges of those adverse conditions that today would seem to favor those who pretend to deny it. And if this time the University is not free, it will be so another time soon. The essence of the human ideal is to be stronger always than every transitory reality. Stronger!

### LA FILOSOFÍA EN LA UNIVERSIDAD DE MÉXICO — PHILOSOPHY AT THE UNIVERSITY OF MEXICO

[p. 226]

#### I.

The Faculty of Philosophy is, especially, the first of the university Faculties; as philosophy is, among the sciences, also the first. Its development will impress the seal of Mexican university individuality, its characteristic personality, upon the course of our academic formation. Just as don Gabino Barreda, by means of the philosophy of Auguste Comte, vigorously stamped the Plan of Studies of the old National Preparatory School, so too, in even greater proportions, another capable educator will be able to impress the form of his philosophical thought upon the whole of our future education.

For this reason the philosophical criterion of the professors who today occupy the chairs of Metaphysics, Ethics, Aesthetics, Epistemology, Logic, Philosophy of Education and Philosophy of Law, in the bosom of the supreme Faculty of our Alma Mater, matters so much to the culture of the Republic.

We note three principal tendencies among the most outstanding professors: traditional philosophy, Hartmannian realism, and critical idealism. Currents of contemporary thought that likewise struggle among themselves in the cultured circles of Europe.

Traditional philosophy, which its adherents adorn with the supreme title of “perennial philosophy,” is scholastic thought, today profoundly renewed, in Germany as in Belgium, France, Italy and Spain. To this tendency —always strong and organic— correspond the professors Dr. Jesús Guisa y Azevedo and Lic. Ignacio Bravo Betancourt.

Dr. Guisa y Azevedo was appointed professor of history of philosophy, as was his colleague don Ignacio Bravo Betancourt, during the period when we had the honor of occupying the post of head of the Institute of Philosophy. The Rector of the Institution was don Manuel Gómez Morín, of distinguished academic achievement.

Guisa y Azevedo, a writer of just and ample reputation, is a doctor of Philosophy and licentiate in Political and Social Sciences from the University of Louvain. The lawyer don Ignacio Bravo Betancourt is a son of the University, and was a distinguished student, among those of his generation, in the Faculty of Jurisprudence.

They expound in their chairs scholastic realism, derived from Aristotle and Saint Thomas. A philosophical tendency that has the most illustrious of traditions —and the most constant— through the centuries. The thought that Scholasticism is a purely “religious” philosophy turns out to be a crass error. For the Aquinatian, the philosopher investigates what belongs to things according to their nature; whereas faith considers them insofar as they were created by God and are related to Him.

[p. 227] Therefore Scholastic philosophy matters to independent thought, apart from every exclusively religious consideration.

Don Eduardo García Máynez, professor of History of Philosophy and of Ethics, is a clear exponent of our university culture, despite his youth. To the exquisite science of the jurisconsult he adds the deepest metaphysical concern. Together we have served, in the Faculty of Jurisprudence, the posts of professors of Philosophy of Law—a subject now SUPPRESSED from the Faculty’s list! ... At that time, don Eduardo García Máynez taught the systematic course of Philosophy of Law and we the historical one. Then we had occasion to verify the exceptional competence of our colleague.

Don Eduardo García Máynez professes the realism of Hartmann, that vigorous philosophical current which has aroused such admiration in the academic centers of Europe. Hartmann has written three books of the first rank: Los principios de una metafísica del conocimiento (1921), the Ética (1926), and El problema del ser espiritual. Hartmann, unlike the scholastics, advocates a systematic philosophical thought, but not a system. According to him, the failure of German idealism lay, as Francisco Romero says, "in its haste to raise vast constructions of thought, great systems, complete and closed"; according to the German philosopher, the time of the great systems has already passed. Philosophy does not wish to build SYSTEMS, but to meditate on PROBLEMS. The categories of knowing coincide only partially with those of being. Between the constructive position and the inquiring one, Hartmann decides for the latter.

"The history of philosophy," says García Máynez, "presents two great spiritual directions: that of constructive systematic thought, and that of problematic thought. These tendencies may occur united, for there are thinkers who, like the Saint of Aquino, while builders of systems, are at the same time men endowed with that marvelous problematic instinct characteristic of metaphysicians of genius. But, as a general rule, those tendencies appear separated. In Proclus and Plotinus, Saint Thomas and Scotus, Hobbes and Spinoza, systematic thought predominates; in Plato and Aristotle, Descartes and Hume, Leibniz and Kant, problematic thought. It is neither chance nor misfortune that these latter have not bequeathed great systems to posterity. This does not mean that Plato and Aristotle, for example, do not aspire to the constitution of a systematic knowledge: it means only that in them the love of the problem, of the difficulty, of the aporia, predominates. And this is because they had a perfectly clear vision that the problem—not the system—is what is essential in metaphysical labor."

The external systematic, logical form is not essential to philosophical elaboration, however many have thought so. "He who thinks methodically [p. 228]," says Kant, "may set forth his thought systematically or fragmentarily. A presentation fragmentary on the outside but methodical at bottom is an aphoristic presentation." That is to say, whenever one philosophizes one thinks with inner coherence, even when the presentation is fragmentary, even when one philosophizes WITH A HAMMER, as Nietzsche said with his habitual eloquence... It is debatable to affirm that philosophy itself must be systematic. The system belonged to a priori philosophy, or to the dialectic of scholastics and Hegelians.

García Máynez has carried this spirit of his teacher into the meditation on the "problem of the validity of Law," offering us a philosophical Ensayo that constitutes one of the most important monographs among all those published by a Mexican university professor.

Don Francisco Luna Arroyo and don Héctor Rodríguez represent the "critical idealism" of the Kantian school of Marburg. Doctor don Francisco Luna Arroyo has just published an important book: Los principios de la ética social, in which he takes in the concept as well as the axiology and realization of morality.

The preponderant character of Dr. Luna Arroyo's philosophical work is signified by the following declaration: the book "takes in, in a systematic sense, the capital themes of ethics. Of course, then, it is necessary to situate this science in the general framework of philosophy. But for this, the preliminary task is in turn imposed of gaining a rigorous concept of this branch of knowledge. Thus a brief discussion is justified concerning the most debated currents of our time, and also of traditional philosophy. The result of this debate energetically displays an ANTIMETAPHYSICAL trait. Philosophy—Dr. Luna Arroyo goes on—in the sense defended, does not have as its object supposed transcendent realities, that is, situated beyond facts, beyond phenomena. Its object is offered rather in something by essence patent: CULTURE. One of the fundamental territories of culture is morality."

Therefore, against Hartmannian realism and scholastic philosophy, Dr. Luna Arroyo raises the banner of critical idealism. The philosophy of immanence which he upholds makes him an assiduous and competent censor of all transcendentalism. Being is immanent in consciousness; and, with everything that would be an investigation of something "situated beyond the world of facts, of phenomena" flatly denied, his orderly meditation on moral problems obliges him to see in morality one of the sectors of culture.

Hence, too, reason—in accordance with this direction of thought—is "constructive." Ontology is summed up in culture; and ethics, in OUGHT-TO-BE. The philosopher is no longer a contemplative, but a [p. 229] legislator. As Ortega y Gasset says in his admirable Ensayo sobre Kant: "The quiet truth is transformed into an imperative. Knowledge ceases to be a passive mirror of reality and becomes a construction. That which is vulgarly called reality is mere chaotic and senseless material that must be sculpted into the body of a universe."

The thing "in itself," maintains Professor Luna Arroyo, is not an object of knowledge. "Traditional metaphysics mistakenly supposes that it has as its object of knowledge a problematic reality that transcends, that lies beyond facts, and that is, for that reason, independent of all consciousness. But this supposed reality must be known in and through consciousness; since consciousness is not passive but necessarily links, unites, relates—in a word, transforms what enters its domains—it is evident that what is known is never something independent of consciousness, no 'thing in itself.'" The immanentism of the Marburg School demolishes the inert Kantian noumenon. Things in themselves are things in me.

As regards the essence of morality, it is understood as an "originary function of culture." Which originary function of culture is morality? Law is a relation that binds individuals in an autarchic and inviolable manner; morality, on the other hand, is a "form of relation that always implies unconditioned ends. It is, by its essence, autonomous, free."

Such is, in broad strokes, the philosophical outlook that the University of Mexico presents today! As can be seen, only materialist systems—historical or not—only sour empiricism and out-of-date positivism, have no place. During our directorship of the Institute of Philosophy, we sought earnestly a competent professor for the teaching of historical materialism, as a philosophical theory, in the Faculty of Philosophy and Letters: but we did not have the good fortune to find one!... May the suitable professor soon appear and make his competence stand out above his colleagues, to give us in the university classrooms themselves something more than declamation empty of scientific content! In this way the materialist theory of history can be set against the robust philosophical positions that have been alluded to above.

Freedom of the chair opens before the students of our Institute the horizons of contemporary thought, far from all fanaticism, red or white. Freedom of the chair means independence from the passions, from the yoke of sterile prejudices, from the irrational movements of public opinion, from the influence of outside authorities, political or religious, that can oppress the conscience!...

Freedom of the chair, Tiberghien has said, "is an offering of respect [p. 230] to the dignity of the student." "Dignity is inseparable from moral freedom, from nobility of character, from elevation of intelligence. The dignity of man consists in tolerance. To differ in opinion is not to be an enemy."

### LA FILOSOFÍA DE LOS VALORES — THE PHILOSOPHY OF VALUES

Perhaps the most valuable thing that the philosophical thought of our time produces is the philosophy of values that is called Axiology or "Estimative," as Ortega y Gasset proposes. This does not mean, in truth, that investigations of values are exclusively contemporary; but it does mean that the philosophical vocation of our century refers, very especially, to this philosophical problem of the conception of life. It is evident that, if we knew everything, we should still lack knowing everything from the standpoint of Axiology. To be is not to be worth. Worth can never be reduced to being. An elementary reflection proves, beyond dispute, the indubitable heterogeneity of being and worth; for there are things that are and are worth nothing, and things that are worth much and are not. Thus, non-valuable beings abound, ontologically they surpass; but axiologically they are nothing. No one has failed, at some time, to distinguish clearly, by virtue of the contingencies of his personal life (always distinct from other existences), that being and worth cannot be identified, that is: that being is not worth. Nor can being be included in one of the species of worth. Who in the course of his existence has not wished at some time that a moment of happiness that has passed beyond remedy might become present again? Heraclitus affirmed that we cannot touch twice the water of the river of living, because when we think of putting our hands into the stream a second time, the water that flows is already other.

Who has not wished that something impossible might be? Would that the beloved being who disappeared forever might live with us, here and now!... But this is impossible. That being, however beloved, will never again be at our side. Our esteem does not revive it, our enthusiasm does not resurrect it. It is most valuable to us, but it does not exist.

Therefore, existence and value are not confounded. They are two different worlds that are joined to each other only by the relation that the thinking subject establishes between the object of his esteem and his own consciousness.

[p. 231] It seems, then, that the fundamental difference between the valuable and the existent would be that which consists in affirming the subjectivity of value and the objectivity of existence. Value would persist within human subjectivity and existence would subsist outside it. Values would lack substantivity. Existence would not. This solution cannot be accepted, because there is something in value that refers it indispensably to a given object. One loves only what IS lovable. One desires only what IS desirable. One adores only what IS adorable. Hence value is not limited to and contained within the sphere of subjectivity. Man, the individual subject, is not its measure. It always refers, intentionally, to an object. The object is the valuable for the subject. Therefore, without objects there are no values, just as there are none without subjects. Objects have worth for subjects. With objects and subjects suppressed, as well as the relations that mediate between them, values are suppressed. They are the indeclinable relations of the objective and the subjective; but something more is lacking. For these relations do not occur outside social life, but within it. If the cultural environment of a given society is modified, values are transformed along with it. Hence these are relations between subjects and objects, through a culture.

It is easy to see and to verify how, outside the interpsychic relations of social life, outside that interdependence among the primary monads, value and values are annihilated. What meaning can a valuable thing have that is valuable for no one, at no time, in no space, for no reason?

Man is not the measure of all things; things are independently of whether the human individual measures them or not. Subjectivism is false. But if this individualist solipsism cannot be sustained philosophically speaking, the social solipsism which, in the face of real objects, establishes logical, aesthetic, ethical, juridical, religious, economic, etcetera, cultural relations is an incontestable thesis; for this reason we admit it, within the position of its metaphysical realism.

Relativism yes; subjectivism no. Relativism yes, because the subject relates to the object in order to declare it valuable, but not directly, but through the universal environment of culture. What can a book such as the Bible or the Divine Comedy be worth to the savages of a remote region of Africa? What can a precious instrument of surgery or of mathematics signify to them? On the other hand, we affirm, inescapably, the existence of a culture, if we succeed in unearthing from the ground an instrument that betrays the social and many-minded work that engendered it. In no other way have the positive fruits of the prehistoric evolution of humanity been revealed, thanks to the ingenuity and the scientific [p. 232] patience of legions of self-sacrificing investigators.

Objects have always possessed their qualities that make them valuable for civilization; but those same attributes have not always been esteemed by men. Radium, not because it has now been discovered, has ceased to be a body sui géneris that acted, from the beginning, upon the other chemical elements, and combined with them in accordance with chemical principles and laws. Today the therapeutic power of vitamins is discovered; but they always exercised it upon the organism, independently of their discovery by Science.

Therefore the object, with its own qualities, is one of the irrefragable terms of the relation that constitutes value; but the subject, with its own attributes and possibilities, is also another of the terms. To value is to relate desires and longings, sensations and feelings of the human being with forms and attributes of objects that are given intentionally in the consciousness of subjects. But this is not all. How, in their pure individual solipsism, would values have universality? How, without recourse to other consciousnesses, would we affirm that something is worth universally? In the essence of value we must put not only the relation of subject and object, but the universal society of spirits and hearts that is humanity, made solidary by its constant and splendid fruit: Culture. So value is distinguished by a profound and essential relativity. It is not something subjective, but objective, socially speaking. To the relativity of subject and object value adds the relativity of the social life in which it is affirmed. Outside the human, outside consciousness, outside the object, value is only a delirious illusion of the new disciples of Plato, which the philosopher of Athens would condemn without remedy. Values are not Platonic Ideas, but social forms which, maintained in the objectivism of human relations with the Universe, display themselves within their fundamental unity, with the rich and varied nuances lent them by "creative evolution."

Truth is eternal, it is a perennial relation between object and subject; but the truths that Culture acquires and synthesizes have been discovered gradually through the centuries. So with Beauty. It is a perennial relation of the subjective and the objective; but it has been shading itself, in the course of time, with the development of the different cultures elaborated by humanity. And so too Justice, which, not because it is affirmed barbarically among primitive peoples, ceases to be recognized in its essence as an irrefragable relation of the objective and the subjective. God Himself is also a gradual revelation, which runs from the incipient forms of fetishism and totemism to the august manifestations of "the Holy," [p. 233] as Rudolf Otto says, in the religions derived from Israel. Social solipsism, not individual solipsism. Consciousness is the matrix of values. And the intentional object gives consciousness its irrefragable direction.

### EL MUNDO ES HISTÓRICO — THE WORLD IS HISTORICAL

Economics considers things from the standpoint of utility: sub specie utilitatis; aesthetics, from the standpoint of beauty: sub specie pulchritudinis; logic considers only relations: it looks at existence sub specie relationis. Morality embraces everything from the standpoint of charity; metaphysics, as Spinoza said, considers in an eternal present whatever is: sub specie aeternitatis. History, on the other hand, embraces everything from the standpoint of time.

Insofar as it is duration of succession, "there is a register in which time inscribes itself," as Bergson has admirably said. This register is the one the historian deciphers and reads.

The ancient philosophers, both Democritus and Aristotle, thought that the system of the world is eternal, they dealt with the incorruptible heaven; the modern philosophers, such as Descartes and Kant, also affirmed a similar thought; but modern physics, thanks to the discovery of the great laws of thermodynamics, has come to demolish the incorruptibility of the universe, and to assure us that the disposition of the world has a direction toward death, toward the extinction of motion.

This gives us a historical universe, which can develop only in the direction marked by the celebrated law of Clausius: "All energy tends to degrade into heat, and heat to distribute itself uniformly among bodies." Which means that the "value" of energy is different; for there are energies of "diverse grade." Nothing is lost, everything is conserved, energy remains constant; but its usable form tends toward zero; in this form, all physical energies will become heat; and the uniform diffusion of heat will bring about the general equalization of temperatures: "All motion, all vibration of masses, all electricity, all transmission of energy, all life as well, will have disappeared."

[p. 234] This means that the world is historical, that nothing is lost from it; but that, like living organisms, the great organism of the universe tends toward its irremediable end.

And the value that physicists grant to the second law of thermodynamics is, in truth, "supreme." "In my opinion," says Eddington, "the law according to which entropy constantly increases (the second law of thermodynamics) holds the supreme position among the laws of nature. Do not think that the glorification of this second law lacks meaning. True, there are other laws in which we have reasons to believe; we feel that any hypothesis that violates them is very improbable; but this improbability is vague and does not place us in the presence of a series of numbers that absolutely stops us."

What is said of the universe is affirmed also of life. Nature exhibits two kinds of phenomena: reversible and irreversible. Theoretically the repetition of reversible phenomena tends to infinity. For this kind of phenomena there is no history. On the other hand, the facts of the second category, the phenomena of degradation, are irreversible, that is, historical.

Old age, senility, signifies the unfailing evolution of living matter. Death is one of the episodes of irreversible life. Beings that manifest vitality are precisely those in which the death of the individual is the natural culmination of biological existence. For this reason life too is historical, as the development of the world is historical; for this reason there is inscribed in all beings a register in which time betrays its passage.

It is true that the second principle of thermodynamics has only a statistical value; but all physical laws have a similar value; and the numbers that secure the validity of the law of Clausius are so formidable that, for that very reason, Eddington asserts that the principle must be placed at the head of all the laws of physics.

Great physicists, illustrious astronomers, eminent naturalists, have reflected that the universe might not be a closed system; new causes might intervene, preventing the fulfillment of the formidable [p. 235] statistical determinism implicit in the second law of thermodynamics. Collisions of stars, stupendous disintegrations of matter, might release fabulous quantities of energy, which are not computed in the conception of Clausius; which would do no more than delay the end. To speak in the language of the jurists: this would not signify a peremptory exception against the principle of Carnot generalized by Clausius, but only a dilatory exception. In sum, seeing things in their synthesis, no sufficient exception would avoid the universal tendency toward death.

Philosophers and the great religions have always fixed the attention of the human spirit on this fearful problem. Schopenhauer says that death is "the inspirer of philosophy"; the great Christian ascetics always opposed to the perishable and transitory world, where time reigns, where history rules everything, the duration without succession of eternity.

This contrast makes us lift our eyes from the corruptible and perishable, to fix the object of our contemplation on other regions of reality evoked for us by mathematical beings, always identical with themselves; by virtue of which physicists investigate the contingent and transitory world of experience. For this reason Plato demanded that in the Academy all be versed in geometry; because geometrical expressions raise the human mind to those regions where Values, the Ideas, reign in their incorruptibility.

In any case, history has the right to give itself as object of knowledge the universality of experience, considered not from the standpoint of eternity, but from the standpoint of time.

### EL CONCEPTO DE LA HISTORIA — THE CONCEPT OF HISTORY

Rickert divides the whole of the sciences into cultural and natural. His teacher and colleague Windelband divides the same whole into "nomothetic" sciences and "idiographic" sciences. The former are sciences of laws; the latter refer to irreducible individuality.

In sum: the idea of the two celebrated German thinkers consists in affirming that, just as the sciences of nature refer to the concept of LAW, those of culture refer to the concept of VALUE.

Philosophy is understood as a theory of values.

[p. 236] It is obvious that the natural sciences are sufficiently distinguished and characterized by the notion of scientific law. Whatever concept one may hold regarding natural law, no one can deny that the purpose implicit in all natural-scientific elaboration is to rise to the attainment of laws.

No theme is more debated in our days than the concept of natural law. It is long since the celebrated statement of Montesquieu passed into the history of philosophical ideas: "Law is a necessary relation that derives from the nature of things."

Most scholars today hold that the laws of physics have only a statistical significance or value. So that the calculus of probabilities is applied universally, from mathematics to sociology.

But this does not forbid accepting the distinction between the sciences that propose the knowledge of laws of nature, and those that have as their object the world or region of values, such as history, in all its diverse kinds; that is, literary history as well as religious, artistic, or political.

In sum: value or law would define, as objects of knowledge, the proper orbit of each scientific order, the natural and the cultural.

The criterion of the search for value, as the proper essence of history, seems to us insufficient. We say that it seems to us merely insufficient, not that it is an erroneous criterion.

History has as its object value, values; but, above all, what matters to it is the characteristic individuality of the things it studies.

The historian does not engage in discussions about values and their intrinsic nature. That is a matter for philosophy, never for history. What the historian proposes as object of knowledge—to avail ourselves of a celebrated expression of Hegel—is the concrete individual, not the concrete universal.

Let us set out various possible cases of historical inquiry. It is political history that we shall treat first.

The historian of Caesar, far from all discussion about the values of culture, which he takes as assumed and admitted, seeks to reflect in his work the personality of Caesar, his rich, inexhaustible personality: his [p. 237] political science, his human intuition, his greatness as a Latin writer, his exact, coherent style.

All the historians of Caesar, those there have been and those yet to be, will return to the same inexhaustible question, absolutely inexhaustible.

Husserl, in his Cartesian Meditations, puts the reader in the position of thinking of the innumerable distinct positions that a cube may have before human sight, as it moves successively and slowly through several distinct aspects.

If a cube, in distinct movements communicated to it from outside, is inexhaustible for thought, one must imagine the infinite that lies before the historian of Caesar, if he traverses his life and, with it, the fullness of its historical manifestations!

Now let us think not of political history but of literary history. The historians of Dante form a legion like those of Caesar. Beginning with Boccaccio, the great Florentine has been commented upon in his historical and poetic action. The Dantean library exists as does that of Cervantes or that of Shakespeare. Nevertheless, the rich individuality of these great poets continues to be the object of literary history; in the same way that the biography of Caesar gives sustenance to the history of Rome.

Therefore, the object of history, which always makes reference to values, to the values of culture, is not properly THE VALUE—whence the insufficiency of Rickert's thesis—but THE EXISTENTIAL, REAL, UNIQUE CONCRETION, which constitutes, properly, "the historical." As Aristotle says: what matters for the historian is not what could have been, what might have been; but "what Alcibiades said or thought." This is the historical. As a great English historian also affirmed: what matters for the historian "is to find out whether John Lackland passed this way."

It is then understood that history requires, in order to be carried out, a form of intuition distinct from the intuition of values, different from the intuition of essences. It is the form of intuition that Bergsonism has vindicated: the intuition of the individual and the concrete. For the historian, as for the painter, unique and singular things are what matter. Velázquez reflecting in an incomparable way the physiognomy of Philip IV! [p. 238] Only that history seeks, unlike art, to lead us to intellectual knowledge, and not merely intuitive, of concrete beings, of absolute individualities.

### LOS MUERTOS — THE DEAD

Les vivants sont de plus en plus gouvernés par les morts.

AUGUSTE COMTE

From them and through them we live and breathe. From the dead who gave us culture and blood. The great philosopher Auguste Comte wrote: Les vivants sont de plus en plus gouvernés par les morts [The living are more and more governed by the dead].

Indeed: what do we owe to the living? Infinitely less than what we inherit from the dead. From the dead come our science, our religion, our art, our customs; hence also our future, built upon the data of the present. "Today" is an instant in time; as time itself is in eternity.

Space is the banal theater of the living. Time constitutes the sacred magnitude of the dead. We are not space, but memory, spirit, duration; but also succession, that is, time. Space fills the society of contemporaries; time gathers the hermetic act of the departed.

With their invisible presence, which guides us at every instant, the action of history in nature and the dominion of tradition over solidarity are manifested. True human solidarity can be conceived only as traditional. The dead live again in our being. Before it was ours, life was theirs. Protoplasm—the physical basis of life—"is an atavistic substance, which we have never seen born, but only reproduced."

Comte affirmed, likewise, that the dead intervene more and more in the destiny of the living: because with each instant that passes the efforts of men accumulate; and we, the latest, are the most indebted.

[p. 239] Bergson and Husserl disappeared from the stage of the world only yesterday. Therefore our fathers could not owe them what we owe. Each new generation incurs a greater debt. Each man who is born finds a world more authentically and religiously subjugated by the dead.

Carlos Pereyra and Enrique O. Aragón have left our company! Pereyra was one of the noblest sons of the Republic, an excellent writer, a most loving historian of his race, a distinguished Hispanist, an eminent Mexican. Aragón was a self-sacrificing teacher, who offered his life for the benefit of his contemporaries. May they rest in peace!

Consider the most extraordinary generations. For example: the work of the Florentines of the Renaissance, starred, it is true, with brilliant efforts and prestige, from Dante to Ariosto and Tasso, from Giotto to Cellini and Galileo. But what is the work of the Italian Renaissance before the age-old heritage of Greece, Rome, Christianity, and the Orient?...

We are children of History; subjects of tradition; bearers of the torch lit across the centuries; passing accidents of an enduring labor; instants in the fugitive course of the hour. Hours of the day; days, years, and centuries in eternity!... Nothing counts before the passage of time!

Comte vindicated for the dead what he called "subjective immortality," as real as it is immaterial. For each time we pronounce a word, we evoke the creators of the language; and each time lightning strikes the staff of the lightning rod, the genius of Franklin shelters us. Subjectively present in culture and civilization, the dead guide us. They were transmuted into the moral atmosphere of the world in which we live. Immaterially, they are immortal!

But... are they so only subjectively?... Perhaps not. Persons disappear, they do not succumb. What in us is unique, the spiritual form of our being, need not dissolve with death. The form does not fail with the body. The spirit is not dispersed by death. It can continue [p. 240] informing another, different life. Perhaps it never succumbs. Not a single sufficient reason can be adduced for its extinction.

If so, this society of the living signifies only a brief instant of exceptional company. The perennial society we shall enjoy with all the departed generations, in another sphere of reality. Humanity will be gathered whole, beyond death, under the peace of God! Immortality can be objective and subjective.

The preeminence of the dead is the secret of the majesty and the interest of history. Everything, except the instant that passes, is historical; and even the instant that passes incorporates in its passage the universality of the historical; because "the present is pregnant with the future" and arose from the immediate past. The more time passes, the more historical man will be. Behold here the secret of the human passion for history.

### EL SENTIDO DE LA HISTORIA — THE MEANING OF HISTORY

Herder is one of the classics of German thought. He was born in East Prussia, in Mohrungen, in 1744. The Bible and liturgical poetry constituted his first spiritual nourishment. A preacher, Trescho, educated him along with his own children; and a surgeon of the Russian army, charmed by the bearing of the young thinker, defrayed the costs of his education. Sent to Koenigsberg to devote himself to medicine, he suffered a collapse upon performing his first dissection.

He had to exchange surgery for theology. Then he met Kant, who allowed him to attend his courses free of charge. Appointed preacher in Riga, on the recommendation of Hamann, he remained in that city until the year 1769. He then traveled through France. In Nantes he accepted the proposal of the Prince of Holstein to accompany him on a journey through France and Italy. In Strasbourg he met Goethe, over whom he exercised notable influence. He later became professor of philosophy at the University of Göttingen; and, finally, Goethe had him appointed preacher and consistorial counselor at the court of Weimar. He was then able to devote himself fully to study. He traveled with the Grand Duchess Amelia through Italy, and died in 1803.

His principal philosophical work is the one entitled: Ideas sobre la filosofía de la historia de la humanidad.

[p. 241] In this admirable book he upholds the fundamental thought that sees in man a being whose task is to realize his own human essence, his "humanity."

Man—unlike the beasts, whose act is always the repetition of a conduct incapable of rising above itself—goes on realizing his destiny, through the vicissitudes of his history, notwithstanding his wickedness and his failures; because, by his very nature, created in the image of God, if he was not born perfect, he can perfect himself, realizing in his life what his intelligence suggests and his will carries out.

For this reason human work is many-minded. Each historical people, in its action, suffered the penalty consequent upon its defects, and attained the triumph proportionate to its own contingent realization of the human ideal.

"Everything that is not a purely passive instrument," teaches Herder, "contains within itself its own end." Man contains in his nature his own end. If the human race is considered as we know it, in accordance with the laws inherent in it, we shall not be able to find for man an end higher than humanity itself, which constitutes his nature; because if we imagine an angel or a god, we shall imagine them as belonging to an ideal world, which is not ours; as beings superior to humanity.

Everything evil, unjust and tyrannical that man has wrought, he wrought against his nature, against humanity. Everything good that history records was likewise done for the sake of humanity. Man cannot imagine any end other than the one that resides in himself.

God made man intelligent and free. The limits He imposed upon man's life are those that depend on time, on situation and on the human faculties themselves.

Never did the Divinity work a miracle to favor peoples, to aid those who suffered the consequences of their acts. He always let those consequences produce all their effects, so that man might learn to correct them.

This law of human nature is as simple as it is worthy of God and fruitful in its consequences for the species. Everywhere, man has been what he himself has made of himself, what he has been able or has wished to become.

[p. 242] The Divinity aided man by forming him; endowing him from the beginning with noble faculties, sufficient to realize, successively, humanity. We cannot expect anything more from God.

As soon as He had formed man, God said to him: “Be my image; be a god upon the earth. Reign and choose. Do the good that your nature contains. I shall not intervene on your behalf with prodigies; for I placed your destinies in your own hands. You yourself must find your support in the eternal laws that I set upon the universe.”

In sum: our mission is to realize ourselves as men; to create Man in the men and peoples of universal history. Providence endowed us with intelligence and freedom, in order to forge and realize ourselves, resting upon the ground of the cosmic laws. And God is with us in our human nature, a copy of the divine; in our contingent and perfectible being, capable of thinking the Good, by reflecting it in our intelligence and externalizing it in action, bringing action into accord with the ideal. This is, according to Herder, the meaning of history.

In Herder's thought the idea of providence is combined with the idea of humanity; for man is a creature of God. But extraordinary providential intervention is excluded from history. Throughout the historical, man's task is to form himself.

### CRISTIANISMO Y HUMANISMO — CHRISTIANITY AND HUMANISM

Herder's thought on the philosophy of history is, in sum, this: man, created in the image of God, contains in his nature his own end, which he goes on realizing, successively, in the diverse peoples and centuries of history.

God is with us in our human nature, a copy of the divine; but the divinity does not intervene on our behalf with prodigies. The humanity yet to be realized constitutes the ideal of history. Everything evil that man has wrought, he wrought against his nature, against [p. 243] humanity. Everything good that history records was likewise done for the sake of humanity.

Providence intervened in creating us; but it never again intervenes in the vicissitudes of our development.

II. In Book XV of his Ideas for the Philosophy of the History of Humanity, the German thinker sets about unraveling the significance of Christianity. He says: “Seventy years before the fall of the Jewish empire, a man was born who produced in the minds of the peoples, as in their customs and their legislation, a revolution as profound as it was unexpected.”

“The KINGDOM he proclaimed, he called THE KINGDOM OF GOD (reserved for the elect); it demanded neither ceremonies nor outward duties, but purity of heart and spiritual virtues. The truest humanity transpires from the discourses of His that have reached us. It lives in His acts, was strengthened by His death, and the name He preferred to give Himself was that of SON OF MAN.”

The philosopher of humanism finds before him, in the course of universal history, the advent of Jesus Christ. As we have just read, he admires the Christ; for “the truest humanity” throbs in the discourses of His that were preserved for us. He also admires the name of predilection that Jesus Christ gave Himself: SON OF MAN. Humanity —the philosopher's fundamental preoccupation— seems to find corroboration, as the end of man, in Christian teaching.

And Herder asks himself what the announced Kingdom, which Jesus established, can consist in.

“The great Founder wished to act as the spiritual savior of his race; forming MEN OF GOD who, however much they might obey different laws, would be guided in their acts by the interest of their neighbor, in accordance with the purest principles; bearing evil with patience, in order to reign as sovereigns in the kingdom of truth and justice.”

And Herder adds: “It is incontestable that such an end is the only one that can fit the intentions of Providence regarding our species.”

The philosopher, on coming before the mystery of Christianity, confesses it. His humanism is converted to Christianity!, for “what higher end than the Christian could be taught to peoples?”

[p. 244] Upon Europe, which Greece and Rome had civilized, there took place, centuries later, the formidable invasion of the Germanic race. Those men of “coarse customs handed down by their ancestors; accustomed to a harsh and severe climate and to the necessity of a military constitution,” became civilized thanks to a spiritual conquest: “the Sign that gave the universe hope and faith was the Cross. A shudder ran through the limbs of the savage warriors. On perceiving the Sign, they laid down their arms.”

So that “IT IS INCONTESTABLE THAT THE ONLY END THAT CAN FIT THE INTENTIONS OF PROVIDENCE REGARDING OUR SPECIES” is to form those men of God, through true love; through the “love that is always more love” (in the magnificent phrase of Simmel): Christian charity.

Herder bows, full of respect, before the figure of the Founder of a Kingdom so great in its object, so unlimited in its duration, so submissive to simple and living principles “whose means are so powerful that the sphere of this earthly life, it was said, grew narrow before Him.”

III. What other revolution was at once so peaceful and so swift? How could the earth be filled, with such feeble instruments, in so marvelous a manner? What episode of universal history engendered an indefinite series of consequences like the one that begins with Jesus Christ?

For this reason the philosopher of historical humanism (who had seen the action of Providence only in the act of creating man in the image and likeness of God), on coming into contact, as he follows the course of time, with the advent of Jesus Christ, writes these lines, which seem to us the expression of the purest and most limpid beauty: “Jesus, may your peaceful image rise, solitary, over the field of history!”

IV. In our wicked century, while evil rages and the clamor of battles shakes the foundation of civilization; in the bustle of the homicidal [p. 245] struggle, Herder's words are repeated with religious unction: “MAY YOUR PEACEFUL IMAGE RISE, SOLITARY, OVER THE FIELD OF UNIVERSAL HISTORY!”

### ANTHROPOS

I. Man —anthropos— is, according to Herder, the only being who rises up on the earth he treads and, with his hands now free by virtue of the vertical posture, undertakes works of art. Man is the maker, fabricator or artist. His intelligence, with the aid of the tongue, elaborates language, basis of history, key of culture, foundation of science, precious bond of civil life.

For —Herder adds— man is the only animal that has a “horizon.” The beasts, keeping their heads perpetually bent toward the ground, only look at the earth and cannot contemplate it. They seek sustenance: toward the earth they bend in order to subsist, and in it they are at last used up.

III. Not so the anthropos, the creature made in the image and likeness of God.

Man looks around, forward and backward; he turns within his circle, within his horizon. He is not only greed and gluttony, but contemplative disinterestedness and creative activity. In this lies the dignity of his significance.

IV. Upon the animal, which is used up in itself, man forges history, with no other mission than that of giving forth from himself all that his nature contains.

He is not perfect in a day, but perfectible through the centuries and the peoples. He does not reach his end at once: he goes on realizing it, as his essential faculties unfold. Human nature is in historical development upon nature.

To look around is to be able to have the universe offered to oneself in distinct but concurrent forms, within social development. Culture is horizon; because, in the first place, man is —as Bergson teaches— the maker who can and knows how to vary the conditions of his making; the intelligent being who creates, above the monotonous animal industry, his own great industry. [p. 246] Animals are reproducers; man is an inventor. To invent is to discover; the inventor knows how to see the hidden; he unveils it, discovers it.

Moreover, another singularity, still more exalted, is, in the second place, that of our stock. We refer to the property of our mind's being speculative; of asking about the being of things, and no longer merely about their use or exploitation.

Man inquires into the being of the world and into his own being. He is the only creature that asks: “what is it?”, and also: “why is it?”

“ALL THIS SPIRIT OF INQUIRY, OF KNOWING WITHOUT INTEREST, MAN OWES TO HIS STANDING BEFORE A HORIZON, which goes on widening indefinitely. We are not tied or confined —like the beasts— to the earth.

And, as we travel along our roads, which are those of history, we men have been able not only to meditate, but to have the universe offered to us as a spectacle.

No one had seen the world before man! Man became a subject of contemplation. One life among the lives ceased to trace, upon the field of existence, the routes of animal greed. Existence became spectacle.

And the disinterested vision of reality was perhaps as noble as, or even more so than, the pure inquiry of thought! For it created fine art; as the latter, science and philosophy.

If we inquire, we philosophize; if we contemplate, we are artists. And in dancing —according to Nietzsche's beautiful expression— we come to be works of art. All this because the anthropos is the creature before his horizon. A creature free before the world, made in the image and likeness of God.

VIII. But our horizon, which is offered to sight, is always a limited circle.

The horizon that surrounds us and sets us free, in the end always limits us; for it always encircles us, however much we enlarge it.

IX. Man feels himself dependent on an ineffable mystery; a mystery alive in his consciousness.

[p. 247] If we raise our point of view, the circle widens concomitantly, the horizon recedes: if we ascend to the highest summits, the radius of the circle grows in the same proportion.

And then there occurs to us the possibility of contemplating ever higher; of glimpsing more deeply.

Where the greatest circle closes —however enormous it may already be—; where space and time limit and intersect one another, like the great circles of a sphere, we notice only constant changes and fleeting appearances.

X. Then we penetrate into our own consciousness, which gives us a perennial present, an eternal Presence, before which time is annihilated and space ceases to be.

Religion is the other distinctive attribute of man, of the anthropos, of the being with a horizon, who, on rising inwardly, finds God. The ascent to the summits has dissolved for us the details of reality. All beings are annihilated in the marvelous distance, full of sunlight; and, despite the illumination, the different beings are lost in Being.

### BIOGRAFÍA E HISTORIA — BIOGRAPHY AND HISTORY

Schopenhauer, from his own point of view, which is that of systematic pessimism, has analyzed the respective value that history, biography, autobiography and poetic creation have as instruction about man.

Systematic pessimism is grounded on the philosopher's voluntarism or atheism. For it is obvious that if the essence of the world is “the will” —perennially greedy and never satisfied—, no work of the will can be lasting. For this very reason, in the anguished race of living, men, like the nations of history, spend their lives in hoping for what they desire (thinking it preferable to what they possess), only afterward, once the object of their longing is attained, to become victims of the inevitable disappointment that characterizes human existence, ever afflicted by being desirous and fickle, by being incorrigibly fickle and desirous!

II. The will in itself, as the essence of the world, is utterly free. Nothing could be or be placed above it. Everything is will.

[p. 248] Yet the cosmic will is objectified in “Ideas,” in Platonic archetypes, which thus turn out to be, according to the philosopher's intuition, “immediate objectifications of the will to live.” The phenomena of the world, the events of history, are the immediate objectifications, lacking meaning and proportion.

What interests the thinker is not that incoherent swarm of meaningless events. Always the same passions, through new cities; always the frustrated outcome, the disproportionate and truncated ideal, and constant failure! Always.

The lure of life is feigned by the unsatisfied will, which lifts the generations into the air. But man, the type, the paradigm, the “immediate objectification of the will to live,” is the same.

For this reason, to the multitude of historical happenings, lacking meaning, as to the multitude of vulgar men —“wholesale product of nature's factory”—, Schopenhauer prefers the works that truly reflect the human type; the great artistic and literary productions; as he also prefers the geniuses, who are capable, before the archetypes, before the Platonic ideas, of a disinterested intuition.

Don Quixote de la Mancha, Hamlet and La vida es sueño teach more about man than the variegated episodes of political and social history.

In the great works of art, “independently of the principle of sufficient reason,” one sees the paradigm, the absolute, which is reflected in aesthetic creation, and shows in its essence the detail of human life, seen for eternity. This is what constitutes the truth of man. Works of art teach, not the meaningless bustle of historical happening, but human nature.

Between history and art are situated biography and autobiography. Both genres are superior to history; because they show the universal in the singular better, and for that reason come intimately close to poetic creation.

The biographer, by confining his effort to a single personality and analyzing it, by secretly sympathizing with it, like the novelist or the poet, sees the [p. 249] Man of men. He disregards the jumble of meaningless events, and approaches the absolute.

And still closer to the human type, and reflecting it, comes the ingenious autobiography. These books do draw back the veils that hide the whole being of man. Schopenhauer, therefore, prefers the biographical and the autobiographical to the purely historical.

In sum: the metaphysician knows (as Aristotle already taught in his Poetics) that “poetry is more philosophical and better than history.” For history carries shackled to its foot the fetter of what is called “reality,” which is positively nothing but the confusion and dispersion of phenomena. Poetry confronts the eternal types, and expresses them.

VIII. Homer, according to the Stagirite, is constrained in the magnificence of his creative fantasy only by two supreme canons: verisimilitude and necessity.

Achilles moves in the absolute, sustained and animated by the Homeric imagination; whereas the historian must refer to the time, the space and the circumstances of the action; to the “historical moment.”

Alcibiades is not, like Achilles, the heroic and free personage of the sovereign world of fantasy; but the protagonist or deuteragonist of a human tragedy, “all too human.”

True universal history is compendiously contained, for Schopenhauer, in eternal art, in genial, absolute creation. The rest is only the useless bustle of an incongruous and fleeting world, the phenomenon as confused as it is transitory. Only the universal, seen in itself, can be an object of science. “History is a knowing, not a science.”

### CIENCIA E HISTORIA — SCIENCE AND HISTORY

I. Schopenhauer's thought on the superiority of autobiography and biography over history depends, in sum, on the fact that what matters to the philosopher is knowledge of man; but not of a particular, singular man, but of Man, of the Platonic human type, of the paradigm.

For this reason, in biography and autobiography the philosophical [p. 250] investigator finds more elements for approaching the human type than he finds in the variegated succession of historical episodes; which, for Schopenhauer, being the products of a blind will that fills time with its pain, lack meaning.

For this reason too he prefers, like Aristotle, poetry to history: Res magis philosophica et melior poesis est, quam historia. [A thing more philosophical and better is poetry than history.] More philosophical and better than history is, for the Stagirite, poetry.

Schopenhauer remarks that the opposition between the two words, poetry and history, brings out with surprising clarity the meaning and the origin of the first term. It signifies the creative, inventive act, as opposed to investigation, to search, to erudition.

III. History likewise cannot be considered a science, in the philosopher's conception. For in every order of things the facts are innumerable, the number of individuals is infinite. Moreover, the variety of their differences is wholly ineffable. How could a science such as history, which deals only with individuals, then be realized? It would be the science of the individual; but we have just seen that this science is utterly impossible; since individuals are infinite, like facts, in every order of things in the world; and the differences that individuals display “cause vertigo in the mind curious to know,” which must then resign itself to ignorance!

Moreover, history refers to what has been only once, and never again exists as it was. Therefore one must abandon all hope of integrating a true scientific knowledge within the possibilities of history.

But there is yet another reason, which paralyzes at birth every attempt to construct history scientifically; and this reason is: all science is subordination, whereas history is only coordination.

In what does scientific coordination consist?: “Science classifies the innumerable multiplicity of facts, and groups it under the concepts of species, which in turn it subordinates to the notions of genus. In this way it opens the path to knowledge of the general and of the particular, which embraces the multitude of individuals, since what is valid for all of them does not require a peculiar examination of each thing considered in itself.” [p. 251]

V. But this is not all; the sciences themselves are subordinated to one another, and form as it were the diverse, intimately connected chapters of science.

Lastly, to complete the unity of scientific thought, one must consider the nature of the philosophical longing, which constitutes the constant search for a supreme unity.

Spencer, for his part, has defined the sciences as “partial unifications of knowledge”; whereas philosophy is “total unification,” or at least aspires to be.

Nothing similar to the foregoing can occur when it is a matter of history. Instead of the subordination of species to genera, of phenomena to laws, of problems and theorems to axioms and definitions (which is what constitutes the proper structure of science), its organism, its system, its unity, history can offer us only an order, a coordination, not a subordination.

It is true that particular events are arranged, ordered into centuries, reigns, epochs and eras: but this is not equivalent to the other. He who knows that the “Thirty Years” War took place in the fifteenth century knows nothing, properly speaking, of the most complex episodes of that war; and what is more fundamental than all this, he can foresee nothing, however circumstantially he may know all the episodes of a single war, in relation to the other wars of history.

Therefore it is only a matter of an ordering, not of the subordination that makes scientific prevision possible.

“If I know, for example, the general laws of the triangle, I shall be able also to state the properties of a given triangle; if I know the characters common to all mammals, I shall be able to affirm them a priori of the bat I have just caught, even before dissecting it. But the same does not hold for history. Here there is no objective generality of the concept; there exists only a subjective generality.”

IX. In sum, history is not a science, according to Schopenhauer's opinion. It is only “a knowing.” But the philosopher himself confesses that it is an [p. 252] essential and indispensable knowing; because even though he prefers knowledge of types to that of individuals, there are not only Platonic types and paradigms; there are individuals and particular events, distributed in time and in space. Therefore, beside poetry, which manages to express the absolute type; beside also the sciences that foresee the future, we need this other “knowing,” in order to approach, as little imperfectly as possible, the knowledge of reality.

### HEGEL Y SCHOPENHAUER — HEGEL AND SCHOPENHAUER

According to Hegel, the process of universal history has as its object to set the Spirit free. Every people that has been capable of rising to the integration of the State is a historical people, one of the “moments” of universal history; for the peoples that did not rise to the form of the State are not historical peoples. And the spirit of each people possesses its particular truth. This passes into the history of humanity.

But the whole of history has an end, an idea; and the idea of universal history is, through the dialectical movement of the centuries, freedom. The Spirit of the universe reveals itself in historical vicissitudes through the realization or attainment of this supreme good.

“To him who looks upon the world rationally, the world appears rational. The two things are ‘reciprocal.’ Reason governs the development of history. Historical events have happened rationally, dialectically. The progress of the world is ‘progress in the consciousness of freedom.’”

In the empires of the classical Orient there is only one free man: the despot who rules them according to his own will.

In the Greek world the consciousness of freedom appears; but only a few are free. Only in the Christian and Germanic world are all men free, or will they become so; for THE REALIZATION OF FREEDOM IS THE DESIGN OF HISTORY.

The absolute, universal idea stands above opposition and struggle. The particular and the finite disappear and, by means of struggle and the vanishing of the particular and contingent, the universal is engendered.

The genius, the “historical individual,” the hero, “realizes the end in accordance with the higher concept of the Spirit.” Heroes are not spokesmen of a world that has already been made; but emblems and authors of the world that will be made.

The great “historical individuals” apprehend the higher universal content. They make it their end. They are the ones who realize the end, in accordance with the concept of the Spirit. In this sense they must be called heroes. Their justification does not lie in the existing state of things. The source from which they draw it is another.

[p. 253] “If they succeed in reaching their end, they do not pass to tranquil enjoyment, they are not happy. WHAT THEY ARE IS THEIR WORK. Perhaps carrying out their end has been bitter for them; and at the moment of attaining it, they die young like Alexander, or are assassinated like Caesar, or deported like Napoleon.”

But they are the ones who have taught other men what they seek; for it is truly difficult to know what one wants.

One may want something and yet be dissatisfied. “THE HISTORICAL INDIVIDUALS HAVE RIGHT ON THEIR SIDE. They are the clairvoyant. They know the truth of their world and of their time. They know what the concept is. The universal that is coming.”

Against Hegelianism, which incorporates into universal History its sense of full and human liberation, Schopenhauer, whose conception of the universe implies systematic pessimism, replies:

“Whoever, like me, cannot help seeing always the same thing in every different history: just as in a kaleidoscope the same things reappear at every turn in different configurations, will never be able to feel such interest or passion for the historical.”

“Nonetheless, history has its peculiar value; because human life is so short and fugitive, and is divided among so many millions of individuals —hurled in precipitation into the jaws, ever immoderately open, of the monster that awaits them: oblivion—, that it is a task worthy of gratitude to save something from it; the memory of what is most important and of greatest interest; the principal events and personages of the universal shipwreck of the world!”

History, from beginning to end, tells only of wars; and in all wars there is only one aim: to rob. Voltaire rightly said that in all wars it was only a matter of robbing. As soon as a people notices in itself an abundance of strength, it falls upon its neighbors, in order, instead of living from its own labor, to appropriate that of others. This is the subject matter of universal History, and of its episodes and heroic deeds.

At the bottom of human development in time, the essential will shows itself, insatiable and utterly free. The principle of the existence of the world lacks reason. It is a blind will to live. The truth of History lies in the saying of Calderón, the poet to whom his Christian feelings gave a more perspicacious knowledge than that attained by the sages of antiquity: “Man's greatest crime is to have been born.”

The Hegelians, for whom the philosophy of history becomes the principal end of Philosophy, must refer themselves to Plato; who never ceases to affirm that the object of Philosophy is the eternal, the immutable; not what already is in one way or another; only the “Ideas” remain, and time is ideal. This is the opinion of Plato and that of Kant.

[p. 254] “All the dreamers busy building speculative constructions on the march of the world or, as they declare it, of history, forget that the capital truth of all philosophy is that it is always a matter of the same being. Becoming and being born are pure appearances.”

### LA FILOSOFÍA DE LA HISTORIA — THE PHILOSOPHY OF HISTORY

I. Art is always beside history; because in a certain way, as Hegel thinks, they are unified. What is art? The possession of nature by man, by virtue of its transformation.

Man cannot take hold of nature except by transforming it. How does man transform nature? By working. To work is to transform what nature is.

The farmer like the sculptor, the laborer like the scholar. All take hold of natural forces and transform them.

II. By imprinting a new form on the real, with our work, we create a new world: a world that is no longer that of nature, but that of culture.

This equation can be formulated: NATURE PLUS WORK EQUALS CULTURE. Culture and not nature!

Man, with his task, incorporates the Spirit into nature. In this way he creates the forms of civilization.

It is no longer a matter of the natural world, but of the synthetic region in which history unfolds.

This new world, added to the natural one, is the orb of thought and freedom; because thought and freedom constitute, essentially, the differential attributes of man.

Work is the action of thought and of freedom upon nature. For this reason art and history are indissolubly unified, as the work of intelligence, effort and freedom.

The baneful error of all naturalism, as an interpretation of history, is perceived with complete clarity. It is true that the matter of history is nature, the cosmic force: but the form that man imprints [p. 255] upon natural energies; what comes to constitute the work of art —agricultural or aesthetic, scientific or literary— is not in itself natural energy, but spiritual, because it is intelligence and freedom, that is, thought.

Just as human culture could not be explained by abstracting from nature, it is vain and impossible to attempt to explain it by abstracting from the spirit.

In culture nature is subordinated to human work. This subordination constitutes the historical. It is history.

The diverse historical peoples are not infinite, they are finite. They cannot, by their action, exhaust the historical. At last there comes the inevitable moment of their development —however lofty and brilliant it may have been—, which turns into irremediable decadence. Incomparable Greece itself could not exercise its luminous historical action lastingly. Rome replaced it with its political hegemony. The sense of the historical was transformed, so that the human, universal stream, infinite in its daring, might be tinted with its new and extraordinary hue. The victory of Rome is that of universality; and with Latin universality there is then conjoined the genial contribution of the Greeks to the many-minded work of thought and freedom.

No historical people can fill history, as no individual genius exhausts thought. Our human destiny is successive; because we do not incarnate perfection or the absolute. Our good is constituted by the effort of all, peoples and men, exceptional nations and heroic individuals. Through the centuries history has meaning, as a daughter of the Spirit, not as an unintelligible synthesis of the blind act of natural forces.

VII. This new world, supernatural; that is, superimposed upon the natural, is governed by thought and freedom.

Rome in turn, when it had given forth all its effort, after having formed the universal consciousness of the peoples, is cast down before the new peoples. Christianity and the Germanic invasion are synthesized, over against Latin universality, as a new element of culture. And freedom proves at last triumphant; because Germanic individualism and Christian charity are superimposed upon the Roman forms, and, by incorporating their remains, engender the cultural movement of modern Europe. [p. 256]

VIII. A philosophy of history that disdains the physical environment of culture is already judged, irremissibly; but a philosophy of history that claims to explain, as Taine thought, by the sole action of “race, environment and historical moment,” the extremely complex development of civilization is likewise impossible today. The Hegelian synthesis of history avoids both reefs. For the Hegelian absolute implies, essentially, its own transformation. It is a being that perfects itself by changing. For this reason the philosophy of history assumes singular significance in Hegel's philosophy. And the history of philosophy is integrated with philosophy itself, as its form in time.

The meaning of history seems to be, in sum, the meaning of philosophy.

### HISTORIOGRAFÍA — HISTORIOGRAPHY

I. The publication of Dilthey's work continues, and has reached the fourth volume of the Spanish version, which we owe to the humanist Don Eugenio Ímaz. Among the essays contained in the volume just issued is the one entitled “Historiografía,” which is like a philosophical synthesis of the history of history, from the Greeks to the nineteenth century.

The Fondo de Cultura Económica earlier published the books of Cassirer and Meinecke on the Philosophy of the Enlightenment and the Genesis of historicism, which almost coincide at several points with the thoughts on historiography set forth by Dilthey; but even if some of the Dilthey opinions are developed or discussed in those books, it is obvious that this essay of Dilthey's, so rich and seminal, can be regarded as the starting point of “historiography.” At least, we ourselves have found no earlier publication that sets out with such ease and vigor the phases of the philosophical conception of history.

III. Let us relish at once, in all its worth, this acute saying: “There have been great historians from the moment the Greeks begin to look upon the bustle of the world with their great artist's eyes.” What Dilthey affirms displays a beautiful [p. 257] truth. Only in Greece could history be engendered. As the historical world unfolds in time, the scientific comprehension of man's historical nature grows, according to the philosopher. Nietzsche's error —according to Dilthey— consisted in thinking that brooding over oneself leads to the comprehension of man. Man is comprehensible not only in history. In “the historical world” one can know about the human, about man's capacity for evil as much as for good. Hermetic brooding over oneself engenders of itself “the great Nietzschean misery of an overstrained subjectivity.”

We think, however, that for the knowledge of man we need as much Nietzsche’s “brooding” as Dilthey’s “understanding.” But it turns out that, if history was born in Greece with Herodotus and Thucydides, if “the highest artistic capacity the world has ever known” gave history of itself (which proves the close relation that obtains between art and history), “the Enlightenment of the eighteenth century, to which its ‘unhistorical’ character is imputed, has produced a new conception of history, with Voltaire, Frederick the Great, Hume, Robertson and Gibbon.”

It is high time to discredit the absurd formulas that stem from a lack of study and knowledge of the different historical epochs. “The ‘unhistorical’ century created the celebrated Voltairean denomination philosophy of history.” Before Voltaire no proper name had been coined for the philosophical meditation on history; although the philosophy of history already existed, at least, since the distant days of Saint Augustine and Orosius.

In the works of the great historians of the eighteenth century “the idea of the solidarity and progress of the human race casts its light upon all peoples and epochs.” For the first time universal history knows a connection that has been drawn from empirical consideration. Voltaire’s Essay on the Customs and the Spirit of Nations is the first universal history, and it inaugurates the philosophical-historical meditation on human culture. “Its foundations are constituted by the completely free application of historical criticism, which stops neither before the most sacred sanctuaries of the past, and a comparative method that embraces all the stages of humanity.”

[p. 258] To achieve the purpose it was necessary to relate, to link scientifically, the natural world with the historical one, and to apply the thought of evolution to the lucid conception of this link.

The eighteenth century practiced all this; “but in the vital feeling of this century lay the limit of its historical enlightenment.” Why? Because the thinkers of the “Enlightenment” look at everything in relation to their own time; and whatever humanity produced and accomplished before is merely preparation, introduction, effort to bring about the “century of lights.”

II. To conceive universal history from this narrow angle led to deforming it; to look askance at the human past from the height of “illuminism” hid from its adepts the characteristic individuality of the different historical epochs, which take on meaning and value, in the opinion of the philosophers of the “Enlightenment,” only insofar as they led man, across two centuries, to the preferred historical standpoint. “These spirited and confident men,” adds Dilthey, “see in the past only the stages that lead to the height they occupy.”

III. Thus the exalted self-consciousness of “illuminism,” forced to look at the panorama of history as the antecedent of the century that was the precursor of the Revolution, engendered of itself the modern philosophy of history; but it could not help subordinating human evolution to its own absolute and abstract ideal. The task of the new century will consist in interpreting the stages of historical evolution with disinterestedness and sympathy, with a real and true “historical sense,” without the generous prejudice of the philosophers of the “Enlightenment,” who were victims of “a divine unconcern in the face of the methodical wisdom of earlier centuries, and a somewhat excessive idea of their own merits, of the happy sovereignty of the new spirit, symbolized by the name of Voltaire.”

### LAS RAZONES DEL CORAZÓN — THE REASONS OF THE HEART

I. Pascal’s saying is famous: “the heart has its reasons that reason does not know.” But, as a rule, it has not been interpreted with the intent of [p. 259] philosophical investigation. One thinks of a region impermeable to pure reason; this hermetic world is not analyzed in itself. To give the phrase of Pascal its due, justifying it, it was necessary that someone carry out its detailed and valid analysis. Only by making the meaning of these “reasons” visible could the Pascalian saying reveal its profound content, its real significance.

That, precisely, is what Max Scheler brought to a successful conclusion. For him, what we call the human heart “is not a pure chaos of sentimental states.” These sentimental states are not associated with the other psychic data according to any causal laws whatever. The human heart has within itself an intimate articulation; it forms “the articulated reverse of the Cosmos of all the possible lovable characters of things.”

What is this structure of the heart? How are its component members articulated?

“What we call the mind or, its symbol, the human heart, possesses something strictly analogous to logic; something that, within its own domain, does not coincide with the logic of the understanding.”

The reasons of the heart are not those on which the understanding has already, previously, decided. It is not a matter of seeing in them the determinism of pure reason; they are not objective reasons. Yet they are “reasons,” in the sense of motives, of desires.

“The heart has ITS reasons; ITS OWN REASONS, of which the understanding knows nothing and can know nothing. That is, it is a matter of objective evidences about facts, for which the understanding is as blind as the blind man is for colors and the deaf man for sounds.”

The Romantics imagined that “the heart” means only “the sum of confused states, of obscure and indeterminate transports or intense forces that push man from one side to the other.”

It is because “the heart,” according to Pascal, means a microcosm that grasps values. These reasons are inaccessible to pure reason. It is a psychic whole that works with precision, and places before our eyes a sphere of facts, rigorously objective: “the most fundamental and objective among all the possible spheres of facts.”

[p. 260] The Middle Ages knew a culture of the heart, as something independent of intellectual culture. This culture is the one that raised the Gothic cathedrals, that arranged and carried out the Crusades, that instituted the mendicant orders, that served as the basis for reforming, within orthodoxy, the relaxed ecclesiastical discipline. But the man of today is ignorant of that exquisite culture, based on “the reasons of the heart that reason does not know.”

In the modern age, “the whole of emotional human life is looked at askance, not as a symbolic language full of meaning, in which objective relations that govern the significance of life are discovered; but as absolutely blind events, which pass through us like any natural phenomena whatever, which it will be necessary to master, at times technically, in order to gain profits and avoid harms.”

The illustrious philosopher, a disciple of Husserl, applies to the investigation of the emotional world the exquisite vindicating method of essences; which leads him in the end to the affirmation and meditation of those “irrational essences” (values), which are given living and actual in human feeling, like the essences themselves, which intuition grasps, starting from phenomena, in order to rise to the ideal world. Thus Scheler completes the work of phenomenology, bringing it closer to Plato. For what Plato is worth in the history of universal thought is that he pinpointed, in all his Dialogues, the problem of the relations between being and value.
