II. BUT THESE VALUES TEND TOWARD UNITY, ARE GIVEN IN THEIR FULLNESS, CONSTANTLY CALLING TOWARD THEMSELVES THE OTHER UNITIES OF THEIR KIND: THE GOOD, THE HOLY, THE BEAUTIFUL, THE JUST, TEND TO SYNTHESIZE THEMSELVES IN THE HUMAN PERSON.

III. This last is a completely unequivocal character of the world of values. The microcosm of the heart completes, in its form, the cosmic work. And since man is capable only of creating historical values, but not absolute values; or, if you like, goods of culture, but not eternal values, human limitation and the tendency toward synthesis (characteristic of the world of values) jointly postulate the existence of another Person, in whom the absolute synthesis of those reasons of the heart, which reason does not know, is effected. In postulating the foregoing, the idea [p. 261] of God arises, as that of a person who is absolutely good, holy, beautiful, just. Thus the sense of Platonism would be realized; the solution of the problem of the relations of the real and the ideal, a problem that occupies the philosopher of Athens in all his Dialogues.

In certain epochs of history, attention has been given by preference to the problem of being. In other, different epochs, as in the Middle Ages, to the problem of value; but philosophy, to fulfill its destiny, can do no other than postulate the existence of the divine Person, who realizes in his being, within his ineffable unity, the solution of Plato’s problem.

### SPENGLER

I. The Library of the Peruvian Society of Philosophy has been enriched with the book entitled La filosofía de Oswald Spengler, by Dr. Jorge del Busto Vargas, professor of sociology and history of philosophy at the Catholic University of Peru.

It seems that in contemporary thought the opinion of critics regarding Spengler’s production is by now unanimous. The Decline of the West is a valuable book as regards the enormous wealth of study and facts that Spengler set out; but the synthesis achieved, the organizing principle, falls far short of being equally valuable. It is, as Messer says, a skepticism, because the philosopher declares the objective knowledge of truth impossible, although, within his skepticism, he establishes certain assertions as true. It is impossible to admit a firm criterion of truth if all the fruits of a culture are subordinated to it, and truth itself is nothing but the exponent of each cultural formation, like art, religion or technique.

Dr. Del Busto Vargas’s book is detailed, precise, valuable.

In its last part, after setting out the outline of Spengler’s work, he takes up the analysis of the antecedents of that philosophy, and refers especially to Goethe and Nietzsche. It is evident that Goethe and Nietzsche were two of the great inspirers of Spengler. But one must also mention Rickert and Frobenius, whose book Culture as a Living Being contains the idea of the Paideuma, which definitively inspired the Spenglerian conception. As regards Rickert, what Messer enunciates, citing the distinguished German thinker himself, is a great truth: “The logical foundations of this morphology —the Spenglerian— had already been refuted long before they were written” (by Rickert himself).

[p. 262] The theorist of the Decline of the West wrote: “I owe the philosophy of my book to that of Goethe, and only to a much lesser extent to that of Nietzsche. In the following words I would not wish to see changed so much as a tittle: The divinity is active in the living, not in the dead. It is in what becomes and transforms itself, not in what is already produced and petrified.”

This thought of Goethe inspires the constant recourse to intuition, which is the only recourse left to Spengler for history.

As regards Nietzsche, the reader of Spengler receives no surprise concerning the idea of opposing “culture” to “civilization,” after having read and meditated the brilliant pages devoted to this same subject in The Will to Power.

But there is another fundamental influence in Spengler’s work. It is that of Bergson. Messer already left it written in this regard: “In this intuitive consideration of the living, of the historical, Spengler feels himself influenced especially by Goethe and Nietzsche (his affinity with Bergson is also much stronger than he admits).”

Goethe’s astonishing thought, which declares the divinity active in the living, in becoming and transformation, not in what is already produced and “petrified,” is the very idea of Bergsonism, which opposes to the “world already made” the “world in the making.” This opposition constitutes one of the principal antitheses of Bergsonism.

Real duration, freedom, the life of the spirit, all fit within the first thesis; matter, on the other hand, as the object of the intelligence that analyzes it from outside, corresponds to the petrified world, to the reality that was already made and done.

In sum, Spengler’s philosophy is an interpretation, based on naturalism and skepticism, that corresponds to an order of ideas already superseded in the historical moment we have reached. But the author’s great erudition cannot be denied, although it must be corrected in many details. The assimilation of the evolution of cultures to the seasons of the year, which grants them a cyclical spring, summer, autumn and winter, within an inexorable “destiny,” amounts to no more than an ingenious but not very profound thought. And on this thought Spengler bases, or tries to base, his prediction of the decline of the West!

[p. 263] The Methuselah-ism, the lack of differentiation between Nature and Culture, proves intolerable for a philosophical reader, after Rickert’s admirable book entitled Natural Science and Cultural Science. The spirit of the philosophy of our time runs counter to all those hasty assimilations between natural becoming and the development of history. Cultural science looks today toward the world of values, and departs from the aim of natural science, which consists in discovering laws or uniformities of nature. Spengler himself has had to declare it so, in affirming: “Not only are there no historical laws, but it is contradictory to strive to find them.” But instead of the concept of law, imported from natural science into the historical field, Spengler, retreating to pagan fatalism, speaks to us of the “destiny” of culture or of “the cultures.” The German philosopher, like the Greek tragedian, does not know, nor can he know, the conditions of Oedipus’s crime; what he knows or pretends to know, and so insinuates to his readers, is that Oedipus is condemned to be a parricide. Spengler condemns European Occident to decline, and to that end wrote his book!

Dr. Jorge del Busto Vargas’s monograph is an essay worthy of claiming the attention of American philosophical thought.

### LA FILOSOFÍA DE LA HISTORIA — THE PHILOSOPHY OF HISTORY

It is an interesting and curious fact that can be verified regarding the production of the great masterworks of the philosophy of history. It consists in this: that after a great historical crisis, philosophical creation, reflecting on historical events, has regularly followed. So it happened with Saint Augustine and the City of God, with Hegel and his Lectures on the Philosophy of History.

Alfred Weber, in his book on the History of Culture (Spanish version by L. Recaséns Siches), says: “In times such as the present, the philosophy of history tends to arise.” In the year 410 (A.D.), when Rome was destroyed and when the whole Greco-Roman civilization shook on its foundations, Saint Augustine wrote his City of God.

[p. 264] The timeliness of philosophical reflection on history and its synchronism with great catastrophes is explicable, because in the great crises of human development the very foundations of civilization seem to be called into question, the formerly unquestionable bases of culture. Just as Saint Augustine tends to save from the universal disaster of Antiquity, thanks to his profound philosophical thought, the perennial work of the Church, the traditions that make up the City of God, so Hegel (who, in accord with the principles of the French Revolution, maintains that the meaning of universal history is the thought and attainment of freedom) aims in his turn to place in the secular work of the State, incarnation of Law, the meaning of human historical formation; because, according to the philosopher’s opinion, the State “marks the path of God in the world.”

We contemporaries have lived, in the first half of the twentieth century, one of these ill-starred epochs, in which the bases of culture and of human coexistence seem to be under revision. Hence there is reproduced in our days, in the work of philosophers, an attitude similar to that represented in their respective centuries by Saint Augustine and Hegel.

Marx creates his materialist theory of history. The economic phenomenon, which had never before appeared in the foreground of the explanation of History, rises in importance until it becomes the fundamental principle of the elaboration of culture.

Besides economic determinism, which has continued to be elaborated, extensively, by the adepts and the critics of Marxism, Spengler and Berdyaev alike represent new interpretations of universal history. In the opinion the former holds, the evolution of Western culture places us contemporaries in the throes of decline. The cyclical evolution of the different cultures —according to Spengler— by virtue of the “cultural destiny,” has been fulfilled for our Western one in a long decadence, which is characteristic of our “historical moment.” So it is impossible to pretend to escape the rigor of “destiny,” and we contemporaries must convince ourselves that an intrinsic fatality leads us to “the decline of the West.”

The prophetic spirit —false or true— that inspires the German thinker also inspires Berdyaev. This latter thinker suggests that we are going [p. 265] toward “a new Middle Ages,” because we happen to live in a state comparable to that of the fall of the Roman Empire and of the ancient civilization of the third century, “when only Christianity spiritually saved the world from definitive ruin and decomposition.”

Everything in the modern world proceeds, according to this second prophet, from the phenomenon he calls “the decomposition of the Renaissance.” The intellectual movements that make up the history of the modern world proceed from humanism, which departs from God and aims at the salvation of humanity by human energies alone. This is what has brought with it and engendered the characters of the present epoch. But symptoms of a spiritual reaction are already noticeable, because the old categories of the modern world and the forces of civilization are not capable of solving the problems.

Yet not everything reduces to the prophetic ideas of thinkers like Oswald Spengler and Nicolas Berdyaev. The philosophical thought of history, without the spirit of prophecy; that is, the authentic philosophical thought on history, has been elaborated in the work of thinkers like Rickert and Dilthey; hence the constant preoccupation with its study is noticeable in our time, in the universities and centers of culture of the world. In Spain, Ortega y Gasset has been formulating his thought of “historical reason,” which, inspired by Dilthey, nevertheless possesses singular originality.

Alfred Weber’s observation is confirmed: “in times such as the present, the ‘Philosophy of History’ may arise or, if we limit ourselves to what can be grasped empirically and to its comprehension as a whole, the ‘Sociology of History.’”

### LA FILOSOFÍA Y LA HISTORIA — PHILOSOPHY AND HISTORY

I. Philosophy considers being sub specie aeternitatis; history looks at it askance sub specie durationis; but both points of view are essential for its explanation and comprehension.

While the metaphysician, in seeking the essence of time, holds it still in his consideration of both essences, interwoven with each other, duration and succession (which, as essences, are immutable), the historian plunges into the events of time, and tries to glimpse them in their genuine individual significance, intuitively. At first sight one would say that an unfathomable separation obtains between the historical and the philosophical; but both, history and philosophy, are universal knowledge. The sciences must confine themselves, necessarily, to the definition of their formal object, to the abstraction

[p. 266] All the possible combinations of history and philosophy have already been realized. These combinations are, namely: the history of philosophy, the philosophy of philosophy, the history of history and the philosophy of history.

The first to arise was the history of philosophy; and the first historian of philosophy, creator and organizer of many other sciences, was Aristotle, in the admirable first Book of the Metaphysics. The Stagirite never undertakes an original philosophical study without referring it to tradition. Thus the First Book we are discussing, after analyzing the nature of science and declaring that philosophy concerns itself above all with the inquiry into causes and principles, analyzes the doctrine of the “ancients” “concerning the first causes and the principles of things.” Only after having studied the thought of the pre-Socratic philosophers does he refer to the system of his master Plato, in order next to “refute the opinions of the ancients concerning principles,” and then to begin the study of “being as being.” Only then does he set out his own philosophical thought.

II. The philosophy of history is born much later, with the work of Saint Augustine and Orosius. The great Christian philosopher and his disciple lay the foundations of the philosophy of history by synthesizing the elements of the Israelite and Christian tradition with the history created by the Greco-Romans: “Two loves raised two Cities: love of self even to contempt of God, the earthly City; and love of God even to contempt of self, the heavenly City. The one glories in itself, the other in the Lord.”

The great historical crisis that the French Revolution signifies produced in Hegel’s philosophical consciousness the other great monument of the philosophy of history, the Lectures on Universal History, just as the invasion of the barbarians, which ruined the hegemony of Rome, engendered the City of God.

III. Today two new historical-philosophical standpoints arise: the history of History and the philosophy of Philosophy.

The history of History is described by its cultivators, for example J. T. Shotwell, in the following terms: “Until very recently, history has had no historians. Histories have been written of almost all the other subjects there are or may be, of literature, of philosophy, of the arts and sciences and, above all, of politics. But until very few years ago, with the exception of a few didactic books, the history of history [p. 267] has remained unwritten. Clio, though the oldest of the muses, has been so busy finding out the past of her sisters that she has forgotten her own, and it seems that her own readers have rarely asked about it.” If one suppresses the foregoing text, the curious circumlocution “histories have been written of almost all the subjects there are AND MAY BE” —because it seems to us absurd to refer the concept of history not only to what “there is” but also to what “may be”—, we are in absolute agreement with the distinguished professor of Columbia University.

The history of history is nothing but the logical extension of historical knowledge in one of its genuine directions. Contemporary erudition has brought forth this relatively new order of activity.

Finally, Dilthey’s thought, which Dr. José Gaos has spread in the classrooms of Mexico, upholds one more activity within the philosophical ones: the “philosophy of Philosophy.” According to Dilthey, the possible attitudes toward the universe are of an infinite variety, and there is nothing that permits us to calculate them and trace their limits beforehand. Nevertheless, by comparing the various systems with one another, it is possible to single out certain principal motives, certain ways of seeing and thinking, which are not exclusive to one philosopher in particular, but are found in some and lacking in others. Bruno, Spinoza, Hegel uphold a philosophical stance of universal sympathy, which leads them to inquire into the unity of the Whole, and makes them feel themselves integral parts of a Universe in which all dissonances are resolved in an infinite harmony.

On the other hand, the philosophies of a Kant and a Fichte uphold as their dominant motive the feeling of personality. For these two great philosophers the supreme value is moral dignity, which secures one’s own independence in the face of a world against which one will always be in struggle.

Instead, then, of a unitary system, Dilthey’s philosophical and historical genius aims at this “philosophy of Philosophy” or, better said, the Philosophy of philosophies. History and Philosophy are, in sum, two routes traveled by the human intelligence in its perennial longing to know, that is, to explain and comprehend.

### EL MESIANISMO HEBREO — HEBREW MESSIANISM

[p. 268] Years ago there was published in Italy a series of “apologies” of the various religions: Catholicism, Protestantism, Hebraism, Buddhism, paganism and Taoism. The authors of each apology were distinguished Italian specialists; for example, the monograph on Taoism is the work of the eminent sinologist G. Tucci. The apology of Hebraism was written by Dante Lattes. It is a brief and substantial monograph, consisting of four parts: the Ideal, the Action, the Future and the Law. The ideal is constituted by the God of Israelite monotheism; the action is symbolized in the Prophets. The future signifies Hebrew messianism; the law is confined to the study of the Pharisees.

The monotheistic ideal is stated in these words of Isaiah: “I am the Eternal and there is no other besides me; Creator of light and of darkness. I am the Eternal who made all things.”

This rigorous monotheistic ideal constitutes the lasting honor of the people of God. Israel is the only historical people that always affirmed absolute monotheism; hence for the understanding of the philosophy of history the Greco-Roman antecedent does not suffice, but one must refer to the Hebrew ideal of the one God. The whole moral world of the West bears within itself the seal of the Israelite ideal.

II. Prophetism, beginning with Amos, already realizes the foundations of a philosophy of history; because the prophet is the constant rectifier of the moral and political life of the Hebrew people. If the kings and the men depart from the national ideal, which is monotheism; if they fall into idolatrous practices through imitation of the neighboring peoples, who constituted the most powerful monarchies in the history of Antiquity, the prophet intervenes to teach the people that one must struggle for an ideal capable of bringing death, but which, likewise, promises future days of happiness. The prophet is, says Dante Lattes, “the fortified city, the iron pillar, the bronze wall against which every established and material power breaks, every form without spirit, every force without morality.”

Israelite prophetism signifies another essential element for the understanding of the philosophy of history.

[p. 269] But in this article we wish to insist above all on the messianic idea within the Hebrew conception. According to this idea, the present is never perfect; “the prophet’s gaze is directed continually toward something that is not reality today, but that must be the reality conquered for tomorrow.”

According to the thesis we formulate, man is not permitted to take refuge in solitude and abstain from all dealings with the corrupt world.

We must live the life that is given us, in order to correct it with constant effort and unlimited tenacity, “because only through will and action can existence be reformed. Beauty must reside in life, not outside it. The highest creation that man can produce by himself consists in converting earthly life into a harmonious, pure life, happy for all.”

The influence of messianism, so conceived, is plainly seen in some of the contemporary social doctrines, which aspire to the attainment of the great good of peoples, not in another region of the universe, but in this one in which contemporary history unfolds, censurable for more than one moral reason.

Whereas Christianity, like Saint Augustine (in whom the two traditions, Greco-Roman and Israelite, converge), opposes to the City of men the “City of God,” implicit in the Church, Hebrew messianism, which does not confess Jesus Christ, EXPECTS THE GOOD IN THE WORLD, IN SPITE OF EVIL; it thinks of its unfailing realization, it asserts that our human mission consists, fundamentally, in never despairing, in believing —this constitutes the essence of messianism itself— that, at the “end of times,” the happiness of peoples will be realized in this sphere of reality.

III. IN SUM, THERE ARE BUT THREE POSSIBLE HYPOTHESES: THE MESSIANIC OPTIMISM OF THE HEBREWS, THE CHRISTIAN BELIEF WHICH, IN ITS RELATION TO HISTORY, SAINT AUGUSTINE FORMULATED IN HIS CELEBRATED BOOK (BY OPPOSING TO THE CITY OF MEN THE CITY OF GOD), AND THE PESSIMISTIC ATTITUDE WHICH, WITH SCHOPENHAUER, DENIES ALL MEANING TO HISTORY.

Hegel’s system constitutes the other great speculative conception of the philosophy of history, and is similar in its intention to the Augustinian thesis; pessimism, on the other hand, knows beforehand, with Schopenhauer, that what is called the history of humanity is fundamentally devoid of meaning; because all historical formations, in their fleetingness, reveal [p. 270] that the essence of man is an unsated and insatiable will, which avidly seeks its own contentment, without ever being able to find it.

Hence the great German writer, to give an idea of the meaning of universal history, resorts to the classical myths of Tantalus, of Sisyphus and of the Danaids. Man, being as he is the creature and phenomenon of the will to live, will constantly seek his good without ever finding it; and will never cease wanting to achieve the impossible, precisely because his essence is will!

To this systematic pessimism, Nietzsche added his epic Dionysian exclamation: “You are beautiful, life, and therefore I love you though you be cruel”... Israelite messianism, on the other hand, is an optimism that believes in the attainment of earthly good among men.

### NIETZSCHE Y DILTHEY — NIETZSCHE AND DILTHEY

Don Eugenio Ímaz publishes, as the crown of his excellent labor, the “theory of the Conception of the World” by W. Dilthey. Also, the translator himself, in number 35 of Jornadas, has offered us his “Siege of Dilthey.” The siege, in the end, overthrew the fortress; and uncovered or unveiled it, in its integrity, before the Spanish-speaking readers of both continents. Here is an effort in which Hispanic-American culture clearly manifests itself.

Among the Diltheyan pages lately put into Spanish, on page 431 of the Theory of the Conception of the World, a note on Nietzsche is inserted, through which the radical opposition that obtains between Dilthey and Nietzsche is manifested in all its magnitude. According to Dilthey, “Nietzsche has embodied the ultimate consequence that can be drawn from the negation of knowledge in its logical course.”

For, first, for Nietzsche, the artist —according to his critic— represented the “creator of culture.” Later, the creator of culture was represented by “the scientific consciousness.” But Nietzsche also doubted this second stance of his, until he affirmed that the true creator of culture is the philosopher who establishes or “creates values.”

“The great misery of Nietzsche is that of a hypertensive subjectivity,” concludes Dilthey. Against this subjectivity and its unheard-of tension, the philosopher of historicism evokes the objectivity of human culture in its integrity. The conflict that obtains between Nietzsche and Dilthey could not be more interesting, more radical!

[p. 271] In this article we are going to try to describe the “subjective tension” of Nietzsche, who rebels against history and its action, for him depressing in some of its essential aspects, in contemporary culture, turned wholly toward the esteem of the historical.

Here are the Nietzschean allegations:

“It is possible —says Nietzsche— to live without remembering; but it is absolutely impossible to live without forgetting.” This forceful maxim is revealing of the philosopher’s stance, which is expressed above all in the celebrated “Untimely Consideration” entitled: “On the Utility and Disadvantage of Historical Studies for Life.”

Life, of course, implies tradition and memory; but the originality of the instant that is lived is real; it cannot be reduced in its integrity to the historical. He who always refers to the past does not live the “today” but a “yesterday,” near or remote.

In five ways the oversaturation of an epoch by history can be dangerous to life. It is so, in the first place, because it weakens personality, engendering contradiction between the inner and the outer world. Further, the excess of historical studies in a given epoch provokes the illusion that said epoch possesses the rarest of all virtues: justice. Further, the excess of historical studies prevents the people and the individual from attaining their own maturity. It also propagates the belief that we contemporaries are retarded beings, mere epigones. Lastly, it provokes skepticism, and even cynicism; because the epoch is insistently oriented “toward a suspicious and selfish practicism, which ends by paralyzing and destroying the vital force.”

Nietzsche fears that history ceaselessly displaces the perspectives of man; for in transforming his horizon it suppresses the real atmosphere that surrounds him, “which no longer allows him to act and feel from the ‘non-historical’ point of view.” Then one may arrive, perhaps, even at “skill,” but never at “wisdom.” The young man grows old through his preference for the historical. How could a generation poisoned with historicism be, in such a case, a generation “creator of values”?

But against the poisoning caused by an excess of historicism there are two antidotes: the “non-historical” and the “supra-historical.” The “non-historical” [p. 272] is the strength to be able to forget and to shut oneself up within a limited horizon. “The ‘supra-historical’ is that which turns the gaze away from becoming, directing it toward what gives existence a character of eternity.”

The reader will have been able to impregnate his spirit, by means of the foregoing eloquent allegories, with the profound, energetic feeling of the aversion of the philologist of Basel toward history. Nietzsche sees a danger in it. It is the danger of eliminating the constant risk of living the present with complete authenticity. Art frees things from relations. It offers them in their full individuality to human intuition. For this reason artistic contemplation, like the creation of works of art, is something that takes us out of time and places us before eternity.

The “non-historical” is this strength to be able to forget, to constrict the horizon, to make it, in truth, authentic as well. It concerns what is here and now, which is not dissimulated in historical becoming, which is not diminished or destroyed by the accumulation of the processes that generate the present instant. This is what Dilthey describes as the “hypertense subjectivity” of Nietzsche; what leads to his “great misery.”

The opposition between the two spirits could not be more profound. To understand the human, the organ, according to Dilthey, is history. According to Nietzsche, history clouds the concrete instant of living. In a forthcoming article we shall see Dilthey’s objections to the Nietzschean position.

### LA OBJETIVIDAD DE LA HISTORIA — THE OBJECTIVITY OF HISTORY

The struggle between Dilthey and Nietzsche is the one that lies between subjectivism, which denies knowledge in its discursive, logical course, and historical objectivism, which cannot accept that “intelligence is a development in the isolated individual, comprehensible from him, but rather constitutes a process in the development of the human race,” as Dilthey declares, in setting forth, in 1880, the fundamental idea of his philosophy. That is to say, for Nietzsche individual subjectivity is the basis of the philosophical attitude, whereas for his critic “the subject in which the will to knowledge occurs is the human race in its development.”

For this reason Nietzsche passes from one ideal to another: from the conception of the artist as creator of culture, to the exaltation of scientific consciousness, and from this second exaltation to that of the philosopher as creator of values.

[p. 273] But this end point at which the Nietzschean philosophical attitude arrives, according to Dilthey, is not authentically Nietzschean; for the exaltation of the positive value of life is found in Thrasymachus and Critias, in Spinoza and Hobbes, in Feuerbach and Stirner, thinkers who expressed forcefully the affirmation of the will and its power.

What ought to have been found in the Nietzschean work is the indication of which is the most valuable —among the motley figures that the will to live produces. That is to say, in Nietzsche there is found an atmosphere of weighing values; it may be said that the climate in which contemporary axiology could arise is owed, in good part, to Nietzsche; but one does not find in this philosopher an attempt at a definition of what value is; although a philosophy of values is constantly extolled.

“We find no answer to this in the books of Nietzsche.” It is because his attitude was negative with regard to the true sciences. He rejected psychology as a science, although he made himself champion of psychological hypotheses about the origin of moral norms, “as if they were scientific results.”

The philologist of Basel offers us the individual detached from the bonds of culture, from its final bonds, and thus loses the connection with something progressive and solid.

By contrast, according to Dilthey, one must pursue an ideal, a fundamental idea of philosophy, which until now has not been placed as the basis of philosophizing. Idealists and empiricists alike have mutilated “the whole and true reality.” In Kant intellectualism arises anew, as a form of mere thinking, “as the original place of the absolute in us”; but empiricism too starts from the basis of a mutilated experience, deformed in advance by a theoretical conception: the atomism of psychic life.

This ideal of fullness is opposed by Dilthey to Nietzschean individualism and subjectivism. For this reason he declares that “intelligence is not a process in the isolated individual, but rather constitutes a process in the development of the human race.”

History has to be appraised with this affirmation of the fullness of experience as the basis of philosophy. Idealism and empiricism are content with similar abstractions. They do not respect total experience, but mutilate it.

[p. 274] Consider, in confirmation of these thoughts of Dilthey on the abstract character of empiricism, the attitude of positivism when it affirms that all knowledge is born of experience and is reduced to it. What positivism considers total experience is no more than one aspect of it; for this reason WE BELIEVE THAT THE POSITIVIST ATTITUDE CAN BE DEFINED BY DECLARING THAT IT IS A SYSTEMATIC PREJUDICE, WHICH SELECTS EXPERIENCE ARBITRARILY.

Dilthey’s constant purpose consists in respect for that “TOTAL, FULL, UNMUTILATED EXPERIENCE, WHICH IS ALL THE WHOLE AND TRUE REALITY.”

Nevertheless, perhaps, for this very consideration of the fullness of life and experience as the basis of philosophy, both the individualist subjectivity of a Nietzsche and the longing for totality of a Dilthey may be fitting!

Diderot once wrote: “I love fanatics; if by chance they find some truth, they set it forth with an energy that breaks and overthrows everything.” One of these fanatical spirits is Nietzsche. His passion against history is formulated in the aphorism: “It is possible to live without remembering; but it is impossible to live without forgetting.” For in truth there is something that refers to the fullness of experience in the vindication, fanatical if you will, but energetic and real, of the present instant in consciousness, which cannot be reduced, absolutely, to the tradition and solidarity of experience and life in history! For this reason Nietzsche is the author of the celebrated expression: “Creation of values”; an expression that defines a very principal aspect of life; it being true, on the other hand, that the contradictory Nietzschean individualism, as Dilthey affirms, first granted the artist the role of creator of culture; then, obeying the atmosphere of positivism that reigned in his time, granted the representation to scientific consciousness —“The cock-crow of positivism!”—; to give the palm at last to the philosopher as creator and establisher of values.

### LA SOCIOLOGÍA DEL PROGRESO — THE SOCIOLOGY OF PROGRESS

I. The family of Auguste Comte was “monarchist and Catholic”; but very soon the young Comte emancipated himself from family rigor, and sought new [p. 275] paths of thought, as he acquired a sturdy scientific education. Nevertheless, he never wholly abandoned the Catholic form of speculation, nor the huge need for order, which he always wished to combine with his idea of social progress.

Tradition and solidarity explain, according to the philosopher, social life; whence he turns out to be a disciple, at once, of the traditionalism of De Maistre and De Bonald. In sum, both the scientific speculation of the eighteenth century and the feeling of progress as a law of the social were combined in the mind of the French thinker with his passion for order and its force in social relations. Hence he did not accept, in its totality, the legacy of the eighteenth century. He always showed himself a cordial enemy of what he called the “negative spirit” of Rousseau and Voltaire. Only counteracting causes of the success of positivist thought would arise, according to him, from the destructive inheritance of the eighteenth century, which Voltaire and Rousseau inspired, each in his own way pretending to destroy that European tradition, dear to Comte.

One of the most brilliant thoughts of the great sociologist is the one that affirms that we the living live from the dead, and ever more so; because, as the conquests of civilization are capitalized, so to speak, in the store of human culture, the more indebted we moderns turn out to be to the benefits that the dead provided us.

On the other hand, no one affirms social progress more than Comte. In this respect he continues the series of great philosophers who, since the Renaissance, affirmed the idea of progress as a necessary element of social evolution. Like Bacon, Pascal and Leibniz, Comte is a staunch devotee of progress. For this reason he finds his “immediate precursors” in Turgot and Condorcet. In reality the celebrated “law of the three states,” theological or fictitious, metaphysical or abstract, and scientific or positive, according to the philosopher’s own expression, is not original to him but to Turgot; and this law is not only that of the intellectual, philosophical and scientific development of humanity; it constitutes the law of its historical evolution. Now this supreme generalization of positivism, which Mill called “the backbone of the system,” is a law of the necessary progress of humanity, of society, and not only of science in its course.

This precious endeavor always to synthesize order with progress is perhaps the most original attribute of Comte’s sociological thought. His sociology, which has not yet been studied in all its parts with the same diligence, always seeks to realize the synthesis of order and progress, owing above all the idea of the progressive to the genial thought [p. 276] of Turgot. Here is the statement the celebrated economist gives of his ideas on social progress: “Before the relation of physical effects among themselves was known, nothing was more natural than to suppose them produced by intelligent, invisible beings, like ourselves... When the philosophers had recognized the absurdity of these fables —without having acquired, nonetheless, true enlightenment about natural history— they imagined explaining the causes of phenomena by means of abstract expressions, such as ‘essences’ and ‘faculties,’ which nevertheless explained nothing. It was much later, by observing the mechanical action that bodies have upon one another, that there were obtained from this mechanics other hypotheses than those that mathematics could develop and experience verify.” The illustrious French philosopher Léon Brunschvicg, commenting on the paragraph of Turgot just copied, says: “Around 1751 positivism is defined by this young man of twenty-five, with a precision that Comte will not surpass” (El progreso de la Conciencia en la filosofía occidental, p. 502 of the French edition).

But it would be a supreme injustice to think that the law of the three states, already enunciated by Turgot, is worth the same in the great economist’s statement as in the vast synthetic speculation of the great French philosopher. This idea of social progress, which Comte inherits from Turgot and Condorcet, is bound closely, in Comtean thought, with the other part of the inheritance received by the founder of positivism: the exaltation of the idea of order, deriving from the Catholic thinkers De Maistre and De Bonald.

Furthermore, the social ideas of Saint-Simon, the celebrated “utopian socialist,” influence the Comtean synthesis; with his demands he impressed the mind of the young Comte, for a time his enthusiastic adept.

The synthesis of order and progress nevertheless required a basis, which Auguste Comte found in love, in social “altruism” (a word Comte himself coined). Then the sociology of progress in its relations with love and order was complete, and the motto of positivism began to run its brilliant career in the world, until it was inscribed on the flag of a great people, Brazil.

This idea of social progress, which Bacon, Pascal and Leibniz championed, is deposited in the sociology of Comte, but intimately bound up with the concern for order. Nothing is more abominable to the philosopher than the course, which believes itself progressive and in the end proves regressive, of the systematic destruction of the past. All this is said, insuperably, in another of Comte’s brilliant aphorisms: “One destroys only what [p. 277] one replaces.” Therefore, departing as much from Voltaire as from Jean-Jacques, the sociology of Auguste Comte devotes itself to its synthetic, profound work.

### LA NOCIÓN DEL PROGRESO EN LA EDAD MEDIA — THE NOTION OF PROGRESS IN THE MIDDLE AGES

Saint Vincent of Lérins died in 456 after Jesus Christ, in the monastery of Lérins, on the coast of Provence. His Memorial, which Cardinal Baronius calls a “golden book,” is often cited by theologians, controversialists and apologists. It contains the practical rule that says: “In the Catholic Church, one must take great care to profess what has been believed always, everywhere and by all”: Quod semper, quod ubique, quod ab omnibus.

In this Memorial the conception of progress is set forth in an extraordinary manner, as the reader will see presently: “Someone will say, perhaps: is there no progress in the religion of the Church of Jesus Christ? There is, on the contrary, a very great one... But it must be a progress of faith and not a change. Progress consists in a thing growing while remaining the same; change occurs if something is transformed into something else. Therefore let wisdom, science, intelligence grow and develop abundantly, from century to century, in individuals as in the whole body of the Church; but let it always be in the same thought, in the same sense and in the same belief.”

No writer of classical Antiquity can be compared to Saint Vincent of Lérins in the matter of the notion of progress. If the foregoing text is already astonishing, the rest of the citation that follows is still more extraordinary; for in it it will be seen how the thinker of the Church supplies the biological reason for progress: “Let the religion of souls follow the law that governs bodies; which, even when they develop over the course of whole years, remain always the same.”

As Eucken observes in his book on the great currents of contemporary thought, Antiquity, whose civilization has chiefly an aesthetic sense, preferred what remains unalterable and perfect to what changes and varies. Being is esteemed above becoming; the theory of forms, created by Plato and developed by Aristotle, presents the forms themselves to us “as models and fundamental forces of things; they preserve themselves immutable throughout the whole cosmic [p. 278] process, which has neither beginning nor end. All modification comes from matter.”

Unlike the great Greek poets, who placed the “golden age” in the remote past, the Christians, in philosophizing, imply the idea of progress in the very notion of the advent of Christ, which gives a new meaning to universal history. Already in Saint Augustine the idea of progress exists, notably; but it is Saint Vincent of Lérins who, with greater perfection, furnishes in his writings the metaphysics of progress. For —he writes— “there is a great difference between the flower of youth and the maturity of old age; but they are the same persons, with their own nature, who pass through the various ages of man. The vigorous limbs of the adult are the same that were tender and weak in the child; and even the organs that develop only very late exist as rudiments in the embryo. There is nothing in the body of the old man that is not already in that of the infant.”

In Saint Thomas Aquinas, in Roger Bacon, very important texts can be found on the affirmation of the notion of progress; but none, perhaps, is more definitive and admirable than the one cited above from Saint Vincent of Lérins.

In sum, one can understand the position of the Christian thinker, who counsels at once, as a criterion of truth, to regard as genuine what has been believed always, everywhere and by all: quod semper, quod ubique, quod ab omnibus, and to hold that progress, nevertheless, occurs within this primal identity. One thing is transformation, becoming something other than what one is; and quite another, to progress within one’s own nature. Progress does not deny the entity that progresses: but it makes it progressive; that is, better each time. Just as life in each living being progresses without changing the nature of the being that lives, so too the Church can progress within its perfect identity; the human personality is precisely this: development without transformation.

Things are transformed, they change, they become things different from what they were; the person, by contrast, like the organism, is progressive. The character of life is, then, progress. Not so matter; material things are not progressive; in evolving, they are transformed. Life maintains its unity and unfolds it. Likewise history maintains its unity in unfolding it.

[p. 279] The modern thinkers, beginning with Nicholas of Cusa, will affirm the idea of progress, from century to century, and the notion will count among its adherents the most profound intelligences. Such a notion becomes a kind of collective belief of the West. Pascal, like Francis Bacon, affirms progress. Leibniz, Kant, Herder, Fichte, Schelling, Hegel, Comte, Spencer, Bergson—all affirm progress! The sole denial comes from pessimism. Schopenhauer, Eduard von Hartmann, Nietzsche consider progress an illusion. Yet Nietzsche, within his idea of the “eternal return,” similar to the “orbicular return” of the Stoics, affirms the advent of the superman. But the first time progress was affirmed with full consciousness of the affirmation is in the writings of Saint Vincent of Lérins, in the fifth century after Jesus Christ.

### ROGER BACON

I. From 1214 to 1292 there flourishes in England one of the greatest minds of the Middle Ages, Roger Bacon. No one equals him as a scientific thinker among all the spirits of that epoch. Here, for example, are a few lines from the Opus majus, Bacon’s principal title before posterity: “According to the rules we have established above, it is easy to conclude that the smallest objects may appear very large and the largest very small, according to whether the glasses through which they are seen are convex or concave... For we can cut and arrange glasses in such a form, with respect to our sight and external objects, that the luminous rays are broken and refracted in the direction and at the angle we desire in order to see objects, whether far or near... We should make, so to speak, the sun, the moon and the stars descend, calling down their images from the earth. Sic etiam faceremus solem et lunam et stellas descendere secundum apparentiam hic inferius.” The telescope, centuries before Galileo, was the offspring of the mind of the “Wonderful Doctor.” And so too he foretold bridges from bank to bank of rivers without intermediate supports, and machines that would overcome the resistance of the air, flying in it...

It is not extraordinary that so extraordinary a cultivator of physical experience should be one of the staunch affirmers of progress.

A representative of the scientific and practical genius of his race, not only physical experience but also the other, “mystical experience,” is affirmed by him. [p. 280] He distrusts the abstract dialectic which, as the second Bacon will say, “binds the spirit but not things”; but he does not distrust mystical experience, which delivers to him the transcendent and supernatural world. In his conception, the natural sciences, as we now say, physics and its truths, are contained in the revelation of Holy Scripture; only man is not capable, because of the limited power of his intelligence, of penetrating the whole hidden sense in the inspired verses. Hence, to mystical experience and faith in revelation there must be added the cultivation of experimental science. Therefore both experiences, the physical and the mystical: revelation and experimental investigation!

In the same “Great Work,” this “Doctor mirabilis” formulates his conviction of progress in the following terms: Seneca, in Bacon’s words, rightly maintained that the opinions of the ancients must have lacked exactness and solidity, because men, novices and clumsy in investigation, groped blindly in the search for and attainment of truth. Seneca also affirmed that a time would come when what is hidden today would be revealed to future generations. But to achieve such discoveries days and centuries do not suffice:

“The future will know what we ignore and will be astonished that we ignored what it will know. Nothing is yet perfect in human inventions, nor has anyone said the last word. The later men come into the world, the broader their lights, because, last heirs of past centuries, they enter into possession of all the goods that the labor of the centuries accumulated for them.”

Here is the most complete affirmation of progress. The great thinker lives convinced that progress is one of the attributes of history; and he refers to Seneca, who was among the ancients one of the few authors in whom the notion of progress is glimpsed.

The consequences Bacon draws, relative to his conviction, are almost as important as the conviction itself: “Let us take care not to submit servilely to every opinion we find in books or in the mouths of men. Let us examine attentively the thought of the ancients in order to supply their omissions and to correct their faults with deference and modesty.”

This attitude accords with the Baconian theories on physical experience and mystical experience; for what is indubitable is Scripture; before [p. 281] it, which is revelation, our human role is that of interpreters; likewise before nature; our role is that of interpreters. By contrast, before the opinion of the ancients we are not to swear by their word nor bow before their teachings however wise they be. One must exercise the critical spirit upon the opinions of the ancients; but, to be sure, “with deference and with modesty.”

The texts cited in this article prove how the idea of progress springs from the Middle Ages and bears a close relation to the Christian tradition. Already in Saint Augustine there is the succession of Nature, Law and Grace; a succession that indispensably implies the thought of progress. In Saint Vincent of Lérins we have been able to find the biological reason for progress in the assimilation of the growth of souls and institutions to the development of bodies, which maintain themselves in their unity within their development. For this reason progress is a modern idea; because it springs from the Middle Ages and Christian philosophy, which is opposed to the Stoic thought of cyclical revolutions, according to the thought of Marcus Aurelius: “The things of the world are always the same, in their orbicular turnings, from century to century.”

### SANTO TOMÁS DE AQUINO — SAINT THOMAS AQUINAS

In his commentaries on the Ethics and the Politics of Aristotle, Saint Thomas Aquinas has bequeathed to us his theory of progress, or rather his theory of intellectual progress: “It belongs to the nature of man to use reason to investigate truth. Therefore man must advance, gradually, in his discovery.” The foregoing is the first thesis that the Aquinate affirms. The thought has two parts, intimately connected with each other: reason is the investigator of truth, in accordance with our human nature. The intellectualism of Saint Thomas holds that only reason attains truth. If this is so, the unavoidable consequence follows that truth is attained “only gradually”; because the human mind is not endowed with the divine power which, without the passage of time, “sees all things in one and brings them to one,” as the Imitation of Christ says. Our reason, discursive and not intuitive, must avail itself of time for the attainment of truth.

This is precisely the second thesis subscribed to by Saint Thomas: “Only time permits great discoveries to be made and ultimate precision to be reached. [p. 282] Not because time, by itself, really cooperates in this difficult work, but rather because the work is accomplished in time.” Then the whole effort of the reason that investigates is a successive effort. Human work is accomplished by parts. Therefore progress is an aspect of intellectual action.

“If someone applies himself to the investigation of truth, time cannot fail to lend him its precious assistance, making him see later what he did not manage to see at once.” This manner of working intellectually, in the individual, explains for the philosopher why intellectual progress has been taking place in history. For “the same will happen for those who come afterward. Inspired by the discoveries of their predecessors, they will make new ones; and this is how the sciences and the arts have developed.” It already appears, in the lines above, that the thought of the moderns, of equating individual development with that of the species, is expressed in the foregoing citation of Saint Thomas. Pascal will maintain that humanity is like a single man who constantly learns. In the Thomist thesis, if individual application to the attainment of the true makes one see later what is not attained at once, the application of the successive generations to the acquisition of science must produce the same result: the forward march, which is precisely what progress means.

For this reason Saint Thomas concludes: “This is how the sciences and the arts have developed. What was known at the beginning was very imperfect. What was added to the first discoveries is very considerable. Each one placed his stone in the intellectual edifice begun by his predecessors.” This last image corroborates the whole doctrine. The work of intellectual progress is accomplished by parts, given the intellectual nature of man. In the same way that a wall is raised or a building constructed, humanity has built or edified the admirable monument of science.

And in his commentary on the Politics what has been said in the commentary on the Ethics is corroborated; for if the cause is lacking, the effect necessarily disappears. For this reason Saint Thomas affirms: “If study and investigation were neglected, time, according to Aristotle’s observations, would be rather a cause of forgetfulness and decadence; equally for man in particular and for men in general.” That is to say, progress is not something irresistible and fatal; it does not constitute a necessary ascent of the mind toward science, but admits regressions, as history proves: [p. 283] “In this way it is explained how several sciences, flourishing in Antiquity, have declined, for not having been cultivated afterward.”

What is incontestable, given the foregoing judicious observations, is that the possibility and the reality of intellectual progress are grounded in the nature of man. But not all progress is intellectual. Would the philosopher of the Middle Ages likewise affirm artistic progress and moral progress? ...

What we verify historically is purely intellectual progress: the development of the sciences and of their technical and industrial applications. It is evident that the physics of today, as science, greatly surpasses the physics of Saint Thomas, as modern industry also surpasses the industry of the Middle Ages; but man is not only pure reason, but feeling and will. Does intellectual, scientific, technical progress assure by itself the progress of morality, the progress of art? Evidently not. And if it is true, as Saint Thomas affirms, that ad hominis naturam pertinet ratione uti ad veritatis investigationem [it belongs to the nature of man to use reason to investigate truth], the exercise of the will and the life of feeling and emotion also correspond to human nature. And the final problem is this: has man progressed in the moral, as he progresses in the intellectual? Perhaps Saint Thomas would answer that, the will having been more contaminated by guilt than the intelligence, it is necessary that moral progress not develop in the same form as the intellectual; but will the development of intelligence and of science ever succeed in overcoming the rebelliousness of the will and directing it toward the attainment of the good? ...

### KANT Y LA IDEA DE PROGRESO — KANT AND THE IDEA OF PROGRESS

In his essay On the Relation of Theory to Practice, Kant considers the idea of progress in relation to the hypothesis of universal determinism.

“The history of the human race can be considered —Kant teaches— as the fulfillment of a hidden plan of nature to produce a perfect political Constitution.” Within this hypothesis, it occurs to us to regard universal history from the point of view of the fullness of association of the human species. The possibility of this consideration cannot be denied.

The species progresses constantly in what concerns culture; [p. 284] and Kant maintains that this is the natural end of man; for if at times progress is interrupted, it can never be wholly halted.

“Nature has willed that man should draw from himself whatever surpasses the mechanical constitution of his animal being.”

Then arises the problem of ascertaining whether the hidden end of nature consists in providing humanity with an easy life. The most elementary observation of human development shows that this is not so. Therefore the possible end of man is not comfort, ease of existence; rather we are to strive so that our life may be worthy rather than easy and pampered; man is endowed with freedom; determinism makes human freedom effective in action.

It is evident that the historical vicissitudes of our race testify against a systematic utilitarianism. The plan of nature is, probably, that humanity realize itself, with effort and heroism, in order to be worthy of the progress it attains.

Individuals and peoples do not imagine that in abandoning themselves to their own interest and struggling among themselves, they are engendering the possibility of an adequate and complete development.

Plato’s Republic is considered an example of an imaginary perfection that can find room only in the mind of a contemplative. And Brucker finds ridiculous the Platonic assertion that declared the principate impossible unless the ruler partakes of the Platonic “Ideas.” Kant maintains, against the foregoing opinion, that to reject political Platonism under the pretext of impracticability is an unacceptable philosophical conclusion; for the ideal Constitution of Nations can be formulated in these terms: “To realize the greatest human freedom possible, founding it upon laws that allow the freedom of each to accord with that of the others.”

Kant does not concern himself with well-being, nor does he express it in his formula; because he knows well that, as soon as the ideal Constitution were realized, the happiness of peoples would be its unavoidable consequence.

In another of the philosopher’s brilliant essays, on the “Idea of a Universal History from the Point of View of Humanity,” Kant formulated several propositions that complete his belief in progress. Society, he says, is an order in which the freedom of each is subject to coexisting [p. 285] with the freedom of the others. Man, always inclined to usurp and to abuse, has need of an authority that obliges him to obey a general will. Where is this authority to be found? “The absolute solution of the problem is impossible; but to approach the ideal incessantly is the only obligation imposed on us by nature.”

What happens is that man is endowed as much with an instinct of sociability as with a tendency to be unsociable. Progress will consist in perfecting sociability at the expense of this antisocial instinct. Of course, the purely intellectual problem cannot be solved in a manner different from that which the Constitution just discussed formulates. If man is free, the freedom of each must necessarily be put in accord with the freedom of the others, within a rational formula. There is no other means of harmonizing human wills in their plurality and efficacy; but this must not obscure how difficult it is “to make rational in his life, in his conduct, the only inhabitant of the earth that is endowed with reason.”

Hence Kant affirms progress as the result of his faith in reason, as a consequence of his belief in freedom. The point is that man lives, historically, in order to bring his will into accord with his reason, his action with his thought, his life with his ideal.

The difficulty of the progressive undertaking is not hidden from the philosopher. He knows well the whole set of negative conditions, of opposing causes to the empire of reason over life; but, pressed to solve the problem of conduct, he formulates in perfect terms the Constitution of human society; and he hopes that the acts of men will slowly converge upon that ideal end. As to happiness, he is not directly concerned with attaining it; for it will be the necessary consequence of the rationality of the design.

Here, in short, is the noble conception of idealism, which brings the second Socrates close to the extreme position proclaimed by that great Athenian citizen. Human effort will cause the will, like the arc of a circle whose ends are determined by the corresponding chord, to coincide at last with the points of the straight line. Right, that which is in itself straight, constitutes the secret of history.

### FICHTE

I. For Kant the form of knowledge is a priori; but, in his system, sensation would be explained by the existence of a transcendent object, distinct [p. 286] from the self, and in itself unknowable. Fichte affirms that matter as well as form are products of the creative activity of the self. Consciousness has as its foundation the very act of production.

First, there is the self positing itself (the thesis); then the self posits the not-self (the antithesis); and finally the self recognizes itself as forming a unity with the not-self; which constitutes the synthesis.

The self in question is not individual, but constitutes the absolute self. The supreme principle of all knowledge is the principle of identity: A is A. In the philosopher's opinion, what this principle affirms, in sum, is the identity of the absolute self with itself.

In this idealism, ought-to-be comes before being. The realist conception rests on weakness of character; for, as Falckenberg says, commenting on Fichte, "he who has risen to the feeling of the freedom of the spirit cannot be a realist, cannot take himself for a thing."

The self and the not-self limit each other reciprocally. The "Doctrine of Science" comprises a theoretical part and a practical one. In the theoretical part it deals with the degrees of knowing, from sensation to reason; in the practical part, with the degrees of willing. The objects of knowledge are only the necessary resistances to action.

The world is nothing but ought-to-be that has become sensible and material; and the various individuals are reduced to different conditions for the fulfilment of duty. A categorical imperative does exist; but it does not come to the self from outside; rather the self gives it to itself. What is called the external world is nothing but an indispensable condition of action; for to act is the absolute destiny of man.

Then, what is virtue? It consists in the concord of the self with itself. And with this definition the principle of identity is corroborated once more.

And it must always be remembered that the great German idealist was among the very rare men who were able to match their mind with their action. So as not to retreat before himself, he abandoned university chairs and delivered [p. 287] at the Academy of Berlin his famous "Addresses to the German Nation," in order to raise the spirit of those who had been defeated by Napoleon. Fleeing before the French armies, he came to give several courses at the very University that Kant honored with his teaching; and he died in Berlin in 1814, of a terrible fever, which he caught at the bedside of his wife, who had been infected with this disease while caring for the wounded, victims of the war. Truly, for this intrepid man, who did so much honor to human nature, the only thing real is the purest ideal, the moral order of the universe. This moral order is Fichte's god.

Treating of the "destiny of man," in another of his most famous books, the great thinker taught: "The final result of all that has been said is the following: The supreme harmony of man with himself, and, as a means of arriving at it, the conformation of all the objects that surround us to the necessary practical concept that we have of them (a concept that determines how they ought to be), is the last and supreme end of man. The correspondence of which we speak is, to use the terminology of the critical philosophy, what Kant called the Supreme Good; which in itself has not two parts, but is completely simple: IT IS THE PERFECT HARMONY OF A RATIONAL BEING WITH ITSELF."

II. In the Destiny of Man too, the Fichtean conception of progress is formulated. It is understandable that this intrepid idealist should have been one of the most fervent believers in the idea of progress. The fundamental reason for his belief is that no man does evil for the sake of evil, but for the good he expects from evil. But Fichte thinks that, once society is constituted as reason demands, evil actions, instead of bringing advantages to their authors, will surely harm them. And, as we saw before, the meaning of the world consists in making all things obey the ideal designs of the spirit. Existence must be conformed to morality; life must be made a copy of ought-to-be. In this consists the philosophy of identity, the doctrine of science, the ideal of the man of good.

And confident in the foundations of his philosophy, the heroic strength of his will and the longing for the realization of universal justice, Fichte says: "A day will come when the thought of evil will be erased from the human mind. No disturbance will any longer prevent, in the course of the centuries, all men from gravitating toward the good, with all the powers of the soul, with all their intellectual faculties. Then the wicked man will find no one to harm with impunity. He will find himself stripped of freedom and [p. 288] of the very will to do evil; for we cannot suppose that he would continue to love evil, if it must always have fatal consequences for him.

As is seen from the foregoing exposition, Fichte's idealism constitutes the prolongation of Kant's idealism. The primacy of practical reason over pure reason has reached its culminating point. The categorical imperative, duty, constitutes the meaning of existence.

### SCHELLING

Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling was born in 1775, in Leonberg, in Württemberg, and died in Ragaz (Switzerland) in 1854. His work constitutes one of the culminating "moments" in the development of German idealism. The philosopher's speculations on the meaning and value of art have an enduring value, because to the clear gifts of his intelligence Schelling adds a profound aesthetic capacity. He may be called, par excellence, the philosopher of Romanticism.

Here is how, in one of his most notable books, the System of Transcendental Idealism, he characterizes his own thought: "All knowledge rests on the concord of the objective with the subjective; for only truth is known, and the true is found in the concord of representations with their objects."

Nature is the substance of all that is purely objective. The self, or intelligence, the substance of all that is subjective. Both notions are mutually opposed. To explain the meeting of the objective with the subjective is the problem of all knowledge.

And there are only two possible cases: either the objective is what is posited first, the problem then being: how does it happen that the subjective agrees with the objective?; or else, if the subjective is supposed as the prior element, the problem is then to find out how the objective comes to be put in contact with it.

The first problem is that of the philosophy of nature. The second, that of transcendental philosophy.

The perfect theory of nature, according to Schelling, would consist in resolving everything natural into the intellectual element. And just as the sciences of [p. 289] nature, rising from facts to laws, tend to make idealism arise from realism, transcendental philosophy makes realism arise from idealism; because it materializes the laws of intelligence into laws of nature. And the philosopher adds this sublime thought, which proves unforgettable to whoever has once fixed his attention on it: "There are only two ways to leave common reality: poetry, which casts us upon an ideal world, and philosophy, which makes the real world vanish completely before us."

It is natural to ask what value Schelling grants to history within his system of identity. History, in his conception, passes through three different periods: Destiny rules the first period; Providence will rule the last; and between the two extends the period of Nature. In the first period, history is tragic. To it belong the decline and splendor of the wonders of the ancient world; the overthrow of empires whose memory is scarcely preserved, but whose ruins make us presume their grandeur.

The second period of history shows us no longer a wholly blind force, but a natural law, under which freedom bends in order to serve a plan of Nature. The fall of the Roman Empire has neither a tragic nor a moral side; rather, in accordance with natural laws, it had to be "necessarily and properly, a tribute paid to nature. And what in these two periods appeared as destiny or as nature will be manifested as Providence; so that what seemed the work of nature or of destiny was already, in sum, the beginning of a Providence that was imperfectly revealing itself. When will the providential period begin? We cannot say; but when this period begins, God will be."

For history is an epic composed in the spirit of God. It offers two principal parts: one represents the withdrawal of humanity from its center to its extreme limit; the other represents the return. The first part is, in a certain way, the Iliad; the second, the Odyssey of history. In the first there is a centrifugal direction, in the second the direction is centripetal. The supreme point of view of the universe is expressed in history.

But it is interesting to know that, notwithstanding the foregoing, Schelling does not affirm the moral progress of humanity as a certainty, but only as a belief.

Some consider only the moral advancement of man, "whose measure [p. 290] we so much desire, in truth, to possess"; others prefer to esteem the artistic and scientific advancement which, "from the historical (practical) point of view, follows rather a retrograde course, with respect to which we may refer to the example of the classical nations: Greece and Rome. And if the sole object of history is the successive realization of the constitution of right, nothing remains to us, as a measure of the advancement of the human spirit, "but the successive approximation to this end, whose definitive fulfilment can be proved neither by experience, as it has developed until now, nor theoretically, a priori. It will never be more than an eternal article of faith of man, engaged in the world of action."

In sum, as was set forth before, moral progress is not affirmed by Schelling with the certainty with which others affirmed it; but it constitutes a human belief; because the notion of history implies that of an infinite progressiveness.
